NOTE the view decides nothing: prices, area gates and unlock days were settled and tested in D-019, and this class turns three answers into RectTransforms. Props are `raycastTarget: false` so they cannot swallow drags meant for the scroll view. NOTE the editor step REFUSES to rebuild an existing hierarchy and only refreshes references, the same promise `MainScreenSceneBuilder` makes by refusing to overwrite a scene, and it finds the canvas via `MainScreenView` rather than taking the first `Canvas` — the HUD is its own Canvas prefab and building inside it would land on the wrong sorting layer. The root is the FIRST sibling because sibling order is the only thing deciding draw order in a Canvas and the grounds are a backdrop. NOTE this ships with NO tests, for the same structural reason as D-022: both types are MonoBehaviours and one is in the predefined Assembly-CSharp. Verification is compilation only — and this time the new files were explicitly added to the temporary csproj and confirmed present in its Compile list before the build was trusted, which is the gap that let a `CS0121` and a `CS0104` reach the user earlier in this session — affects: MetaGroundsView.cs (new), MetaGroundsSetup.cs (new), codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md, meta-plan.md (Adım 7, M10 closed) — 2026-08-20

D-024: The shop ships, and with it the meta system runs end to end for the first time: `MetaShopView` lists a location's remaining offers, previews where a selected prop would stand, and commits a purchase; `MetaGroundsView` gains the ghost preview and a `ViewedLocationChanged` event; `MetaShopSetup` builds the strip. Mechanically: offers come from `MetaPurchase.ShopItems`, each row's state from `MetaPurchase.Evaluate`, and the commit runs `Evaluate -> Wallet.TrySpendSoftMoney -> OwnedMetaItemIds.Add -> GameSession.Save -> grounds.Refresh + shop Refresh` — rationale: `meta-plan.md` Adım 8. **The commit order is the substance of this decision.** `TrySpendSoftMoney` is asked even though `Evaluate` already answered Ok, because the wallet is the compiler-enforced single writer of the balance (D-010) and its own answer is the authority; if the two ever disagree, a player must not end up owning something they did not pay for, and a refusal aborts before anything is recorded. **The flow is atomic by construction rather than by effort**, which is worth stating because it fell out of earlier decisions instead of being designed here: the money and the owned set both live on the same `GameSession` and `Save` writes them together, so a crash before `Save` loses both (the money comes back) and reaching `Save` writes both — "paid but did not receive" is not a state this code can produce. The ownership key comes only from `MetaCatalog.OwnershipKey`; a hand-joined string would be a second spelling of it, and that failure is the nasty kind — the purchase is written but never recognised again, so the player pays and keeps seeing the item on sale. Three further decisions worth keeping visible. (1) **Only three of the six verdicts reach the screen**, and that is what the enum bought: `ShopItems` drops owned and never-for-sale rows and a locked location empties the list, leaving buyable / too expensive / area-locked — and an `AreaLocked` row shows "Needs Square" rather than a dead button, which is the whole reason that verdict exists as its own case rather than a bool false. (2) **The row selects, the Buy button inside it commits.** Previewing where a prop goes must not cost money, and one tap doing both would make that impossible. (3) **The HUD is deliberately not touched** — it has followed the live wallet since D-022, so the coin count drops on its own; adding a second update path here is how two numbers start disagreeing. NOTE the GHOST preview is not a return of what D-016 deleted: that was a CATALOG field, authored per prop and part of the data model, while this is a translucent copy the view draws while a row is selected and destroys when it is not. It is held apart from the spawned props and redrawn AFTER them, so buying some other prop does not make it vanish for no visible reason, and it is skipped when the selected item does not belong to the viewed location. NOTE `ViewedLocationChanged` is a plain event rather than a reference to the shop, keeping the dependency one-directional — the grounds must not know what else is on screen. NOTE no tests, structurally: both types are MonoBehaviours in the predefined Assembly-CSharp; the purchase RULES have 25 tests from D-019 and this step calls them. Verification is compilation only, with the four touched files confirmed present in the csproj Compile list first — the gap that let a CS0121 and a CS0104 reach the user earlier in this session. NOTE finally that prices are still unauthored (open question M1): the shop works, but `MetaCatalogValidator` reports a purchasable prop priced at 0 as an error, so a usable catalog needs Adım 9 — affects: MetaShopView.cs (new), MetaShopSetup.cs (new), MetaGroundsView.cs (ghost preview + ViewedLocation + ViewedLocationChanged), codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md, meta-plan.md — 2026-08-20

D-025: The main screen becomes one button, and the day number moves onto it: `MainScreenView` loses `dayValueText` and writes "Continue Day X" into the Play button's own caption; `MetaGroundsView`'s location bar (`locationLabel`, `previousButton`, `nextButton`, `lockedHintLabel`) becomes OPTIONAL; `MainScreenSceneBuilder` stops creating the title and day captions — rationale: the user stripped the screen to Play + HUD and both components were still treating the deleted objects as REQUIRED, so every scene load logged two errors. The design change is the user's ("day value kısmını da butona continue day x şeklinde kullanalım") and it is the better shape anyway: the button now says what it will DO rather than a number beside it saying where the player is. Three decisions worth keeping visible. (1) **The button's label is found inside the button, not wired as a field.** The caption is already a child of the Play button in every scene that has one, so a serialized reference would be a third thing to forget on a screen whose missing references had just cost two errors. This is not the runtime lookup D-013 argued against: that was about finding an object elsewhere in the SCENE, while this searches the button's own children, and a button with no label degrades to a warning rather than a broken screen. (2) **The location bar is optional rather than removed.** With one location there is nothing to switch between, so requiring it forces scene clutter for a feature with no content yet — and deleting it is a legitimate authoring choice, not a mistake. It becomes meaningful when a second restaurant exists, and its absence is then visible on screen (no name, no arrows) rather than silent. `MetaGroundsSetup` still builds it, for whoever starts a fresh scene. Only four references stay required — `sessionHost`, `catalog`, `scroll`, `background` — because without them the screen cannot draw anything at all. (3) **`MainScreenView` still reads the PROFILE rather than the session this scene has carried since D-022**, which means the profile is loaded twice on this screen. Accepted rather than overlooked: taking it from the session needs another serialized reference, which is exactly the failure mode being cleaned up here, and the only value read cannot change while the player sits on a menu. NOTE the third log line the user reported was not an error at all — `HudWalletSource ... live GameState (session host: MainScreenRoot)` confirms D-022 resolved correctly, which is worth recording because that line is the only visible difference between a live HUD and one silently reading the save file. NOTE also trimming the scene builder changes nothing today, since it refuses to overwrite an existing scene; it exists so the builder and the scene cannot drift permanently apart — affects: MainScreenView.cs, MetaGroundsView.cs, MainScreenSceneBuilder.cs, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md, meta-plan.md — 2026-08-20

D-026: A new player opens the game with an AUTHORED coin balance (`GameConfig.StartingSoftMoney`, set to 1000 in `GameConfig.asset`), and the main screen gets an optional two-tap **Start Over** button that deletes the save file and reloads the scene — rationale: both halves are the user's ask ("main menuye bir tuş eklesek de en baştan başlatsa oyuna, bir de oyunun en başında 1000 coinimiz olsun"). Four decisions worth keeping visible. (1) **The number lives on GameConfig, not in code and not on EconomyConfig.** Content data is never embedded in code (root CLAUDE.md), and `GameConfig` is the only config asset BOTH scene roots already hold — `EconomyConfig` is the per-ticket tip math consumed by `EconomyCalculator` and is unreachable from the main screen, so a field there would mean a new serialized reference that can sit quietly unwired, which this screen has already paid for twice. The asset was edited in the same commit: a new serialized field reads 0 out of an existing `.asset`, the exact trap that produced the stale `EconomyConfig.asset` fixed in 50be70c. (2) **The grant is a property of the NEW-PLAYER PROFILE, not of the wallet.** `PlayerProfileStore.NewPlayer(grant)` is the one place that answers "what does a player who has never played hold" (it already answered it for lives), and `GameSession` passes it as the `Load` FALLBACK. So the money reaches the player through the existing `Wallet.ApplyPersistedBalances` call — no second writer of a balance, and the grant lands inside the day-start snapshot, so a new player's first retry cannot revert it away. A saved file always wins, so nobody is topped up on a later launch; an unreadable file (corrupt, or from a newer build) is treated as a new player deliberately, because this build cannot know what those numbers meant and dropping such a player to zero coins is the worse failure. (3) **Reset DELETES the file and reloads the scene; it does not patch anything.** Writing a fresh profile over the old one would require `PlayerProfileStore` to know every field — the payload knowledge it is built without — and a field forgotten there leaves a scrap of the old player behind. Reloading MainScreen rebuilds the session, the HUD, the grounds and the shop through their existing single writers, where editing the live `GameState` would need a second writer for the balance, the day index and the owned props. The view writes no value at all: it commands the file's owner and navigates. (4) **Two taps, and the button is optional.** One mis-tap must not be able to erase a real player's progress, and the cheapest guard that cannot be missed is the button contradicting itself for a few seconds (caption authored as `resetConfirmLabel`, restored by a coroutine so it cannot keep saying "tap again" after that tap stopped working) — a confirmation popup would be a second piece of UI to build and wire for one button. `resetButton` is optional so a MainScreen built before this existed still runs, which also means the existing scene needs `MainScreenResetButtonSetup` (menu step, touches only the open scene, never saves) — `MainScreenSceneBuilder` refuses to overwrite an existing scene, and hand-editing `MainScreen.unity` YAML is the review-proof edit D-007 declined — affects: GameConfig.cs, GameConfig.asset, PlayerProfileStore.cs, GameSession.cs, MainScreenView.cs, MainScreenSceneBuilder.cs, MainScreenResetButtonSetup.cs, GameSessionTests.cs, PlayerProfileStoreTests.cs, MainScreen.unity (Editor step, user-run) — 2026-08-20

D-027: The shop D-024 shipped is REVERTED — `MetaShopView` and `MetaShopSetup` are deleted, and the pieces `MetaGroundsView` had grown only for them (the ghost preview, `ViewedLocation`, `ViewedLocationChanged`, and `Refresh` being public) go with them. D-024 is superseded, not deleted: a decision record describing code that no longer exists is the decision-log version of a map that lies — rationale: requested by the user, who anticipated it when asking for the pre-shop commit ("belki geri alır farklı bi dükkan yaparız ama şimdi commit at da buraya geri dönebilelim") and then asked for it. No fault was found in the shop; a different design is wanted. **What was NOT reverted is the substance of this decision.** (1) `git reset` to the `meta-system-before-shop` branch was rejected outright: that commit predates the shop, so returning to it would also have deleted D-025 (the "Continue Day X" button, the optional location bar) and D-026 (the reset-progress button, the 1000-coin opening balance, `GameConfig.StartingSoftMoney`, `PlayerProfileStore.NewPlayer`) — the second of which is another session's work and has nothing to do with this request. The revert is therefore surgical. (2) `MetaResolver` and `MetaPurchase` STAY, with their 25 tests. Prices, area gates and unlock days are rules about the catalog, not about a screen; whatever shop replaces this one needs the same six verdicts. What was deleted is UI and the commit flow, not the model. (3) `PlayerProfile.OwnedMetaItemIds` STAYS at schema v4. Reverting it would mean v4 → v3, which makes every existing save unreadable — the cost of undoing a UI decision cannot be player data. The set is therefore back to the state D-020 recorded on purpose: it exists, and nothing writes it, because the only writer was the shop. NOTE the removed accessors were removed rather than kept "in case the next shop wants them": they had exactly one caller each, and an entry point with no caller reads as one that is used somewhere. Re-adding them is three lines. `Refresh()` went private for the same reason. NOTE the scene needed a manual step, and only because the user had already run `Build Meta Shop` and saved: deleting `MetaShopView` leaves `--MetaShop--` as a missing-script shell, which the user removes in one click. The scene YAML was NOT hand-edited to do it — that object is a GameObject plus RectTransform, Image, ScrollRect, the component and three children, i.e. a dozen interlinked fileIDs, which is exactly the edit D-007 declined; one click is cheaper and safer. NOTE the intermediate state is harmless: the shell does nothing but show a warning in the Inspector — affects: MetaShopView.cs (deleted), MetaShopSetup.cs (deleted), MetaGroundsView.cs, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md, meta-plan.md, MainScreen.unity (the user deletes one object) — 2026-08-21

D-028: Runtime code never resolves a scene reference — by name, by type, or by walking children. Every one is a `[SerializeField]` the author wires. Mechanically: `MainScreenView` gains `playLabel` and `resetLabel` fields and loses both `GetComponentInChildren<TMP_Text>()` calls; `MetaGroundsSetup` finds its root by COMPONENT instead of by the name `--MetaGrounds--`, and its now-unused `FindChild`/`RefreshExisting` helpers are deleted; `MainScreenResetButtonSetup` drops `FindButtonByName` entirely and its `Create` hands the caption back so both fields are wired from the object it just built; `MainScreenSceneBuilder` wires `playLabel` too — rationale: stated as a project rule by the user ("bundan sonra isimle arama falan yok sen serialize field yapıyorsun ben de sahnede sürüklüyorum"), generalising the instruction D-013 already recorded for the HUD's GameManager field. **The concrete failure that prompted it is worth keeping**: `MetaGroundsSetup` looked its root up by name, the user renamed the object, and the step therefore concluded the grounds did not exist — it would have built a SECOND hierarchy while its own log claimed it had left the existing one alone. A search is a guess, and it fails SILENTLY the moment the hierarchy changes. NOTE an argument for a lookup was made and overruled, and the record matters more than the outcome: D-025 defended finding the Play caption with `GetComponentInChildren` on the grounds that a third serialized field is a third thing to forget. The user rejected it. The objection is sound — a forgotten field is visible in the Inspector, whereas a search that starts matching the wrong object is visible nowhere. **Scope was decided by the user rather than assumed**, after an audit of every lookup in the project turned up three categories: (1) two runtime lookups, both converted; (2) editor tooling, where NAME lookups go but type lookups and `AssetDatabase.FindAssets` stay — they run at author time under the user's eye and the step logs what it wired, and removing them would break the Day Editor's config bar; (3) three sites the rule does not reach at all. **That third category is the part to remember**: `WorldTrayView`, `BoardItemDragHandler` and `TicketCardsView` call `GetComponentsInChildren` over children the code itself spawned at run time. Those objects do not exist at author time and vary in number, so no field can point at them. They are not exceptions to the rule — they are outside its subject, which is guessing which EXISTING scene object is meant, not reading the parts of something you just built. NOTE an unwired caption is a WARNING, not an error: a button with no label still works, it just says nothing, and killing the screen over a cosmetic gap is disproportionate; the buttons themselves stay required. NOTE also the reset step now treats its serialized field as the ONLY record of whether the button exists, so an author who clears the field and keeps the object gets a duplicate — accepted, because the alternative is the name search that caused this — affects: MainScreenView.cs, MetaGroundsSetup.cs, MainScreenSceneBuilder.cs, MainScreenResetButtonSetup.cs, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-21

D-029: The second shop begins as a SKELETON: `MetaShopView` owns a market button and a panel that opens and closes, and nothing else; `MetaShopSetup` builds that container in the scene. — rationale: `meta-shop-plan.md` Ş1, whose whole purpose is that each step is verifiable BY EYE, because this work is visual and I cannot see the screen. **Deviation from my own plan, deliberate:** the plan put the ghost API (`ShowGhost`/`ClearGhost`, public `Refresh`) in Ş1 too. It moved to Ş3. D-027 removed those exact members with the reason *an entry point with no caller reads as one that is used somewhere* — re-adding them in Ş1, where nothing calls them, would carry dead API for two steps and re-earn the criticism I had just recorded. Ş3 both adds and uses them. **What this step does NOT do is most of it:** it reads no data, writes no data, and touches no rule. `MetaResolver`/`MetaPurchase` and their 25 tests are untouched, the profile schema is untouched, and the panel is EMPTY — the rows come from the catalog at run time in Ş2 because their number depends on the location and on what is already owned, and the catalog is their single authority (D-015). Three things are load-bearing rather than cosmetic. (1) The panel is toggled from CODE, not by a Button-to-SetActive wire in the Inspector, even though the wire would do today's job: this class will own the shop's state machine (KAPALI/AÇIK/ÖNİZLEME) and the ghost's lifetime hangs off these same transitions, so `SetOpen` is the single place visibility changes. The first shop's ghost could leak precisely because its visibility had more than one owner (D-024). (2) Depth is sibling order inside ONE Canvas, and no second Canvas is created: the bug that ate the HUD this session came from two Canvases both sitting at sorting order 0. The grounds are the FIRST sibling, the shop root the LAST, and the market button is created AFTER the panel so it stays on top of it — not styling: the panel is a bottom sheet covering the bottom-right corner, so a button drawn underneath could not be tapped to close it and the shop would be a one-way door. (3) The panel background is `raycastTarget = true`, because the grounds behind it are a `ScrollRect` and a panel that does not eat raycasts lets a drag on the panel scroll the map — which reads as the panel being broken, not as a layering mistake. `MetaShopSetup`'s existing-shop path deliberately refreshes NOTHING: its references point at objects it created, so re-pointing them would require a search, which D-028 forbids. It names any empty field instead and leaves the fix to the author's drag or a delete-and-re-run. Both of `MetaShopView`'s references are REQUIRED (unlike the grounds' optional location bar, D-025) — a shop with no button cannot be opened and a button with no panel does nothing, so neither has a degraded mode worth shipping. Shop art is a PLACEHOLDER (coloured square reading SHOP) because the project has no shop art at all (MS5); a borrowed icon from another system would read as final. **Not done, and known:** the top-gap bug in `MetaGroundsSetup` (`viewport.offsetMax` insets the TOP edge while the location bar is anchored to the BOTTOM, so the inset was always on the wrong edge) is still in the code. The user fixed their own scene by hand and declined the code fix ("ya üst boşluğu düzelttim ben zaten"), so a scene built from scratch — the second location, when it comes — will show the gap again.

D-030: The shop's list is built by cloning an authored, INACTIVE row template kept in the scene -- `MetaShopRowView` binds it, `MetaShopView` clones one per offer, `MetaShopSetup` builds the template and the ScrollRect around it. — rationale: `meta-shop-plan.md` Ş2. The project already had two ways to make repeated UI: `TicketCardsView` instantiates a prefab ASSET, `TicketCardView` clones an authored template living in its own hierarchy (`modificationRowTemplate`, `timerDividerTemplate`) with a bind-only component on it (`ModificationSlotView`). A shop row is structurally the second case — a repeating icon+text row whose count only the catalog and the player's purchases know — so it follows it. Concretely: styling the one template restyles every row, and there is no second asset to keep in sync with the scene. **Which location is shopped comes from `MetaGroundsView.ViewedLocation`, which D-027 had deleted.** That deletion's reason was *no caller*, and Ş2 supplies the caller, so the objection lapses rather than being overridden. `ViewedLocationChanged` did NOT come back: the list is rebuilt every time the panel opens, and the only moment the player can walk to another location is while it is closed, so the event would be a publisher written before its subscriber. The payoff is that `MetaShopView` has **no `MetaCatalog` field**: the catalog reference stays in one component, where two of them free to point at two catalogs would break D-015's single authority with nothing on screen looking wrong. Four things are load-bearing. (1) The BUY listener is wired ONCE in `MetaShopRowView.Awake` and `Bind` only swaps the stored callback — adding it inside `Bind` would stack silently the first time a row is rebound, and a BUY that fires twice is the worst bug this screen could have. (2) `rowsParent` is forced ACTIVE and the template forced INACTIVE in `Start`: hiding the container to get the sample row out of sight is what silently hides every real row, a trap `TicketCardView` had already written down. (3) `Instantiate` copies the template's inactive state, so every clone needs an explicit `SetActive(true)` — the same two lines `TicketCardView` needs. (4) Scroll height is computed by `VerticalLayoutGroup` + `ContentSizeFitter` rather than authored: sixteen offers overflow a bottom sheet and the count changes with every purchase. All six of `MetaShopView`'s references are REQUIRED, unlike the grounds' optional location bar (D-025): listing the first location's props when `grounds` is unwired would present a WRONG list as though it were right the day a second restaurant exists, which is precisely the class of silent failure D-028 was written about. **MS1 and MS2 are settled by this step.** MS1 (price in the row) is yes, my recommendation, because otherwise comparing prices means opening every item's popup one at a time. MS2 stopped being a question: `MetaPurchase.ShopItems` already keeps the unaffordable and the area-gated in the list and drops only the owned, with the reason in its own comment — Ş2 draws that answer as-is. **Ş2 deliberately does NOT show WHY a row cannot be bought** (red price, disabled BUY, "Önce Meydan"): that is Ş5, and doing it here early would mean two places deciding what a verdict looks like. Every row looks the same and every BUY logs. Rows are destroyed and rebuilt rather than pooled — sixteen rows built on a tap do not need a pool, and a pool adds a path where a row survives with stale contents; `MetaGroundsView.Clear` made the same call.

D-031: The shop's preview is real: a row's BUY puts a translucent ghost of the prop where it would stand and a confirm popup over it, and `MetaShopView` owns the whole state machine that governs both. — rationale: `meta-shop-plan.md` Ş3, whose stated job is not drawing the popup but **closing every path by which the ghost could leak**. Two fields hold the machine: `isOpen` (KAPALI/AÇIK) and `pendingItem` (ÖNİZLEME when non-null). It stores the ITEM rather than a bool so the popup's labels and Ş4's commit read the same value and cannot come to disagree about which prop is being bought. Every transition passes through `ApplyVisibility`; every exit from a preview passes through `ClosePreview` — cancel, tapping outside, tapping a second row, the market button, closing the shop, and a location change on the grounds side. That single door is the decision: the first shop's ghost could survive the panel that summoned it (D-024) because more than one thing decided when it went away. **The list and the popup are never both on screen, and that is machine behaviour rather than styling.** The panel is a bottom sheet covering most of the map, and the ghost stands ON the map — so previewing with the list up would hide the very thing being previewed, and the feature would read as broken rather than as hidden. It also matches how the user described the flow ("ilk hayalet şeklinde yapının hayalet halini göreceğiz ve ardından üstünde yine bir popup olacak"). The consequence is that panel visibility is `isOpen && !previewing` — `activeSelf` is neither field on its own — and that rows are NOT destroyed while previewing: cancelling brings the list back rather than rebuilding it. Tapping a second row REPLACES the preview, because that is a change of mind and not an error. `ShowGhost` draws through `CreateProp`, the same path the owned props take, and `PropScale` became one method instead of two call sites so that it cannot drift: a preview that lies about where the prop lands is worse than no preview. Only the alpha differs, and it is a serialized field because it is cosmetic. The ghost is deliberately NOT in `spawnedProps` — sharing that list would give it the behaviour wanted today while tying two different lifetimes together, and in Ş4 "was the ghost destroyed, or the prop" becomes a question worth being able to answer. `Refresh` clears it, because a ghost belongs to one location's layout. **Two things this step deliberately does NOT do.** `Refresh()` stays PRIVATE: Ş3 buys nothing, so nothing changes what `IsActive` answers, and the same argument that deferred the ghost API out of Ş1 (D-029) applies to `Refresh` here — it goes public in Ş4, where a purchase gives it a caller. And the popup's BUY changes no state, it only logs: closing would make Ş3's own by-eye verification ambiguous ("did it buy, or just close?") and Ş4 would then have to write the closing a second time. On the editor side the popup's SIBLING POSITION carries the design — after the panel so it draws over the list, before the market button so that button still draws over it, because the machine can go from ÖNİZLEME straight to KAPALI and a backdrop covering the market button would strand the player in a preview with a ghost and no way out. Backdrop and box share ONE root the view toggles rather than being two objects switched by hand, since two switches is one that gets left on. The backdrop is a real `Button`, not a bare `Image` — the tap has to be HANDLED, not merely blocked (MS6) — with `Transition.None`, because a flashing dismiss area reads as a button the player was supposed to aim at. The box is `raycastTarget = true` or taps meant for it fall through and cancel the popup being read. MS3 and MS6 are settled by this step as recommended: cancelling removes the ghost, and tapping outside cancels.

D-032: The confirm popup is placed ON the previewed prop, and the map zooms toward it — by an amount derived from the prop's own size rather than authored per prop. — rationale: the user's two requests ("popup itemin üstünde çıkmalı" and "mümkünse zoom yapılsın iteme doğru") plus their own observation about the limit: *"trashbine zoom yapılır da fencelere yapılamaz büyüklüklerinden dolayı"*. **That limit is implemented as arithmetic, not as a special case.** The zoom is the one that would make the prop fill `previewFill` of the viewport, clamped to `[1, previewMaxZoom]`. A trash bin is a small fraction of the map, so the ratio is large and it zooms to the ceiling. A fence as wide as the grounds needs a ratio BELOW one to fit, so the clamp holds it at 1× and the map does not move at all. The user's rule therefore falls out of the formula, and a prop of some middling size added tomorrow gets a sensible number without anyone authoring one — which a per-prop flag or a hand-kept list of "zoomable" items would not have given. Three implementation choices carry weight. (1) **The ScrollRect is DISABLED while previewing.** Not a workaround: the player is answering a question, not exploring, so panning should be off. It also keeps the ScrollRect's own bounds arithmetic out of a fight with a scaled content rect, and horizontal scrolling is off on this map, so a zoomed-in view would otherwise be unreachable sideways. (2) **Framing is a world-space delta** — where the ghost is versus where it should be — never anchor arithmetic. The background is width-stretched with a top pivot, and hand-rolled maths would keep working right up until one of those conventions changed, then focus the wrong spot silently. (3) `Canvas.ForceUpdateCanvases()` after the scale change, because the ghost's world corners are otherwise read at the previous scale and the framing lands off by exactly the zoom factor — the same measure-before-layout trap already recorded for `Refresh`. The popup box gained a **bottom-centre pivot** so the view can place it in one assignment ("your lower edge sits just above the ghost") instead of doing half-height arithmetic; the pivot is the only reason the box is no longer screen-centred, and since its children anchor to the box's unchanged rect, nothing inside it moved. It **flips below the prop** when the prop sits near the top of the screen — better than covering the thing it describes — and is clamped away from the screen edges. Positions are computed in the parent RectTransform's local space rather than world units, because the box's size is measured there and mixing the two would drift by whatever the canvas scaler is doing on the device. The prop is framed BELOW the viewport centre (`previewFocusHeight`, default 0.38) precisely so there is room above it; centred, the box would flip underneath every time and the request would not be met. **No new entry point.** Focusing happens inside `ShowGhost` and restoring inside `ClearGhost`, so the six exits D-031 funnelled through one door now restore the zoom too — there is no seventh path where a zoomed map could get stuck. `RestoreFocus` is guarded by a flag rather than by comparing values, because a preview that happened to zoom by 1× still turned scrolling off and must still turn it back on. `previewFill`, `previewMaxZoom`, `previewFocusHeight` and the popup's gap are all serialized: they are look, and look is quicker to settle in the Inspector than to argue about in a comment. Zoom is instant — easing it is Ş6's business, and adding it now would make it harder to see by eye whether the framing is right.

D-033: The preview's camera move is animated with DOTween instead of snapping, and the destination is MEASURED rather than derived. — rationale: the user asked for it directly ("ışınlanmak yerine tween ile gitse ya zooma"); D-032 had deferred easing to Ş6, and that deferral is simply overtaken. DOTween because it is already this project's idiom — four UI files use the same `DOKill()` / `DOAnchorPos(...).SetEase(...)` shape — and duration plus ease are serialized, because the feel of a screen is not something to settle in a comment. **The interesting part is how the destination is obtained.** D-032 framed the map by a world-space delta (where the ghost is versus where it should be) and explicitly rejected pivot/anchor arithmetic, because the background is width-stretched with a top pivot and hand-rolled maths keeps working right up until one of those conventions changes, then focuses the wrong spot silently. But that delta can only be read once the scale is applied — which is exactly what a tween must not do. Rather than give up the robust version, the map is now put at its destination for an instant, the end scale, the end position AND the ghost's settled edges are measured there, and it is snapped back before anything renders. Nothing is computed; everything is observed. That measurement is also why **`MetaShopView` needed no change at all**: `TryGetGhostBounds` now reports the SETTLED edges, so the shop places the popup once, at the position the prop is travelling to, and the map slides in underneath it. The two alternatives — repositioning the popup every frame, or delaying it until the tween finished — would both have tied the shop to the map's animation length, i.e. created a timing dependency between two components that currently have none. **The tween exposed a latent bug, now fixed.** `ShowGhost` used to call `ClearGhost`, so walking from one row to the next restored the old framing first. While the move was instant that was invisible; with a tween it means the "before" framing gets re-captured from a position halfway through the return tween, and cancelling would then send the map somewhere it had never been. So the two are split: `ShowGhost` calls `DestroyGhostObject` (no restore) and only a real exit calls `ClearGhost`; and the pre-focus framing is captured only when not already focused. Row-hopping now goes straight to the new frame, and cancel always returns to the true starting frame however many rows were browsed. One ordering detail is load-bearing: scrolling is handed back in the return tween's `OnComplete`, never at its start. The `ScrollRect` is `Clamped` and pulls the content toward its bounds every `LateUpdate`, so re-enabling it mid-tween would have both of them dragging the same value. `OnComplete` does not fire on a `DOKill`, so `FocusGhost` disables the flag itself as well — an interrupted return therefore leaves a coherent state from either side rather than depending on the tween finishing. `DOKill` before every tween and in `OnDisable`, since a tween outliving the transform it drives is DOTween's classic null and both meta screens are torn down by a scene load. The popup itself is still not animated: the request was about the zoom, and a popup entrance is a line in Ş6.

D-034: The meta shop can spend money. `MetaShopView.OnConfirmBuyClicked` runs `MetaPurchase.Evaluate` → `Wallet.TrySpendSoftMoney` → `OwnedMetaItemIds.Add` → `GameSession.Save` → `ClosePreview` → `MetaGroundsView.Refresh` → rebuild rows. — rationale: `meta-shop-plan.md` Ş4. **This step adds no rule and no writer.** `MetaPurchase` decides whether a buy is allowed (six verdicts, 25 tests, D-019), `Wallet` decides whether the balance carries it and stays the single writer of the balance, `GameSession.Save` decides what reaches disk, and the profile schema already carried `OwnedMetaItemIds` from D-020. What is new here is only the composition. **Atomic by construction, not by care taken in this method:** the balance and the owned set live on the same `GameSession`, and `Save()` writes both together, so "the money went but the item did not arrive" is not a state this code can express. That is what makes the literal ordering above safe to read. Two apparent redundancies are deliberate. `Evaluate` runs a SECOND time here even though the row was drawn from it, because the panel stays open across purchases (MS4) and the balance the row was drawn against goes stale; the cost is one function call. And the wallet is asked even after `Evaluate` approves, because `Wallet` is the single writer of the balance and its answer is the authority — one is the decision, the other is the transaction, and only the transaction can refuse on the real number. `MetaGroundsView.Refresh()` is **public again, and only now**. It was public for the first shop, went back to private when that shop was reverted (D-027) on the grounds that an entry point with no caller reads as one that is used somewhere, and it stayed private through Ş1 (D-029) and Ş3 (D-031) on the same argument, since neither of those steps bought anything. A purchase changes what `MetaResolver.IsActive` answers while nothing else would notice, so the caller this method needed came into existence exactly at this step — which is the argument being honoured rather than waived. **`HudWalletSource` was NOT touched, and that is the part worth recording.** A main-screen HUD reading the save file once would sit on a stale balance after a purchase, and the root CLAUDE.md forbids the obvious patch ("Do not add a second null-check-and-fall-back to any view; extend the source instead"). No extension was needed: D-022 had already widened its `sessionHost` field from `GameManager` to `SessionHost` and the main screen now supplies a session, so `LiveState` is non-null there and the source is subscribed to `GameState.SoftMoneyChanged`. The HUD is therefore already live and the coin count falls on its own. The one thing that can still make it look otherwise is an empty `Session Host` on the HUD instance, which drops the source into save-file mode — it logs which mode it picked on every scene load for exactly this reason. A non-`Ok` verdict logs and leaves the popup OPEN: saying WHY on screen is Ş5's job, and doing it here as well would put the appearance of a verdict in two places. **No new tests, and that is not a gap.** Every decision is in `MetaPurchase` and covered; what remains here is composition inside a MonoBehaviour, which EditMode cannot exercise. Moving the ordering somewhere testable would mean `MetaSystem` depending on the session, and `MetaSystem` referencing only `Data` is precisely what keeps the rules testable in the first place. A failed `Save()` leaves the session correct in memory and the file one purchase behind, with `PlayerProfileStore` logging why; there is no partial write to recover from because the profile is one file written whole.

D-035: The shop hides what the player cannot reach, dims what they cannot afford, and lists the rest affordable-first then cheapest-first. `MetaPurchase.ShopItems` gained a `softMoney` parameter and now does both the filtering and the ordering. — rationale: the user's four changes to Ş5, given together: an area-locked prop should not appear at all until it unlocks, a prop they cannot afford should look darker, buyable things should be at the top, and the order should be by ascending price. **This reverses half of the plan's MS2, which was my recommendation, and the reversal is theirs.** I had argued for showing area-gated props because "seeing what is still to come is most of what a shop is for". The user kept that for unaffordable props and rejected it for area-locked ones. Implemented as asked, with one cost named rather than argued: nothing on screen now tells the player that buying the Square opens more props, so the discoverability that MS2 was protecting is gone for that case. That is a "coming soon" affordance for a later step (and it needs M7 answered — which props the Square gates), not a reason to keep showing rows the user does not want. **Both changes went into `ShopItems`, not the view.** That function's documented contract already was "everything the shop should list for a location", and it is that contract which changed; putting the filter in a MonoBehaviour would move a listing rule out of the tested layer. Ordering went in with it, which is why the signature gained `softMoney` — "buyable at the top" is a function of the balance, and splitting filter from order would answer "what does the shop show, and in what order" in two places. **The sort is an insertion sort, deliberately not `List.Sort`.** `List.Sort` is unstable, so two props at the same price would be free to swap places between one open of the shop and the next — a defect that presents as "it sometimes reorders itself", which is the hardest kind to be told about. Ties therefore keep authored catalog order, and `SortsBefore` answers false on equal keys precisely to preserve that. `MetaResolver.ActiveItems` made the same call for the same reason (its comment once claimed `List.Sort` was stable and was corrected), and both orderings are now pinned by tests. On the view side the dim is a `CanvasGroup` **alpha** and never `interactable`: over the panel's dark background less alpha reads as darker, which is the user's wording, and one knob fades icon, name, price and button together. The row's BUY stays tappable on purpose — it opens the preview, and seeing where a prop would stand and what it costs is what makes it worth saving for; disabling the row would turn it into dead furniture. What now refuses **visibly** is the popup's BUY, which is `interactable` only when the money is there. Until this step that button was live and did nothing on a short balance: it called `Evaluate`, got `NotEnoughMoney`, logged, and left the screen unchanged — a dead button in exactly the sense the plan warned against. The commit path still re-checks everything (D-034); this only stops the player asking a question whose answer is already on screen. The view neither re-sorts nor re-filters, and reads affordability as price-vs-balance rather than calling `Evaluate` again — everything `Evaluate` could still refuse on has already been filtered out, so money is the only question left. `Evaluate` keeps all six verdicts including `AreaLocked`: it is the general decision function, and a prop being hidden from one list does not make the verdict wrong. **Tests were rewritten, not deleted** — the old `ShopItems_KeepsUnaffordableAndGatedProps_...` asserted the now-false half of MS2 — and three were added: owning the area brings the gated prop BACK (so the filter test cannot pass by dropping gated props forever), the two-key ordering, and equal prices keeping authored order, which is what pins the insertion sort against someone reaching for `List.Sort` later. **They have not been RUN**: Unity holds the project lock, so this step is verified by compilation only.

D-036: `MetaShopSetup` offers to REBUILD a shop whose references a later step added, and `MetaShopView` hides itself before validating. — rationale: the user reported the shop panel and popup stuck on screen at Play with a dead market button. Read from `MainScreen.unity`: the scene's `MetaShopView` had only `marketButton` and `panel` wired — the Ş1-era shop — so the nine fields added by Ş2/Ş3/Ş3b/Ş5 were null, `Start` returned early from validation, nothing was ever switched off, and no listener was attached. Two separate defects, and **neither of them was the user's mistake.** **(1) The tool put the burden in the wrong place.** The step promised not to rebuild an existing shop, which was right in Ş1 — the point was to protect hand-made layout. But every later step added objects AND required references to that hierarchy, and the step could only warn that fields were empty. I wrote "delete the root and run it again" into three consecutive postflights; when an instruction has to be repeated three times and the scene is still broken, the thing to fix is the tool, not the reader. It now names what is missing and asks whether to rebuild. The promise it actually made was never "never rebuild" but "never rebuild BEHIND YOUR BACK", and asking keeps that intact — a complete shop is still left untouched. Repair-in-place was rejected: the list and the popup are nested hierarchies (viewport → content → template; root → backdrop + box), and threading new pieces into a half-built one in the right order would mean maintaining two construction paths forever. `RequiredFields` is deliberately the same list `MetaShopView` refuses to run without — a second opinion about what "healthy" means would let this step bless a shop the screen then rejects at `Start`, which is exactly the confusion being fixed. A property that does not EXIST counts as missing too, because that means step and component are out of step and the author should see it. **(2) The screen did not fail safe.** `Start` returned early while leaving the panel and popup in whatever state the scene had saved, so a mis-wired scene showed the player a shop pasted over the map. Both are now switched off BEFORE validation, each behind its own null check: a missing reference is a console error, not a full-screen overlay. That removes the visible half of the complaint even when nothing is wired at all. `MainScreen.unity` was NOT edited to fix this. D-007 declined to hand-edit this project's scene YAML because such a change cannot be reviewed, and that still holds — the repair runs from the menu item, under the author's eye, through `Undo`, and without saving the scene.

D-037: An unaffordable BUY is RED, both on the row and in the confirm popup, on top of the dim D-035 introduced. — rationale: the user asked for it ("satın alınamayanlar aynı zamanda buy kırmızı halde olsun") — "aynı zamanda", so the fade stays and the red rides on it. The red is the project's existing destructive-action red from `MainScreenResetButtonSetup`, so the game does not grow a second red for the same idea. Both colours are serialized. **No new serialized reference, therefore no scene rebuild.** The button's graphic is `Button.targetGraphic` — the button's own declared graphic, already wired by the setup step. That is not the kind of lookup D-028 forbids: nothing is being guessed about which object was meant. Adding a dedicated `Image` field would have been more explicit but would have cost another rebuild of the shop, which is exactly the round D-036 had just been spent recovering. Verified rather than assumed: writing to `Graphic.color` is NOT clobbered by the Button's `ColorTint` transition. `Selectable` applies the state tint through `CrossFadeColor` on the CanvasRenderer, which MULTIPLIES with `Graphic.color` instead of overwriting it — which is also why the green the setup step authors shows up at all. In the popup that multiplication is the point: the disabled state darkens the red, so the button reads as "red, and off" rather than the generic grey that says nothing about WHY. The colours live on `MetaShopView` and are passed into `Bind`, not held by `MetaShopRowView`. The row stays a binder that decides nothing, and two components each holding the same pair of colours is a pair that can drift. They are applied on EVERY bind, not only the unaffordable one, so a row always shows one of the two rather than sometimes inheriting whatever the inactive template was authored with — the setup step's green now only decides what the template looks like in the Editor. The row's red BUY stays tappable, keeping D-035's call: red says "you cannot afford this", not "do not press this", and the preview it opens is still worth seeing.

D-038: The confirm popup's full-screen backdrop is removed. — rationale: the user deleted it from the scene and asked for it out of the code ("backdropu kaldırır mısın koddan sildim ben inspectorda"). This was not optional once they had: `confirmBackdrop` was a REQUIRED reference, so with the object gone `Start` failed validation and the whole shop stopped working. The backdrop did two jobs and they are worth separating. **(1) Tap outside to cancel (MS6) is GONE.** I had recommended it and it is the expected mobile behaviour, but the object is the user's call; CANCEL remains, so the player is never stranded in a preview — D-031's set of exits loses one member without any of them closing. That also makes the cancel button the ONLY way out, which is why it stays required and why its comment now says so. **(2) Blocking taps aimed at the map costs nothing**, because the `ScrollRect` is already disabled for the duration of a preview (D-032), so a drag landing behind the popup cannot pan anything. A side effect the user may well have wanted: the map is no longer dimmed during a preview, which shows the ghost more clearly rather than less. The field was DELETED rather than made optional. An unused serialized field reads as one that is used somewhere — the same argument D-027 made for a public method with no caller. Two follow-through details mattered more than the deletion itself. `"confirmBackdrop"` had to come out of `MetaShopSetup.RequiredFields`, or D-036's rebuild prompt would have reported a permanently missing field and offered to rebuild the shop on every single run — the cost of that list being the single definition of a healthy shop is that it has to be maintained with the component. And four comments that described the backdrop as present were rewritten rather than left: the box's `raycastTarget` in particular was justified by "or taps fall through to the backdrop and cancel the popup being read", which is no longer true, so it is now documented as ordinary panel behaviour that nothing relies on. A comment that survives the code it describes is the same defect as a stale map line.

D-039: A prop may carry its own SHOP ICON, separate from the art it is drawn with on the map. `MetaItemDefinition` gains an optional `shopIcon`; `ShopIcon` returns it or falls back to `sprite`. — rationale: the user asked whether the shop's row icons could be different sprites; there was no such field, the row was showing `item.Sprite`, the same image the prop is drawn with on the grounds. **The fall-back lives in the data class, not in the shop.** "Empty means use the prop's art" is one rule, and a rule spread across its callers is one a caller eventually forgets — here that forgetting would draw nothing for every prop without a custom icon, which reads as missing ART rather than as missing code. **`Sprite` deliberately keeps doing what it did**: it is what the map prop and the purchase ghost use. That split is the point of the feature rather than an oversight — the ghost answers "how will this look where it stands" and the icon answers "how do we represent this in a list", and a preview drawn from a list icon would be a preview that lies, which is the same reason D-032 kept `CreateProp` as the single authority for placement. The field is optional and an empty one is NOT a validation error: every catalog authored before today stays valid and behaves exactly as it did. A prop with no sprite at all is already reported, so "no icon anywhere" is covered without a second warning. Two things were checked rather than assumed. The constructor parameter was inserted after `sprite`, in the middle of the list, which would silently reassign arguments for any positional caller — every caller passes named arguments past the first two, verified before the edit. And `MetaEditorWindow` draws prop fields BY NAME rather than through a default inspector, so the new field would have been invisible in the Meta Editor without an explicit line; that is now noted on its map entry, because it will apply to the next field too. Two tests: an authored icon wins and leaves `Sprite` untouched, and no icon falls back to the art. Like Ş5's, they compile but have NOT been run — Unity holds the project lock.

D-040: The next Day-unlocked prop is shown before it opens — a faint silhouette on the grounds that fills in from the bottom as days pass, with the completed percentage over it. — rationale: the user's request. Three design forks were genuinely undecidable from the description and were put to them: where the fill STARTS (there was no second number on a Day-unlock prop, so the percentage had no denominator), how many props preview at once, and whether the label counts up or down. Their answers: the wait starts at the previous Day-unlock milestone, only the next prop previews, and the label shows completed percentage. **The chosen start needs no new authored data**, which is why it was recommended: the wait begins at the most recent Day-unlock day the player has already passed, and day 0 when there is none, so every existing catalog works unchanged. The accepted consequence is that pace varies — unlocks eight days apart fill slowly, two days apart fill fast. That is the catalog's own rhythm showing through rather than a defect, and the alternatives each cost a number per prop or a global one. **The user's actual question was whether THEY had to prepare anything — alpha versions, prefabs. They do not.** One sprite is enough: the faintness is a runtime colour alpha and the fill is `Image.Type.Filled` with a vertical bottom origin. It does take TWO layers rather than one, because a single Image cannot be both faint AND partly solid — `fillAmount` cuts the image off, it does not fade it — so the back layer says "this is coming" and the front layer says "this much of the wait is done". The silhouette goes through `CreateProp`, the same path the real prop and the purchase ghost take, so the preview cannot disagree about where the thing will stand; the fill is its stretched child, inheriting that placement instead of computing it again. **Area gating applies to the preview too**, and that is the one judgement call inside the rule: what holds an area-gated prop back is a PURCHASE, not time, so a bar creeping toward full would promise "wait and it comes" when the honest answer is "buy the square". A prop whose day has passed while its area is unowned therefore shows nothing at all — the same silence `IsActive` already gives it. The visuals are built in CODE rather than cloned from an authored template, which is the weaker choice on style and the right one here: a template would mean a new object reference on `MetaGroundsView`, which would mean re-running Build Meta Grounds — the step that refuses to rebuild what exists, i.e. exactly the trap D-036 was written about. Tuning is serialized instead (silhouette alpha, label size, colour, offset), and new serialized values are born with their defaults without anyone touching the scene. A missing TMP font costs the caption, not the fill. `preserveAspect` stays unused on these props — their size already comes from sprite pixels times the background scale, and it interacts badly with `Filled`. The rule lives in `MetaResolver` with five tests, because "which prop is next" and "how far along" are questions about the catalog. Nothing is persisted: the preview is derived every time, so D-020's rule that the save file holds purchases only is untouched. Like the rest of this session's tests, they compile but have NOT been run — Unity holds the project lock.

D-041: A Day-unlocked prop opening is now an EVENT: the meta screen zooms to it, the prop rises from silhouette to solid, and the map zooms back out. Profile schema goes to v5 with a `LastCelebratedDayIndex` marker. — rationale: the user's request, of which this is the FIRST HALF. They asked for two things — the celebration, and being forced back to the main menu when an unlock happens — and I split them and **reversed the order** deliberately: the forced return on its own would be a REGRESSION (marched to the menu and shown nothing for it), while the celebration on its own is a complete gain. The split is only possible because of the next point. **The main screen works out what it owes for ITSELF** — `MetaResolver.DayUnlocksBetween` over the profile's marker versus the current day — rather than being handed a message by the day scene. So it needs nothing from the forced-return step, and the player gets the celebration however they came back: through Day Complete, through Game Over, or by quitting and relaunching two days later. **No cross-scene static was used**, and that was the tempting shortcut. This project's posture is "no persistent scene, every scene builds itself from scratch", and its documented hand-off between the two scenes is the save file. A static would also have destroyed the celebration silently if the app closed in between, where a stored marker shows the missed unlock at the next launch. **v5's default is dangerous, which is the whole reason it carries a migration.** Read as 0, an existing save claims every Day-unlocked prop the player got days ago is still owed a fanfare, and they would be met with a queue of them. `UpgradeToCurrent` sets the marker to `CurrentDayIndex` — whatever they have, they have seen. This is the project's second real migration and the same shape as v3's `Lives`, which is exactly what the root invariant's "check whether 0 is safe" exists for. A NEW player at 0 needs no migration because the comparison is strictly-after, so a prop authored at day 0 is scenery rather than an event; both directions are pinned by tests. **The marker's single writer is `MetaGroundsView`**, the thing that plays the celebration, because "has this been shown" is only knowable by whatever showed it. `GameManager` remains the single writer of the balances and the day index; a different datum having a different writer is what the invariant allows. It is written only after the WHOLE queue finishes, so an interrupted celebration replays next launch — replaying is the cheap failure, swallowing is the expensive one. Four implementation choices carry weight. (1) Started only from `Start`, never `Refresh` — a purchase also calls `Refresh`, and a shopping trip is not the moment for a day-unlock fanfare. (2) `FocusGhost` became `FocusOn`: two callers now, and writing a second framing routine would have broken D-032's single-authority argument. (3) The real prop is HIDDEN while an animated copy takes its place, so the opening is something the player watches rather than something that already happened before the map arrived; the two-layer fill is shared with D-040's upcoming preview rather than copied. (4) Skip is a transparent full-screen `Button` parented to the CANVAS as its last sibling — not to the grounds, which are the canvas's FIRST child (D-029), so a catcher inside them would sit under the market button and let the shop open mid-celebration; and a Button rather than `Input.GetMouseButtonDown` so it does not depend on which input backend the project is configured for. `try/finally` guarantees the framing returns and the catcher goes, because a screen left zoomed in under an invisible full-screen button is unusable. Six tests, none RUN — Unity holds the project lock. **Still to do: the forced return** ("Next Day" routing to the main screen when the next day opens a prop), which is the second half of the request.

D-042: Finishing a day that opens a Day-unlocked prop sends the player to the MAIN SCREEN instead of straight into the next day, so D-041's celebration cannot be skipped. — rationale: the second half of the user's request, deliberately done after the first (D-041) because on its own it would have been a regression — marched to the menu and shown nothing. **Almost nothing was written; the behaviour already existed.** `ReturnToMainScreenFromCompletedDay` persists the next index, saves, and loads the main screen, which is exactly the wanted outcome. Its own comment also explains why it does NOT go through `AdvanceToNextDay`: that method clears the board, discards trays and refills slots, which microseconds before the scene unloads is either thrown away or an event cascade through views that are already tearing down. That reasoning covers the new case identically, so the unlock branch must not touch `AdvanceToNextDay` either — and does not. **The decision went into `GameManager`, not the popup.** `DayCompletePopupView` already asked one question and branched on it; adding a second would have made a popup the place where scene routing is decided. It now asks one thing — `TryContinueIntoNextDay` — and hides itself only if there is still a day scene to hide in. Rule in `MetaResolver`, decision in `GameManager`, view deciding nothing. **"Sent back and shown nothing" is made unrepresentable rather than merely unlikely.** The check goes through a new catalog-level overload of `DayUnlocksBetween` that resolves the same location the meta screen will open on. It is the same location by construction: the celebration only ever starts from `Start`, and at that moment the viewed location IS the newest unlocked one. Both sides therefore call one function instead of computing the answer twice — with one location authored this is invisible, which is exactly why it was worth getting right now rather than after a second restaurant exists. The two overloads reintroduce the `null`-names-neither ambiguity that produced a CS0121 on `DefaultLocationIndex` earlier in this session; noted at the declaration, and no bare null is passed. `GameManager` gains one OPTIONAL serialized `MetaCatalog`. Optional, because a day scene must not be broken by a missing reference over what is, from the day's side, a routing nicety — but NOT silent: `Awake` says so once, since an empty field and a catalog with no upcoming unlocks look identical on screen. That is the same posture, for the same reason, as `HudWalletSource` logging which mode it resolved to. **No asmdef change, verified before planning**: `Scripts/Bootstrap` and `Scripts/UI` carry no asmdef of their own, so both live in `Assembly-CSharp`, which already references `MetaSystem` through `MetaGroundsView`. No new dependency arrow, so the root invariant on one-directional arrows is untouched. This step writes no data and adds no schema — v5 from D-041 is sufficient, and the celebration marker's single writer stays `MetaGroundsView`. Two tests cover the rule. The DECISION itself (which scene loads) is not covered and cannot be: it is a MonoBehaviour calling `SceneFlow`. Stating that plainly rather than implying the step is fully tested — what is tested is the question, not the routing.

D-043: After the unlock celebration, the map pans on to the NEXT prop's ghost and lingers there before zooming out. — rationale: the user asked for it. Small and entirely presentational: no rule was added, so no test was added either — the prop being looked at is what `MetaResolver.NextDayUnlock` already returns, covered by five tests from D-040, and everything new here is the order the camera moves in. **The timing works out for free, and that is worth recording rather than rediscovering.** D-040 measures a prop's wait from the PREVIOUS unlock, so the moment one opens, the next one reads exactly %0. The peek therefore says "the counter for this one starts now" using nothing but the number already on screen — no extra copy, no emphasis, no second label. It is also not a new drawing pass. `Refresh` runs BEFORE the celebration and already has the new day index, so the silhouette on screen is the prop AFTER the one being unlocked, drawn at the right progress. The peek only has to focus it; drawing another would have put two copies of the same thing on the grounds. The peek gets its OWN duration rather than reusing `celebrationHoldSeconds`: that beat is "look at what you got" and this one is "and this is next", and sharing one number means breaking one while tuning the other. `skipRequested` is reset before it, so a player who skipped the reveal is not taken to have skipped the peek as well — if they want past that too, one more tap does it. Nothing coming (everything open, or the next prop area-gated and therefore not drawn) falls through to the caller's `finally`, which remains the single place the framing is restored, so the zoom-out still has exactly one owner.

D-044: The required-pool guarantee budget is counted over **active** tickets only and reaches the queued lookahead solely once every active ticket is already covered. Board spawning stays **order-triggered and nothing else** — `OnOrderPlaced` remains `BoardDistributor`'s only entry point, and the urgency override is evaluated on that same path — rationale: a playtest screenshot showed three active tickets, a board holding one ketchup hotdog and one cola, and no legal move: every visible ticket wanted something else. `SelectGuaranteedTickets` drew the budget from ONE pool of active + upcoming tickets, so with the shipped balancing (`guaranteedTicketCount: 1`, `earlyTicketWeightDecay: 0.5`, a 10-deep lookahead, i.e. ~13 candidates of which 3 are on screen) the single guaranteed slot regularly went to a ticket the player could not see — and because selection is deliberately STICKY (D-001) and a queued ticket never leaves the pool, it then held that slot for many rounds while all three visible tickets got nothing. Reproducing the exact algorithm over the authored Days (2000 simulated days, a perfect instant player): **18.7% of distribution rounds left ZERO active ticket guaranteed and 2.3% left all three slots full with nothing completable**, costing ~0.3 forced life losses per day; after the change both are 0.0% and the only round with no active ticket guaranteed is the one at day end that has no tickets left. This is a **restoration, not a redesign**: `ExpoTheExplorer/CLAUDE.md`'s locked Board/Food Distribution rule already reads "the minimum set needed to complete ACTIVE tickets — at least one ticket must always be completable", and a ticket that has not arrived completes nothing. Everything else about D-001's scheme is untouched — the arrival-weighted lottery, the Poisson/Manual budget, stickiness, and the "at least one, not every ticket" reading all stand, and reaching into the lookahead still happens when the budget exceeds the number of active tickets (`OnOrderPlaced_GuaranteedCountExceedsActiveTickets_ReachesIntoUpcomingQueue` passes unchanged). A queued ticket already picked keeps its spawned items and stays in the set — nothing here ever un-spawns — it simply stops consuming the active budget, and starts counting again once it reaches a slot. **A second change was built and then reverted on the user's instruction, recorded here so it is not re-proposed.** Diagnosis surfaced a related hole: D-001's "any active ticket under `UrgentTimeThresholdSeconds` is guaranteed unconditionally" can only be evaluated when something calls into the distributor, and the sole caller is ticket assignment — so if a round ever left nothing completable, the player delivers nothing, nothing is assigned, no further round runs, and that rescue rule fires exactly never in the situation it was written for. A time-driven entry point (`OnTicketBecameUrgent`, plus a per-slot urgency edge detector in `GameManager.Update`) was implemented for it and then removed: the user's decision is that **spawning is order-triggered, period** — no timer, no per-frame board poll — and the about-to-expire check belongs on the `OnOrderPlaced` path, which is where it already lives. That holds together because of the first change rather than in spite of it: with the active-first budget every round already leaves at least one ON-SCREEN ticket completable, so reaching a no-move board now requires the player to strand it themselves (parking a required item in a tray, which they can undo by dragging it back out) instead of it arising from the distribution rule. Consequence to keep visible: `Update` ticks ticket timers and nothing else, so if the guarantee ever looks starved again the lever is the active-first budget or `guaranteedTicketCount` in the Day file, never a new trigger. NOTE this changes balance in the player's favour on identical Day files (~0.3 fewer forced life losses per day) and no Day JSON was touched: `guaranteedTicketCount: 1` on Days 03–07 means one of the three visible tickets is covered, which is a designer's call — the fix only guarantees that the one covered ticket is always one the player can actually see. NOTE also what was ruled out while diagnosing, so it is not re-investigated: `TraySlot.Matches`/`RequiredItemKey` agree with the spawner field for field, `BoardGrid`'s pending-spawn queue was not involved (2 of 20 cells occupied), and all three hotdog modifications do have authored sprite layers, so the board really was showing an item no active ticket wanted rather than mis-drawing one it did — affects: BoardDistributor.cs, BoardDistributionTests.cs (2 new cases), GameManager.cs (touched and then fully reverted — no net change), codemap-core.md (also repaired 5 `MISSING-role` Core lines on this path — Ticket, TruncatedPoisson, RequiredItemKey, BoardGrid, BoardItem — and a `dep:` on BoardDistributor still naming the BoardDistributionConfig that D-007 deleted) — 2026-08-23

D-045: The upcoming-prop silhouette respects SortOrder instead of being drawn on top of everything. — rationale: the user reported the fryer sitting BEHIND the sauces in the Meta Editor but IN FRONT of them in game. The catalog settled it: `fryer` is a DayUnlock prop at `sortOrder = 0` that opens on day 12, so it is not active yet and is drawn as D-040's upcoming ghost — and `DrawUpcoming` appended that ghost after the active-prop loop, making it the last sibling and therefore frontmost whatever its depth. The editor canvas (`MetaLocationLayoutGUI.SortedIndices`) has always sorted, so **the editor was right and the game was wrong**, which is the worse way round: the preview tool the author trusts was telling the truth while the game contradicted it. Fixed by placing the ghost at the sibling index its SortOrder asks for, not by forcing it forward. The reason is the reason the preview exists at all (D-032, D-040): it shows where the prop WILL stand, and depth is part of where — a ghost pulled to the front tells the truth about position and lies about occlusion. The consequence is accepted rather than worked around: a prop authored at a low SortOrder really is behind the scenery, so its ghost can be partly or wholly hidden, and the celebration zoom is what still makes it findable. The catalog was NOT edited — the fryer's `sortOrder = 0` is a content decision, and the code was the thing disagreeing with it. A SECOND site in the same class had the same defect and is fixed with it: the celebration's temporary copy was also appended, so a prop would jump to the front for the length of its own celebration and drop back when the copy went away. It now takes over the hidden real prop's exact sibling index, which makes "the copy and the original sit at the same depth" true by construction rather than by a second calculation. `SortedInsertIndex` takes the location so it can break ties by authored catalog order — the same single ordering rule `MetaResolver.ActiveItems` and the editor both apply; inferring authored order from the active list would have been a second, quietly different rule. **Deliberately NOT changed, and flagged rather than settled:** the PURCHASE ghost is still forced to the front (D-031). The same truth-in-preview argument applies to it, but the shop zooms to it and names it in a popup, so occlusion there costs comprehension — and it was not what was reported. Left for the author to decide. **Process note, because it cost real time.** Two partial edits to this file were made through Bash, which this project's root invariant forbids ("C# changes are made only with the Edit/Write tools"). Meanwhile another session had overwritten `.claude/preflight/current.md` with its own BoardDistribution preflight, so the user's APPROVE hashed that file instead of this plan and the guard correctly refused the third edit — leaving `Assembly-CSharp` broken on a missing helper (`CS0103`). Had the Edit tool been used from the start, the guard would have refused the FIRST edit and the mismatch would have surfaced before anything broke. The invariant is not bookkeeping: it is what makes the preflight gate able to stop a bad edit.

D-046: A location may carry a larger copy of its grounds art, drawn behind them, so the edges keep going when the map zooms in. One optional field, `backgroundBgSprite`, centred with no offset. — rationale: the user added `Art/Meta1/BackgroundBg.png` and asked that whatever applies to the background apply to it too, sitting exactly behind. **The offset field this started as was deleted because the art was fixed instead.** The first export was measured (by scale sweep and correlation) at 1074x2550 with the grounds sitting at (117, 387) — asymmetric, where centring would have been (110, 353), a 34px vertical error that shows on screen. That was going to cost a `Vector2` in the catalog. The user re-exported it as 1129x2444: +600 height (300 top, 300 bottom), +276 width (138 each side), both growth ratios equal to within one pixel of rounding, and correlation then found the grounds at exactly (138, 300) — dead centre, deviation zero. So centring is now a CONTRACT on the art rather than a number in data, which is what keeps this one field instead of a field plus a pair that goes stale on the next export. Measuring rather than trusting is the point: the first export would have failed the same assumption. **The grounds Image is now a container that never draws.** A child always renders over its parent's own graphic, so a continuation layer cannot sit behind a parent that draws — the art therefore moved into runtime children, ordered [0] continuation, [1] grounds, [2..] props. The alternative, inserting a real container object into the hierarchy, would have meant a new serialized reference and another delete-the-root-and-rebuild round (D-036); as built, the scene is untouched and `background` is still the ScrollRect content, the zoom target and the props' parent, with every authored normalized position still measured against the same rect. The continuation is a CHILD precisely so scrolling, the preview zoom and the framing are inherited rather than recomputed — a second transform calculation is the "second authority" trap this class has now avoided several times (D-032, D-040, D-043). It is sized from its own pixels times the SAME `PropScale` the grounds use. **Scroll range is deliberately unchanged**: the content is still sized from the grounds, so the extra art is clipped by the viewport and fills the edges under zoom instead of letting the player pan into empty space. `artLayerCount` exists because `SortedInsertIndex` returns SIBLING indices and props now start after these layers; forgetting that shift would place ghosts behind the grounds art, invisible — the same silent-depth defect D-045 was reported for. The Meta Editor canvas draws the continuation too, in the same order, because a preview that leaves out a layer is one that gets trusted and then contradicted — which is exactly what D-045 was reported for. **A plan correction worth recording:** the preflight said `MetaEditorWindow` would need a line for the new field. It does not. That window lists the ITEM fields by name (D-039's lesson) but draws the LOCATION with a generic `PropertyField`, so a new `MetaLocation` field appears on its own under the "Location & full prop list" foldout. No second editing surface was added for one datum. No new tests: one data field and a drawing order, with the rules layer untouched.

D-047: A successful meta purchase CLOSES the shop. `MetaShopView.OnConfirmBuyClicked` ends with `SetOpen(false)` followed by `grounds.Refresh()`; the panel does not come back and the row list is not rebuilt — rationale: the user's instruction ("satın alma kısmında bir şey satın alındıktan sonra yeniden dükkana dönmesin"). **This reverses MS4, which is the part worth recording.** MS4 kept the panel open on purpose so a player buying several props in a row would not have to tap the market button between each one, and that reasoning was about the *number of taps*. It ignored the geometry: the panel is a bottom sheet covering most of the map, so restoring the list puts the props NOT bought yet on top of the one that was just paid for. The payoff of a purchase is the prop standing on the grounds, and until now the player never got to see it — the map travelled back and the list slid straight over it. One extra tap to reopen the shop is the cheaper of the two costs, and it is a tap the player takes only if they actually want a second prop. **Mechanically the change is a subtraction, not an addition.** `SetOpen(false)` already does everything this path needs — it routes through `ClosePreview` (ghost destroyed, framing tweened back, D-031's single door), calls `ApplyVisibility` once for both the panel and the popup, and drops the rows via `ClearRows` — so the fix is to use that door instead of `ClosePreview` alone, which left `isOpen` true and had `ApplyVisibility` switch the panel straight back on. `RebuildRows()` is deleted from the path rather than moved: a closed panel has no rows to build, and the next `SetOpen(true)` builds them against the balance as it will be at that moment rather than as it is one frame after a purchase. Clearing `isOpen` inline would have worked too and was rejected — the first shop's ghost leaked precisely because a transition took a shortcut around the single door (D-024/D-027), and a second such shortcut is how that returns. **The refusal paths are deliberately untouched:** a non-`Ok` verdict from `MetaPurchase.Evaluate` and a `Wallet.TrySpendSoftMoney` refusal both still `return` with the popup up and the ghost standing, because closing the shop is now the SIGNAL that money moved — doing it on a refusal would read as a purchase that happened. `grounds.Refresh()` stays AFTER the shop comes down so the newly-owned prop is redrawn solid on a map nothing is covering; it is safe there because the return tween runs on `background.rectTransform` while `Refresh` rebuilds that object's children, which is the same order the previous code already relied on. NOTE nothing else moved: the double `Evaluate`, the wallet-is-the-single-writer ordering, `session.Save()` writing balance and ownership as one file, and the atomic-by-construction property are exactly as D-020 left them — affects: MetaShopView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-24

D-048: A purchased prop is PLACED, not merely present — it falls the last `placementDropHeight` into its spot while fading in, and the map takes a short damped shake as it lands. `MetaShopView` calls `grounds.RefreshAfterPurchase(item)` in place of `grounds.Refresh()`; everything about what that looks like lives in `MetaGroundsView` — rationale: the user's instruction ("satın alma olunca aldığımız item hafif yukarıdan fade in olarak gelse ve kamerada düşme animasyonunun bitmesiyle hafif titrese"). It completes D-047: closing the shop gave the player a clear view of the prop they had just paid for, and this is the moment that view is worth having. Six choices carry weight. **(1) One public method, not two.** `RefreshAfterPurchase` composes the redraw and the animation because the two are order-dependent in a way a caller cannot see: the prop must be pushed up and made translucent in the SAME FRAME `DrawProps` drew it, since a bought prop is drawn solid and in place, and yielding even one frame first would show it standing there and then jerk it back into the air. A `Refresh()` + `Animate()` pair is a pair that is eventually called in the wrong order, and the failure would be a visual stutter nobody could name.
