The shop therefore says only WHAT was bought. **(2) A frame loop, not a tween.** `CelebrateOne` already animates this way and the reasons transfer exactly: a `try/finally` can then guarantee the finished state through any interruption, and no tween is left pointing at a `RectTransform` that the next `Refresh` destroys. `DOVirtual.EasedValue` supplies the easing curve, so the `Ease` field stays serialized and tunable like `previewTravelEase` without a tween owning the object. **(3) The drop waits `previewTravelSeconds` first.** The shop has just closed, which restored the preview framing over exactly that duration, and `CelebrateOne`'s own comment states the rule — an animation played against a moving background reads as one confused motion instead of two clear ones. `WaitOrSkip` was deliberately NOT reused for that wait: it honours `skipRequested`, which belongs to the celebration's full-screen catcher, and a leftover `true` from an earlier celebration would silently skip the wait. **(4) The shake moves the CONTENT, not the viewport.** Shaking the viewport moves the mask window and opens a sliver of empty screen at the edges; the content carries D-046's oversized continuation layer as a child, which is precisely the art that keeps the edges covered while it moves. The ScrollRect is suspended for the shake's duration — D-032's call, for D-032's reason: it is Clamped and drags the content toward its bounds every `LateUpdate`, so leaving it enabled would have the two of them pulling at one value and the shake would come out as a stutter. It is handed back under `RestoreFocus`'s own `!focused` condition, so that flag never has two owners. There is no race with `RestoreFocus`'s `OnComplete` (which also re-enables scrolling): the shake starts a full `placementDropSeconds` after the wait ends, so the tween has long since completed. **(5) A damped sine, negative first, not per-frame randomness.** An impact has a direction — the ground gives way under the thing that hit it — and random jitter at 60fps reads as a glitch rather than as weight. The amplitude falls linearly to zero, so the last frame is already home and the `finally` has nothing to correct. **(6) Every number is serialized on the view, none in the catalog.** D-015 holds `MetaCatalog` to price/position/art/unlock, and "how hard the map shakes" is none of those; the six values sit beside `ghostAlpha` and `previewTravelSeconds`, and a prop authored tomorrow needs no new content to get this behaviour. NOTE the interruption path is where the real risk was, and it is closed in one place: `Clear()` stops the routine BEFORE destroying props, which makes "the props are gone" and "nothing is still animating them" the same moment — and `Clear` is the only place props are destroyed, so it is the only place that has to know. The routine's `finally` then snaps the prop to its landed position and full alpha (null-checked, because the likeliest stopper is the `Refresh` that destroyed it), puts the content back, and returns scrolling. NOTE also the fade lerps toward the prop's OWN alpha rather than to 1: `CreateProp` leaves props opaque so the two are the same number today, but a translucent prop would otherwise be faded up to solid and stay wrong until the next redraw — affects: MetaGroundsView.cs, MetaShopView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-24

D-049: The preview zoom is HELD through the placement animation and the map travels out only once the prop has landed and the shake is over. `MetaGroundsView` gains `placing` (the claim), `ReleaseFraming()` (the one way it ends), a `placementGeneration` counter and a snapping `RestoreFocus(animated: false)`; `MetaShopView.OnConfirmBuyClicked` calls `grounds.RefreshAfterPurchase(item)` BEFORE `SetOpen(false)` — rationale: the user's instruction ("animasyon falan bitene kadar zoom geri dönmese de animasyon bittikten sonra zoom out yapsak"). It is the third and last step of the purchase feel (D-047 closed the shop, D-048 dropped the prop in): the player previewed the prop at a magnified size and the payoff is watching it land at that size, not watching it land as a speck after the map has already pulled away. **A flag, not a parameter.** The obvious alternative was to thread a "keep the framing" bool through `SetOpen` and `ClosePreview`, and it was rejected: "may the zoom be given back right now" is `MetaGroundsView`'s question, and answering it in the shop would put a second owner on a piece of state whose whole design (D-031) is that it has one. The flag is honoured inside `RestoreFocus`, next to the `focused` guard that is already there for the same kind of reason, so every path that asks for the zoom back — and there are two on a purchase, `SetOpen`'s `ClearGhost` and `Refresh`'s own `ClearGhost` — is suppressed in one place. **The call order in the shop is load-bearing, and it was found by wiring the change rather than by reasoning about it.** The first attempt kept `SetOpen(false)` first and did nothing at all: the shop's exit runs `ClearGhost`, whose second half is `RestoreFocus`, before the grounds have heard about the purchase, so the zoom-out was already under way when the flag went up and the placement simply waited for it. Reversing the two calls fixes it, and they share one frame with no render between, so nothing is visibly half-done — the panel closing and the drop beginning are the same instant. **Two waits and a scroll block died with this change,** which is the part worth noticing: D-048's fixed `previewTravelSeconds` wait existed because the map was travelling during the drop, and it no longer is; and the shake's disable/re-enable of the `ScrollRect` was dead the moment the framing was held, because `FocusOn` turned scrolling off when the preview opened (D-032) and `RestoreFocus` is what turns it back on — the whole placement now runs inside a window where it is already suspended. Dead code that claims to own a flag is worse than no code, so it went rather than being left as insurance. What replaced the wait is `while (DOTween.IsTweening(content))`: zero frames in the normal case, and exactly the remainder for a player who taps BUY before the travel INTO the zoom has finished — a fixed number would have been too long in the first case and the wrong length in the second. **Interruption ownership is the subtle part and is settled with a generation counter.** Every claim bumps `placementGeneration`, and a placement's `finally` hands the map back only if its own generation still matches. Without it, a second purchase would have the first placement's teardown travel the map home from under the second one's drop. A location change is the opposite case and belongs to its own caller: `Step` calls `CancelPlacement`, which bumps the generation and gives the framing back SNAPPED, because `Step` sets the new location's scroll position one line later and an animated return would still be moving and would overwrite it — the player would arrive somewhere they never asked for. `Clear` deliberately does not make that decision: it cannot tell a location change from a purchase, and the two want opposite things. NOTE both interruption cases are unreachable in today's scene — the catalog has one location and D-025 makes the arrows optional — and are written down because the second location is exactly what makes the first one bite, far from this code. NOTE also the two units differ on purpose and it is not a bug: the drop is measured inside the content, so it grows with the zoom and stays the same fraction of the prop's own height, while the shake moves the content's `anchoredPosition`, which lives in the viewport's space and is therefore a fixed number of screen units at any magnification. A camera shake is about the screen; a falling prop is about the prop — affects: MetaGroundsView.cs, MetaShopView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-24

D-050: The day scene is played AT the expo the player decorates. `SampleScene`'s background becomes their own grounds — the unlocked location's art with the props they OWN standing on it — composed and blurred once at day start. New: `MetaLayout` (the prop arithmetic, extracted), `MetaBackdropView` (the day-side view), `MetaBackdropSetup` (the menu step); `MetaGroundsView` routes its scale and size through `MetaLayout` — rationale: the user asked to "blur the meta part and carry it into the other scene", and then asked the question that shaped the design: *"metada yeni bi şeyler aldıkça gündeki arkaplan da değişecek mi"*. **The first plan said no and that made the feature pointless**, so it was reversed before any code was written: a backdrop showing only the static location art would have left the decoration loop with no reach into the game, and "you are playing at the expo you decorated" is the whole reason to put it there. It now recomputes from the owned set at every day start, so a prop bought today stands in tomorrow's backdrop — and NOT halfway through the current day, which is correct, because the grounds are not something the player can change while a day is running. **The blur needs no shader, and that is a consequence of a fact about this project rather than a preference**: the URP package is installed but `GraphicsSettings` assigns no pipeline asset, so the built-in pipeline is what actually runs and URP renderer features are unavailable. Progressive halving through `Graphics.Blit` fills the gap exactly — a bilinear sample at half resolution lands precisely between four source texels, so each step is an exact four-texel box average and N steps compound into a wide blur. The result is deliberately left SMALL: stretching a tiny texture across the screen is itself a bilinear filter, so the upscale finishes the blur for free and the memory held for the rest of the day is a few kilobytes. Composition runs at `composeScale` 0.25 for the same kind of reason — the output is about to become soft shapes, and full resolution would spend ~11MB of transient memory on detail thrown away one step later. **The layering was already there and was not invented.** `SampleScene` carries a `Backgroundcanvas` at sorting order -100 with the world sprites at -2, `InGameCanvas` at -1 and the popup canvas on top; the backdrop takes over the graphic already sitting on it, so nothing about the day's render order changes. **The extraction is the part with real risk and it was taken deliberately.** Two things now draw the same prop, and `MetaGroundsView`'s own comment had long warned that "every place that draws a prop must compute it identically or a preview lies about where the thing will land" — written when there was one such place. Copying the four lines would have been the safe-looking choice and the wrong one, so `PropScale`/`PropSize` moved into `MetaLayout` and the grounds now ask for them. The POSITION deliberately did not move: Unity's anchors implement the same rule better, because an anchored prop rides the background through every resize without a line of layout code, and `MetaLayout.PropRect` is that rule spelled out only for the medium that has no anchors. If the two ever disagree, the anchors are right, and that is written into both files. NOTE the catalog gained NOTHING: a blur is an operation, not art, and D-015 holds `MetaCatalog` to price/position/art/unlock. NOTE the view writes nothing at all — it reads the catalog and the owned set, and every failure path (no session, no catalog, no unlocked location) leaves the `RawImage` exactly as the author left it, which IS the fallback; a `fallbackTexture` field was considered and rejected as a second place to author the same thing. NOTE the final `RenderTexture` is owned and released in `OnDestroy`, and cleared off the `RawImage` first, because graphics memory is not garbage collected and a `RawImage` pointing at a released texture draws whatever the driver left there. NOTE also sprites are blitted through `sprite.textureRect` rather than the whole texture: the difference is invisible until the art is packed into an atlas, at which point every prop would become a collage of its neighbours. **Not verified in Unity**: no test pins `PropRect` against a real `RectTransform` — that would be the mechanical guarantee that the two drawing paths agree, and it fell outside the approved manifest — and the scene change itself is a menu step the author runs, not something written here — affects: MetaLayout.cs (new), MetaBackdropView.cs (new), MetaBackdropSetup.cs (new), MetaGroundsView.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-24

D-051: Every GameObject the grounds spawn is now destroyed through one method, `Detach`, which calls `SetParent(null)` **before** `Destroy`. This fixes the "prop deleted by my purchase" bug — rationale: the user reported that buying fences made the drink fridge's upcoming-silhouette disappear, and their first description of it — *"arkaplanda kaldı"*, it stayed in the background — was literally correct. It was misread here as a framing complaint, because the props they could still see happened to be the ones in the top half of the map, which made a scroll problem look plausible. **The mechanism**: `Object.Destroy` is deferred to the end of the frame, and until then the object remains in its parent's child list. A redraw destroys the old art layers, props, silhouette and ghost and then immediately appends the new ones, so for the rest of that frame the background carries BOTH sets. That is harmless for anything that only appends — the new props keep their relative order — but `DrawUpcoming` moves its silhouette to an **absolute** sibling index from `SortedInsertIndex`, computed as though the dead children were already gone. In the reported case the dead block was 13 children (2 art layers + 9 props + 1 silhouette + 1 ghost) and the computed index was 12, so the silhouette was inserted among the corpses — behind the new `BackgroundArt` layer — and disappeared. **The arithmetic was never wrong; its input was.** `SortedInsertIndex` counts what SHOULD be there, and the fix makes the hierarchy agree with it rather than teaching the arithmetic about corpses, which would have spread knowledge of a Unity lifetime detail into a sorting rule. `DestroyImmediate` would also have worked and is the wrong tool at run time. **Two facts about when this bites are worth keeping.** It has been live since D-045 introduced `SetSiblingIndex` for the silhouette, and it fires on every redraw that had something to clear — a purchase or a location step — never on the first draw, because `Clear` finds nothing then. It only became *visible* with D-047, which closes the shop after a purchase and so finally lets the player look at the map at the moment it is wrong. NOTE `CelebrateOne`'s animated copy goes through `Detach` too: it lives under the background and the celebration reveals props one after another, so there is always a next absolute index to shift. NOTE the celebration's full-screen skip catcher does NOT, because it is parented to the Canvas, where nothing computes an absolute index. NOTE also `CelebrateOne`'s other `SetSiblingIndex` call is safe on its own terms — it reads `realProp.GetSiblingIndex()`, a RELATIVE lookup, which is the shape that never had this problem — affects: MetaGroundsView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-24

D-052: The Day Editor's `Main Dish Weights` list is no longer authored — its MEMBERSHIP is derived from the Mains picked under Food Selection, by `DayEditorMetaModel.SyncMainDishWeights`, and the list's add/remove buttons and each row's `Food` picker are gone — rationale: the user asked for the list to fill itself from the selected main dishes. The two halves of one setting had been sitting in two places with nothing tying them together: a designer picked a burger under Food Selection and then had to remember to hand-add a burger row below, and forgetting silently fell back to `MainDishWeight.DefaultWeight` — a Day that looked balanced because the list looked populated. **The split of ownership is the actual decision, not the auto-fill.** Membership is derived and has exactly one writer (Food Selection); the numbers in each row — weight, λ, max ceiling — stay hand-authored content and the sync never touches them, which is why an existing row is carried over **by reference** rather than rebuilt. Leaving the `+`/`-` buttons in place would have created the second writer the root invariant forbids, and the sync would have silently undone whichever the designer used last; the row's `Food` field is read-only for the same reason (repointing it would either be reverted on the next sync, or author a weight for a dish the Day never serves). **The single call site is the load-bearing part.** It sits at the end of `DrawFoodSelection`, not in the three writers — `SetFoodAllowed`, `FromDayJson`, `CopySettingsFrom` — because that section owns the input and, at `PropertyOrder` -5, draws before the list at -3: one call therefore covers opening a file, clicking a tile, the per-category All/None, Select All/Clear All, a catalog assigned late in the toolbar, and a new Day seeded from the previous one, and a future fourth writer for free. Three hand-placed calls would have been three places to forget. The price of putting it in a draw method is that it runs every repaint, so `MainDishWeightsMatch` early-outs with indexed loops and no LINQ — **do not add allocation to that path**; only the rebuild allocates, and only when the selection actually moved. `FromDayJson` was deliberately NOT touched: the existing `DayEditorModelConverterTests` fixture never sets `category`, and `FoodCategory.Main` is 0, so all three of its foods are Main-category — syncing on the load path would have broken tests over a fixture quirk, and the draw path sits outside them. NOTE one behaviour change, taken knowingly: a leftover row for a Main this Day does NOT serve is dropped, so it disappears from the file on the next open-and-save. That row was already dead — `TicketFactory` only picks from the pool built out of `AllowedFoodItemIds`, so its weight was never read. NOTE `DayEditorTicketGeneration.ModificationCountLambda` becomes unreachable on this Day's own Generate (every Main in the pool now has a row of its own) and was kept anyway: it still travels to the runtime config clone, and removing it would be a Day-JSON schema change for a value that costs nothing. It is NOT dead in general — the single-ticket "Generate Random Ticket" button builds its factory from the shared `TicketGenerationConfig` asset rather than the Day's clone, so that path still reads the asset's own weights and fallback. That inconsistency predates this change and was left alone. **Not verified in Unity**: the editor project is open, so the five new EditMode tests were compiled (`dotnet build` on both csprojs, 0 warnings) but not executed — they need a Test Runner pass, and the inspector's draw order (-5 before -3) is asserted from `PropertyOrder`, not observed — affects: DayEditorModel.cs, DayEditorModelConverterTests.cs, codemap-editor.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-24

D-053: No UnityEngine **value** attribute goes into `DayEditorModel.cs` any more — the four `[UnityEngine.Min]` on `DayEditorTicketRuntime` became Odin's `[MinValue]`, which is what every other numeric setting in the file already used — rationale: the user reported that the Ticket Runtime box swallowed the mouse wheel (the page would not scroll with the cursor over it) and that its fields arrived a frame late while scrolling. **The mechanism is the hosting, not the attribute's meaning.** `DayEditorWindow` is an `OdinMenuEditorWindow` whose menu items are plain `DayEditorModel` C# objects, so there is no `SerializedObject` behind them; `UnityEngine.MinAttribute` is drawn by Unity's `MinDrawer : PropertyDrawer`, which requires a `SerializedProperty`, so Odin must bridge it through an emulated one. That bridge takes its height from Unity's `GetPropertyHeight`, which disagreed between the layout and the repaint pass (the late fields), and processes the event against its own rect (the dead wheel). **This is the SECOND time the same bridge broke drawing in this window**, which is why the rule is now file-wide rather than another one-off fix: the first was `[UnityEngine.Range]`, whose slider ran past the window's right edge with its number field unreachable, and `[Min]` was the last survivor of that clean-up. `[Tooltip]` and `[HideInInspector]` stay — Odin reads both natively, they have no drawer — and an audit confirms they are the only UnityEngine attributes left in the file. **One design-visible change came with it, and it is a fix rather than a side effect**: the three patience limits now floor at **0**, not 1. A 0-second limit must stay authorable, because it is precisely what `DayValidator` refuses the Save over with the reason spelled out — `FromJson` deliberately does not clamp those three for that reason, and `FromDayJson_ZeroTimeLimit_IsNotSilentlyRepaired` pins it on the load path — yet `[Min(1f)]` had made that path unreachable by hand, so the inspector was silently preventing the one value the validator exists to catch. Negatives stay blocked, being nonsense rather than a reportable authoring mistake. `UpcomingQueueSize` floors at 1, matching `FromJson`'s own `Mathf.Max(1, ...)`. **Not verified in Unity**: the editor is open, so this was confirmed only by a clean `dotnet build` and by the attribute audit; whether the wheel and the frame lag are actually cured has to be seen in the window. If they are not, the next suspect is named in the preflight — the Food Selection grid derives its HEIGHT from `lastFoodGridWidth`, which is only written on Repaint, so a scrollbar appearing can feed back through columns -> rows -> height — affects: DayEditorModel.cs, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-24

D-054: A ticket's (or Start Board cell's) modifications are picked from a **visual tile grid of what the owning food actually offers**, through ONE helper — `DrawModificationPicker` — replacing the unfiltered `ModificationConfig` ObjectField and the blank-row "+ Add Modification" button — rationale: the user asked to see only the modifications specific to the ticket's current main dish, and to see them visually. **The constraint turned out to be the runtime's own rule, not a new one.** `TicketFactory.Create` pulls modifications from `main.AvailableModifications` and nothing else, and `CreateModification` takes direction from `ModificationConfig.AllowedDirection`, rolling a coin only for `Both`. The old picker offered every `ModificationConfig` in the project and appended entries with `IsAddition` left at false, so a Hotdog could be given a Burger's modification, and an AdditionOnly modification could be authored as a removal — "remove Extra Ketchup": valid data, impossible content, and a topping the dish has no sprite layer for. `DayEditorModification.ForConfig` is now the only way one is hand-authored and it seeds direction from the type; `CanFlipDirection` is why only a `Both` gets the right-click flip. Those two are deliberately pure and static so the one rule that can silently go wrong has four tests, while the IMGUI around it has none. **The scope was widened on purpose and declared before approval**: the same modification editor existed TWICE, byte-identical, in the ticket editor and the Start Board cell editor. Fixing one would have left the file carrying two divergent pickers, so both now call the helper — the owner is `entry.MainItem` for a ticket and `entry.Item` for a cell, which is the only thing that ever differed. The grid follows the Food Selection grid's conventions (stride, blue highlight, fade, tooltip, width-measured-on-repaint) because it is the same gesture, but keeps its **own** `lastModGridWidth`: the two sections need not be the same width and one shared field would let each overwrite the other's column count every repaint. Direction is shown by the same +/- badge `DayEditorTicketCardPreview` draws on a card, so it is read off the tile instead of a checkbox. **Two things are deliberately not silent.** A modification the owning food does NOT offer — authored earlier, or stranded by changing the food afterwards — is surfaced in a warning list with an x rather than dropped, because it still ships in the Day JSON and the designer has to see it; this is the same call `DayEditorMainDishWeight.FromJson` makes for a deleted `FoodItemConfig`. And the badge falls back to a `+`/`-` label when `TicketCardVisualsConfig` is unassigned, because direction is the one thing on the tile that must never be invisible. NOTE the picker filters nulls out of `AvailableModifications` before drawing, so a deleted modification asset leaves a gap in the food's list rather than a blank tile. **Not verified in Unity**: the editor is open, so this is confirmed only by clean `dotnet build`s on both assemblies and by the four direction tests, which have not been executed either — the grid's layout, the click/right-click split and the badge placement all need a Day Editor pass — affects: DayEditorModel.cs, DayEditorModelConverterTests.cs, codemap-editor.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-24

D-055: A Day authors **how many tickets of each patience type** Generate lays down, and lays them down **Patient first, then Normal, then Impatient**. New: `TicketGenerationJson.patient/normal/impatientTicketCount`, `DayContentGenerator.BuildPatiencePlan`, a "Patience Mix" box with a live readout in the Day Editor — rationale: the user asked to choose the mix instead of getting a uniform roll, and to have the sequence ordered. **This closes a gap the code had flagged itself**: `TicketFactory.PickRandomPatienceType`'s own comment reads "which PatienceType a ticket gets is an unspecified balancing decision (GDD never states a distribution). This uniform roll is a placeholder, not a design choice." **The counts do NOT become a second authority on how long a Day is.** `TicketsRequiredForDay` stays that, and the three counts are measured against it: a shortfall pads with Normal, an excess is cut off the tail — which takes Impatient first, then Normal, so over-authoring loses the harshest tickets rather than the Patient ones that were asked for. The user chose this over a remainder-style third field and over proportional weights. **The fixup is not silent, and that is the point**: `DrawPatienceMixReadout` states the effective counts before Generate is ever pressed, and it gets them by *calling* `BuildPatiencePlan` rather than repeating its arithmetic — a preview holding its own copy of the rule is a preview that eventually lies. **The mismatch warning is deliberately not a `DayValidator` rule.** `DayValidator` validates a `DayDefinition`; these counts live in `editorMeta`, which the runtime never reads (D-006, and `DayCatalogParser` was checked — it only reads the `runtime` block). Putting them into `DayDefinition` so the validator could see them would invert that arrow, so the warning sits in the section where the designer is typing. **The ordering needs no sort pass**: the plan is built in `PatienceOrder` and handed to the loop in sequence, so the output is sorted by construction. `PatienceOrder` is written out explicitly because `PatienceType` declares `Impatient, Normal, Patient` — the exact reverse — and iterating the enum would have opened every Day with its harshest tickets. **All three counts at zero means UNAUTHORED, not an empty Day**, which is the `DayJson` "tell unauthored from authored zero" rule: a Day file written before these fields carries none of them and reads them as 0, so `BuildPatiencePlan` returns an empty list and `GenerateCore` falls back to the old roll. The feature is therefore opt-in per Day and no already-authored Day changes balance; already-generated sequences were never at risk either, since each ticket carries its own `patienceType` and the counts only steer the next Generate. NOTE `BuildPatiencePlan` is public for the tests and the readout, and is the only place the rule exists. **Not verified in Unity**: the editor is open, so this is confirmed only by clean builds across DaySystem, Core, TicketSystem, Assembly-CSharp, Editor and the test assembly, plus eight new tests that have not been executed — the box's layout and the readout's wording need a Day Editor pass — affects: DayJson.cs, DayContentGenerator.cs, DayEditorModel.cs, DayContentGeneratorTests.cs, DayEditorModelConverterTests.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-24

D-056: A COMPLETED day pays the player one Gem per star it scored, at an authored rate (`GameConfig.GemsPerStar`, set to 1 in `GameConfig.asset`), awarded in `GameManager.OnDayCompleted` immediately before `SaveProfile()` — rationale: the user's ask ("kazandığımız yıldızlar gem olarak gelsin bize her yıldız 1 gem"). Four things worth keeping visible. (1) **Gems had no earn path at all until now.** `Wallet` could only ever subtract them (paid Continues), so `WalletTests` could not even tell "untouched" from "spending not refunded" by balance. `Wallet.EarnGems` is that path, and it is one-directional exactly like `EarnSoftMoney` — a negative amount is ignored rather than quietly subtracting — so it can never become a second spending route around `TrySpendGems`' affordability check. That also makes a 0-star day, or an authored rate of 0, a no-op instead of a case the caller has to remember. `Wallet` stays the compiler-enforced single writer of both balances; no new dependency arrow exists, since Bootstrap already reaches both DayLifecycle and ProgressionSystem. (2) **The rate is authored, the star count is not.** `DayLifecycleManager.StarCount` (3 minus lives lost, D-008) is a RULE and stays in code; what a star is WORTH is a balancing number and would violate the root invariant if compiled in. It lives on `GameConfig` rather than `EconomyConfig`, repeating D-026's reasoning verbatim — `EconomyConfig` is the per-ticket tip math consumed by `EconomyCalculator`, `GameConfig` is the asset both scene roots already hold — and `GameConfig.asset` was given `gemsPerStar: 1` in the same task, because a new serialized field reads 0 out of an existing `.asset` (the trap behind the stale `EconomyConfig.asset` fixed in 50be70c). (3) **The trigger carries the correctness, and it was already there.** `TicketSlotManager.IsDayComplete` guards the `DayCompleted` publish, so the handler fires exactly once per attempt and only on the winning path: no double payment, and a failed or abandoned day pays nothing — which is the right answer anyway, since it earns no stars. Ordering against `SaveProfile` is load-bearing: award first, or the gems the player watched land in the HUD are absent from the file they relaunch into. (4) **Replaying a finished day does not farm gems.** Earned gems are deliberately NOT recorded in the per-day spend ledger, so `RevertToDayStart` — which `RetryCompletedDay` calls before the redo — restores the day-start snapshot and takes them all back; the second attempt's stars therefore REPLACE the first attempt's rather than stacking, and the atomic-day rule (D1) reaches the new currency for free, including the clamp when a Continue was funded by the day's own gems. NOT DONE deliberately: no "+N gems" row on the Day Complete popup — it would need new hand-dragged Inspector fields, and the HUD's `GemsView` already ticks up at the instant the popup opens. **Not verified in Unity**: the editor is open, so this is confirmed by clean builds of `Assembly-CSharp` and the EditMode test assembly plus six new `WalletTests` cases that have not been executed — affects: GameConfig.cs, GameConfig.asset, Wallet.cs, GameManager.cs, WalletTests.cs, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-24

D-057: A day's earnings are no longer credited as they are made — nothing reaches the player's balance during a day, and the Day Complete popup PERFORMS the payout: the earned stars seat one at a time, each releases a gem that flies to the HUD gem counter, then coins appear around a small circle at the receipt's Total and fly to the coin counter, with every landing icon crediting its own share — rationale: the user's ask ("bölüm başarıyla bitirilene kadar para ve gem artmasın... popuptan bir animasyonla... tween ile gitsin"). Five things worth keeping visible. (1) **The animation IS the payout, not a skin over one.** The alternative — credit at `OnDayCompleted` and make the HUD views lag behind the real balance — was rejected because it needs a "hold the display" mode on `SoftMoneyView`/`GemsView` that must be armed BEFORE `GameManager.OnDayCompleted` runs, and subscriber order (GameManager subscribes `DayCompleted` in `OnEnable`, the popup in `Start`) is the wrong thing to hang money on; `EventBus.Publish` is synchronous and this project has been bitten by cascade ordering before. Crediting on each landing instead means the HUD views needed NO change at all — they are already reactive, so what the player watches arrive and what they own are one event rather than two kept in step. (2) **`DayRewardPurse` holds a DEBT, which is why it does not break the single-writer invariant.** Its numbers have never been in the wallet; `Wallet` still owns both balances, and `GameManager` — the only class holding both — pairs every `Take` with exactly one `Earn`. Every `Take` returns what was ACTUALLY taken, so a miscounted animation can underpay but can never mint money the day did not make, and `ShareOf` differences two running totals so the shares sum to exactly the total with no coin lost to rounding. (3) **It cannot lose the player money.** `AdvanceToNextDay` and `ReturnToMainScreenFromCompletedDay` commit the remainder before doing anything else, so an unwired `rewardFlight`, a skipped sequence, or a tap on Next Day mid-flight all pay in full; killing a tween deliberately does not run its `OnComplete`, so a destroyed icon never claims its share and the commit pays it as one lump. `RetryCompletedDay` DISCARDS the purse instead — a voluntary redo voids the debt, and `RevertToDayStart` takes back whatever the flight had already handed over, which together are what stop a finished day being farmed for its reward. `OnDayCompleted` still saves, and still saves none of the reward: what that write banks is lives and the day index, so a force-quit on the popup costs the ~2s of money in flight and nothing else. (4) **A real gameplay consequence, accepted rather than hidden: a paid Continue can no longer be funded by the day's own earnings**, because those earnings do not exist until the day is completed. It is forced by the ask and it agrees with the atomic-day rule (D1), but it does make Continues strictly harder to afford — revisit `LivesConfig`'s two prices if that bites. (5) **No new asset, no prefab.** The flying icons are built in code from two serialized sprites (13 per completed day is event frequency; pooling would be cost with no saving), and the feel knobs are serialized fields on the one component that reads them rather than a `RewardFlightConfig` with a single instance — the same shape `BoardAnimationConfig`'s own knobs have, so the "content is never embedded in code" invariant is satisfied without a hand-authored GUID. **Not verified in Unity**: the editor is open, so this is confirmed by clean builds of `Assembly-CSharp`, `ProgressionSystem` and the EditMode test assembly (the new test file was not yet in Unity's generated csproj, so it was compiled through a temporary clone and the compiler command line checked for it) plus 14 new `DayRewardPurseTests` cases that have not been executed — and the whole animation is unwired until the seven Inspector references are dragged, listed in the report — affects: GameManager.cs, DayCompletePopupView.cs, DayRewardFlightView.cs, DayRewardPurse.cs, DayRewardPurseTests.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-24

D-057: The Start Board cell's item picker is a **visual tile grid fed from the Day's own food pool**, replacing a raw unfiltered `ObjectField`; the tile-grid geometry the window's now-three grids share moved into one `DrawTileGrid` — rationale: the user asked for the cell picker to look like Food Selection and the ticket's modification picker instead of hunting through ScriptableObjects. **The visual change carried a real fix with it.** That `ObjectField` was completely unfiltered — not even category- or pool-restricted, unlike the ticket's own Main/Side/Drink popups — so it could place food on the board that this Day's Food Selection excludes, which `DayValidator` then refuses (it checks the board against the same `AllowedFoodPool`). The editor was authoring data its own validator rejects; feeding the grid from the pool closes that without a separate rule. Clicking the selected tile again empties the cell, which is what took over from the old "None" entry, and the existing Clear Cell button stays. An item outside the pool — authored earlier, or stranded by deselecting it from Food Selection afterwards — is surfaced in a warning rather than silently cleared, the same call the modification picker makes, because it still ships in the Day JSON. **The extraction was declared before approval and bounded on purpose.** This was the third grid, and all three shared the "count columns from the width measured on the last repaint" scaffolding — which is precisely the code carrying this window's documented scrollbar bug (sizing against `EditorGUIUtility.currentViewWidth` forced a horizontal scrollbar onto every inspector row). Three copies would have scattered that lesson across three places. Only the geometry moved; **per-tile drawing stayed with each caller**, because they do genuinely different work (toggling a selection, drawing a direction badge, single-select) and only the measuring is common. NOTE each grid keeps its OWN `lastWidth`, passed by reference: the cell picker draws inside a fixed 220px column and settles on ~3 columns while the other two get the full content width, so one shared measurement would have them overwrite each other's column count every repaint — nothing inside `DrawTileGrid` may assume the inspector's width, and the 220px column was deliberately not widened because it sits beside the grid preview and this window's history is full of fixed widths causing overflow. NOTE the ticket's own Main/Side/Drink remain name popups (`DrawCategoryItemField`); the user did not ask for those and they were left alone, which is the obvious next step if they want it. NOTE the tile *chrome* (highlight, fade, sprite, tooltip) is now duplicated three ways and is the natural follow-up extraction — it was left out because the approved scope said geometry only. **No tests**: the change is drawing plus which pool feeds it, the add/update/remove semantics around it are untouched, and there is no new pure rule to pin. **Not verified in Unity**: confirmed only by a clean `ExpoTheExplorer.Editor.csproj` build and by grepping that the grid geometry now exists in exactly one place — how the grid wraps inside 220px needs a Day Editor pass — affects: DayEditorModel.cs, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-24

D-058: The Start Board cell editor is **two-state**: an empty cell shows the whole grouped food grid, a filled one shows only the item's own tile and gives the room to that item's modifications — and nothing at all when it has none. New: `DrawModificationPicker`'s `hideWhenEmpty` — rationale: the user asked that clicking a food stop showing everything else, that only its modifications appear and only if it has any, and that clicking again or Clear Cell bring the whole larder back. **The cell is what selects the state, so there is no flag to keep in sync and nothing to reset.** That also means emptying needs no "go back" path of its own: clicking the selected tile again and the Clear Cell button both leave the cell empty, and the next draw is already the grid. The click branches are now separate too, which removed the compare-against-`current` subtlety the one-branch version needed — in the empty branch a click can only be a pick, and in the filled branch it can only be an unpick. `hideWhenEmpty` suppresses the header AND the "this food has no modifications" box, which is noise in a 220px panel; the ticket editor leaves it at the `false` default, where that same box is useful advice because it names the `FoodItemConfig` to go author them on — so the ticket side is unchanged by construction rather than by inspection. **One deliberate exception, and it matters more than the feature**: `hideWhenEmpty` never silences the orphan list. D-054 made modifications the owning food no longer offers visible precisely because they still ship in the Day JSON, and quieting them in order to tidy up an empty state would have undone that decision sideways. "Show nothing" means nothing to show, not nothing to admit. NOTE the assumption that the selected tile itself stays visible was stated before approval and is what makes click-again-to-empty reachable; hiding it too would leave Clear Cell as the only way out. **No tests**: the state is read straight off `entry?.Item` and the add/update/remove semantics around it are untouched, so there is no new pure rule to pin, and the rest is IMGUI. **Not verified in Unity**: confirmed by a clean `ExpoTheExplorer.Editor.csproj` build and by checking that the ticket editor's call site still passes no `hideWhenEmpty` — affects: DayEditorModel.cs, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-24

D-059: A ticket's Main / Side / Drink are picked from **visual tile grids** (`DrawItemSlot`), and `Modifications` moved to sit directly under Main Item. `DrawCategoryItemField` is deleted, which removed the **last raw `ObjectField`** in `DayEditorModel.cs` — rationale: the user asked for the ticket's item slots to look like the cell picker and Food Selection, and for modifications to move under the main item. **Nothing about what is selectable changed.** The category filter and the `AllowedFoodPool` restriction are exactly the ones the name popup already applied — a Side still cannot land in the Main slot, a food this Day did not select is still not offered — so this is presentation only, and it is built out of `DrawTileGrid` and `DrawCellItemTile` rather than new drawing code. **The modifications move is structural, not cosmetic**: the picker is keyed on `entry.MainItem` because `TicketFactory` takes a ticket's modifications from the main dish and nothing else, so sitting between Side and Drink made it read as belonging to the whole ticket. Under the dish, the relationship is visible. **A deliberate inconsistency with D-058, stated before approval**: slot grids stay OPEN when filled, where a filled Start Board cell hides its alternatives. The common action on a ticket slot is SWITCHING (Hotdog to Burger) and hiding the rest would make that two clicks; the cell hides them because a filled cell hands its 220px of room to the modifications below, which a slot in the wide column does not need to do. Clicking the highlighted tile still clears the slot, which is what took over from the popup's "None" — Side and Drink are genuinely optional, so clearing had to stay reachable. **One narrowing, deliberate**: the deleted popup fell back to an unfiltered `ObjectField` when no catalog was assigned; that is now a HelpBox. Not a regression but an alignment — Food Selection and the cell picker already behaved that way, and with no catalog Generate is blocked too, so there is no meaningful work to do down that path. NOTE all three slots SHARE one `lastTicketSlotGridWidth`, which does not contradict the per-grid fields elsewhere: those exist because those grids sit in sections of different widths (the cell picker's fixed 220px column), while these three are drawn one after another in the same column, so one measurement is the correct one. NOTE a slot holding food outside the pool is surfaced in a warning instead of being injected into the option list the way the popup did — same intent, said out loud, and consistent with the cell and modification pickers. **No tests**: the selectable set is unchanged and the rest is IMGUI. **Not verified in Unity**: confirmed by a clean `ExpoTheExplorer.Editor.csproj` build, by grepping that `DrawCategoryItemField` and every `ObjectField` are gone from the file, and by reading back the final section order — how three grids stack in that column needs a Day Editor pass — affects: DayEditorModel.cs, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-24

D-060: A day's **stars come from a score, not from a count of surviving lives**: `StarScore = clamp01(SavedSeconds / TotalTicketSeconds - wrongDeliveries x 0.20 - timeouts x 0.05)`, where `SavedSeconds` is the seconds still on each delivered ticket's clock, summed, and `TotalTicketSeconds` is every authored ticket's own time limit for that Day, summed. 3 stars at >= 0.55, 2 at >= 0.30, and **completing the day is worth 1** — rationale: the user asked for speed to matter ("biletlerin toplam süresini o gün için hesaplıycak ve sonra kullanıcının kaç saniyede bölümü tamamladığını hesaplıycak"), which D-008's rule ignored entirely: under it every mistake cost exactly one star no matter how the day was played, and playing fast bought nothing. Now a fast player can absorb one slip, which is the whole point of the change. **The user's stated form was wall-clock — total ticket time minus how long the level took — and it was replaced with per-ticket seconds after the problem was shown to them: three slots run CONCURRENTLY** (`GameState.TicketSlotCount`), so a day whose tickets total 390s (day_11: six Patient at 45 + four Normal at 30) is over in ~130-150s and `390 - 140` is hugely positive for everyone — every player would score 3 stars. Wall clock would need dividing by the slot count plus a tail correction (day_00's five Patient tickets have a floor of 90s, not 225/3 = 75s, because the last slots cannot stay 3-wide), and that correction would have to be re-derived every time the slot count or a Day's shape changed. Per-ticket seconds need none of it: they are immune to how many slots happen to be busy, they are already read at delivery for the tip tier, and they can be shown per ticket later. **The two penalties are deliberately unequal, and that asymmetry is the design**: a timed-out ticket contributes 0 to the numerator while its limit stays in the denominator, so it has already forfeited its whole share of the day's clock (~0.11 on a 10-ticket day) — charging it the full 0.20 again would bill one mistake twice. A wrong delivery costs a shake and a scatter, i.e. almost no time, so its bill has to be explicit or failing would be cheaper than being slow. The net cost of either lands near 0.20, which is roughly one star band. **Two rules survive from D-008 unchanged**: the ceiling is 3, and a day finished with >= `GameState.DefaultStartingLives` failures scores 0 — that is only reachable by paying to continue, which refills lives but deliberately does not reset the failure count, since it is still the same attempt. The completion floor is new: below the two-star threshold a COMPLETED day is worth its first star, because finishing is not nothing. **The four numbers live on a new `StarScoreConfig` asset**, not on `GameConfig`: `GameConfig.GemsPerStar` is what a star is WORTH once earned and stays there, while this is what EARNS one — and the star curve is the number a designer retunes dozens of times during balancing, which should not sit in the same asset as the board dimensions that must never move. The rule itself stays in code (`DayLifecycleManager`), where it is testable, exactly as D-008 left it. **Three single-authority moves came with it, none optional**: (1) the override-vs-patience time-limit rule moved out of `TicketEntryFactory` into `ResolvedTicketEntry.TimeLimitSecondsWith`, because `DayDefinition.TotalTicketSeconds` would otherwise be a second copy of it — one deciding the ticket the player plays, the other the score they are graded on; (2) the delivered ticket's remaining seconds ride on `DeliveryPayoutResult` rather than being re-read from the `Ticket`, so the tip paid and the time scored come from one measurement of one instant (and it is the last moment that number exists — the slot refills immediately after); (3) `GameManager` now hands `TicketSlotManager` and `TrayManager` two DIFFERENT named methods (`HandleTicketTimeout` / `HandleWrongDelivery`) instead of one shared `HandleLifeLoss`, so the cause is decided in the one class that composes both and no system had to learn what kind of failure it causes. Their signatures did not change. **The Day Complete popup shows the arithmetic** (time bonus / mistakes / net, as integer percents): a hidden score would leave a player who lost a star unable to tell which half of the day cost it. Those three rows are OPTIONAL serialized fields — the popup already exists in SampleScene and a required field would stop the whole receipt from appearing until the scene is wired; unwired they are skipped and `Start` logs it once. **The numbers are starting estimates, not balanced ones**: real `E` depends on when `BoardDistributor` puts items on the board, so the thresholds are meant to be re-set from play — which is why the popup shows the net score at all. **Verification**: `dotnet build Assembly-CSharp.csproj` clean (the new `StarScoreConfig.cs` IS in `ExpoTheExplorer.Data.csproj`, checked per the trap in the C#-verification notes); the EditMode suite could not be run from the session (Unity holds the project lock), so `DayLifecycleManagerTests` — rewritten with the score table, the wrong-delivery-vs-timeout pair, the clamps and the no-config case — is unrun. NOT verified in Unity: the two wiring steps (the config into GameManager, the three text rows into the popup) are the user's — affects: StarScoreConfig.cs, StarScoreConfig.asset, DayLifecycleManager.cs, EconomyCalculator.cs, DayDefinition.cs, TicketEntryFactory.cs, GameManager.cs, DayCompletePopupView.cs, DayLifecycleManagerTests.cs, DayTicketSlotManagerIntegrationTests.cs, TicketSystemTests.cs, ExpoTheExplorer/CLAUDE.md, fingerprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-24

D-061: The star score is shown as a **fill bar the three stars stand on**, and the bar's fill is what **paces the star seating** — each star pops the moment the fill crosses its own notch — rationale: the user asked what UI the new score deserves and chose this over two flat readout layouts. D-060 left the rating a hidden number: a player who lost a star could not tell how close they had been, and three bare percentages under the star row read as "each star is worth this much" rather than as one line of arithmetic (that is exactly how the first pass looked on screen). A bar with the thresholds marked on it answers "where am I, where is the line, how much did I miss it by" in one glance, which no number does. **The bar is scaled 0..ThreeStarScore, not 0..1**: full has to mean maxed, and a bar that keeps filling after the last star has been earned communicates nothing. Consequences that follow from that choice: the three-star notch IS the right edge, the two-star notch sits at `TwoStarScore / ThreeStarScore` (0.55 at the shipped thresholds), and the **one-star notch is the left edge** — it is the completion floor, earned by finishing rather than by speed, so it is already lit while the bar is still empty. **No threshold position is placed by hand.** `DayLifecycleManager` derives both numbers (`ScoreProgressToMaxStars`, `TwoStarProgressPosition`) from the config it is already the single reader of, and `StarScoreBarView` writes them as ANCHOR fractions on three marker RectTransforms that the star objects are parented under — so retuning `StarScoreConfig` moves the notches AND the stars, with nothing to re-drag, and a Canvas Scaler cannot drift them the way a pixel position would. Hand-placing them would have been faster to build and would have started lying at the first tuning pass, which is guaranteed to happen (D-060 shipped estimates on purpose). **The pacing change is the actual feature**, not the bar: `DayRewardFlightView`'s star loop used to wait a fixed `starSeatInterval` between stars; it now waits on `CurrentFill >= PositionOf(i)`. Two animations that merely happen at the same time do not say "my speed earned that star" — one driving the other does. The gem-per-star payout is untouched (shares are still per star index), so the money cannot drift out of this. Three guards, each for a way the wait could hang a coroutine that is HOLDING the day's payout: the wait also breaks on `IsFillFinished`, `CurrentFill` reads 1 when the fill Image is unwired, and `PositionOf` returns the right edge for an out-of-range index rather than a free pass at 0. **Everything is optional and degrades to D-057's behaviour**: an unwired bar leaves the fixed-interval rhythm verbatim, so the scene keeps working before it is built and the popup's own validation is untouched (the bar joins the three D-060 percent rows in the one-line "not wired" log rather than becoming a required reference). It is its own component rather than more fields on `DayCompletePopupView` because the FLIGHT has to talk to it directly and the popup does not own the flight's sequence; the popup hands it the two numbers, the flight drives it. One ordering subtlety is guarded explicitly: `Play` begins by finishing any previous performance, which on a REDO of a completed day would snap the bar to the new target for one frame before the fill it had just reset began — hence `fillStarted`, and hence two named methods (`SnapToTarget` cuts a performance short, `ShowTargetWithoutAnimating` is the broken-reference path where none can happen). `Prepare` runs before `popupRoot.SetActive(true)` on purpose: anchors resolve on activation, unlike the flight's own position measurements, which need the layout to exist first. **Verification**: `dotnet build` clean on both `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj` (`StarScoreBarView.cs` IS in the generated csproj, checked per the new-file trap); four new EditMode cases cover the two derived numbers, the retune-moves-the-notch case, the zero-threshold divide and the no-config case — unrun, Unity holds the project lock. NOT verified in Unity: the bar does not exist in `SampleScene` yet — building it and re-parenting the three stars under the markers is the user's editor step, and until then the popup runs on the old rhythm — affects: StarScoreBarView.cs, DayCompletePopupView.cs, DayRewardFlightView.cs, DayLifecycleManager.cs, DayLifecycleManagerTests.cs, ExpoTheExplorer/CLAUDE.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-25

D-062: **A star's position is the scene's business; no code writes one.** D-061's marker mechanism — three RectTransforms whose x anchors were written at runtime from StarScoreConfig, with the star groups parented under them so each star stood on its own threshold — is REMOVED, on the user's instruction after seeing it on screen: "yıldızlar hep oldukları yerde kalsınlar hareket etmesinler hiç" — rationale: the mechanism worked exactly as designed and the design is what was rejected. Three 100px stars spread across a 450px bar stop reading as a three-star rating: the row's gestalt is the thing players recognise, and standing the stars on their thresholds traded it away for information (how far the next star is) that the user did not want to buy at that price. Two cheaper fixes were offered first and declined -- shrinking the stars, and insetting the fill's track so the outer stars stop hanging off the bar's ends (the user had already re-parented the markers under Fill for that second one). **What survives is the half that carries the meaning without moving anything**: the fill still travels, and `DayRewardFlightView` still waits on `CurrentFill >= PositionOf(i)` before seating each star, so the star still pops at the moment its threshold is crossed. That is timing, not movement, and it keeps "my speed earned that star" legible with the stars sitting in whatever row the scene puts them in. `PositionOf` therefore stops being a place on screen and becomes purely a moment in the fill's travel; `DayLifecycleManager.TwoStarProgressPosition` keeps its job of saying WHEN, having lost its job of saying WHERE. **The removal is a writer deleted, not a feature flag**: leaving the fields in place and simply unwiring them in the Inspector would have "worked" (PlaceMarker no-ops on a null marker) and was rejected as a state nobody can read -- the next person to wire those three slots would silently get moving stars back, and with all three markers authored at the same point (0,0 inside Fill, as the scene had them) clearing the slots would have stacked the three stars on top of each other. Deleting the fields makes the old behaviour unreachable rather than one drag away. **Cost of the reversal, stated plainly**: the visible link between a star and the threshold it stands on is gone, and the bar no longer marks its thresholds at all -- it shows progress toward three stars and nothing else. If that link is ever wanted again, the honest form is small notch TICKS positioned from the config (marks may move; stars may not), not a return to moving the stars. **Verification**: `dotnet build Assembly-CSharp.csproj` clean. No test changed: the two derived numbers and their four EditMode cases are untouched, since what was removed consumed them rather than producing them. NOT verified in Unity -- the user still has to lift Star1/2/3 out of the markers back into their row and delete the three empty markers; Unity drops the dead serialized fields on the next scene save — affects: StarScoreBarView.cs, ExpoTheExplorer/CLAUDE.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-063: A wrong delivery left the player holding a broken item, and the fix is the same rule in three places: **nobody writes to a transform whose owner is mid-gesture, and nothing half-initialised is interactable.** The bug was one trigger with two faces — an item dropped into a tray raised the counter to `1/2` but drew nothing on the plate, and the item being held sat pinned up-and-left of the cursor instead of following it. Both come out of the 0.5 s window `WorldTrayView.OnTraySlotScatterBegin` opens by calling `BeginFlyInOverride(trayPosition, ScatterShakeDuration)` so the scattered items stay hidden while the tray shakes. **Face one, a scale-zero snapshot.** For that whole delay `BoardView.RefreshCell` leaves each scattered container active, `CurrentItem` assigned, parked on the tray at `localScale = Vector3.zero`, with its collider live — a stack of invisible but fully grabbable items exactly where the finger just released. `ApplyPickupVisuals` then snapshotted `homeScale`/`homePosition` FIRST and called `transform.DOKill()` second, so it captured that zero and cancelled the tween that would have undone it; `OnEndDrag`'s own `localScale = homeScale` re-applied the zero after `PlaceInSlot`, so the item stayed invisible in a slot `TrayManager` had correctly counted. Fixed by completing rather than cancelling, and before the snapshot: `transform.DOKill(true)`. Every tween that can be live on a board item at pickup — fly-in, snap-back, tray-settle — ends at that item's real resting place at full scale, so completing them is what makes the snapshot mean anything. **Face two, two writers on one transform.** `PlayWrongOrderShakeThenScatter` collected the tray's slot children once and then wrote their world X every frame for the full 0.5 s, snapping them back on complete, never rechecking whether the player had since picked one up. `OnDrag` set X from the finger, the next `OnUpdate` overwrote it back toward the tray, and Y kept following normally — and since a held item hovers a full cell above the finger by design (`DragFeelConfig.offsetFraction: 1`), the result read as "stuck up and to the left". Fixed with `IsShakeable`, checked EVERY FRAME rather than filtered once up front, because the pickup happens DURING the shake; `finalItem` is deliberately given a null handler so it still shakes, since it is being released rather than held even though its `OnEndDrag` has not run yet. **The third fix is the one no symptom pointed at.** `RefreshCell` read a reused container's fly-in destination straight off `container.position`, on the reasoning that a reused container "never moved". That holds only while every tween that ever moved it ran to completion, so a single interrupted fly-in stranded the container and then baked that stranded spot in as the cell's destination for good. It now recomputes from `CellPosition(x, y, -0.1f)` — the cell is the authority on where its item belongs, not the transform. Paired with holding the collider disabled for the length of `flyInDelay`, which closes the window rather than just surviving it. **What was NOT changed, and why.** `TrayManager`/`TraySlot` are correct throughout: the counter read `1/2` because the item genuinely WAS accepted — the model was right and the view was lying, which is why every fix landed in the view. **One narrower window is left open and is not a regression**: an item picked back up out of a tray mid-shake has already had a copy scattered to the board by `ScatterBackToBoard`, so the player ends up one item ahead. Closing that means changing who owns the scatter, which this task deliberately did not touch. **Verification**: `dotnet build Assembly-CSharp.csproj` clean, 0 errors (the single CS0618 `ContactFilter2D.NoFilter()` warning is pre-existing, on an untouched line). No EditMode test changed or added — all three fixes are MonoBehaviour/DOTween view code with no seam the EditMode suite can reach. NOT yet verified in Unity: the reproduction is manual (deliver a wrong order, then immediately grab an item near the tray). **Rests on one engine assumption**: DOTween's `DOKill(true)` completes a tween still inside its `SetDelay` rather than discarding it. If that is false, the collider gate closes the same hole anyway and the `DOKill(true)` degrades to a no-op rather than a regression. **Found on the way, not fixed**: `Data/BoardAnimationConfig.asset` carries only 4 of its class's 16 serialized fields — every `scatterShake*`, `delivery*`, `ticket*` field and `popInJumpPower` is absent, so those 12 run on their C# initializers and cannot be tuned in the Inspector until the asset is re-saved in Unity (fourth instance of this trap after D-004, `EconomyConfig`, `TicketCardVisualsConfig`); `GameOverPopupView.cs:55` throws `NullReferenceException` in `OnDestroy` on every play-mode exit; and `UpdateHoveredTray`'s fixed 8-collider `OverlapPoint` buffer can fill up with stacked tray items and miss the tray's own hitbox, silently breaking the highlight and the `OnEndDrag` drop fallback — affects: BoardItemDragHandler.cs, BoardView.cs, WorldTrayView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-064: **The lives HUD is a row of heart sprites, it lives only in the day scene, and lives stop persisting.** Three changes the user asked for together, and the third is the one with teeth. **(1) `LivesView` was rewritten** from a TMP label rendering `"3/3"` into a heart-row driver. The user had already built and saved the row: `HUDCanvas`'s prefab INSTANCE in `SampleScene` carries one added object, `LowerPanel` → `HeartPanel` → `Heart{1,2,3}`, each an `Image` on `EmptyHeart.png` with a single `FilledHeart` child on `FullHeart.png`. **That hierarchy dictated the API**: the empty heart is the PARENT and the filled heart its CHILD, so switching a parent off would take its filled child down with it — the view takes ONE `GameObject[] filledHearts` and an `emptyHearts` array would have been actively wrong rather than merely redundant. It binds a serialized `GameManager` and subscribes to `GameState.LivesChanged` **and** `MaxLivesChanged` from `Start`, caching the `GameState` it subscribed to so `OnDestroy` cannot unsubscribe from a different object. **(2) "Not on the main menu" needed no code at all, and that is the finding.** The obvious implementation — hide the row when there is no live session — is DEAD since D-022 gave the main screen its own `SessionHost`: `LiveState != null` is true in both scenes, so the menu can no longer be detected from the session. The user's structural answer sidesteps it entirely: the row is on the prefab INSTANCE, not the asset, so `MainScreen` never receives it. Verified from YAML — `HUDCanvas.prefab` is untouched by that edit and the instance's `addedObjects` is exactly `[LowerPanel]`. **The hazard this creates is real and is recorded rather than coded around**: Apply All / Apply Added GameObject on that instance would push the row into the asset and put hearts on the menu, the same shape as the `HudWalletSource` override trap. The old readout it replaces is `UpperPanel/HealthUI` (`HeartImage` + `HealthText`) INSIDE the prefab, which is what was putting lives on both screens; deleting it is a hand step for the user. **`HudWalletSource` lost `Lives`/`MaxLives`/`LivesChanged`** — `LivesView` was their only reader and the save-file branch behind them died with the profile field, so the class is now exactly what its name says. `HudCanvasPrefabSetup` wires two views, not three: leaving `LivesView` in its target list would not merely be dead, it would make `FindProperty("walletSource")` miss and log the tool's own out-of-step error on every run. **(3) Lives stopped persisting, which required overturning a documented rule.** `PlayerProfile.Lives` is gone, `CurrentVersion` 5 → 6 — the first version to REMOVE a field, and it carries no upgrade branch by design, since `JsonUtility` drops a JSON key with no matching field. `GameSession` neither applies nor writes it; `LivesManager.ApplyPersistedLives` was deleted (single writer, one fewer entry point). The rule overturned: **`AdvanceToNextDay` used to skip `LivesManager` deliberately** — its comment read "Lives are NOT reset on a successful advance, only a failed retry pays that cost" — and it now refills like every other day-start path. Leaving the skip would have split behaviour by ROUTE rather than by rule: menu-then-Play giving three hearts while Next Day kept a half-empty row, for no reason a player could see. `LivesManager.RetryDay` → **`RefillForNewDay`**, because with the advance path calling it, three of its four call sites were no longer retries; `GameManager.RetryDay` keeps its name, being a genuine retry. Two D-014 ordering rules are now dead but their lines stay with comments saying so: `ReturnToMainScreenAbandoningDay`'s refill-before-save (the save can no longer carry a life count) and `RetryCompletedDay`'s `SaveProfile`-last. **Tests**: the three `ApplyPersistedLives` clamp cases went with the method, replaced by `NewState_OpensAtAFullBar` — the one thing that now decides a day's starting lives; `Lives_ComeFromTheProfile` was INVERTED into `Lives_IgnoreTheProfile_AndOpenAtAFullBar` rather than deleted, because "the load path stopped reading a value" is exactly what a later save-field addition could quietly undo; and `Save_WritesNoLivesKey` asserts against the file's raw TEXT, because a reloaded object cannot tell an ignored key from an absent one. The v3 payload deliberately still carries `"Lives":2`, which is what proves dropping an unmatched key is safe rather than assumed. **Verification**: `dotnet build ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj --no-incremental` and `dotnet build Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj --no-incremental` both clean, 0 errors (4 pre-existing CS0649 warnings in an untouched `TicketGenerationConfig.cs`). **NOT run**: the EditMode suite itself, which needs the Unity Editor — the save-format change is the part that most wants it. **Not done, and it contradicts the code**: `ExpoTheExplorer/CLAUDE.md` Section 3 still states that lives persist and that `CurrentVersion` is 4; it is outside this task's approved manifest — affects: LivesView.cs, HudWalletSource.cs, HudCanvasPrefabSetup.cs, GameSession.cs, PlayerProfile.cs, PlayerProfileStore.cs, LivesManager.cs, GameManager.cs, LivesSystemTests.cs, PlayerProfileStoreTests.cs, GameSessionTests.cs, codemap-ui.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-editor.md, blueprint.md — 2026-08-25

D-065: **Keys — the meta resource that gates playing.** Plan: `.claude/key-plan.md` (5 steps); this entry records step 1, the model, which ships `KeyConfig` + `KeyManager` + 27 tests with **no caller anywhere in the game**, so behaviour is unchanged and no gate closes. **The four rules are the user's, given as answers this turn**: cap **5**; 40 Gems **fills to the cap** rather than adding 5, so the cap is never exceeded and there is one rule instead of two; a key is spent **only when leaving a LOST day** (Retry or Main Menu), which makes a paid Continue free and makes starting a day a GATE rather than a price; and the 30-minute clock **runs while the game is closed**, with backward-clock protection. **Keys are not lives, and the distinction is the whole design**: D-064 had just made lives a per-day allowance that resets every morning and is never saved, so the thing that persists and paces the player needed a home of its own. Neither reads the other; they are adjacent in the HUD and nowhere else. **The count lives in a PRIVATE field on `KeyManager`, not on `GameState`** — a deliberate departure from `Lives`, whose public setter leaves its single-writer rule resting on a comment, where here the compiler holds it; and `GameState` is the central state of a DAY, while a key outlives the day, the scene and the session. The step-1 verification compiled `KeyManager` under its real assembly name against the real `Core.dll`, which proves the other half: **it is a compile error for this class to assign `GameState.Gems`**, so the 40 Gems necessarily go through `Wallet`, still their single writer (new one-directional arrow `KeySystem → ProgressionSystem`, identical in shape to `LivesSystem`'s). **The clock is injected** as a `Func<DateTime>` defaulting to `DateTime.UtcNow`, and this is the project's **first and only** wall-clock dependency (a scoped grep confirmed zero prior uses of `DateTime` in `Scripts/`). That is not decoration: every rule in this class is a time rule, and a class reading the clock directly would put all of them out of reach of a test — for a rule the player experiences as "when do I get to play again", untestable is not acceptable. **`Refresh` is idempotent and cheap and every mutating method calls it first**, so correctness never depends on a tick existing; a per-second `Update` in step 3 will be display only, and per-frame was rejected outright (≈60 clock reads/s for a value that changes 1800× less often). **Three rules a naive implementation gets wrong, each pinned by a test**: a FULL bar drags the anchor to `now` so idle time is never banked — otherwise a key spent after three idle days is handed straight back; a partial interval survives because the anchor advances by WHOLE intervals rather than to `now`, or every `Refresh` would rob the player of up to 30 minutes; and a BACKWARD clock pulls the anchor to `now` rather than freezing the player until real time catches up, which for a clock set months back is effectively forever. Forward clock abuse stays possible, is unsolvable client-side, and costs the player nothing but their own pacing. **`TryRefillWithGems` checks the cap BEFORE touching the wallet**, so a full player is never charged — there is no path that spends without granting. **The save format's `-1` marker is decided here even though persistence is step 2**: `Keys` is the third field where 0 is not a safe default (0 keys is a locked-out player, exactly v3-`Lives`'s trap), but `PlayerProfileStore` has no config and must not learn what a key means, so it writes `-1` for "this save predates keys" and `KeyManager.ApplyPersisted` resolves it to the cap. A zero anchor means "no timestamp", which is what keeps `System.DateTime` out of the store as well. **`KeyConfig.asset` is deliberately NOT written by the AI** — hand-written `.asset` YAML has silently shipped missing fields four times here (D-004, `EconomyConfig`, `TicketCardVisualsConfig`, `BoardAnimationConfig`) — so the C# defaults carry the authored numbers and the asset is created from the menu in step 2. **Verification**: the three new files are absent from Unity's generated `.csproj`s, so a plain `dotnet build` would have looked at none of them and reported success — a known way for this project's build check to lie. Compiled instead through three throwaway csprojs that reproduce the real assembly boundaries (`KeySystem` outside Core's `InternalsVisibleTo`): 0 errors, 0 warnings. `dotnet build Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj` and the EditMode package also clean. **NOT run**: the 27 EditMode tests, which need the Unity Editor — and for a step whose entire safety net is those tests, that is the gap the user has to close. **CORRECTION, same turn**: the verification described above was INCOMPLETE and the user hit it — Unity refused to compile with CS0234/CS0246, because `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.asmdef` lists its references EXPLICITLY and `KeySystem` was not among them. The three throwaway csprojs reproduced assembly BOUNDARIES faithfully (which is what proved the `Gems` write is a compile error) but not Unity's asmdef GRAPH, so they never asked whether the reference was DECLARED. Fixed by adding the reference; proved by rebuilding Unity's own generated Tests csproj with the one `ProjectReference` Unity will add on its next regeneration — clean. A repo-wide check now backs it: every `using ExpoTheExplorer.*` across all 16 asmdefs resolves to a declared reference. **Standing rule from this**: adding an asmdef means editing the reference list of every asmdef that consumes it, in the same task — the symptom otherwise surfaces in the TEST file, far from the new system. It also forced a real map repair: the test asmdef's codemap line carried `sys: ?`, defended in its own note as honest because the assembly belongs to no single system; that is an argument for a shared owner rather than a question mark, so blueprint gained a `Testing` entry and the line now names it. **Also fixed here, a debt from last task**: `fingerprint.md` and `ExpoTheExplorer/CLAUDE.md` still asserted that lives persist and that `CurrentVersion` is 4; both were false, and this system is built directly on that save format — affects: KeyConfig.cs, ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.KeySystem.asmdef, KeyManager.cs, KeySystemTests.cs, codemap-core.md, blueprint.md, fingerprint.md, key-plan.md, CLAUDE.md — 2026-08-25

D-066: **Keys persist — save v7, and the `-1` marker that keeps the file boundary ignorant.** Step 2 of `.claude/key-plan.md`; D-065 built the model, this wires it to the session and the save file. **Still nothing on screen and no gate closed** — the only player-visible change is that an invisible counter now survives quitting. `PlayerProfile` gains `Keys` and `LastKeyRegenUtcTicks`; `GameSession` builds `KeyManager` and both writes and restores it; `GameManager` and `MainScreenRoot` take a required `keyConfig`. **The marker is the decision worth recording.** `Keys` is the third field where 0 is not a safe default (after v3's `Lives` and v5's `LastCelebratedDayIndex`), but it differs *in kind* from both: every earlier migration KNEW the right answer, and this one does not — the answer is `KeyConfig.MaxKeys`, and `PlayerProfileStore` has no config reference and must never gain one, since that would both break its file-boundary role and put a second copy of a balancing number in the codebase. So `UpgradeToCurrent` writes `-1`, the marker crosses the boundary, and `KeyManager.ApplyPersisted` — the one place the cap is known — resolves it. **0 could not double as "absent"** because it is a real, reachable count meaning "locked out, go wait"; had the upgrade written 0, every existing player would have launched unable to start a day with nothing on screen explaining why. `NewPlayer` takes the identical path, because "never played" and "played before keys existed" deserve the identical answer. `PlayerProfileStore.KeysAbsentMarker` is public so the value has one name across the upgrade, `NewPlayer` and the tests — but the reading end deliberately accepts ANY negative rather than referencing the constant, so a hand-edited `-2` means the same thing and cannot lock someone out. **The anchor needs no migration at all**: `0` means "no timestamp recorded" and the load path starts the clock then, which is also what keeps `System.DateTime` out of the store. It is stored as TICKS because `JsonUtility` round-trips a `DateTime` as `{}` with no error — the same trap `PlayerProfile` already documents for auto-properties and `HashSet`. **`Save` writes BOTH halves**, and that pairing is load-bearing: persisting the count alone would restart the interval in flight on every launch, handing a player who quit 29 minutes into a wait a fresh 30 every single time. **A fifth ordering rule** joins `GameSession`'s four: `KeyManager` is built after the `Wallet` (the refill is charged through it) with `ApplyPersisted` immediately after. `keyConfig` is a REQUIRED ctor parameter rather than optional — an optional one leaves a null `KeyManager` and pushes a null check into every future caller — so both roots guard for it, `GameManager` before building the session (otherwise the failure is a `NullReferenceException` from inside a constructor rather than a sentence naming the field), and `MainScreenSessionSetup` fills it plus WARNS when it could not, because an unfilled field takes the whole main screen down rather than degrading one widget. **Verification**: the asmdef trap from D-065 was checked FIRST this time and `ExpoTheExplorer.Session.asmdef` did need `KeySystem` — caught before writing a line of code rather than by the user's compiler. The repo-wide asmdef graph check passes across all 16 assemblies. Because Unity generates `.csproj` from asmdefs and had not regenerated yet, the real `Session.csproj` was patched in place with the `ProjectReference` Unity will add (the file is gitignored, so Unity overwrites it anyway) and the FULL tree built clean: `Assembly-CSharp-Editor` (covering `GameManager`, `MainScreenRoot`, the setup tool) and the EditMode package, 0 errors. **NOT run**: the EditMode tests, which need the Editor. **Still owed by the user before this step is real**: `KeyConfig.asset` does not exist yet — it is created from the menu, deliberately not written as YAML by the AI (four prior silent field-drops: D-004, `EconomyConfig`, `TicketCardVisualsConfig`, `BoardAnimationConfig`) — and until it is created and dragged into both scene roots, both scenes refuse to build a session and say so — affects: PlayerProfile.cs, PlayerProfileStore.cs, GameSession.cs, ExpoTheExplorer.Session.asmdef, GameManager.cs, MainScreenRoot.cs, MainScreenSessionSetup.cs, PlayerProfileStoreTests.cs, GameSessionTests.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md, key-plan.md, fingerprint.md, CLAUDE.md — 2026-08-25

D-067: **The key readout goes on screen, and it deliberately takes the OPPOSITE route to the heart row.** Step 3 of `.claude/key-plan.md`. `KeysView` renders "current/max" in the shared HUD prefab, `HudWalletSource` forwards the count and runs the display tick, `HudCanvasPrefabSetup` wires it. **Still no gate and no spend** — those are steps 4 and 5. **The routing decision is the substance.** D-064 gave `LivesView` a direct `GameManager` reference and took it *off* `HudWalletSource`; this step does the reverse for keys, and the two are consistent rather than contradictory because the widgets have opposite shapes: the heart row is a `SampleScene` object added to the prefab INSTANCE (which is what keeps hearts off the menu), so it can hold a scene reference; the key readout is prefab CONTENT shown on both screens, and **a prefab asset cannot store a scene reference at all** — the exact problem `HudWalletSource` was built for in D-013, whose `sessionHost` field is already the per-scene override.
