Both gerekçeler are written into the two files so the next reader does not "fix" one into the other. **Keys hang off the SESSION, not off `GameState`** (D-065 keeps the count private to `KeyManager`), which has two mechanical consequences in `HudWalletSource`: the subscribe sits OUTSIDE its live-vs-save-file branch, and the `OnDestroy` unsubscribe sits BEFORE that method's null-return — pairing it with the `LiveState` check would leak the subscription in exactly the case where a session exists but `LiveState` does not. **The display tick is display-only, by contract.** D-065 made `Refresh` idempotent and had every gate call it, so no rule anywhere depends on this tick existing; its only job is that the number climbs while the player watches. It runs **once per second, not per frame** — 60 clock reads a second for a value that moves every 30 minutes is three orders of magnitude of waste — and on **unscaled** time, so a popup that pauses the game by zeroing `timeScale` cannot freeze the wait being counted down. It lives on the source rather than the view so there is one ticker per scene, and because step 5's popup will want the same freshness. **A missing session renders NOTHING rather than a number.** Since D-022 both screens are meant to have a `SessionHost`, so no session is a lost reference, not a mode — and D-014 deliberately removed mode branches from views, which this does not reintroduce. The only number the view could invent is "0/5", which would tell the player they are out of keys: a far worse failure than a label left as the scene authored it. **No tests, structurally**: both files are in the predefined `Assembly-CSharp`, which an asmdef test assembly cannot reference (D-012). Every key RULE is already covered by `KeySystemTests`; what this step adds is render and tick. **Verification**: asmdef graph clean across 16 assemblies. `KeysView.cs` was initially absent from Unity's generated Compile list — the known way a plain `dotnet build` reports success without looking at a new file — but Unity was running and regenerated mid-check, so the passing build genuinely compiled it (confirmed by finding the `Compile Include` line and its `.meta`). `Assembly-CSharp-Editor` and the EditMode package both clean, 0 errors. Unity had also regenerated `Session.csproj` with the `KeySystem` reference D-066 predicted by hand. **Owed by the user**: a key SPRITE does not exist in the project yet (`Art/UI/` has no key image), and the prefab surgery — rename `HealthUI`→`KeysUI`, swap the sprite, add `KeysView`, run the HUD menu step — is manual, because prefab YAML is not written by the AI — affects: KeysView.cs, HudWalletSource.cs, HudCanvasPrefabSetup.cs, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md, blueprint.md, key-plan.md — 2026-08-25

D-068: **Giving up on a lost day costs a key — and that forces a documented rule to narrow.** Step 4 of `.claude/key-plan.md`. Both exits from a lost day charge one key: the Game Over popup's Retry and its Main Menu. **No button is disabled and nothing is blocked yet** — step 5 adds the check, and per the user's correction this turn it will be a check AT CLICK TIME that opens a popup, never an `interactable = false`: a greyed button does not tell the player why, a popup does, and it can offer the two ways out (wait, or 40 Gems). **Gated on `State.IsAwaitingContinue`, not on which method ran.** The user's rule is that a key is spent when a day is LOST and then left, so the flag states it literally — and it keeps `DebugTicketDeliveryController`'s R key from eating a key when it replays a day that was going fine. `RetryCompletedDay` stays free, because a voluntary redo of a day that SUCCEEDED is not losing one. **Two ordering rules, both silent if broken**, which is why each is spelled out at the line: (1) the flag is read BEFORE `LivesManager.RefillForNewDay` clears it — read it after and giving up quietly becomes free; (2) `RetryDay`'s save is LAST, after `DayRetried` has already triggered `wallet.RevertToDayStart` — move it earlier and a failed attempt's earnings get banked, which is precisely the "a day attempt is atomic" contract this file exists to hold. **`RetryDay` writing to disk at all is the narrowing.** That path deliberately wrote nothing, on the reasoning that a failed day was never permanent. A key spend is permanent, and if it is not written the player can press Retry, force-quit, and have the key back — retries become free and the economy is decorative. What reaches disk is the ROLLED-BACK wallet, i.e. the same figure `ReturnToMainScreenAbandoningDay` already writes, so no new money is banked; only the key becomes real. D-012 narrowed the same rule once before, for the abandon path; this is the second and last narrowing. **The spend result is deliberately ignored**: at zero keys the player must still be able to leave, because blocking that exit is a softlock — `TrySpendKey` floors at zero and the exit proceeds. **One private helper, not two call sites**, because `GameManager` is unreachable from the EditMode suite (predefined `Assembly-CSharp`, D-012) and having exactly one copy of the rule to read is the only protection it has; `KeyManager` also gains a forwarding property beside `LivesManager`, which step 5's popup will read. **Verification**: `Assembly-CSharp-Editor` builds clean, 0 errors, and both call sites were re-read after the edit to confirm the flag is captured before the refill and the save lands after the revert. **Not testable here**, and that gap is real rather than acceptable-by-default: the *rules* of spending are covered by `KeySystemTests`, but *when* it is called is not covered by anything — affects: GameManager.cs, codemap-core.md, key-plan.md, CLAUDE.md — 2026-08-25

D-069: **The out-of-keys popup, and the gate that lives inside it.** Step 5 of `.claude/key-plan.md`, the last one — the key economy is now complete end to end. `NoKeysPopupView` explains the block and offers the only two ways forward (wait for the timer, or 40 Gems for a refill to the cap); `MainScreenView`'s Play and `GameOverPopupView`'s Retry ask it on every click. **The gate lives on the popup, not in its callers.** `HasKeyOrShow()` returns true when the player may proceed and otherwise shows itself and returns false, so there is ONE answer to "what happens when there are no keys" rather than two copies drifting apart — and neither caller needs a session reference, which matters because `MainScreenView` has never had one. The name states the side effect deliberately: a gate that silently opens a window would be a trap at the call site. **Nothing is ever disabled** (the user's explicit correction two turns ago): both buttons stay pressable and the check happens on the click, because a greyed-out control tells the player nothing about why, while the popup names the reason and hands them both exits. **It fails OPEN.** Missing references, or a scene whose `SessionHost` has no session, return true and log loudly at `Start` — a forgotten drag must never leave the player unable to play at all. An uncharged key is the smaller wrong by a wide margin, and it would otherwise be indistinguishable from a bug in the economy itself. **The close button is REQUIRED, as a softlock guard**: the popup only self-closes when a key ARRIVES, so a player with no keys and not enough Gems would otherwise be stuck in it for up to a full regen interval — the user caught this while reviewing the preflight, where it had been marked optional. **Main Menu is deliberately not gated at all**, for the same family of reason: blocking the only exit from a lost day at zero keys is a softlock, so it always works and simply spends nothing (D-068). **`Hide()` is deliberately not called on Retry's refused branch** — the Game Over popup has to stay behind the explanation, or refusing a retry would drop the player onto a finished day with no UI whatsoever. **Auto-closes on `KeysChanged > 0`**, because its single claim stops being true the moment a key lands: the player waits, the key arrives, the window gets out of the way and Retry works. It owns a 1-second **unscaled** redraw that runs only while visible; `SecondsUntilNextKey` refreshes for itself, so the popup is correct even in a scene with no HUD, and unscaled means a paused game cannot freeze the wait it is asking the player to sit through. A failed Gem purchase leaves the window OPEN — the only possible cause is insufficient Gems, since it is shown at zero keys — so the player keeps seeing the price they are short of. **One instance per scene rather than a shared prefab**: `sessionHost` is a scene reference, and a prefab asset cannot carry one (the lesson D-013 wrote down about the HUD). **Verification**: `NoKeysPopupView.cs` was absent from Unity's generated Compile list, which this time surfaced as a hard error rather than a silent pass because the new type is referenced — the csproj (gitignored, Unity regenerates it) was patched and the tree built clean, 0 errors. **No tests, structurally**: all three files are in the predefined `Assembly-CSharp`, unreachable from an asmdef test assembly (D-012); every key RULE is covered by `KeySystemTests`, and what this step adds is when the question is asked plus render. **Owed by the user**: the popup hierarchy is built by hand in BOTH scenes — root, message, countdown, Gem button, close button, optional not-enough-Gems label — then `NoKeysPopupView` is added and wired, and dropped into the `noKeysPopup` field on `MainScreenView` and `GameOverPopupView`. Scene YAML is not written by the AI (D-007) — affects: NoKeysPopupView.cs, MainScreenView.cs, GameOverPopupView.cs, codemap-ui.md, blueprint.md, key-plan.md, CLAUDE.md — 2026-08-25

D-070: **Haptics, as a system: eight authored moments, one vendor file, one buzz per frame.** Nice Vibrations 4.1.2 was imported by the user and is now wired to the game through a new `HapticsSystem`. **Which moment plays which haptic is content, not code** — `HapticConfig.asset` carries a row per moment (preset, priority, enabled), so retuning the feel is an Inspector edit and never a code change; `Reset()` seeds all eight, because a missing row is silent and silence is the hardest failure to notice. **The vendor is quarantined in exactly one file.** `HapticsBinder` is the only place in the project that names `Lofelt.NiceVibrations`; everything above it speaks in `HapticMoment`, and the config stores the project's own `HapticPreset` MIRROR of `HapticPatterns.PresetType`. That mirror is the deliberate cost of keeping `ExpoTheExplorer.Data`'s reference list EMPTY — which in this project is how "data depends on nothing" is said — and it is translated by a written-out nine-arm switch rather than a cast, so a renumbering upstream breaks the build instead of quietly playing the wrong haptic. Swapping the haptics vendor is now a one-file change. **The per-frame coalescer is a correctness fix, not an optimisation.** `EventBus.Publish` is synchronous and its handlers publish in turn, so moments routinely arrive together: a correct drop queues `ItemDroppedInTray` then `OrderDelivered`, the last life queues `LifeLost` then `GameOver`. Requests are collected and `HapticsBinder` drains them once in `LateUpdate`, highest priority winning and ties going to whoever asked FIRST — one frame of latency (~16 ms, below what a hand resolves) bought in exchange for one clear answer instead of a smear. **Two moments were left OUT for that same reason** rather than added and then lost to the priority rule: `TicketAssigned` (a delivery cascades straight into the next ticket's assignment) and `TraySlotScatterBegin` (a scatter only ever happens alongside a life loss, which outranks it). Coin landings are out too, on a different ground: their count scales with the day's earnings, so one buzz per coin turns the phone into a buzzer — if they are ever wanted they need a rule (first + last, or every Nth), not a call site. **The split between `HapticsService` and `HapticsBinder` is forced by the same predefined-assembly constraint as D-012**: the binder needs `GameManager`, which lives in `Assembly-CSharp`, and an asmdef cannot reference a predefined assembly — so the testable half is the half that made it into the asmdef, and `HapticsSystemTests` pins the priority rule against the two real cascades. **One component, two modes**: with `gameManager` wired (day scene) the binder subscribes to `TicketDelivered`/`LivesChanged`/`LivesDepleted`; left EMPTY on the main screen, which has no `GameState` at all, it is only a play surface the shop calls directly — an empty field there is a valid setup, not a mistake, so it is not validated. Life loss is read from `LivesChanged`'s DROP, so a refill or a paid Continue never buzzes. **Everything fails open**: an unwired config or binder costs a haptic and logs once, never a frame — the same stance D-069 took on the key gate, and for the same reason. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.HapticsSystem`, `Assembly-CSharp` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode` all build clean, 0 errors, and — the trap this project has been bitten by twice — each new file was confirmed PRESENT in Unity's generated Compile list first, so the clean build is not the silent pass of a file nobody compiled. Unity's own log carries no `error CS` after the latest compile. **The EditMode suite was NOT run**: Unity holds the project lock, so it is owed from the Test Runner. **Owed by the user**: create `HapticConfig.asset`, and in BOTH scenes add a `HapticReceiver` (Nice Vibrations wants exactly one per scene, like an `AudioListener`) and a `HapticsBinder`, then drag that binder into `BoardView`, `DayRewardFlightView`, `MetaShopView` and `MetaGroundsView`. Scene YAML is not written by the AI (D-007) — affects: HapticConfig.cs, HapticsService.cs, ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.HapticsSystem.asmdef, HapticsBinder.cs, BoardView.cs, BoardItemDragHandler.cs, DayRewardFlightView.cs, MetaShopView.cs, MetaGroundsView.cs, HapticsSystemTests.cs, ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.asmdef, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-071: **Haptics, second pass: seven more moments, and a button mechanism that touches no view.** D-070 shipped the system with the six moments that carry the game's core loop; this closes the list the user and the AI worked through together — button taps, the invalid drop, the reward ceremony's gem and coin landings, a refused purchase, a spent key, and the out-of-keys gate. **Buttons get a component, not a call site in seven classes.** `HapticButton` hooks its own `Button.onClick` and asks for a serialized moment, so `MainScreenView`, `DayCompletePopupView`, `GameOverPopupView`, `MetaShopView`, `MetaShopRowView`, `MetaGroundsView` and `NoKeysPopupView` were all left untouched. The deciding reason is mechanical rather than aesthetic: **the shop's BUY button lives on an INACTIVE template row that `MetaShopView` clones per offer**, so the obvious alternative — a `Button[]` list on `HapticsBinder` — would have missed the button most worth feeling, because a scene list cannot name objects that do not exist yet. A component on the template rides along with every clone, and since `Awake` never runs on the inactive template, only the live rows register. **Coins buzz first and last only, and that came out of arithmetic**: `coinSpawnInterval` is 0.06s with up to `coinCount` icons, so one haptic per coin fires faster than a preset lasts — the hand would read half a second of undifferentiated rumble rather than ten ticks. Two deliberate beats say it better: the money started arriving, and it has finished. Gems need no such rule (at most three, spaced by the star rhythm). Both are requested on ARRIVAL, inside `Launch`'s `onArrive`, because the buzz belongs to the counter ticking up. **`UiTap` sits at the bottom of the priority table (5) on purpose**, which is the coalescer earning its place a second time: one tap produces both the press and its consequence — BUY gives `UiTap` + `PropPurchased`, a refused buy gives `UiTap` + `PurchaseRefused`, Retry at zero keys gives `UiTap` + `BlockedByNoKeys` — and in every case the player feels the OUTCOME, with the tap surviving only when nothing else happened, which is exactly what it means. **New moments are APPENDED to the enum, never sorted in.** Unity serializes an enum field by value and every authored row in `HapticConfig.asset` stores one, so an insertion would silently re-point every later row at the wrong moment: an asset that still loads, still validates, and plays the wrong haptic for everything past the insertion. **A trap in the first pass was closed with code rather than an instruction**: `Reset()` only runs on a brand-new asset, so an asset created in D-070's turn would carry no rows for these seven and all of them would be silently dead. Telling the author to hit Reset would have worked and would also have discarded every preset they had tuned; instead an editor-only `OnValidate` GROWS the asset with any missing moment at its default and touches nothing already there. That is append-only, consistent with what the file already asks (silence a row by unticking Enabled, not by deleting it), and the runtime meaning of a missing row is unchanged because `OnValidate` does not exist in a build. **`GameManager` was touched, deliberately and minimally**: the key spend lives there and nowhere else, so `KeySpent` is one optional field and one line beside `TrySpendKey`. It is requested unconditionally rather than on that call's result, because at zero keys the spend floors and returns false and that is precisely when the player most needs telling; D-068's rule is untouched. **A known gap, stated rather than hidden**: most routes through `SpendKeyForLostDay` replace the scene, so the request can be lost before `LateUpdate` flushes it — a device check, not worth a special immediate path that would bypass the coalescer for one moment only. **`DropRejected` fires only on the two `OnEndDrag` branches that genuinely refuse**; the relocation branch is silent because moving an item to another empty cell is the drop WORKING, and `OnPointerUp`'s settle-back is silent because `ItemPickup` already fired on that press. **Deliberately still out**: the ticket-timer urgency pulse, the one remaining item from the design conversation and the only one needing new code (the card has no threshold-crossing event, and it is the haptic whose dose is easiest to get wrong) — the user chose to feel this batch on a device first. **No new test**: what this pass adds is moments, not rules, and the existing `TheDefaultTable_CoversEveryMoment` iterates the enum, so forgetting to seed one of the seven turns that test red on its own. **Verification**: `Assembly-CSharp`, `ExpoTheExplorer.Data` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode` build clean, 0 errors; `HapticButton.cs` was absent from Unity's generated Compile list, so the csproj was patched, built and restored rather than trusting a pass that never saw the file; `UNITY_EDITOR` was confirmed defined in the Data project, so the `OnValidate` block was genuinely compiled and not skipped. EditMode suite still owed — Unity holds the lock. **Owed by the user**: Add Component → Haptic Button on every button that should buzz (on the shop's TEMPLATE row for the shop), binder dragged in; plus the binder into the new `haptics` fields on `GameManager` and `NoKeysPopupView` — affects: HapticConfig.cs, HapticButton.cs, BoardItemDragHandler.cs, DayRewardFlightView.cs, MetaShopView.cs, NoKeysPopupView.cs, GameManager.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-072: **Haptics, third pass: the three silent successes.** A sweep for what was still uncovered turned up three gaps that were inconsistencies rather than omissions — in each case the sibling event already buzzed and this one did not. **A paid Continue succeeding** (`GameOverPopupView.OnGemClicked`) was the worst of them: the most expensive tap in the game, hard currency spent to come back from a day that was over, and it felt exactly like tapping a menu button, because the gem button's `UiTap` was all that frame produced — `LivesChanged` moves UP on a refill and `HapticsBinder` deliberately reads only the drop. Its refusal side needs nothing, unlike the shop's: `Show()` disables that button when the player cannot afford it, so an unaffordable Continue is never a click to answer. **A Gem key refill succeeding** (`NoKeysPopupView.OnGemClicked`) is the same family and got its OWN moment rather than sharing the Continue's, even though both are "Gems spent, resource restored" — a rescue mid-day and a meta top-up are different stakes, separate rows let them be tuned apart, and the defaults already differ (Success vs MediumImpact). That is deliberately NOT the same reasoning as D-071's single `PurchaseRefused` for every refusal: refusals all say the same thing (no, nothing happened), while these two successes differ in what they cost and what they save. **The meta celebration** (`MetaGroundsView.CelebrateOne`) was a whole choreography with no haptic at all while its purchased sibling `PropLanded` had one. `PropUnlocked` is a separate moment on purpose: both end with a prop standing there, but a bought prop FALLS and hits the ground with the map shaking under it, while this one rises from silhouette to solid — giving a gradual reveal an impact haptic would describe a blow the screen never struck. **Its anchor is the line that snaps the reveal to finished**, which is the one place both routes reach: the fill running its course, and the player tapping past it. Skipping skips the ANIMATION, not the event, so it buzzes either way. Not inside the reveal loop, for the reason the coin burst already established — that loop drives `fillAmount` frame by frame, so one request per iteration would be a second of undifferentiated rumble instead of one beat. Several props unlocking at once each buzz, like the star row, and cannot machine-gun: `CelebrateOne` resets `skipRequested` per prop so each needs its own tap, and the coalescer caps the frame regardless. **Enum entries appended, not sorted in**, for the third time and the same serialization reason (D-071): the asset was at fifteen authored rows, every one storing a value, and `OnValidate` grows it to eighteen on the next load without touching what is there. **Deliberately still out**: the not-enough-Gems branch of the key refill (offered in the same conversation, deferred; `PurchaseRefused` would cover it in one line), splitting timeout from wrong delivery, and the ticket-timer urgency pulse. **No new test** — this adds moments, not rules, and `TheDefaultTable_CoversEveryMoment` iterates the enum, so a missing seed turns it red on its own. **A comment written in this turn was wrong and was corrected before it shipped**: it claimed the key-refill refusal already had a moment, which it does not — the line now states the asymmetry as the scope decision it is. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data`, `Assembly-CSharp` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode` build clean, 0 errors; all four touched files were confirmed present in Unity's generated Compile lists first (`HapticButton.cs` from D-071 has since been imported, so no csproj patch was needed this time). EditMode suite still owed — Unity holds the lock. **Owed by the user**: one drag — the SampleScene binder into `GameOverPopupView`'s new `haptics` field; `NoKeysPopupView` and `MetaGroundsView` already carry theirs from D-070/D-071 — affects: HapticConfig.cs, GameOverPopupView.cs, NoKeysPopupView.cs, MetaGroundsView.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-073: **The danger pulse: one condition, one rhythm, two amplitudes.** A ticket whose remaining time enters the danger zone now says so twice at once — an authored danger image blinks in over the card, and the card itself dims and brightens with it. **The danger zone is not a new number.** `TicketCardsView.TimerFillColorFor` already turned the bar red at `EconomyConfig.CriticalRatio`, and that same ratio picks the tip tier (CLAUDE.md — Tip Tiers), so the critical tier was simply NAMED (`IsInDangerZone`) and the colour method now CALLS it: "the bar is red" and "the card is pulsing" are one expression, and the window where one says the ticket is in trouble while the other stays calm is no longer expressible. Authoring a danger threshold of its own would have been a third number the player can already see the wrong answer to, since the bar sits on the card. The warning (orange) tier was left out deliberately: two thirds remaining is where the tip starts stepping down, not where a ticket is about to be lost. **The pulse is computed inside the per-frame timer read, NOT run as a looping tween, and that is a lifetime decision rather than a style one.** `PlayDeliveryTransition` does `canvasGroup.DOKill()` and re-fades that exact CanvasGroup, so a loop tween surviving the hand-off would fight it for the alpha and the card would flicker through its own exit animation. `Update` is *already* silent for the whole transition (`transitionInProgress`), which means a pulse computed there cannot overlap the one animation it could break — the suppression the file already relies on does the job a tween would have needed new bookkeeping for, and `RefreshTimer` was chosen because the card polls its slot every frame anyway (`RemainingSeconds` is mutated directly, so there is no event to bind). Cost is a cosine and two property writes per card, on a canvas the draining `fillAmount` already dirties every frame. **One cosine, two amplitudes**: the danger image takes the full 0→1 swing and genuinely blinks out, while the card only dims to `BoardAnimationConfig.TicketDangerDimAlpha` (0.45) — a card that vanished each cycle would take the timer bar the player is watching with it. Both read the same value, so the icon and the ticket cannot drift out of step, and authoring the floor at 0 gives the card the full blink too: the choice lives in the asset. The phase comes from `Time.time` rather than a per-card accumulator, so all three cards pulse together — three alarms out of step read as clutter, one rhythm reads as an alarm. **A zero period means solid-on, not a strobe.** This project has been bitten twice by an asset carrying a value the code did not expect, and clamping a stray 0 to some minimum would have produced a ~20Hz flash — unreadable and a real accessibility hazard — so the code branches on it and shows the danger image steady instead. **`dangerImage` is the one optional reference on this card**, against the file's own convention of failing loudly: `ValidateReferences` sets `isValid` false, which would take the WHOLE card down — an invisible ticket — over a decoration that has a working degraded mode (the card still dims). It warns once per card at `Initialize` instead, naming the prefab, so the feature cannot be silently absent. It is toggled with `Image.enabled` rather than `SetActive` so it keeps its layout footprint and never slides the card's other rows. **The alpha has one owner at a time**: `ResetDangerPulse` hands it back explicitly — `RebuildContent` calls it before writing its own alpha (0 for an empty slot, 1 for a ticket), so a card mid-pulse when its ticket is delivered, cancelled or times out does not carry that frame's dim over to whatever fills the slot next. **No haptic, deliberately** — D-071 and D-072 both deferred the ticket-timer urgency pulse for the same reason (the buzz whose dose is easiest to get wrong; the user wanted to feel the existing batch on a device first). The threshold now living in exactly one place means adding it later is one line against `IsInDangerZone` rather than a new comparison. **No new test**: this adds a look, not a rule — `IsInDangerZone` names an expression `TimerFillColorFor` was already comparing, and both touched views need a scene to exist. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data` and `Assembly-CSharp` build clean, 0 errors (the 4 remaining `CS0649` warnings are pre-existing, in `TicketGenerationConfig`); all three touched files were confirmed present in Unity's generated Compile lists first, so the clean build is not the silent pass of a file nobody compiled. Nothing was verified in the Editor. **Owed by the user**: add the danger visual to the `TicketCard` prefab as ONE `Image`, outside any layout group, with a sprite of their own (the repo has no danger/warning art) — the two variants `TicketCard Into` and `TicketCard UpDown` inherit it from the base — then drag it into the new `Danger Image` field; and confirm `BoardAnimationConfig.asset` shows 0.6 / 0.45 for the two new Ticket Card fields rather than 0. Prefab YAML is not written by the AI (D-007) — affects: BoardAnimationConfig.cs, TicketCardsView.cs, TicketCardView.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-074: **The danger flash speaks in colour, not transparency — and it switches rather than fades.** D-073 shipped the urgency signal on the wrong channel and the user rejected it on sight ("niye transparanlıkları artıp azalıyor kötü olmuş... renkleri daha cırtlak olmalı"). The reasoning behind the reversal is mechanical, not aesthetic: **`CanvasGroup.alpha` fades everything printed on the card**, so dimming it took the customer name, the dish photo, the modification rows and the timer bar down with the paper — the card was at its least readable at the one moment its numbers matter most, which is the opposite of what an urgency cue is for. Tinting `background.color` touches only the paper and leaves every glyph and icon fully opaque. **And the shape changed with the channel: a square wave, not a cosine.** A smoothly interpolated flash reads as *fading*, which was the specific complaint; an alarm switches. `Mathf.Repeat(Time.time, period) < period * 0.5f` — lit for the first half, rested for the second — and the period is now the only speed knob. **The danger icon blinks hard on/off** (`Image.enabled`) instead of ramping its alpha, so the sprite the author draws keeps its own colours at full saturation rather than being seen through a half-transparent version of itself; it shares the paper's phase deliberately, so both halves of the cycle say one thing (DANGER / normal) instead of trading places and reading as motion. **The two colours live on `TicketCardVisualsConfig`, the period stays on `BoardAnimationConfig`**, and that split is the point: how loud the alarm is, is a visual choice belonging beside the ticket sprites and the bar's three colours (and shared with the Day Editor's preview through the same asset); how fast it blinks is timing. `TicketCardsView.DangerFlashColorFor(bool lit)` takes a *half* and answers with a colour, so the card asks which side of the blink it is on and holds no opinion about the palette — the same single-lookup shape `TimerFillColorFor` already had. **The rest colour is the one knob for "how garish":** left white, the paper flashes red and returns to its own artwork; put a second loud colour there and it ALTERNATES between two (red/yellow). One field, both designs, no code change. **`ticketDangerDimAlpha` was DELETED rather than left unread** — it existed only for the alpha pulse, and dead config data in this project has a way of being re-authored later by someone who assumes it does something. **The paper returns to the PREFAB's own tint, not to the flash's rest colour**, which are two different things that happen to look alike at the default: the rest colour is half of a choreography, `authoredBackgroundColor` is the card's own paint. It is captured once in `Initialize`, because after the first flash the live value is an alarm colour and anything reading it later would learn the wrong thing. **`canvasGroup.alpha` is now untouched by the danger code**, so the delivery transition is its single owner again — the alpha coordination D-073 had to reason about simply stopped existing. What did NOT change: the threshold (`IsInDangerZone`, still `EconomyConfig.CriticalRatio`, so the flash still begins exactly when the bar turns red), the per-frame placement inside `RefreshTimer` and its no-tween argument, the shared `Time.time` phase across all three cards, the 0-period meaning (flash off, icon solid, never a clamped 20Hz strobe), and `dangerImage` staying the card's one optional reference. **A stale asset was found while diagnosing the user's "I can't change the blink period"**: `BoardAnimationConfig.asset` serializes only 4 of its 20 keys — the whole Delivery Success and Ticket Card blocks are absent from the file, so those values have always come from their C# initializers. This is exactly the trap `EconomyConfig.asset` carried until 50be70c, and it is the user's fix to make (touch any field in the Inspector and Unity re-serializes all of them); it is recorded on the codemap line so the next reader does not diagnose it twice. `TicketCardVisualsConfig.asset` was checked and is healthy, which is a second reason the two colours belong there. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data` and `Assembly-CSharp` build clean, 0 errors; no new file, so no csproj patch. Unity's own last compile (17:13) is newer than the D-073 sources and carries no `error CS`. Nothing was verified in the Editor. **Owed by the user**: still D-073's one wiring job (the danger `Image` on the `TicketCard` prefab, outside any layout group, its sprite theirs to choose), plus tuning the two new colours under `TicketCardVisualsConfig.asset` → Danger Flash, and re-serializing `BoardAnimationConfig.asset` as above — affects: TicketCardVisualsConfig.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.cs, TicketCardsView.cs, TicketCardView.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-075: **One menu item against a trap recorded four times: the configs get re-serialized, and a config edit finally sticks.** The user hit the same wall twice in one session — "blink period değerini değiştiremiyorum", then "bu yanacak rengi ben seçebilsem ya" — and offered the choice of moving the danger colours onto the prefab instead. They chose to keep them in the config and fix the reason it felt unreachable, which is the better half of the trade: `TicketCardVisualsConfig` is one place for all three TicketCard variants and is the same asset the Day Editor's preview reads, while two colours on a prefab would be three copies free to diverge with no player-visible reason. **The wall has two halves and one fix covers both.** A `.asset` does not update itself when its class gains a field — Unity leaves the absent key at the C# initializer and writes nothing — so `BoardAnimationConfig.asset` was down to 4 serialized keys out of 19, the whole Delivery Success and Ticket Card blocks missing. And a ScriptableObject edit made in the Inspector lives in memory until `AssetDatabase.SaveAssets` runs, so a value typed and then lost to the next script recompile looks exactly like a field that refuses to change. Dirtying every config under `Assets/Data` and saving once writes each object WHOLE: every declared field lands in the file, and from then on there is somewhere for an Inspector edit to land. **It changes no value, and that is the property that makes it safe to run on all twelve assets at once.** A key present in the file keeps its authored value (it was loaded from there); a key absent from the file is written at the initializer the game is *already* running on — the behaviour after is identical, the file simply stops lying about what it holds. The only subtraction is a key whose field no longer exists (`TicketCardVisualsConfig.asset` still carried `timerWarningRatio`/`timerCriticalRatio`, moved to `EconomyConfig` back in D-011), which is the same cleanup `50be70c` did by hand. **The folder, not the asset.** A one-asset button would not have prevented the fifth occurrence; this trap is on the record four times (D-004's `BoardDistributionConfig.asset`, where it silently disabled D-001's urgent-ticket guarantee; D-011's `EconomyConfig.asset`, where every tip rate came from a code default; D-063's finding on `BoardAnimationConfig.asset`; and today's D-074). **The before/after report counts top-level non-`m_` keys in the YAML rather than reflecting over the class**, deliberately: the question is "what does this file carry", and what Unity does or does not serialize is precisely the thing that has been wrong here before — asking the file avoids adding a second theory about it. A nested asset like `MetaCatalog` therefore reports a small number, which is its shape and not an error. **`DataTooling` is a new blueprint system line**, not a DayEditor file: this tool names no game type at all (it works on `ScriptableObject` and a folder path), so filing it under the Day authoring tools would imply a dependency it does not have — and a codemap line needs a real `sys:`, never `?`. It is K3: editor-only, and runs zero times in a build. **No test**: the deliverable is a menu click whose proof is `git diff Assets/Data` showing the files grow. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Editor` builds clean, 0 errors — and because the new file was absent from Unity's generated Compile list, the csproj was patched, built and restored (the D-071 procedure), so the pass is not the silent one of a file nobody compiled. Nothing was run in the Editor; the tool has not been invoked. **Owed by the user**: press **ExpoTheExplorer → Re-serialize Data Configs** (top level of that menu, beside Day Editor -- NOT the Project window's right-click Create tree, whose ExpoTheExplorer/Data submenu is the CreateAssetMenu list for these same configs and is what the first attempt found instead) once Unity recompiles, read the before/after lines in the console (expect `BoardAnimationConfig.asset: 4 -> 19`), and check `git diff Assets/Data` before committing — then the Danger Flash colours and the blink period are ordinary editable fields — affects: DataConfigReserializer.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-25

D-076: **The delivery receipt: a correct delivery finally says what it earned.** Until now success said "it worked" three ways — the tray grows and lifts, the ticket card slides out and its replacement drops in, the `OrderDelivered` haptic — and said nothing about the money, while `GameManager.OnTicketDelivered` was already computing the entire `DeliveryPayoutResult` (order value, tip, rate and **tier**) and handing it straight to `DayLifecycleManager.RecordDelivery`. So the one variable the player actually controls — speed, which picks the tip tier — was being decided invisibly. A number now rises and fades over the delivering slot, in that tier's colour, with the tier's authored word above it. **A RECEIPT, NOT A PAYMENT, and that boundary is the whole design.** D-057 deliberately deferred the money to the day-complete ceremony so the payout has ONE performance; a per-delivery coin flying to the HUD would give it two. So nothing here touches `Wallet`, reads `PendingReward`, or animates the HUD — it states a figure and leaves. **The figure is the banked one, not an approximation**: `RecordDelivery` rounds once per delivery (`Mathf.RoundToInt(payout.Total)`), so rounding that same value on screen matches the day's running total to the coin — which is what makes it safe to show a number the player will be paid much later. **The payout is passed IN as the struct that was just banked**, never recomputed: handing the view an `EconomyCalculator` would be a second opinion about what one ticket was worth, and it is what keeps this a view rather than a second economy. That also settled where the call goes — the tray and the card both learn a delivery happened but neither is ever told its value, so `OnTicketDelivered` is the only place with both facts. **No new event was added.** The obvious alternative — a `GameState` EventBus carrying the payout — would have made `ExpoTheExplorer.Core` name a type from `EconomySystem`, an arrow pointing the wrong way (and a reference cycle waiting to happen). A serialized field on `GameManager` was already the established shape for exactly this: it mirrors `haptics` field-for-field, optional and silent when unwired, so a forgotten drag costs a readout and never a delivery. **The colours are the timer bar's own three**, read from `TicketCardVisualsConfig` rather than authored again: the receipt then speaks the language the bar spent the whole ticket teaching ("my bar was green, the number came up green, it paid more"), and retuning the bar's red retunes the receipt's red. The `TipTier`→colour switch lives in the view because `ExpoTheExplorer.Data` references nothing by design and cannot name the enum — the same split the config's `PatienceType` colours already use. **The tier words are three authored strings** (`PERFECT`/`GOOD`/`LATE` as defaults), so an author who wants numbers only empties the fields and no code changes; the word and the amount share ONE TMP label (`word\namount`) rather than two references that could disagree about alignment. **An authored INACTIVE template is cloned per delivery and destroyed on complete** — the `TicketCardView`/`MetaShopView` template pattern, not pooled, because two slots can be delivered in the same frame and a single reused label would have to interrupt itself. It fades through `TMP_Text.alpha` rather than `DOFade`: this project's DOTween carries no TextMeshPro module, and a `CanvasGroup` instead would put an extra component requirement on every authored template. **`slotAnchors` is the one optional reference and falls back to the view's own RectTransform**, deliberately fail-open: a receipt in roughly the wrong place still tells the player what the delivery earned, while refusing to draw reads as a broken feature — and it means the thing can be seen working after two drags, gaining per-slot precision when the anchors are placed. A short anchor array warns rather than errors. **A map repair rode along**: `blueprint.md`'s Bootstrap line claimed `depends on: DaySystem, BoardDistribution, TicketSystem` while `GameManager` has been constructing and calling `EconomyCalculator` all along — `EconomySystem` was added, since the lie sat on exactly the handler this task touched. **No test**: the addition is a readout, and the arithmetic behind its number is already pinned by `DayLifecycleManagerTests`/`DayRewardPurseTests`. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data` and `Assembly-CSharp` build clean, 0 errors, with `DeliveryPayoutView.cs` inserted into Unity's generated Compile list first (it was absent, being new) and the csproj restored afterwards — the D-071 procedure, so the pass is not the silent one of a file nobody compiled. Nothing was verified in the Editor. **Owed by the user**: author an INACTIVE TMP label on the day canvas (its font and two-line layout are the receipt's whole look) into `Label Template`; drop three empty RectTransforms over the trays into `Slot Anchors` in slot order; wire `TicketCardVisualsConfig` and `BoardAnimationConfig`; drag the view into `GameManager`'s new `Delivery Payout View`; and run D-075's re-serialize menu item so the two new Delivery Payout numbers land in the asset — affects: DeliveryPayoutView.cs, GameManager.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-077: **D-076 REVERTED — the delivery receipt is out, and it is not to be re-proposed.** The user asked for it ("olur yapalım hadi"), saw it described, and rejected it on sight ("yok ya geri al bunu beğenmedim"). Reverted the same turn: `DeliveryPayoutView.cs` deleted (with its `.meta`, so Unity does not report a missing script), `GameManager`'s optional field, its one call and the `ExpoTheExplorer.UI` using removed, and `BoardAnimationConfig`'s two Delivery Payout numbers with their properties taken back out — the config is exactly what it was before D-076. `OnTicketDelivered` is once again two lines: compute the payout, record it. **Two things were deliberately NOT reverted.** The blueprint's Bootstrap arrow (`EconomySystem`, added by D-076) STAYS: the arrow was never the receipt's — `GameManager` has constructed and called `EconomyCalculator` since long before this, so the line was simply wrong and reverting a map repair would put a lie back. And D-074's danger flash and D-075's re-serialize tool are untouched; "bunu" was this feature, not the session. **The finding survives the feature**, and it is worth keeping on the record rather than losing with the code: a correct delivery still says "it worked" three ways (tray, card, haptic) and never says what it earned, while `OnTicketDelivered` computes the full `DeliveryPayoutResult` and shows none of it — so the one variable the player controls, speed picking the tip tier, is still decided invisibly. The two cheaper shapes offered alongside the receipt and not chosen are also still on the table: a short green flash on the tray (success currently whispers while a wrong delivery shakes and scatters, so failure reads louder than success), or a "paid" stamp riding the card's existing exit. **Do not rebuild the rising number without being asked for it by name.** **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data` and `Assembly-CSharp` build clean, 0 errors after the revert — which needed one extra step worth recording: Unity had already imported the new file and REGENERATED `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` with it, so deleting the source left the generated project pointing at a missing file (`error CS2001`) until that Compile entry was removed. Unity drops it on its own next refresh; a revert done between two Unity refreshes has to clear it by hand to get an honest build — affects: DeliveryPayoutView.cs (deleted), GameManager.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-078: **A broken heart rises off the tray that cost the life — and the slot index had to be threaded to make it truthful.** The user asked for a broken-heart sprite appearing on the tray and drifting up while fading out, and chose (over a single fixed spot) that it appear on the tray where the failure actually happened. That choice is what forced the plumbing, and the reason is a blind spot rather than a preference: **`WorldTrayView` can only see half of the life losses.** It detects a timeout by polling its item count and reacting when it falls to zero, so a ticket that expires over a tray the player never touched leaves no trace there at all — and an animation that plays for a wrong delivery and for *some* timeouts reads as a bug, not as a signal. The only place that sees every loss is `GameManager.HandleLifeLoss`, where both routes end. **So both `loseLife` delegates widened from `Action` to `Action<int>`.** This does NOT walk back D-060: that decision keeps `TicketSlotManager` and `TrayManager` ignorant of the failure's CAUSE, and the cause still splits in GameManager's two named entry points. A slot index is not a cause — it is the index both classes are already working on (`i` in the timeout loop, `slotIndex` in the batch resolve), handed on so a view can point at the ticket that failed instead of guessing between three trays. `TrayManager` already took `deliverTicket` as an `Action<int>`, so its two delegates now match. Fifteen test construction sites moved from `() =>` to `_ =>`, mechanically; the EditMode assembly compiling is what proves none was missed. **The heart is world-space, not canvas**, because the thing it is drawn over is: the trays and every item in them are `SpriteRenderer`s, so a sprite rising from one sits in their layer at their scale with no camera projection, and it fades through the same SpriteRenderer alpha `WorldTrayView` already fades a whole tray with on a delivery. Cloned from an authored INACTIVE template per loss and destroyed on complete — never pooled, since two can be drifting at once and one reused sprite would interrupt itself. Parented to its anchor with `worldPositionStays`, so it keeps the template's authored scale (rather than inheriting a tray's) and still dies with that hierarchy; the rise is a WORLD tween so one authored distance means the same at a scaled tray as at an unscaled one. `Ease.OutSine` on the rise against `Ease.InQuad` on the fade, so it is still solid while moving fastest and thins as it slows — drifting away rather than a light switching off. **It is asked for AFTER `LivesManager.LoseLife()` has run**, never before: a heart flying up for a loss that did not happen is the one lie this readout must not tell. **Both causes are treated alike** — to the player a heart is a heart, and which mistake spent it is what the wrong-order shake or the vanishing card already says. It fires on the *last* life too, alongside the Game Over popup, because that is still a life lost and a special case there would go quiet at the system's most important moment. Optional and silent when unwired, the shape `haptics` established (D-070); `slotAnchors` is optional even inside that, falling back to the view's own transform, because a heart in roughly the wrong place still says a life is gone while silence looks broken. Two numbers on `BoardAnimationConfig` (rise 1.2 world units — the tray itself lifts 1.5 on a delivery, for scale — and 1.0s). **No new test**: what was added is a readout, and the delegate's widening is covered by the paths already pinned (`TraySystemTests` on the wrong delivery, `DayTicketSlotManagerIntegrationTests` on the timeout). **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data`, `ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.TicketSystem`, `ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.TraySystem`, `Assembly-CSharp` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode` all build clean, 0 errors, with `LifeLostHeartView.cs` inserted into Unity's generated Compile list first and the csproj restored after (the D-071 procedure). The EditMode suite was not RUN — Unity holds the lock — but it compiling is the specific thing this task needed from it. **Owed by the user**: find a broken-heart sprite (the repo has none), build an INACTIVE world-space SpriteRenderer from it — watch its sorting layer, behind the tray it will never be seen — into `Heart Template`; drop three empty Transforms over the trays into `Slot Anchors` in slot order; wire `BoardAnimationConfig`; drag the view into `GameManager`'s new `Life Lost Heart View`; and run D-075's re-serialize item so the two Life Lost numbers land in the asset — affects: LifeLostHeartView.cs, GameManager.cs, TicketSlotManager.cs, TrayManager.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.cs, TraySystemTests.cs, TicketSystemTests.cs, DayTicketSlotManagerIntegrationTests.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-079: **The heart the author places IS the heart that flies.** D-078 shipped the broken heart as a template plus three anchors — clone at the anchor, animate the clone, destroy it. The user replaced that with something simpler on the spot ("direkt trayin içine nerede başlamasını istiyorsam oraya inaktif image olarak yerleştirsem"), and it is the better shape: with a template and an anchor, the thing you place is not the thing you see, so a start position had to be read off two objects at once and neither of them showed you the result. Now each slot's heart is one INACTIVE `SpriteRenderer` parked inside its own tray at exactly the position, scale and sorting layer it should start from; `Show` switches that object on, drifts it up, fades it, and puts it back. No template, no `Instantiate`, no anchors array, and one fewer authored object per tray. **Living inside the tray was CHECKED, not assumed** — this project has been bitten by two writers on one property more than once. `WorldTrayView.PlayWrongOrderShakeThenScatter`, the animation that runs on the very same frames as a wrong-delivery heart, collects only the SLOT children and shakes the tray in X; it never touches a child's alpha. The two sweeps that DO fade every child renderer (`GetComponentsInChildren<SpriteRenderer>(true)`) both live in `PlayDeliverySuccess` — the delivery path, which by definition never coincides with a life loss. So the heart rides the tray's rattle while it climbs, which is the tray moving its child, not a fight over the same property. **What replaced the clone's guarantees**: a clone was always born fresh, so the animated object's state needed no thought; a reused authored object does. The rest position and colour are captured once in `Start` and re-applied at the START of every play (not only at the end), and a flight still in the air is `DOKill`ed and restarted from rest — otherwise a second loss on the same slot (a wrong order, then its replacement ticket timing out) would begin halfway up and half faded. The put-back on complete restores position and colour as well as deactivating, for the same reason. The rise stays a WORLD tween even though the heart is parented, so one authored distance still means the same thing at a tray an author has scaled. **An empty array entry warns and shows nothing** rather than falling back to the view's own transform as D-078 did: the fallback made sense for a clone that needed *somewhere* to spawn, but a heart whose whole point is the position the author gave it has no sensible default. Everything else from D-078 is untouched: the `Action<int>` widening, the call after `LivesManager.LoseLife()`, both causes treated alike, the two `BoardAnimationConfig` numbers. **Verification**: `Assembly-CSharp` builds clean, 0 errors — and this time the file was already in Unity's regenerated Compile list (it had been imported), so no csproj patch was needed. Nothing verified in the Editor. **Owed by the user (revised, and shorter)**: park an inactive broken-heart SpriteRenderer inside each of the three trays where it should start, drop the three into `Slot Hearts` in slot order, wire `BoardAnimationConfig`, drag the view into `GameManager`'s `Life Lost Heart View`, and run D-075's re-serialize item — affects: LifeLostHeartView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-080: **The broken heart is a canvas Image, not a world sprite — asked as "why can't I just place an Image?", and the answer was that it should be one.** A UI `Image` only renders under a Canvas, and `TrayArea` is a world object, so D-079's design necessarily asked for a `SpriteRenderer`. But the user's instinct was the better design and the reason is a hazard D-079 had accepted: a sprite parked inside the tray has to win a sorting-order fight with the tray itself (`Default`, order -2) and with every item sitting in it, and **losing that fight is silent** — the heart simply never appears, which is the worst failure mode a decoration can have. A canvas draws over the world entirely, so there is nothing to lose. It also removes the second hazard by construction instead of by inspection: the hearts are no longer children of the tray, so `WorldTrayView`'s animations — the wrong-order shake, and the two sweeps that fade EVERY child renderer on a delivery — cannot reach them. D-079 had to prove those sweeps never coincide with a life loss; now the question cannot be asked. What is lost is that the heart no longer rides the tray's rattle, which nobody asked for. **The rise unit changed with the parent**, and that matters more than it looks: the distance is now an `anchoredPosition` tween in RectTransform units, so the authored default moved from 1.2 (world) to 120 (canvas — the same scale as the ticket card's 150-unit exit lift). A 1.2 left in that field would have produced a heart drifting a pixel and looking broken. Caught because `BoardAnimationConfig.asset` did not carry the field yet: the user had already run D-075's re-serialize tool (the asset is at 29 keys, up from 4, and `ticketDangerBlinkPeriod` is authored at 0.8 rather than its 0.6 default — the tool works and the danger flash is being tuned), but that run predates these two fields, so the corrected C# default is what they will get. Everything else from D-078/D-079 stands: the authored object IS the animated object, rest position and colour captured once and re-applied at the start of every play, a flight in the air killed and restarted, both life-loss causes treated alike, the call placed after `LivesManager.LoseLife()`. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data` and `Assembly-CSharp` build clean, 0 errors; `DOTweenModuleUI` was confirmed present in `Assets/Plugins/Demigiant/DOTween/Modules/` before relying on `Image.DOFade`/`DOAnchorPosY` — the same check that sent D-074's danger flash to `TMP_Text.alpha` when the TextMeshPro module turned out to be missing. **Owed by the user**: three inactive broken-heart Images on the day scene's canvas, positioned over the three trays where each should start, dropped into `Slot Hearts` in slot order (0 = leftmost); wire `BoardAnimationConfig`; drag the view into `GameManager`'s `Life Lost Heart View`; re-run the re-serialize item so the two Life Lost numbers land in the asset — affects: LifeLostHeartView.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-081: **The three GDD 5.2 powerups were built, and buying them was moved to the main screen.** The user reopened a scope that had been marked "deferred, do not build" in both the GDD and the project CLAUDE.md since v0.7. Plan and step-by-step record: `.claude/powerup-plan.md`. **The shape is KeySystem's, deliberately**: charges live in `PowerupManager`'s own private array rather than on `GameState`, because a charge outlives the day, the scene and the session, and a private field makes the single-writer rule a compile error instead of a comment. Gems keep `Wallet` as their one writer — a purchase asks through `TrySpendGems`. Every number (starting stock, Gem price, per-day grant) is authored on `PowerupConfig`; the save carries three named charge fields at profile v9. **PowerupSystem does not know what a powerup DOES.** The three effects need tickets, the board and the trays; taking references to them would have pointed one system at three others, so each effect is handed in as a `Func<bool>` by `GameManager` — the day scene's composition root — exactly as `TrayManager` and `BoardDistributor` already avoid each other. **That seam is also the two-screen split, not just tidiness.** The user moved buying to the main screen ("bu marketi... gem ile powerup alma işi aslında mainscreende yapılmalı"), because a store opened mid-service suspends the very time pressure a powerup exists to relieve. Both screens build a `GameSession` and therefore both hold a `PowerupManager` — the menu needs the counts to sell against — but only `GameManager` registers effects, so a use button that ended up on the menu by mistake physically cannot spend a charge: `TryUse` finds nothing registered and refuses. The rule is enforced by what is ABSENT rather than by a scene check. **The `Func<bool>` return value is a rule, not plumbing**: false means the effect had no work to do, and GDD 5.2 says such a press costs no charge. All three have reachable moments with nothing to do (no active tickets, an empty board, a finished day), so this is the difference between a convenience and a resource quietly lost — and it is the one property that cannot be tested from the manager's side, which only ever sees a bool. **A shared gate sits in front of all three**: `!IsAwaitingContinue && !IsDayComplete`. When the last life goes the day freezes behind the Continue popup, and spending there would be paying for a world the player cannot see; `BoardItemDragHandler` gates dragging on the identical flag. **Two boundaries were crossed knowingly.** First, `Ticket.RemainingSeconds` now has three writers — the constructor opens it, `TicketSlotManager.Tick` spends it, `PowerupEffects` puts it back. The first two are "time moves forward"; this is the only place it moves back. Routing it through `TicketSlotManager` to preserve one writer was rejected because that class would have had to learn what a powerup is. The boundary is pinned by a test, per `ownership.md`'s shared-ownership rule. Second, Auto-Collect moves items through `WorldTrayView.TryAcceptDrop` — the ordinary drop path — rather than writing into `TrayManager`, because a tray's contents are drawn entirely by the dragged object being reparented and a data-only collect would be correct in state and show an empty tray. **One press = the tickets in hand**: the active tickets are captured by INSTANCE and a slot stops the moment its ticket is replaced, or filling a tray would deliver it, assign the next ticket, spawn its items, and chain until the Day ran out. **Noise Clear was corrected mid-build** and the correction is the reason this entry is worth reading twice: it was designed (and half-planned) as a few seconds of dimming, and the user replaced it with outright removal ("soluklaştırmıycak... direkt boarddan kalkıcak"). That made the step SMALLER — no timer class, no `BoardView` change at all, since `BoardGrid.RemoveItem` already publishes `CellChanged` — and made the powerup stronger than its authored price assumes. Its rule is by IDENTITY, not by count: a ticket wanting one burger protects every burger, because a player calls a third one "too many", not "unwanted". The user asked directly whether it could break the guaranteed-ticket rule; `BoardDistributor` was read rather than assumed about, and the answer is two independent guarantees, both now tests — everything an active ticket requires is kept, and `SpawnMissingRequiredItems` caches nothing, so a recount re-spawns any gap on the next order. **Known consequence, recorded not fixed**: `leakedTickets` lets an upcoming ticket leak only once, so clearing that leaked item removes its noise contribution permanently. **What was deliberately NOT built**: no cooldown or per-day use cap (the stock is the limit); no confirm popup on the purchase (a prop is a placement decision, a charge is a few Gems); no event-reward earn path (there is no event system — day completion is the candidate GDD 5.2 itself named). `TicketRequirements` was extracted to Core in the same work with three consumers; the same rule still lives twice more inside `BoardDistributor` and that is left as a small task of its own rather than silently half-done.
affects: PowerupType.cs, PowerupConfig.cs, PowerupManager.cs, PowerupEffects.cs, TicketRequirements.cs, TraySlot.cs, PlayerProfile.cs, PlayerProfileStore.cs, GameSession.cs, GameManager.cs, MainScreenRoot.cs, MainScreenSessionSetup.cs, PowerupBarView.cs, PowerupShopView.cs, PowerupShopSetup.cs, AutoCollectRunner.cs, BoardView.cs, PowerupSystemTests.cs, PowerupEffectsTests.cs, TicketRequirementsTests.cs, blueprint.md, powerup-plan.md — 2026-08-25

D-082: **Day 0 opens with a forced first move: everything darkens except one hotdog and one tray, and a ghost hotdog loops between them until the player makes that move.** The user's ask, in their words: only one hotdog selectable at first, a ghost sliding to one of the trays, forced on the player, then the game continues normally -- refined mid-plan to "everything except 1 hotdog and 1 tray darkens", and then to the target tray's ticket card staying lit with them. **The content lives in the Day file, not in a config asset.** `runtime.tutorial` names the source cell and the target tray; a `TutorialConfig` asset was rejected because it would be a SECOND authority for what happens on Day 0, free to name a cell the Day's own `boardTimeline` never fills, with nothing checking the two against each other. Keeping both halves in one file is exactly what let `DayValidator` check the pairing -- a rule no other file is positioned to make. **The JSON block needs an explicit `enabled` bool**, which is the one place in this schema a bool is not a redundant toggle: `JsonUtility` returns a zeroed instance rather than null for a missing object, and every field of a zeroed tutorial block is a REAL cell and a REAL tray index, so absence cannot be detected by content the way `boardDistribution` and `ticketRuntime` detect it. The resolved side has no flag -- `DayDefinition.Tutorial` is null or it is a tutorial, so presence IS the answer and a half-configured one is unrepresentable. **`TutorialDirector` references nothing at all.** Three ints in; two questions and a completion out. It does not know what a `BoardItem` or a `Ticket` is, which is what makes every rule reachable from a test with no MonoBehaviour and no scene -- the same trick `PowerupSystem` uses to avoid pointing at the board, the trays and the tickets at once. Both gates answer TRUE once the step is done, so a finished tutorial and a Day that never had one are indistinguishable downstream; that is what keeps the two call sites reading as ordinary permission checks. **It is not a framework**: one authored step exists, so there is no step list, no interface and no state machine. **The darkening never writes a colour it does not own.** Tinting each renderer was the obvious approach and would have lost -- `BoardView` rewrites its layer colours on every `RefreshCell`, so the board would flicker back to full brightness on the next spawn. Instead a black quad covers the camera at sortingOrder 500 and the two lit things are lifted +600 above it, under `BoardItemDragHandler`'s +1000 drag boost, so an item the player actually picks up stays visible over the dim for free. Ticket cards needed the other half: Canvas UI composites above everything the camera renders, so the quad cannot cover them -- the two non-target cards get a stretched black `Image` child each, which leaves the `HorizontalLayoutGroup` undisturbed and lets the **target card stay lit by doing nothing at all**. **Nothing was authored in the editor.** No prefab, no scene object, no art, nothing dragged into an Inspector, and `SampleScene.unity` was never opened: the spotlight is built at runtime by the TARGET `WorldTrayView`, which is the only object already holding both of the ghost's endpoints (a serialized `BoardView` for the source cell, its own transform for the destination). The ghost is a CLONE of the live board item with colliders and every MonoBehaviour stripped, so it always shows the exact food and modifications being asked for. `unitymap.md` could not answer which objects those were -- it is UNMAPPED by a known generator bug -- so `SampleScene.unity` was parsed directly; that finding is what collapsed the editor step to nothing. **A pre-existing bug was fixed on the way through, not silently.** Refusing a press in `BoardItemDragHandler.OnPointerDown` never stopped a drag: UGUI still calls `OnBeginDrag`/`OnDrag` for the same gesture and their `CurrentItem == null` guards pass, so an item the Continue popup had "blocked" was still picked up and flown around with none of the pickup bookkeeping done -- and the comment above the method claimed the opposite. A per-gesture `pickupRefused` flag now gates all five handlers, which is what makes the tutorial's refusal real and closes the old leak with it. **Two guards cover the softlock, on different doors**: `DayValidator` errors at authoring time (the Save button is gated on it), and `GameManager` refuses to arm when the source cell is empty at Day Start, logging the cell and running the day as an ordinary one -- a hand-edited Day file never passes through the editor at all. Arming lives inside `ApplyDayStartBoardPreSeed` because all FOUR day-start paths run through it, and the director is REBUILT rather than kept, so Day 1 -- which authors no tutorial -- opens every gate again instead of inheriting Day 0's lock. Powerups are blocked while the step runs: Auto-Collect would sweep the very item the player is being told to drag. **A content bug was caught by checking rather than assuming.** The block was first authored at cell (2,0), which the Day's own `boardTimeline` fills with a hotdog carrying EXTRA MUSTARD, against tray 0, whose ticket wants a plain one -- the forced move would have been a wrong order costing a life on the game's first interaction. Moved to (1,2), a plain hotdog, verified against `TicketSlotManager.FillEmptySlots` assigning ticket *i* to slot *i*. **`DayValidator` deliberately does NOT check that pairing**, and this is the recorded limit of the guard: the rule for what a ticket requires lives in `TicketRequirements.RequiredCounts` and takes a runtime `Ticket`, so re-deriving it from a `ResolvedTicketEntry` would be the FOURTH copy of a rule D-081 consolidated to stop exactly that. The validator catches "no item on that cell"; "the wrong item on that cell" is still on the author. **The Day Editor carries the block through load/save without a UI** -- `ToDayJson` rebuilds the runtime section field by field, so any field the model does not name is silently DELETED the first time a Day is opened and saved, which would have meant day_00 losing its tutorial because someone retimed a ticket. **Deliberately not built**: no "seen it" profile flag, so the step replays on a Day 0 retry (persisting it would bump the save version, and the board is re-seeded anyway); the HUD is not dimmed (a darkened currency counter reads as a bug, not as focus); the two unpickable hotdogs are not greyed -- the ghost is the affordance.

D-083: **The Day 0 tutorial became a SEQUENCE, and its second step teaches modifications.** The user's ask: right after the first forced move, the extra-mustard hotdog at (2,0) must be dragged to its own tray, with the ghost as before, plus an arrow on the mustard ON the hotdog, a second arrow on the modification ON the ticket, and a line of text saying modifications must be read. **This is the repetition that earned the generalisation D-082 deliberately refused.** That entry shipped one step as one step and said a second moment would be what justifies a list; it arrived, so `tutorial` is now `steps[]` -- and still not a framework: a list and an index, no interface, no state machine class, no per-step subclass. **`Completed` became `StepChanged`**, because with more than one step the interesting moment is the boundary rather than the end; it also fires for the end and for an abort, which is how a tray knows to tear its spotlight down. **The pairing was verified, not assumed** -- the same check that caught D-082's bug. At Day Start slot *i* holds ticket *i*, so after step one delivers slot 0, tray 2 still holds the extra-mustard order and (2,0) still holds the extra-mustard hotdog; both steps were re-verified item-for-item and modification-for-modification against the shipped JSON before this was called done. **Finding the mustard needed a fact the game already computed and threw away.** A food's layers are just a stack of sprites, so "which of these IS the extra mustard" had no answer; `ResolvedLayer` now carries the `ModificationConfig` that made it visible, which is non-null only for a `HiddenByDefault` layer -- one that is on the item BECAUSE of a modification. The arrow then reaches the matching child by index, since the container's children are those layers in BoardView's own construction order; both ends of that coupling say so. **The arrows live in two rendering worlds**, the same split the dim already forced: the one on the hotdog is a `SpriteRenderer`, the one on the ticket is an `Image` parented to the modification row itself, so it follows the card's `HorizontalLayoutGroup` instead of being positioned against a screen that can re-flow. `TicketCardView` exposes that row read-only, handing back a `RectTransform` rather than the `ModificationSlotView` so a caller gets a position and no way to repaint a row that is rebuilt from the ticket on every refresh. **Still no art and still nothing to wire**: the arrow is drawn into a texture in code, and the message borrows the ticket card's own font, so it matches the game's type without a font being dragged into an Inspector. **The step boundary exposed a real ordering hazard, fixed rather than papered over.** Unity defers `Destroy` to the end of the frame while the next step's spotlight is built immediately, so a spotlight that restored its lifted sorting orders in `OnDestroy` would have put the OLD step's renderers back after the NEW step had already lifted its own -- permanently wrong ordering. `Dismiss()` now restores synchronously, and `RestoreAll` is idempotent so `OnDestroy` still covers the paths that never reach it (a retry, a scene unload). **The softlock guard had to move.** D-082 checked the source cell once, at Day Start; that is not enough for a list, because the board moves as the tutorial is played and a later step's item can be gone by the time its turn comes. Every step is now re-validated against the LIVE board as it begins, and a step that has become impossible aborts the whole tutorial with a logged reason. `DayValidator` gained the matching authoring-time rule plus one only a whole-file reader can make: two steps naming the same source cell is a guaranteed softlock, since the first consumes that item. **The message is content, not a string literal** -- authored in the Day JSON beside the step, in ENGLISH, which was checked against the game's existing UI ("Orders delivered", "Continue Day X", "Start Over") rather than assumed from the language of the conversation. The text: *"Modifications matter. This order wants extra mustard - bring the hotdog that already has it."* -- the rule as a headline, then the specific action, so it pairs one clause to each arrow. **Deliberately not built**: no message on step one (its ghost speaks for itself); no per-step timing or skip button; and the tutorial still replays on a Day 0 retry, since persisting a "seen it" flag would bump the save version.

D-084: **The tutorial's two gates left a hole that could strand the day in one gesture, and a third gate closes it.** Reported by the user: during the tutorial the item could be picked up and dropped on another board cell, and then it could not be picked up again. That is the whole failure -- the pickup gate permits exactly ONE cell, so the moment the item sat anywhere else it was beyond reach, no tray would accept anything, and the step could never be completed. **It was a known risk that got lost between design and implementation**: the very first design pass for D-082 named the board-to-board relocation branch as something the tutorial had to refuse, and the shipped code gated only the pickup and the tray drop. Worth recording as a process fact rather than just a bug: the two gates that WERE written are the two that appear in the feature's happy path, and the third is on a branch you only reach by doing something the tutorial does not want -- which is exactly the kind of path a happy-path reading misses. **The fix adds no movement code.** `BoardItemDragHandler.OnEndDrag` now asks `GameManager.IsBoardRelocationAllowed()` before the `TryGetCellAt && IsCellEmpty` branch; refusing it falls through to the snap-back that was already there, which tweens to `homePosition` and touches `BoardGrid` not at all -- so the item returns to its starting cell in the view and never left it in the model. That is precisely the behaviour the user asked for ("başlangıç yerine geri dönsün") and it cost one condition. **The gate is its own question rather than `IsPickupAllowed(destination)`.** The two agree on today's content, but they ask different things -- may this cell be picked up FROM, versus may an item be put down at all -- and a single predicate serving both would be true by coincidence of the current authoring rather than by rule. Same reasoning that kept the pickup and tray gates separate. **Both directions are pinned by tests**, and the second half matters more than it looks: written backwards, this gate would lock every board in the game forever, so there are tests for "refused during a step" AND for "released once the tutorial ends or aborts". **Not changed**: no content, no Day file, no scene, no asset -- this is a rule, and with no tutorial running the new check answers "allowed" and every drop path behaves exactly as it did.

D-085: **The tutorial's step message was drawn every time and never once visible: it was behind its own backing plate.** Reported by the user as simply "the text does not appear". The cause is a UGUI rule rather than anything about fonts, anchors or the Day content: **a Graphic is drawn before its children, always**. The first version put `TextMeshProUGUI` on the outer object and parented the dark plate underneath it, so the plate painted straight over the glyphs. The code even carried a comment claiming the plate was "added FIRST so it draws behind the glyphs" -- `SetAsFirstSibling` orders SIBLINGS, and a child is never behind its parent, so that reasoning was wrong and the comment made it look considered. **The fix inverts the two**: the plate is now the parent and the text its child, inset by a padding derived from the font size. No anchoring, position, font or fade changed -- the placement was never the problem, which is worth stating because "text not showing" invites moving things around. **The second half of the fix is that it can no longer fail silently.** `BuildMessage` had three quiet `return`s (no card, no Canvas, no borrowable TMP font) and this method fails invisibly by nature -- a step with no message looks exactly the same as a step whose message was dropped -- so each now logs which condition declined. That is the difference between the next occurrence being reported as "the text does not appear" and it naming itself. **Not changed**: day_00, the config numbers, the arrows, the ghost, the dim. `enableWordWrapping` was replaced with `textWrappingMode` in passing since the project's TMP version provides it.

D-086: **The tutorial's whole UI half rested on a false premise about Canvas rendering, and the invisible message was only its most visible symptom.** D-082 wrote, and D-083/D-085 inherited, that "Canvas UI composites over everything the camera renders, so the target ticket card stays lit by doing nothing at all". **That is true only of Screen Space - OVERLAY canvases.** This scene's `InGameCanvas` -- which carries every ticket card -- is Screen Space - **Camera** at sortingOrder **-1**, and the dim is a SpriteRenderer at 500. A camera-space canvas sorts against sprites like any other renderer, so the dim covered the entire canvas: the target card, the modification arrow parented to it, and the step message were all built correctly and then buried. **The user reported it as "the text still does not appear" after a first fix (D-085) that was real but not sufficient** -- the plate/child ordering was genuinely wrong and genuinely fixed; it just was not the only thing wrong. **The lesson is the shape of the mistake, not the mistake.** The premise was carried in a comment from `WorldTrayView`, reused across three decisions, and never checked against the scene -- while the scene was readable the whole time. It was settled here in one step by parsing `SampleScene.unity` for canvas render modes and sorting orders, which is what should have happened before the first line was written. **Two fixes, one cause.** The message now builds its OWN Screen Space - Overlay canvas, copying the game canvas's `CanvasScaler` so the authored font size keeps meaning what it means elsewhere; its sortingOrder is **-1 on purpose**, keeping it above every camera-rendered thing (Overlay always is) but BELOW `Popup Canvas` at 0 -- a ticket can time out mid-step, and a tutorial line floating over the Game Over popup is worse than one hidden behind it. The target card is raised by adding a `Canvas` with `overrideSorting` at 800: above the dim, below a dragged item so an item dragged across it still passes in front, and its arrow rides along as a child. That component is destroyed on teardown -- the card is not ours to destroy, only what we added to it. A card that already has a Canvas is left alone rather than reconfigured. **`DimCard` is deliberately kept** even though the non-target cards now sit under the world dim regardless: it makes "the other cards are dark" hold by construction rather than by the current render mode, which is precisely the assumption that failed here. **Not changed**: day_00, the config numbers, the ghost, the world-space dim and the board-side lifts, all of which were correct.
