D-087: **The tutorial's third step introduces the three powerups, and a step now has a KIND.** The user's ask: after the last forced move, a panel with three stacked rows -- each powerup's button image on the left, its description on the right -- with the wording left to me. **This is where the step list earns a second shape.** D-083 generalised one step into a list; this generalises a list of one shape into a list of two, and no further: `TutorialStepKind` has exactly two values, no per-kind class, no interface, and a third kind is a design decision that has to be argued for at that enum. A step with no `kind` is a `ForcedMove`, so the two authored steps and their JSON did not change. **The kind is mirrored, not shared.** `Tutorial` keeps its own copy of the enum for the same reason it keeps its own `TutorialStep`: taking DaySystem's would put the first entry on an assembly whose empty reference list is what makes its rules testable with no Day catalog. `GameManager` translates, written out as a switch rather than cast, so a kind added upstream without teaching Tutorial about it stops the build. It is the one file holding both enums, which is what the using-alias at its top is for. **Where the words live was the real decision.** The three powerups had NO name and NO description anywhere -- the shop hand-labels its rows in the scene -- so both strings went onto `PowerupConfig`, beside each powerup's cost and charges. They describe the POWERUP, not the moment it is explained, so the shop can read the same two strings later instead of keeping a copy free to disagree. The tutorial's own JSON says nothing but `"kind": "PowerupIntro"`. The descriptions: Auto-Collect *"Fills your trays with what the current orders still need."*, Time Reset *"Gives every waiting customer their full time back."*, Noise Clear *"Clears the board of every item no current order asks for."* -- one line each, verb first, each naming the difficulty GDD 5.2 says it relieves. **The icons forced the ownership choice.** They are not on `PowerupConfig`; they are the sprites on the three live `Button`s, so `PowerupBarView` builds this step -- the same rule that has the target tray build a move step's spotlight: whichever object already holds what the step needs. Its three button fields stay private and only a read-only `IconFor` is added, because a caller has no business reaching the `Button`, which is what spends a charge. **Time stops while the panel is up**, and that is correctness rather than polish: three descriptions take longer to read than a Patient ticket has, so a running clock would make the tutorial itself cost lives. Its own condition in `Update`, NOT a second use of `IsAwaitingContinue`, which means "the Continue popup is up" and is read by four other places. All three gates refuse during an intro step for the same reason -- anything reachable behind a modal is a way to lose a life while reading -- and the panel additionally carries its own raycaster and backdrop, so its modality does not depend on a rule living in another system. **Two completions, kept separate**: `NotifyTrayAccepted` and `NotifyReadingFinished` each ignore the other kind, so a stray tray drop cannot skip an unread panel and a dismissal cannot stand in for a move. **Two guards that would have been silent bugs**: `DayValidator` applies its cell and tray rules to `ForcedMove` steps only (an intro step names neither and would have been rejected for leaving them at zero), and `EnsureCurrentTutorialStepIsPossible` skips the board check for a non-move step (it would otherwise validate an intro step against its unused (0,0) and abort the whole tutorial). **Deliberately not built**: dismissal is a "Got it" button rather than tap-anywhere, because a reflex tap on a just-appeared modal is how a player closes it without reading a word; and the panel does not light the real HUD buttons, it copies their sprites, which is what "each row has that powerup's button image" describes.

D-088: **The main screen gets its own first-run tutorial -- a welcome that says what the game is, then an arrow at the store telling the player to renovate -- and it deliberately shares no code with the day scene's.** The user asked for the store hint and then added the welcome ahead of it. **The reuse question was the real decision.** `TutorialDirector` exists, works and is tested, but its entire surface is board cells, trays and drop gates, and this screen has no board, no trays and no Day; the only way to reuse it was to carry Day-shaped fields into a screen with none of them. So the two flows share the `Tutorial` system name and nothing else, which is written into blueprint.md so the next reader does not "fix" it into one. **"First opened" is derived, not remembered.** The condition is day index 0 AND nothing owned -- state that already exists -- rather than a `PlayerProfile` flag, which would have meant a save-version bump. It stops on its own the moment the player finishes a day or buys a prop, and it has a property a flag does not: a player who ignored the hint is offered it again. **The welcome is modal and the hint is not**, and that asymmetry is the design: the welcome is a wall of text, so a backdrop with its own raycaster stops a stray tap falling through to Play and starting a day the player has not read about; the hint ASKS them to tap the store, so the store must stay tappable, and opening it counts as dismissal alongside its own button. **The arrow is parented to the store button**, so it follows the layout rather than a hard-coded corner -- which is also why teardown destroys it explicitly: it is the one piece of this view living outside the view's own hierarchy, and it would otherwise hang on the button forever. **Where the words live**: `[TextArea]` fields on the component, authored in the SCENE. Not a config asset, because every other player-facing string in this project is already scene text (the shop's rows, the main screen's labels), so an asset would have been the odd one out; the values in code are the starting draft, the scene is the authority. The drafts: *"Welcome to the expo -- You're running the food stall. Every customer brings one order, drag the matching items off the board into their tray before their patience runs out. Serve them well and the day pays."* and *"Spend what you earn on the grounds. Tap the store to start renovating."* **`MetaShopView` exposes only `MarketButtonRect`** -- the RectTransform, not the Button, so a caller gets a position and no way to press the store on the player's behalf. **Asked rather than assumed**: whether the hint should also point out the expo grounds themselves (what "renovate" refers to). The user chose the store button alone. **This one has an editor step**, unlike D-082 through D-087: the day scene had objects that already held everything a step needed (the target tray, the powerup bar), and this screen has none, so a new object with two dragged references is honest rather than invented.

D-090: **Play does not start a day until the player has bought their first building, and it says so rather than going dead.** The user asked for a Play button that cannot be pressed until then. **That half was declined, deliberately and out loud**, because the project has a locked rule against it -- CLAUDE.md/D-069: *"No button is ever disabled... a greyed-out button does not tell the player why"* -- and a dead Play on a brand-new player's first screen is the strongest case that rule will ever see. The outcome the user asked for is delivered in full (no day starts), but the press ANSWERS: the arrow reappears on the store with its own line, *"Your stall isn't ready for customers yet. Tap the store to renovate it first."* Reverting to a literal grey-out is two lines if they want it. **The condition is derived, not stored**: owning nothing IS the state, so there is no new profile field and no save-version bump -- and it is the same condition the welcome tutorial reads, so the two can never disagree about who counts as new. Nothing un-buys a prop, so it can never re-trigger; Start Over wipes the profile and correctly brings it back. **It is read from the live session, never from the profile snapshot `MainScreenView` loads at Start** -- buying the building is exactly the event that opens this gate, and a snapshot taken before it would keep the player locked out after they had complied. **Every uncertainty resolves toward LET THEM PLAY** (unwired reference, no session, null list), which is not defensiveness: this gate sits on the only path into the game, so failing closed is an unplayable build that looks exactly like a save bug -- the same stance, and the same reasoning, as the key popup's fail-open. **The wiring rides the existing menu item** rather than a second one, because the gate and the tutorial are one feature: a project where only one of two setup steps had been run would have a Play button that blocks and explains nothing.

D-091: **A dev tab that plays any Day, and the number it writes is a POSITION, not a Day number.** The user asked for a tab in the editor to reach whichever level they want, and chose the dev reading of it: pick a Day, start the game there. `ExpoTheExplorer/Play From Day` lists the catalog and one click sets the saved profile's day and enters play. **The whole correctness of it sits in one field's meaning.** `PlayerProfile.CurrentDayIndex` is a position in the RESOLVED catalog, not a Day file's own `dayIndex` — `DayCatalogNavigator.GetDayAt` indexes the list directly, and `DayCatalogParser` DROPS a Day whose ids fail to resolve, sliding every later Day down one. The two numbers agree today only because `day_00..day_40` are contiguous and all resolve. So the window runs the SAME parse the game runs (`DayJsonSource` + `DayCatalogParser`) and writes the row's position. Listing `Resources/Days` by filename would have been half the code and would have sent the tester to the WRONG Day precisely when a Day file is broken — i.e. precisely when they are debugging it. **No runtime code changed.** The tool only calls existing public API, and it writes exactly one profile field through `PlayerProfileStore.Save`, so the save file keeps its single writer and its version stamp and no new schema field exists. It touches neither wallet, keys, powerup stock nor owned props: a tester at Day 30 wants their real inventory, and seeding one here would be a second answer to what `PlayerProfileStore.NewPlayer` already answers. **Two entry points, because they test different things**: *Play* opens the day scene directly and deliberately skips the key spend and the meta screen (the main screen's job, not what is under test), while *Main Screen* takes the real route in. **It refuses to jump while play mode runs** rather than half-applying: `GameSession` reads the profile once at construction, so a mid-play write would be invisible on screen and then overwritten by the running session's next Save. **It confirms first**, because this edits the real save file — moving back to Day 3 to check a tutorial is indistinguishable at the file level from losing thirty days of progress. Editor-only in every sense: `Assets/Editor`, `includePlatforms: Editor`, ships in no build — which is what makes "edit the player's save" a safe capability to build at all. It gets its own blueprint system (`PlayTesting`) rather than a home inside `DayEditor`, for DataTooling's reason: it authors nothing, it runs the finished catalog in the other direction, and it needs a `ProgressionSystem` arrow that `DayEditor` does not have and should not gain.

D-092: **A runtime cheat panel on SRDebugger, and the day index finally gets a single writer.** The user installed SRDebugger and asked for a debugger with gems, money, key refills, powerup grants and "go to whichever level we want". **The panel commands owners and assigns nothing.** Every cheat routes to the class that already owns the data — money through `Wallet`, keys through `KeyManager`, charges through `PowerupManager`, lives through `LivesManager`, the day index through `GameSession`. A cheat menu is the most tempting place in a codebase to write `state.Gems = 9999`, and one such line hands a balance a second writer and voids the root invariant for a debug button. Two owners had no free write path at all and gained a dev-only one **inside themselves** rather than exposing a field: `KeyManager.DebugSetKeys` (the only other ways were a 40-Gem purchase that refuses at the cap, and spending) and `PowerupManager.DebugGrant`. **The second is not `ApplyPersisted`, and that distinction is the whole point:** `ApplyPersisted` assigns the three counts and publishes nothing, because at load time nothing is subscribed — reusing it would set the numbers correctly and leave the HUD showing the old ones, so the cheat would look broken while working perfectly. **The day jump forced an ownership question the project had been getting away with.** `GameState.CurrentDayIndex` was written in three places — `GameManager`'s day advance and its completed-day exit, plus `GameSession`'s constructor — while `fingerprint.md` asserted `GameManager` was its single writer. That was simply false, and invisible only because the three never ran together. The user wanted to jump **from the main screen**, where `GameManager` does not exist, which is where a split ownership stops being a documentation error and becomes a real one. So `GameSession.GoToDay(int)` is now the single writer and the other three call it, the constructor included — a single-writer rule that exempts its own constructor is worth nothing. `GoToDay` clamps (an index past the last authored Day resolves `CurrentDay` to null and throws on the first ticket) and deliberately does **not** save: callers already decide that, two of them write several fields in the same breath, and saving here would double-write on those paths. It inherits D-091's hard-won rule — the number is a POSITION in the resolved catalog, not a Day file's `dayIndex`. **The jump is main-screen only and refuses inside a day rather than half-working**, because leaving a running day is a wallet decision `ReturnToMainScreenAbandoningDay` already owns and a cheat button has no business answering differently; afterwards it reloads `MainScreen`, since `MainScreenView` reads the day once in `Start` and the meta views read `CurrentDayIndex` without subscribing, so anything less leaves the screen lying. **Two traps are handled out loud rather than silently.** Granting money mid-day would VANISH on the next retry (the atomic-day contract), so the grant re-takes the day-start baseline and logs that it did — the price is that this day's legitimate earnings stop being revertible, which a tester chasing a revert bug must not discover the hard way. And the profile is written only at a few day-lifecycle moments, so a `Save Now` button exists because otherwise every cheat is lost on quit. **Binding is serialized, not searched:** `SROptions` is constructed by SRDebugger with no scene presence, so `DebugMenuBinder` carries a `[SerializeField] SessionHost` a human drags in (D-013 ruled out runtime lookups for this binding), and it registers the HOST rather than the session — `SessionHost.Session` is null until that host's `Awake` runs and Unity does not order `Awake` across GameObjects, so caching the session would be a per-load race. `Scripts/Debug/` carries **no asmdef and that is load-bearing**: the panel is a `partial` half of SRDebugger's global `SROptions`, and a partial's halves must share an assembly. Everything is wrapped in `UNITY_EDITOR || DEVELOPMENT_BUILD`, and SRDebugger's own trigger moved to `DevelopmentBuildsOnly` — it shipped set to `Enabled` with no entry code, which would have let any player triple-tap the release iOS build and mint gems. `DebugTicketDeliveryController` was deleted into this: its 1/2/3/N/R shortcuts are now buttons, because a keyboard cheat cannot be pressed on the device the game ships to. **It supersedes D-091's `PlayTesting` window**, which the user will remove separately.

D-093: **The debug panel gets its own opener, because SRDebugger's is a corner rect nobody can hit.** The user reported that triple-tapping did nothing and pointed out the real requirement: "mobilde de debuglamam gerekicek". SRDebugger's shipped trigger is a small `RectTransform` pinned to ONE screen corner and sized at runtime from screen DPI — so tapping the middle of the screen three times is a no-op, which reads exactly like a broken install, and on a phone that corner is both a few millimetres wide and the place the game's own HUD already lives. Verified it is NOT the enable-mode: `DevelopmentBuildsOnly` resolves through `Debug.isDebugBuild`, which is **true in the editor**, so the trigger was enabled the whole time; and it is not an Input System mismatch either — SRDebugger reads `Keyboard.current` under `#if ENABLE_INPUT_SYSTEM` and this project is Input-System-only. So `DebugMenuBinder` grew an opener of its own: **a whole-screen multi-finger touch** (default three), which cannot be missed and collides with nothing, since no mechanic in this game is played with three fingers at once. It calls `SRDebug.Instance.ShowDebugPanel` directly, which is not gated by the trigger's enable mode at all. SRDebugger's own trigger and shortcuts are left on — this is an addition, not a replacement. **The keyboard path is deliberately modifier-free**, and that is not a detail: SRDebugger's shortcuts are Ctrl+Shift+F1..F4, so an unguarded `keyboard[F1].wasPressedThisFrame` fires on Ctrl+Shift+F1 too, both openers run on one keypress, and the panel toggles open and straight back shut — a bug whose symptom is "the shortcut does nothing", i.e. the exact complaint this opener exists to fix. It was written and caught before it shipped. `Update()` is frame-frequency, which the cost model normally makes us argue for: it is a few memory reads and an int compare, O(1), at the cheapest tier, in a file compiled out of release builds — nothing measurable. **The mobile consequence the user must act on:** everything here sits behind `UNITY_EDITOR || DEVELOPMENT_BUILD`, so the *Development Build* checkbox in Build Settings is what decides whether a phone build has a debug panel at all. That is the intended posture (D-092 moved the trigger to `DevelopmentBuildsOnly` because it shipped as `Enabled` with no entry code), but it means a release TestFlight build has no panel by design; making one reachable there is a separate decision, and the honest lever for it is the entry code rather than deleting the compile guard.

D-094: **The day scene gets a settings menu, and the only genuinely new thing in it is that time stops.** The user asked for a settings section in `SampleScene` with Retry and Main Menu guaranteed, and picked the rest from a shortlist: Resume, a haptics switch, a day + star readout, pause while open, and a confirmation on both exits. **The pause is the feature.** Every ticket runs its own countdown, so a menu that let them drain would charge the player lives for reading it — `GameState.IsPaused` is a third gate on the one `TicketSlotManager.Tick` call, beside `IsAwaitingContinue` and the tutorial's reading hold. Three separate conditions rather than one combined one, so a clock that never restarts still says which holder forgot to let go. `Time.timeScale` is deliberately untouched (this project has never used it): only the ticket clock stops, and tweens, the reward flight and the UI keep running, which is what a menu over a frozen day should look like. **No economy rule was invented here, and that was a real temptation.** A mid-day give-up is FREE on both routes today — `SpendKeyForLostDay` is gated on `IsAwaitingContinue`, so `RetryDay` from a running day spends no key and writes nothing, and `ReturnToMainScreenAbandoningDay` reverts the wallet and saves without a key either. Charging for it is arguably the consistent reading of D-068 ("charging one route but not the other just teaches them which button is cheaper") and it is **left alone on purpose**: it is a balance change, it belongs to whoever tunes the key economy, and slipping it in under "add a settings menu" would make it findable only by a player who noticed their keys draining. Flagged, not done. **Two confirmation panels rather than one shared panel whose message code rewrites**, and that is the root invariant rather than a style call: a shared panel puts both authored questions into C#. Same reason the day caption receives only a NUMBER and the word beside it is a scene label. `SettingsPopupView` contains no authored string at all. **The menu button removes itself whenever the day is over, won or lost**, rather than staying put and refusing — a visible, pressable control that silently does nothing reads as the game hanging. On a WON day it would be worse than useless: this menu's Retry is `RetryDay`, the lost-day path, while a completed day has its own Retry with its own money rules on the Day Complete popup, and two buttons answering that question differently is a bug waiting for a player to find it. Four subscriptions cover every edge with no per-frame check, and one of them is not the obvious one: the way back from a completed day is `TicketAssigned`, **not** `LivesChanged`, because a day finished with all three hearts refills 3 → 3, which publishes nothing, and the button would have stayed gone for the rest of the session. **`ConfirmRetry` closes before it retries**, which is not tidiness: `RetryDay` resets the day in place with no scene load to tidy up after it, so a pause left standing would freeze the fresh attempt with no menu on screen to lift it. **The haptics switch is one bool that cost a save version.** `PlayerProfile.HapticsEnabled` at v10, and the first bool in that file — a bool has no spare value to mean "absent" the way `Keys` and the charge fields use `-1`, so the absent case can only be answered by a version branch, which writes `true`: absent-reads-as-false would switch haptics off for every existing player with nothing on screen to connect it to. Deliberately **no `= true` initializer** on the field, tempting as it was — it would have covered the migration case by accident and made the two answers inseparable, which is exactly what `UpgradeToCurrent`'s own comment refuses to rest on. **It is not saved on the flip**, and that is the one real compromise: `GameSession.Save` writes the whole profile, so a mid-day write would bank an unfinished day's earnings and break the atomic-day contract. The value lives on `GameSession` and reaches disk at the next ordinary save point; flipping it and force-quitting mid-day loses the flip, and nothing else. A settings file separate from the profile would fix that and is over-engineering for one bool today. **The mute is read in `HapticsBinder.LateUpdate`, after `TryTakePending`** — that is what makes it a mute rather than a second set of rules: every request still reaches the service, so coalescing and priority behave identically either way, and no stale moment is left pending to fire minutes later when the switch comes back on. It fails ON in every uncertain case, for the reason the key gate fails open: always-on haptics are a nuisance, a game that silently cannot vibrate looks like the vendor being broken on that device. **It has an editor step** (`ExpoTheExplorer/Settings Popup/Build In Day Scene`) because the menu is fourteen required references across three nested panels, and by hand that is thirteen chances to wire one confirmation's Yes button into the other. It builds its **own canvas** rather than hanging off the HUD prefab instance, where an Apply All would push a day-scene menu onto the main screen — the hazard the blueprint already records for the heart row. It also wires each button's `HapticButton` at edit time, which is a different thing from the runtime lookups D-013 ruled out: the reference lands serialized in the scene exactly as a drag would leave it.

D-095: **Start Over leaves the main menu; the debug panel takes it over.** The user asked whether the main screen's Start Over button could move into the debug panel. Half of it was already there — `Save > Delete Save File` has called the same `PlayerProfileStore.Delete()` since D-092 — so the work was the missing half and the removal. **The missing half was the RELOAD, and it is the half that mattered.** D-026 built that button to delete *and* reload, because everything on the main screen — the session and its wallet, the HUD, the grounds, the shop — is built from the profile in `Awake`/`Start`; rebuilding the scene is the only path that resets every one of them through its existing single writer, where patching the live session would need a second writer for the balance, the day index and the owned props. The panel now does the same, and **only on the main screen**: reloading out of a running day would abandon that attempt without settling its wallet, which is a question `ReturnToMainScreenAbandoningDay` already answers, so inside a day it deletes and says to leave first — the same host test `Go To Day` makes. **The two-tap confirm did not come across, deliberately.** It existed because a *player* could mis-tap a button sitting on a screen players use; someone who opened a debug panel with three fingers and walked into the Save category is not making that mistake. Carrying the guard over would have been cargo-culting the mechanism while its reason stayed behind. **Removal touched three places, one of which the maps did not name:** `MainScreenView` (the field pair, the two authored confirm knobs, the arm/disarm coroutine, `OnResetClicked`, and the now-unused `using System.Collections`), `MainScreenResetButtonSetup.cs` (deleted whole — it existed only to retrofit the button into an already-built scene), and `MainScreenSceneBuilder`, found by grep rather than by the codemap, which built and wired the button when creating MainScreen from scratch. `WireView` now takes the Play button alone. **Nothing about ownership moved**: `PlayerProfileStore` is still the save file's single writer and both the old button and the panel only ever COMMANDED it — deleting a caller cannot create a writer. **D-026 is superseded, not deleted**, and specifically its two-tap design is left standing in the record: this decision moves a testing convenience out of shipping UI, it does not rule that players may never reset their progress. If a real player-facing reset is ever wanted, D-026 describes how that guard worked and is where to start. The scene keeps the button object until the user deletes it by hand — the same one-click removal D-027 chose over hand-editing scene YAML.

D-096: **An area unlock is the one purchase that brings the shop back, and it waits for the animation to finish first.** The user asked for it in those terms: the shop reopens when the selected item unlocks an area, not on the others, and "açılmak için animasyonun bitmesini beklemeli". **This does not reverse D-047.** The shop still closes on every purchase, and the screen still ends on the prop rather than on a list — that stays the default and every ordinary prop keeps it. What D-047 could not see is that one purchase in this game has a payoff that is *partly a list*: `MetaPurchase.ShopItems` **hides** area-locked props entirely (D-019 plus the user's 2026-08-21 instruction that reversed MS2's "show what is coming"), so buying the paved square makes a handful of rows exist that did not exist a moment ago — and closing for good leaves them behind a market button the player has no reason to tap again. An area unlock would feel like it bought nothing. **The condition is the catalog's own `UnlocksArea` flag**, read at the commit, so authoring a second expansion in the Meta window needs no code and no id list anywhere. **The wait is a poll, and the callback shape was rejected on a real hazard rather than on taste.** `MetaGroundsView` gained one read-only property, `IsSettlingPurchase`; the shop waits on it in a coroutine. A completion callback on `RefreshAfterPurchase` was the obvious alternative and is wrong here: that method's early exits (a celebration is running, the bought prop is not among the active ones) finish **synchronously**, so the callback would fire *before* the shop's own `SetOpen(false)` — which D-049 requires to run after — and the reopen would be undone by the close that follows it. The grounds would have had to promise "never called synchronously", which nothing in the type system can hold. A property costs one bool and one tween query per frame for the ~1s a placement lasts, at most once per purchase, and it keeps each side owning what it already owned: the grounds decide when the animation is over, the shop decides what to do about it. **It waits for the WHOLE settle, not just the drop** — `placing || DOTween.IsTweening(content)`, which spans the fall, the ground shake and `RestoreFocus`'s journey back out. The two halves are needed because the framing changes hands between them, and the second starts in the same call that clears the first, so there is no frame where the property reads false in the middle; a placement that never zoomed the map leaves no tween and settles the instant the drop ends, which is correct rather than a gap. Reopening at the earlier moment would slide a bottom sheet covering most of the map over a map still travelling — the shop interrupting the payoff it is meant to reward. **`SetOpen` is now also where a pending reopen dies**, which is what makes the auto-open safe: every caller of it is either the player working the shop or the purchase path, so a timer from a second ago can never act on a decision the player has already overtaken. The purchase path is unharmed because it starts its wait *after* its own `SetOpen(false)`, and the coroutine clears the handle before calling `SetOpen(true)`, so that cancel cannot kill the coroutine calling it. The one abandon case `SetOpen` does not cover is **walking to another location** — that goes through the location bar, not the shop — so it is checked explicitly against the location the purchase was made on; reopening there would rebuild the rows for grounds the player never asked to shop. **The failure mode is deliberately soft:** if the wait somehow never ends, the shop simply stays closed and the market button still works. Nothing changed in the rules layer, nothing was added to the save file, and there is no editor step — the shop already holds the serialized `MetaGroundsView` reference this needs.

D-097: **The tutorial holds the day's clock for the whole of its run, not only for the steps that ask the player to read.** The user reported it as a fact rather than a theory: "tutorial sırasında arkadaki biletlerin süreleri akmaya devam ediyor". They did. **This widens D-087 rather than reversing it, and the interesting part is why the narrow version looked complete at the time.** D-087 stopped the clock for `IsHoldingForReading`, reasoning that the powerup panel carries three descriptions and takes longer to read than a Patient ticket has — a real argument, correctly applied, that happened to be stated about the wrong property of the step. The property that matters is not "this step involves reading"; it is **"the game is holding the player somewhere they cannot play"**, and a forced move does that just as completely: it refuses every board cell but one and every tray but one, so the three tickets already sitting in the slots cannot be worked on at all while their timers run. A first-time player working out which item goes where is reading the board for the first time, at first-time-player speed, and can lose a life to a ticket the tutorial itself forbade them to touch. **The fix is the condition widening, one line: `Tutorial.IsHoldingForReading` becomes `Tutorial.IsActive`.** It stays a fourth early-return of its own in `GameManager.Update` rather than folding into `IsAwaitingContinue` or `IsPaused`, for the reason D-094 gave when it added the third: all of them mean "hold the ticket clock", they are held by different things for different reasons, and a combined condition makes a stuck clock impossible to attribute. **It cannot strand the clock**, and that is by construction rather than by care: every way a tutorial ends — the last step completing, `EnsureCurrentTutorialStepIsPossible` aborting an impossible one, `Abort()` — moves the same `IsActive` this gate reads, so there is no third state where the gates are open and time is still held. **`IsHoldingForReading` survives, unchanged and still on `TutorialDirector`**, because it has a second reader with a different question: `PowerupBarView` builds and destroys the intro panel from it, and that genuinely is asking "is this step a panel". Deleting it and comparing `Current.Kind` at that call site would have moved a fact about the step list into a view. **Nothing else needed to change**, which is the sign the gate was in the right place already: `TicketSlotManager.Tick` stays a dumb "advance by this many seconds" with no tutorial flag inside it, `RemainingSeconds` keeps its single writer, and the ticket cards' timer bars freeze on their own because they only ever read that field. No config, no save version, no scene, no editor step. **What the player sees is the absence of something**, deliberately: no paused badge and no frozen-clock icon — the tutorial's dim is already the signal that the game is being held, and the bars simply stop.

D-098: **A tutorial step's unused fields are zero, not absent — and zero is a real tray.** The user pasted a console error: `WorldTrayView: the tutorial step's source cell (0, 0) has an item in the board model but no rendered container`. Day 0's third step is `{"kind": "PowerupIntro"}` and authors nothing else, so `sourceX`, `sourceY` and `targetTraySlotIndex` all deserialize to **0**. `WorldTrayView.IsTutorialTarget` compared `step.TargetTraySlotIndex == slotIndex` **without asking the Kind first**, so tray 0 read a step that named no tray as naming *it*, and went looking for an item to point at in a cell no step had named. **The collision is not a coincidence and this is the part worth remembering: `StepOneTray` in day_00 is 0.** The unset value is indistinguishable from a legitimate authored one, so the bug could never have been caught by looking at the number — only by asking what kind of step produced it. **Every other reader already asked.** `GameManager.EnsureCurrentTutorialStepIsPossible` returns early for a non-move step (D-087), `DayValidator` skips them in all three of its tutorial checks, and `TutorialDirector`'s own gates return `false` before touching the fields. `WorldTrayView` was the one place that did not, which is what made it look correct in review: it was surrounded by call sites doing the right thing. **The fix puts the answer in the step list, not in the view:** a new `TutorialDirector.SpotlightTraySlotIndex` returns the authored tray for a `ForcedMove` and **-1** for everything else — no step, a panel, a finished tutorial, an aborted one — and the view asks it instead of doing arithmetic on `Current`. That is the same reasoning `IsHoldingForReading` was created under: which kinds have a tray is a property of the steps, and a `Kind` comparison inside a `MonoBehaviour` is that property leaking into rendering. The sentinel is safe by the rules that already exist: slots are `0..TicketSlotCount-1`, and `DayValidator` refuses an authored index outside that range. **The visible symptom was the lesser one.** The error only fired because cell (0,0) happened to have no rendered container; had an item been sitting there, the tray would have raised a *real* spotlight — dimming the board and looping a ghost from an arbitrary item to tray 0 — on top of the powerup panel, with no error at all. A LogError that fires on the harmless branch of a bug is luck, not diagnostics. **It also repaired two sentences without touching them:** `RaiseTutorialSpotlight`'s failure message asserts "the model has an item the view never built a container for", which is only true because `GameManager` refuses to start a *move* step whose cell is empty — a guarantee that never covered the panel step it was printing about. **Four tests pin it** (`TutorialDirectorTests`), and the first asserts the trap before the rule: the panel step's tray field really is 0, and 0 really is a tray a real step uses. The same pass renamed `AForcedMove_DoesNotStopTheClock` to `AForcedMove_IsNotAReadingStep` — D-097 had made the old name assert the opposite of the shipped behaviour while its body stayed correct, which is the cheapest kind of lying test to leave behind. No config, no save version, no Day-JSON edit, no editor step: a panel step authoring no cell is correct content, and the code is now able to say so.

D-099: **A held day resolves nothing — the failure's own cascade waits for Continue.** The user asked whether the popup stops the game "generally, not just the timers", having watched new items spawn behind a Game Over popup. **The premise needed correcting before the fix: nothing was "still running".** There is no `Update` and no coroutine in any system; the only clock is `GameManager.Update`'s tick, and D-097 had already stopped it. What the user saw was not a world that kept going — it was **the failure's own event cascade landing after the popup had already opened**. `Tick` → `loseLife(i)` → last life → `IsAwaitingContinue = true` → *popup shows* → **then** `CancelTicket(i)` → `AssignTicket` → `TicketAssigned` → `BoardDistributor.OnOrderPlaced` (new items) and `TrayManager.OnTicketAssigned` (orphaned tray scattering). `TrayManager.TryAddItem` had the identical shape: `loseLife(slotIndex)` then `ScatterBackToBoard`. Both orderings are correct and deliberate — the life must be spent before the consequence, which is what makes D-078's broken heart land on the right tray — so the bug was never the order; it was that the consequence had nowhere to wait. **The fix is a deferral, not a reordering.** Moving the cascade *before* the popup would not have worked: the spawn and the scatter are tweened over several frames, so a one-frame head start still plays behind the popup. Each system now leaves its own action undone while `IsAwaitingContinue` stands — the timed-out ticket is left standing at 0 seconds, the wrong-order tray keeps its items — and performs it on the first live frame after the hold. Nothing is queued or stored: the postponed work is each class's OWN existing method on its own data, so all that is remembered is which slot it is owed on. **The resume is detected by READING the flag in `Update`, and rejecting the event was the main design decision.** A "day resumed" event on `LivesManager` is the obvious shape and is a trap here: the hold is lifted by four callers — `TryContinueWithSoftMoney`, `TryContinueWithGems`, `RefillForNewDay`, and SRDebugger's refill — so the invariant would be "every future caller remembers to publish", and the cost of one forgetting is a slot that never refills and a day that can never complete, discovered by a player rather than by a compiler. Reading the flag has no such failure mode: it is the same flag the gate above it already reads, in the one method that was already asking. **Deferrals are per-SLOT, and that is the one place a simpler version is genuinely wrong.** Two tickets can expire in the same `Tick`; deferring only "the one that ended the day" leaves the second sitting at 0 to expire again on the next live frame — spending a life from the bar the player just paid to refill. **Order on resume is tray, then tickets**: that is the order the failures happened in, and a deferred cancellation assigns a new ticket whose required items are spawned *from the board*, so the scattered items belong back on it before that question is asked. Each flag is cleared **before** its action, because both actions publish synchronously back through `GameManager` into these same classes. **Three clean-up paths already existed and now carry the deferrals too**: `ResetSlotsForNewDay` and `DiscardAllForNewDay` clear them — a deferral carried across a reset would have the new day open having already thrown a ticket away or dropped the old day's food on the new board — and the abandon path destroys the scene. **It also closes a bug nobody had reported:** with the timed-out ticket left standing, `AllSlotsEmpty` cannot go true behind the popup, so a last-ticket-last-life frame can no longer raise the Day Complete popup on top of Game Over. **What deliberately did NOT change:** `GameState` gained no field and no event, `LivesManager` was not opened, `BoardDistributor` stays order-triggered with no timer and no poll (the D-044 reversal of 2026-08-23 stands), and the two `loseLife`-first orderings are untouched. The settings popup needed nothing — it cannot be opened while awaiting Continue, its backdrop blocks board input, and the clock is stopped, so nothing can cascade behind it. Nine EditMode tests cover it across the two suites, including the same-frame double expiry and both reset paths.

D-100: **The delivery waits for the last item to land.** The user's report was mechanical and exact: the ticket “doesn't wait for the item I just put in the tray to go to its place — it goes straight away”. It was right, and the reason was that the drop which COMPLETED an order was the one drop that never got seated. Every other accepted item is reparented into a slot and flown to it over `TraySettleDuration` (`BoardItemDragHandler.PlaceInSlot`); the resolving one went to `PlayDeliverySuccessAndDestroy` instead, which grew and lifted it **from wherever the finger let go**, on its own transform, in parallel with the tray leaving. So the item never travelled and the tray never waited. **The fix is one hand-off, not a delay:** `WorldTrayView.TryAcceptDrop` now calls `PlaceInSlotAndDeliver`, which seats the item exactly as any other drop does and passes `PlayDeliverySuccess` as that settle tween's `OnComplete`. The wait is **the tween itself** rather than a `DOVirtual.DelayedCall(TraySettleDuration)` — a timer would be a second copy of a duration the tween already owns, and the two could drift apart the moment the ease or the number changes. **`PlayDeliverySuccessAndDestroy` is deleted rather than delayed.** Once the item is seated it is an ordinary child of the tray, so the tray's own grow/lift/fade already carries it and `DestroySlotChildrenImmediate` already cleans it up; running the old parallel animation on a parented item would double both the scale and the lift. That is also why `PlayDeliverySuccess` stopped taking a `finalItem` at all — there is no longer a special item to know about. **The ticket card needed nothing**, and that is the quiet reason the wait could be inserted at all: `SuppressPollUntilDeliveryTransition` is still called in the delivery frame and holds the card on the delivered ticket until `PlayDeliveryTransition` fires, however long that now takes. **Two guards ship with it, and both close holes the longer window opens rather than adding behaviour.** (1) `deliveryInProgress` — the tray refuses every drop from acceptance until its slots are emptied, because `TrayManager` emptied that tray and handed the slot to the next order the instant the batch resolved: an item accepted in that window would be counted by the model and then destroyed unseen. That window existed before this change (the grow/lift) and this lengthens it by one settle, so the guard belongs to the change. Auto-Collect never reaches it — its `ReferenceEquals` check stops a slot at the same instant the guard goes up. (2) `Update` releases that flag if the delivering item is destroyed before its tween completes, because **DOTween kills such a tween instead of completing it** — a retry landing in the gap would otherwise leave a tray that refuses drops for the rest of the session. **The wrong-order path is deliberately left alone.** Its shake still starts on the un-parented final item the instant the batch resolves: that beat is an interruption by design, and seating an item that is about to be thrown back onto the board would only stretch it.

D-101: **The outcome gets watched before it gets covered.** The user asked for both end-of-attempt popups — the win and the loss — to arrive “2-3 seconds delayed, so the player can see the animation”. Both were instant: `DayCompletePopupView` on `DayCompleted`, `GameOverPopupView` on `LivesDepleted`, each doing its work inside the subscriber itself. The delivery that finished the day and the broken heart that ended the run were therefore both played **underneath a popup**. `Show` is now the wait and `ShowNow` is the old body, scheduled through `DOVirtual.DelayedCall(...).SetLink(gameObject)`. **The popup is delayed; the outcome is not** — and that split is the whole decision. `LivesManager` sets `IsAwaitingContinue` and `TicketSlotManager` sets `IsDayComplete` before either publishes, so the day is already held for the entire wait: the clock is stopped, the board refuses every pickup on the loss side, no ticket is assigned on the win side, every slot is empty so `TrayManager` refuses every drop, and D-099's deferred scatter is still sitting in the tray. **Delaying the EVENT instead was rejected** for precisely that reason: the hold and the popup would come apart and the day would keep playing behind the animation the wait exists to show. **The two numbers live in `BoardAnimationConfig`**, not in a popup config and not as scene fields, because each one's job is to be longer than durations already in that file — the win delay than `TraySettleDuration + DeliveryGrow + DeliveryFade + DeliveryReentry` (D-100 added the first of those), the loss delay than `LifeLostHeartDuration`. A number kept away from the ones it must outlast can be tuned into covering the very animation it was added to reveal. Both default to 2.5s; both read 0 as “instant”, which is the old behaviour. **Both references are OPTIONAL and outside `ValidateReferences`, and that is the risk decision rather than laziness:** the failure this project has actually suffered is a popup that never appeared — an empty `rewardFlight` slot once failed validation, returned out of `Start`, and left a finished day showing nothing at all. So an unwired config costs the pause and nothing else, and a forgotten drag can never strand a day. Adding the fields made `BoardAnimationConfig.asset` stale in the way D-075 documented; the re-serialize menu item is part of shipping this.

D-102: **The last life loses its tray the same way every other life does.** The user reported that no tray animation plays when their life ends. It was true, and the cause was one day old: **D-099 defers the last life's scatter by leaving the tray UN-CLEARED**, and `WorldTrayView.TryAcceptDrop` forks on `count == 0`. So the deferred case fell into the ordinary "tray not full yet" branch, the item settled quietly into its slot, and the shake, the wrong-order visual and the scatter never ran — three lives fail loudly and the fourth fails silently. **D-099's own justification had already expired**: it names exactly one hazard, "throwing this tray's items across the board is the most visible thing that can happen behind a Game Over popup", and D-101 gave that popup a 2.5s delay — there is no behind any more. So the deferral is removed and the final wrong order shakes, flashes and scatters like the three before it. **`deferredScatters`, `ResolveDeferredScatters` and its `DiscardAllForNewDay` clear are deleted rather than left unset**, along with `GameManager.Update`'s call: a deferral nothing sets is dead code, and dead code shaped like a safety net is worse than no safety net. **The view side needed no change at all** — clearing the tray is what puts `TryAcceptDrop` back on its existing `newCount == 0` fork, and `PlayWrongOrderShakeThenScatter` then runs as written. That is the tell that the deferral was never a visual decision: it changed a MODEL fact the view reads to decide which beat to play. **The TIMEOUT half of D-099 stays**, and the asymmetry is the point: `ScatterBackToBoard` only returns the tray's own items and assigns nothing, while a deferred cancellation replaces the ticket and spawns a whole board round — a fresh order appearing on a day that just ended is wrong whether or not a popup is covering it. **A last-life TIMEOUT therefore still shows no tray scatter, and that is a named remainder rather than an oversight**: closing it would mean letting `TrayManager` scatter on behalf of a ticket `TicketSlotManager` has not cancelled, across a dependency arrow that is one-directional by design. The four EditMode tests were **inverted, not deleted** — the last-life test now asserts the scatter happens, and `DiscardAllForNewDay` keeps a test of the property it always really had (it empties without scattering, because the board is wiped in the same reset). `GameOverPopupDelay`'s tooltip gained the scatter: the wait must now clear `ScatterShakeDuration` and the board fly-in as well as the heart, and 0 puts the scatter back under the popup — the old problem, now an authored choice with its consequence written down.
D-103: **The settings button greys out instead of vanishing, and the resume flag is cleared before the event that announces it.** The user reported that after a failed day the HUD's settings button stays gone until they place something in the tray, and asked for the visibility toggle to become an `interactable` toggle — explicitly adding that the display fault must not simply become an interactability fault, i.e. Retry must leave the button usable. Both halves were needed, because **the second half was a real ordering bug hiding behind the first**. `LivesManager.RefillLivesAndResume` wrote `state.Lives = state.MaxLives` and only then `state.IsAwaitingContinue = false`; `GameState.Lives` publishes `LivesChanged` **from its setter, synchronously**, so `SettingsPopupView.OnLivesChanged` ran inside that assignment and read the flag as still `true`. It switched the button off again on the very frame Retry was meant to bring it back, and since nothing publishes afterwards the button stayed gone until the next `LivesChanged` — **which is the next life LOST**, i.e. the wrong tray drop the user described. Switching to `interactable` alone would have shipped the identical bug as a permanently grey button. **The fix is the order, not a re-read:** the flag is cleared before the Lives write, so every synchronous subscriber sees a resume that has already finished, and the "full bar of lives while still awaiting Continue" pair — true for no frame a player can observe — is never handed to anyone. This is the third time this project has been bitten by `EventBus.Publish` being synchronous, so the rule is written down here: **a state change and the event announcing it are not independent; the state is finished first and the publish goes last.** `LoseLife`'s ordering is deliberately NOT mirrored — it sets the flag after the drop, but no subscriber reads it on that edge, and rearranging a working path for symmetry is churn. **The button now stays active for the whole day and only `interactable` moves**, with `Start` switching the object on once so a scene saved while the old code had hidden it still opens correctly; nothing touches its active state after that. The disabled LOOK is the Button's own Disabled transition, authored in the scene — no colour is written in C#, and the root invariant holds. **`Open()`'s guard is unchanged**: a day that is over, won or lost, still refuses, for D-094's original reason (the popup on screen already offers those exits under rules that fit it, and this menu's Retry is the lost-day path). **The completed day greys the button too**, not just the failed one — they share the one code path, and a control that vanishes on one ending and dims on the other would be worse than either. **Two EditMode tests pin the order from the only place it can be observed**, a `LivesChanged` subscriber asserting the flag is already clear inside the handler; the existing end-state assertions stayed green throughout the bug, which is exactly why the new ones assert something else — what broke was never the final values, it was the state the event carried on the way there.

D-104: **Withdrawn.** A camera shake on the wrong-order fail animation was built on 2026-08-27 and taken straight back out at the user's request ("beğenmedim, geri al"). `CameraShakeView.cs` was deleted, every touched file reverted, and the three `BoardAnimationConfig` fields removed. The number is retired rather than reused, so a comment anywhere referring to D-104 is referring to something that never shipped. The reasoning is preserved in the preflight archive only.

D-105: **An empty powerup is a way into the shop, and the day stops while it is open.** The user placed an inactive "Add" object inside each of the three powerup buttons in `SampleScene` and asked for it to light up when that powerup hits zero, for a tap on a zero powerup to open the shop "like on the main screen", and for the game behind it to stop. All three parts reverse a rule this project had written down twice — **"buying happens on the main screen only; the day scene only spends"**, and **"pressing a powerup with no charges does nothing at all, no popup"** (GDD 5.2, root `CLAUDE.md`). Both are now void, and the reversal answers the old rule's actual argument rather than ignoring it: the objection was that *opening a store mid-service suspends the very time pressure a powerup exists to relieve* — and the day is **held still** behind this shop, so the pressure is paused rather than suspended, which is a different thing from a store that lets the clock run. **No second shop was written.** `PowerupShopView` is the same component on both screens; it gained a public `Open()` that reports whether the panel actually came up, an idempotent `Close()`, and a `Closed` event, and its `openButton` left the required list because in the day scene there IS no opener — the empty powerup is it. `panel` and `closeButton` stay required, because a shop with no way out is a trap when it is also the thing holding the day. **The view still knows nothing about pausing**, which is the property that lets one component serve a screen with no day behind it: the caller holds the pause. **`PowerupBarView` is that caller**, and the branch lives in `OnPressed`, read from the LIVE charge count rather than from what `Render` last drew — the count moves under an open shop, and a stale read would spend a charge the player meant to buy. Order is load-bearing: `shop.Open()` must SUCCEED **before** the pause is taken, or a shop that could not open leaves a day frozen with nothing on screen able to lift it. It is gated on `GameManager.CanUsePowerups` — now public — so buying under the Game Over popup, over the day-complete receipt or mid-tutorial is refused for the same three reasons spending there is. **The `Add` badge is switched on the COUNT alone, deliberately not on "a shop is reachable"**: it says *this powerup is empty*, which is true either way, and a badge that also encoded whether a reference had been dragged would make a wiring mistake look like a full stock. Everything degrades to the pre-D-105 behaviour with no shop wired: dim, unpressable, no popup — and `ShopIsReachable` additionally requires a `PowerupManager`, because `Start` attaches no click listeners without one and a live button that does nothing is worse than a dim one. **`GameManager` became the single writer of `GameState.IsPaused`** (`HoldPause`/`ReleasePause` over a `HashSet` of holders), and `SettingsPopupView` was migrated onto it. That is the root single-writer invariant, and the bug it prevents is concrete: two panels can now freeze a running day, and two views each assigning one bool means whichever closes first starts the day running underneath the other. **A set, not a counter** — a counter's failure is silent, since one panel releasing twice lifts someone else's hold; adding or removing a holder that is already in or out is a no-op, which is what makes releasing from both the close path and `OnDestroy` correct rather than merely tolerated. **The day-scene shop gets a full-screen raycast-catching backdrop and the menu's does not**, and that asymmetry is the second half of "the game stops": `IsPaused` stops the ticket CLOCK, not a finger. The board sits in the half of the screen the bottom sheet does not cover, and `BoardItemDragHandler` routes through the `EventSystem` — so without something swallowing those touches a player could keep dragging items into trays, and batch-deliver an order in a day that is meant to be held still. The backdrop deliberately does **not** close on tap: its whole job is to swallow, and a mis-aimed tap dismissing a shop mid-purchase is worse than reaching for the close button already sitting in the sheet's corner. `PowerupShopSetup` builds both variants from one file rather than growing a second near-identical step to drift out of sync, and fills the bar's `Shop` field itself.

D-106: **The powerup shop's table gets a legend.** The user looked at the built panel and asked for the column headings the table never had: `name` over the powerup names, `count` over the owned numbers, `price` over the Gem prices. **Built as a ROW, not as three placed labels**, through the same `HorizontalLayoutGroup` and the same three column widths the powerup rows use — that is the only construction that keeps a heading over its column, because the panel's width is a fraction of the screen and therefore unknown at build time, so anything hand-placed drifts the first time the shop opens on a different aspect ratio. **The unlabelled `BuySpacer` is load-bearing rather than tidiness:** without a fourth element of `BuyWidth`, the layout group hands the BUY column's width to the three headings to share and every one slides right of what it labels. It is deliberately blank — a heading over a column of buttons has nothing to say. **No background Image, dimmer and smaller type**, which is the whole visual difference between a legend and data: a heading carrying the rows' faint tint reads as a fourth, unbuyable powerup, exactly the mistake a legend must not make. The three strings are constants beside the existing row labels under the same rule those were written under — fixed identities of the columns, written once into scene objects the author is free to retype, nothing reads them back, and nothing about them varies per save or per Day. **`PowerupShopSetup` will now ADD this to a shop it did not build**, which is the first exception to its "reports, never repairs" posture and a narrow one: `TryAddHeaderRow` inserts a first child and touches nothing else, so hand styling survives — whereas rebuilding the panel to obtain three labels would be the destructive way round. **Whether a header is already present is decided STRUCTURALLY, never by name** (D-028; `MetaGroundsSetup` is the cautionary tale, where a renamed object made a name search find nothing and it would have built a second hierarchy while logging that it had not): the three wired BUY buttons name the three real rows, so a rows container whose first child is not one of them already has something there and is left alone. Rename the header, restyle it, or replace it with your own and re-running the step does nothing; delete it and it comes back.

D-107: **The shop closes on a tap outside it.** The user asked for it plainly while checking the wiring: "bu shop dışında bi yere tıklarsak pencere kapansın". It **reverses D-105**, which built the day-scene backdrop as deliberately non-dismissing on the reasoning that a mis-aimed tap would close a shop the player is mid-purchase in, with a close button already sitting in the sheet's corner. That cost is real but small next to what it bought: a full-screen block whose only exit is a corner X is the shape players read as being trapped, and every other modal on a phone closes this way. **It needs no inside/outside hit-test, and that is why it is a wired `Button` rather than a pointer handler doing geometry.** The sheet is a CHILD of the backdrop and carries its own raycast-target Image, so UGUI hands a tap on the sheet to the sheet and only a tap outside it ever reaches the backdrop — the hierarchy does the filtering, and there is no rect comparison to drift out of step with a restyle. The field (`backdropButton`) is OPTIONAL, like `openButton`: the main screen's shop is a bottom sheet with nothing behind it to tap, and a shop without one behaves exactly as it did. **Transition is None**, which matters more here than on an ordinary button: the default ColorTint would flash the whole dim layer lighter on every press, so a tap near the sheet's edge would look like the screen blinking. Closing this way goes through the same `Close()` as the X, so it raises `Closed` once and the day-scene caller releases its pause — the pause needs no new path. `PowerupShopSetup.BuildBackdrop` now returns the Button and fills the field, so a freshly built day-scene shop has this without any dragging.

D-108: **The meta shop gets the powerup shop's modal treatment.** The user asked for it in one sentence — "başlık ekleme header ekleme ve arkada panel ekleyip ona da dokununca çıkma ve sağ üst köşeye x şeklinde kapatma tuşu ekleme gibi şeyleri meta shopuna da yapalım orada sadece item listesi var" — i.e. the four things D-105 to D-107 gave the powerup shop: a title, a column-heading row, a full-screen backdrop that dismisses on an outside tap, and an X in the corner. Before this the meta shop was a bottom sheet with a list and nothing else: the only way out was the market button, back in the screen's opposite corner. **The code half is two optional fields and one handler**, and the constraint that shaped them is the class's oldest rule: `SetOpen` is the single owner of the panel's visibility, because the first shop's ghost leaked precisely when that ownership was split (D-024/D-027). So the X and the backdrop are CALLERS, never a second door — a button wired straight to `SetActive` would skip `ClosePreview` and `CancelPendingReopen` and reintroduce the same class of bug. They close unconditionally rather than toggling, unlike the market button: both are only reachable while the shop is up. **`panel` now points at the backdrop, with the sheet as its child**, which is what makes the block and the list appear and vanish as one modal — and it also means the backdrop correctly disappears during a preview, since the ghost stands on the map and must be visible. The outside-tap needs no inside/outside test: the sheet carries its own raycast-target Image, so UGUI hands it the taps that land on it and only the rest reach the backdrop. **This is NOT a re-do of D-038.** That removed a backdrop from the CONFIRM POPUP at the user's request, so CANCEL is the only way out of a preview; the preview is untouched here, and the two surfaces are different questions. **The editor half is the first setup step in this project that RESTRUCTURES an existing hierarchy** rather than only appending to one — it reparents the sheet under a new backdrop. That is justified by the alternative: rebuilding the panel to obtain a backdrop and three labels would throw away every bit of hand styling, which is the more destructive of the two mistakes available. It is guarded, and every branch goes through `Undo`, so one Ctrl+Z removes the whole upgrade. Nothing moves on screen, because the shop root is already full-screen and the backdrop inherits an identical rect. **Idempotency is structural, never by name** (D-028): the sheet is a direct child of the root until it is wrapped and a grandchild afterwards. The consequence is written down rather than hidden — delete the Title or the Header later and re-running will not bring them back, since the wrap is what the step asks about; only the X has a serialized field of its own to test. `BuildFresh` calls the same upgrade method instead of carrying a second copy of the four builders, so a fresh shop and an upgraded one cannot drift into looking different. **The headings copy their horizontal placement off the row template's own `Name`/`Price` rects**, read through `SerializedObject` because those fields are private — and that is not a shortcut but the only correct construction: a meta row is hand-anchored (icon left, name stretched, price and BUY right) rather than laid out by a group, so nothing else guarantees a heading stays over its column when the list is restyled. The `Header` sits in the SHEET and never in the scrolling `Content`, because a legend that scrolls away with the list is worse than none. `closeButton` and `backdropButton` are deliberately kept OUT of `RequiredFields`: that list drives `TryReplaceIncomplete`, which offers to DESTROY the shop and rebuild it, and an optional addition must never be able to trigger that dialog.

D-109: **The drop that succeeds gets its own haptic — D-071's silence there was a mistake, not a design.** `OnEndDrag`'s successful-placement branch was the last half-gesture in the drag: `ItemPickup` answered the grab and nothing answered the release. D-071 left it silent on the reasoning that "a relocation is the drop WORKING, not failing", which is a correct observation with the wrong conclusion — dropping into a tray is also the drop working, and that has buzzed since D-070. The user caught it by feel, from the player's side rather than the code's. `ItemPlacedOnBoard` now fires there. **One moment for both origins the branch covers** — board-to-board and tray-back-to-board — because `ItemPickup` does not care where the item came from either; splitting would make the same gesture feel finished or unfinished for a reason the player cannot see. If board shuffling turns out noisy on a device there are two cheap exits, both recorded at the line: untick `enabled` in the asset, or narrow to `!wasOnBoard`, which the branch already computes one line above. **`RigidImpact` was chosen because it was the last impact preset with no default row**, and that was the point rather than a coincidence: the drag now has four outcomes and each needs its own answer — `LightImpact` picking up, `RigidImpact` onto the board, `MediumImpact` into the tray (the real move, so the fuller one), `SoftImpact` refused. Sharing `DropRejected`'s `SoftImpact` would have made "it landed" and "it did not" identical in the hand, which is the one distinction this branch exists to draw. **Priority 16 is for readability, not arbitration**: this branch publishes `CellChanged`, nothing haptic subscribes to it, and delivery/life-loss only ever arrive through the tray path — so it collides with nothing and simply sits inside its own gesture family between `ItemPickup` (10) and `DropRejected` (18). **Appended to the enum, for the fourth time and the same reason** (D-071, D-072): the asset holds eighteen authored rows and each stores a value, so an insertion would re-point every later row; `OnValidate` grows it to nineteen on the next load without touching what is there. **No test and no editor task** — this adds a moment rather than a rule, `TheDefaultTable_CoversEveryMoment` iterates the enum so a missing seed turns red on its own, and no serialized field was added, so there is nothing to drag. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data`, `Assembly-CSharp` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode` build clean, 0 errors; both touched files were confirmed present in Unity's Compile lists first. EditMode suite still owed — Unity holds the lock. **Still true after four passes**: none of the nineteen moments has ever been felt on real hardware — Nice Vibrations does not vibrate in the editor — affects: HapticConfig.cs, BoardItemDragHandler.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-25

D-110: **Auto-Collect decides the whole press before it moves anything, and the board it decides on is the one the player pressed.** The user reported two faults in one sentence — it fetches ingredients that were not on the board, and it sometimes leaves an order unfinished that could have been finished — and then dictated the algorithm: know what is on the board first; walk the tickets in order and finish the ones the items in hand can finish; then put what is left into the tickets that were not finished; and **do not look at the tickets that arrived after that first round**. Both faults came from one shape. `TryFindAutoCollectItem` answered "what should this slot take NEXT" and `AutoCollectRunner` re-asked it after every single move, against the LIVE board. **Fault one is the backfill.** `BoardGrid.RemoveItem` refills the cell it empties from the pending-spawn queue, and a delivery cascades synchronously into the next ticket's required items spawning — so the moment slot 0 delivered, slots 1 and 2 helped themselves to what that delivery had just produced. The existing `ReferenceEquals` guard stopped only the DELIVERING slot from continuing; it never stopped the other two from eating the new arrivals, and from the player's chair that is ingredients appearing from nowhere. **Fault two is greed.** Walking slot 0, 1, 2 and grabbing whatever each one happened to want is blind to the others: one burger on the board, slot 0 wanting burger + a cola that exists nowhere, slot 1 wanting only that burger — slot 0 took it, stranded it in a tray that could never resolve, and the completable ticket got nothing. **The fix is a PLAN, not a better search.** `PlanAutoCollect` reads the board ONCE into a budget of `key -> cells` and pays every ticket out of that fixed pot: pass 1, slots in order, completes any ticket the budget can pay IN FULL; pass 2, slots in order, partially fills only the ones pass 1 could not. Asking "can this be FINISHED" before spending anything is the entire second fix, and closing the list before the first move is the entire first one — an item that spawns during the run cannot enter a list that was closed before it existed, and neither can a ticket. **Coordinates survive the mutation and are still not trusted:** removing an item only ever backfills the cell it emptied, never moves another, so a planned cell stays valid — but each move re-checks the exact `BoardItem` INSTANCE at its cell before taking it, because taking whatever landed there instead is precisely the bug being removed. That is why a move carries its item and not just an (x, y). **Greedy in slot order rather than an optimal assignment**, which is the rule the user asked for and can never complete fewer tickets than the old code did. **A tray's junk goes home instead of being worked around.** The first draft SKIPPED a slot whose tray held something its ticket never asked for, since that tray can never resolve; the user rejected that and asked for the item to be thrown back on the board and counted in — "eğer istenmeyen malzeme varsa onu boarda atıp dahil etse". So `UnwantedTrayItems` names them (a mis-drop, or a surplus copy beyond the required count) and `WorldTrayView.ReturnItemsToBoard` sends them back BEFORE the snapshot is taken, which both un-jams the tray and returns the item to the pot for whichever ticket does want it. It needed no new tray API: `TrayManager.RemoveItem` already exists for a player pulling an item out by hand, and the visual half is the move `BoardItemDragHandler.OnEndDrag` already performs for a tray pickup dropped somewhere invalid — remove, `RequestSpawn` under a fly-in override from this tray, destroy the object. It lives on `WorldTrayView` because a tray item's GameObject is a child of one of its three slots and nothing else can take it out without leaving the model and the view disagreeing. **`lastKnownCount` is resynced in that same call**, because `Update`'s poll reads a drop to exactly zero as "cleared externally" and would run `ClearAllSlotVisuals` over objects that are already gone. **THE POWERUP STILL CANNOT COST A LIFE, and that is now structural rather than incidental.** The tray's batch check fires the instant the tray is FULL, so the plan either fills a slot exactly — a correct order, hence a delivery — or deliberately leaves one space free (`MaxMovesFor` is the tray's free space, and pass 2 stops one short of it). The old code had this property by accident, from only ever offering items the ticket was short of; a tray holding junk the runner could not send home (the player is holding it mid-drag) would have broken it, and now cannot. **A press that only sent items home still costs a charge**, asked and answered explicitly: the board visibly changes and a tray guaranteed to cost a life is un-jammed, so `Run` returns true for it. **`MaxMovesPerSlot` is deleted with the loop** — a closed plan cannot spin, and a hang guard shaped like a design number is worse than none. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.PowerupSystem`, `Assembly-CSharp` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode` build clean, 0 errors. The nine `TryFindAutoCollectItem` tests were REPLACED, not deleted — thirteen `PlanAutoCollect` cases and six `UnwantedTrayItems` cases, including one that pins the stranded-burger report and one that pins the never-fills-a-doomed-tray property. EditMode suite still owed — Unity holds the project lock, so it must be run from the Test Runner window — affects: PowerupEffects.cs, AutoCollectRunner.cs, WorldTrayView.cs, PowerupEffectsTests.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-27

D-111: **Items travel at half speed between the board and the trays, and the Day Complete popup steps back to make room.** The user's report was about legibility rather than any single animation — "çok hızlı dağılıyor... çok karışık duruyor" — and named the two travels: board → tray and tray → board. **No code was written, and that is the point of the entry.** Both travels were already single authored numbers, so this is a values-only edit to `BoardAnimationConfig.asset`: `traySettleDuration` 0.2 → 0.4 (`BoardItemDragHandler.SeatInSlot`'s settle tween — every accepted drop flies on it, Auto-Collect's included, since D-110's runner goes through the ordinary drop path) and `popInDuration` 0.2 → 0.4 (`BoardView.RefreshCell`'s `DOJump` plus its matching `DOScale`). **`PopInDuration` is deliberately left as ONE knob for every board arrival** — a returned item, a wrong-order scatter, a timeout scatter, a fresh spawn from Starting Point. Splitting it so the powerup's own returns could be slowed alone would mean a new field and a second number to keep in step, and the complaint was general. **The third number is arithmetic, not taste.** `dayCompletePopupDelay` goes 1 → 1.5 because its own tooltip states the rule — keep it comfortably above `TraySettleDuration + DeliveryGrowDuration + DeliveryFadeDuration + DeliveryReentryDuration` — and that sum was **exactly 1.0 against a delay of exactly 1.0**, flush, with no comfort left even before this. Doubling the settle takes it to 1.2, so an unchanged delay would have started covering the very delivery D-101 added the wait to reveal: the number that exists to reveal an animation would have been quietly re-tuned into hiding it by a change made somewhere else. That is the trap this entry is really recording — a duration is never local when another duration is defined as being longer than it. **`gameOverPopupDelay` stays at 1.0**, checked rather than assumed: its scatter constraint is `ScatterShakeDuration` + the board fly-in = 0.5 + 0.4 = 0.9, still under. The margin is now 0.1s, so it is the next number to move if these are pushed further. Its OTHER documented constraint — above `LifeLostHeartDuration`, which is 2.0 — has been unmet since D-101 and is untouched here; the heart is a long fade and the popup arriving over its tail is evidently the authored intent, but it is written down rather than left to be rediscovered. **Pacing was NOT re-proposed**: a staggered Auto-Collect (one item every authored interval, on a coroutine) was built on 2026-08-26 and reverted at the user's request the same day, so each item now flies slower while a press still starts them all together. **No field was added**, so D-075's stale-asset trap does not apply and nothing needs re-serializing; no scene, prefab or `.cs` was touched, and no codemap line went stale — affects: BoardAnimationConfig.asset — 2026-08-27

D-112: **The slowdown belongs to Auto-Collect, not to the game. D-111's three values are reverted and its intent is kept.** The user's correction was immediate and exact — "ben sadece auto collect yavaş olsun istiyordum bu durumu sadece auto collect için böyle bırak diğerlerini eski haline al". D-111 had read "her şey çok hızlı dağılıyor" as a statement about the game's feel; it was a statement about **one press**. So `traySettleDuration` 0.4 → 0.2, `popInDuration` 0.4 → 0.2 and `dayCompletePopupDelay` 1.5 → 1 all go back exactly, and the slower flight survives only where it was wanted. **The split had to be made at the CALL, not at the number**, and that is the whole shape of this entry: each duration has exactly one consumer (`BoardItemDragHandler.SeatInSlot` and `BoardView.RefreshCell`) and both are shared with ordinary play *by design* — D-110 deliberately routes the powerup through the ordinary drop path so no drop rule can drift between a finger and a powerup. Splitting the DURATIONS would have meant giving the powerup its own delivery path, which is exactly the thing that design forbids. **`autoCollectTravelMultiplier` is a multiplier rather than a second pair of durations**, and that choice is about what is being authored: the thing worth writing down is the RELATIONSHIP — "slower than a finger" — and two absolute numbers stop expressing it the first moment someone retunes the base feel, silently, with nothing red. One knob, and `1` restores the pre-D-111 behaviour exactly. **It reaches the two paths through mechanisms that already existed for this exact purpose.** On the board side, `BeginFlyInOverride` already carried an origin and a delay to say "this particular appearance is different"; it now carries a `durationScale` beside them, and `RefreshCell` applies it to the fly-in only inside the bracket — a spawn, a wrong-order scatter and a timeout keep their authored speed, because `BoardView` cannot tell those apart and the bracket is the thing that knows. On the tray side, `PlaceInSlot`/`PlaceInSlotAndDeliver`/`SeatInSlot` take a `travelMultiplier` defaulted to 1, so every existing caller is untouched and the number exists only on the path that asks for it. **`WorldTrayView.TryAcceptAutoCollectDrop` reads the multiplier off its OWN serialized config**, which is the detail that kept this task free of editor work: had `AutoCollectRunner` needed the number, it would have needed a `BoardAnimationConfig` reference, and this project's rule is that a runtime never searches for one — meaning a new serialized field and a manual drag in the scene. The tray already has the config; the runner asks the tray. **One consequence is accepted rather than papered over**: with the popup delay back at 1.0 and an Auto-Collect settle of 0.4, a day whose LAST delivery came from the powerup runs 0.4 + 0.2 + 0.35 = 0.95s of settle, grow and fade before the popup — the visible part still finishes first, and only the next tray's 0.25s re-entrance (an empty tray growing back on a finished day) falls underneath it. Raising `dayCompletePopupDelay` again would re-introduce precisely the global slowdown this entry exists to remove. **Adding the field made the `.asset` stale in the way D-075 documents**; the key was written into the YAML in the same task, which is what the re-serialize menu item would have produced, so no editor step is owed. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Data`, `Assembly-CSharp` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode` build clean, 0 errors (the four `CS0649` warnings in Data are pre-existing, in `TicketGenerationConfig`).
