No test was added — the multiplier changes a tween's length and nothing an EditMode test can observe; the number's effect is a feel judgement made in Play mode — affects: BoardAnimationConfig.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.asset, BoardView.cs, BoardItemDragHandler.cs, WorldTrayView.cs, AutoCollectRunner.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-27

D-113: **An item taken without a finger still has to do the pickup's bookkeeping — the delivering item was invisible because `homeScale` was never written.** The user reported it precisely: "tepsiye mesela yerleşince teslim edilen biletin hotdogu gözükmüyor" — the order delivers, the model is right, and the one item that FINISHED the ticket is not drawn. **The cause is one unwritten field.** `homeScale` is assigned in exactly one place, `ApplyPickupVisuals`, reached only from `OnPointerDown`; Auto-Collect raises no pointer event at all, so on that path the field holds the C# default, `Vector3.zero`. `PlaceInSlotAndDeliver` then applies it — `transform.localScale = homeScale` — to undo a pickup grow that never happened, and the item goes to scale zero for the whole delivery animation before being destroyed. **The asymmetry is the whole diagnosis**: `PlaceInSlot`, the ordinary non-completing drop, never touches the scale, so every other auto-collected item in the tray looked correct and only the completing one vanished. That is what the user's sentence says, and it is what pointed at the right line. **The file had already met this failure from a different cause** — the comment above `ApplyPickupVisuals` describes an item that "dragged invisibly … stayed invisible after landing in a tray slot even though TrayManager had correctly counted it", when a delayed fly-in let a ZERO be snapshotted. Same symptom; the powerup reaches it by a path where the snapshot never ran. **The fix gives the finger-less caller the finger's first three lines and nothing else.** `SettleForAutoCollect()` completes any live tween and then snapshots `homePosition`/`homeScale`; the grow, the hover offset and the sorting boost stay behind, because those belong to a hand. `AutoCollectRunner` calls it once per move, before handing the item over. **`DOKill(true)` — COMPLETE, not kill — is the second half and closes a quieter fault**: the powerup can take an item whose board fly-in is still running, and then `BoardView`'s `DOJump` and the tray's settle tween write the same transform at once. Completed, the tween leaves the item at its own cell at full scale, which is both a trustworthy snapshot and exactly where a finger would have grabbed it; killed, it would be seated from wherever it was mid-flight. This is the same trade `ApplyPickupVisuals` already documents, and D-112's doubled flights had doubled the window. **`homeScale`'s initialiser is now `Vector3.one`**, not as a safety net but because zero states something false: every board container rests at scale one by construction, `RefreshCell` tweening both its fly-in and its plain pop-in to exactly that. **A wrong first diagnosis is recorded rather than quietly dropped**, because the thing it found is real: `ResolvePlacementSlot` decides which tray slot an item is seated in by reading `Transform.childCount`, and `Object.Destroy` is deferred to the end of the frame — so a destroyed-but-still-parented child keeps counting, and in the one frame where `ReturnItemsToBoard` destroys junk and the collect phase seats new items, the overflow search can find every slot "occupied" and fall through to its last-resort `return preferred`, stacking two real items at one position. Its own comment states the invariant that breaks. Two pre-existing windows of the same family exist: the delivery callback destroys all three slots' children and clears `deliveryInProgress` in the same callback, and `ClearSlot` leaves phantoms parented for `SlotClearDuration`. **Left unfixed on purpose** — it is not what the user is seeing, and the honest fix is to stop asking `childCount` and ask `TrayManager.GetContents` instead, which is a task of its own. **Verification**: `Assembly-CSharp` builds clean, 0 errors. No test — both files live in `Assembly-CSharp`, which no asmdef may reference, and the fault is a rendered scale — affects: BoardItemDragHandler.cs, AutoCollectRunner.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-27


D-114: **The Day Editor says whether a Day is saved, and lets you take an edit back.** The user could not tell whether the Day they had been editing was written to disk ("kaydedip kaydetmediğimi göremiyorum"), asked for the state to be shown, for a prompt when leaving a Day with unsaved edits, and for a button that undoes them. **Dirty is a comparison, not a flag.** `DayEditorModel.savedSnapshot` holds the Day serialized through `ToDayJson` at load and after every save, and `HasUnsavedChanges` asks whether the model still serializes to that. The obvious alternative — `isDirty = true` from every writer — cannot work in this inspector: the food tile grid, the Start Board cell picker, the ticket strip's drag-reorder and the All/None buttons are hand-drawn IMGUI that no `[OnValueChanged]` ever sees, so one writer would always be forgotten, and a dirty flag that misses a change is worse than no flag. The comparison has exactly one thing to stay in step with, `ToDayJson`, which was already the single serializer. Cost is not a question here: the same draw pass already calls `ToDayDefinition()` several times through `Validate()` for the three InfoBoxes, and the check is throttled to ~10/s for the one Day on screen. **Checked rather than assumed: `DrawFoodSelection` calls `SyncMainDishWeights` during the draw, so a Day whose file disagrees with the derived Main-dish list would mutate itself on first draw and show as dirty untouched.** All 43 `day_*.json` were compared against the catalog's Main order (`burger`, `hotdog`) filtered by each Day's `allowedFoodItemIds`: **0 out of sync**, so the baseline is taken at load and D-052's single caller of `SyncMainDishWeights` stays single. **Leaving a Day is detected by watching the selection, not by Odin's event**: `SelectionChanged` fires from inside `OdinMenuTreeSelection`'s own bookkeeping and "Keep Editing" has to re-select the Day being left — mutating the list Odin is iterating — so `OnImGUI` compares `SelectedValue` against the Day drawn last pass, and the dialog runs from `EditorApplication.delayCall` because a modal dialog opened mid-`OnGUI` leaves that pass's layout half-built. A Day that has never been written gets **Save / Later**, not Save / Discard: discarding a fileless Day means deleting it, which is Delete's job and Delete asks first. **`OnDeleteRequested` and `OnDestroy` gained the same guard because they are pre-existing silent-data-loss paths** — `ForceMenuTreeRebuild()` re-runs `BuildMenuTree`, which re-reads every Day from disk and threw away other Days' unsaved edits without a word. Revert restores in place (`RestoreFrom`) rather than handing back a fresh model, because the menu tree holds that exact reference, and it drops keyboard focus first so a focused number field cannot write its editing buffer back over the restored value. Indicators: a coloured bar at the top of the Day, `*` beside the Day in the list, `Day Editor*` in the window title. **Nothing was added to the Day JSON schema** — the snapshot is in-memory only. **Verification**: `ExpoTheExplorer.Editor` full `-t:Rebuild`, 0 errors; the 43-file sync check above. Not exercised in the Unity Editor by me — affects: DayEditorModel.cs, DayEditorWindow.cs, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-27

D-115: **Each powerup is introduced on its own Day, and the lesson ends with the player actually pressing it.** The user replaced the single panel that explained all three powerups at once ("her biri ayrı günlerde tanıtılacak", 2026-08-27): three things to remember at a moment when the player has used none of them is not teaching. Each powerup now gets a **panel naming only itself**, followed by a **forced press** — and the clock behaviour is the user's own description: stopped while the text is up, running again the moment "Got it" is pressed, stopped again when the press is asked for, running again once it lands. **The schedule lives on `PowerupConfig`, one block per powerup** (`introDayIndex`, `useTrigger`, `triggerPatienceRatio`, `useInstruction`, `introCharges`, `minimumChargesForForcedUse`) — the user chose a ScriptableObject over the Day Editor explicitly ("editöre eklemeyelim, SO ile tutalım"). It went onto the EXISTING asset rather than a new one because that asset already holds the name and description this panel prints and is **already dragged onto `GameManager`**: a second asset meant a hand-authored `.asset` with an invented GUID and a second serialized field for the user to drag. **This ENDS a dual authority rather than creating one.** `PowerupIntro` is gone from `DaySystem.TutorialStepKind` (the enum is gone entirely, since one value remained) and from `day_00.json`, so a Day file can author only forced MOVES and the asset owns only powerup steps; `GameManager.BuildTutorialSteps` concatenates them, moves first. The JSON `kind` field is deliberately KEPT with one legal value, because deleting it would let `JsonUtility` silently ignore a leftover `"PowerupIntro"` and turn a panel into a forced move on cell (0,0) into tray 0. **The structural change is a third director state.** `IsActive` used to mean both "a step is running" and "freeze everything" (D-097 hung the clock on it), and Time Reset cannot be taught that way: its press waits for a ticket to fall to a third of its limit, and a step that stops the clock while waiting for the clock is a deadlock. So a step can now be CURRENT but not ARMED — gates open, day running — and `NotifyTicketPatienceRatio(float)` arms it. `IsArmed` is the freeze/gate answer everywhere `IsActive` used to be, which changed nothing for the kinds that arm immediately. The trigger arrives as a plain float, so the Tutorial assembly still references nothing and still knows nothing about tickets; `GameManager` computes the lowest `RemainingSeconds / TimeLimitSeconds` over the three slots once per tick, **after** the tick, so the number matches the bar the player sees. **Charges are a FLOOR, not a grant** (`PowerupManager.EnsureAtLeast`, the user's rule: 2 on introduction, 1 more for Time Reset if it was spent before the moment). `ArmTutorial` re-arms on every day-start path including both retries, so an adding grant would have made replaying an introduction Day a charge farm; a floor is idempotent and leaves a well-stocked player alone. **The forced-press step completes on the PRESS, not on the effect** — GDD 5.2 lets an effect with nothing to do refuse and cost no charge, and a step waiting for success would leave the player pressing a button that will not advance. **The new spotlight deliberately dims nothing**, which is the opposite of the move steps and is the point: Time Reset is taught at the moment a ticket is running out, and the thing the player must see is that ticket's timer bar draining. It is four pulsing bars parented just outside the target button (Overlay would draw *over* it — D-086) plus the authored sentence, and it needs no new serialized reference. **Every failure path skips the lesson rather than blocking the day**, including the one that would genuinely softlock: a forced press against a button nobody dragged in. **Starting schedule** (one Inspector field each): Auto-Collect Day 0, Noise Clear Day 3, Time Reset Day 6. **CORRECTION, same day: the first cut of this crashed the Editor.** `PowerupSettings.Tutorial` shipped as `tutorial ??= new PowerupTutorialSettings()`, and expanding the Tutorial foldout in the Inspector killed Unity every time (four crash reports, 22:23-22:43). The reports are an `NSException` out of `-[NSView addCursorRect:cursor:]` under `-[GUIRenderView resetCursorRects]` -- AppKit rejecting an invalid rect, which is a hard process kill rather than a C# error, which is why nothing appeared in the console. The mechanism: assigning a serialized field from inside a property getter puts a mutation in the middle of an IMGUI pass, and IMGUI runs Layout and Repaint as two passes over the same event -- a control set that changes between them produces a garbage rect, and the first text field to hand one to AppKit takes the process down. **The lazy allocation was worthless to begin with**: Unity never deserializes a `[SerializeField]` of a `[Serializable]` class as null, so the branch it guarded is unreachable. The getter is now pure, and `useInstruction` dropped its `[TextArea]` for a plain string because a text field is what registers the cursor rect that actually died. **That fix did not work, and neither did the two after it.** Dropping `[TextArea]`, then dropping `[Range]` (the only one in the project), each left the crash exactly where it was -- same stack, every time. The cause is not any single field: Odin's `GeneralDrawerConfig` has **`enableUIToolkitSupport: 1`**, so IMGUI is drawn inside `IMGUIContainer`s, and a UIToolkit element's resolved geometry is **NaN until its first layout pass** -- which is exactly the frame a foldout expands in. Any cursor rect registered that frame reaches AppKit as NaN. **The resolution is that the six schedule fields are now FLAT on `PowerupSettings`** (`tutorialIntroDayIndex`, `tutorialUseTrigger`, ...), the nested `PowerupTutorialSettings` class is deleted, and `PowerupConfig.cs` is back to exactly one `[Serializable]` type -- the depth that already worked before this task. A flat field has no foldout to expand, so the frame that produced the bad rect does not exist. The user asked for this shape independently of the crash ("orayı açma kapamalı yapma"), so it is not a workaround imposed on the design. **Two rules this leaves behind: never write to a serialized field from a property getter on a config, and do not nest a `[Serializable]` class inside another one on an asset this Editor draws.** The 0..1 range moved from `[Range]` to `Mathf.Clamp01` in the getter, which is the better place regardless -- an attribute only constrains the Inspector, and a value hand-edited into the YAML walks straight past it. **First play-test found the lesson blocking its own powerup.** Pressing Auto-Collect on Day 0 advanced the step and collected nothing: `AutoCollectRunner` moves items the way a finger does, through `WorldTrayView.TryAcceptAutoCollectDrop`, and that path asks `IsTrayDropAllowed`, which an armed `PowerupUse` step answers false for every tray -- so the effect found nothing it could do and cost no charge, which is correct behaviour for an effect with nothing to do and completely wrong here. `TryAcceptDrop` gained a `respectTutorialGate` flag: the finger overload passes true, the Auto-Collect overload passes false. The gate constrains the PLAYER, and a powerup's own machinery is not the player. Safe by construction rather than by care -- `CanUsePowerup` already refuses every powerup during a forced move and behind a panel, so the bypass is reachable only while a `PowerupUse` step names that powerup. Noise Clear and Time Reset touch no gate of that kind. **AND THAT FIX WAS ONLY THE LOWER HALF: a second play-test showed the press still ran nothing, because the block above it was a single letter.** All three effects were registered as `() => CanUsePowerups() && <effect>`, and `CanUsePowerups()` is the SHOP's gate -- it answers false for every powerup while any step is armed. So the lambda short-circuited before the effect was reached, returned false, correctly spent no charge, and the step advanced on the press having done nothing. The button was already passing the per-powerup `CanUsePowerup(type)`; the effect behind it re-asked the global one. The three registrations now take `CanUsePowerup(<their own type>)`, which asks the same two day-liveness questions and then asks the tutorial about THAT powerup -- identical outside a lesson, permissive only for the powerup being taught inside one. `CanUsePowerups()` is left with exactly one caller, `PowerupBarView.OpenShop`, which is the question it was written for. The two blocks sat one behind the other, which is why the first fix looked like a failure rather than a partial success. The tempting one-line alternative -- advance the step before running the effect -- was rejected because a second powerup scheduled on the same Day (one Inspector field away, now that the schedule is config) would put a panel in front of the effect and reproduce the bug. **The config surface was cut from six schedule fields to three (2026-08-28), on the user's reading of the Inspector, and one of the three cuts was a real dual authority rather than clutter.** `triggerPatienceRatio` held 1/3 -- which is `EconomyConfig.CriticalRatio`, the single authority for where a ticket's timer bar turns red and its tip tier drops (CLAUDE.md, Tip Tiers). Keeping a second copy meant a lesson that could fire at a moment the bar did not mark, teaching against what the player can see; `GameManager` reads the economy's number when it builds the deferred step, and the field is gone. It was also drawn on all three powerups while meaning anything on only one, which is what the user noticed first. `useInstruction` was the `Description` reworded -- one sentence authored twice -- so the spotlight prints the Description and the field is gone. The two charge floors became one `tutorialCharges`: how generous the lesson is stays a dial, but "at least one charge or the press is impossible" is a rule of the mechanic and is now a constant in `GameManager`. `chargesPerDayCompleted` was raised as unnecessary and deliberately KEPT: it is a locked GDD 5.2 earn path (day completion + Gem purchase) recorded in the root CLAUDE.md, it is already authored at 0 on all three powerups so the path is dormant rather than active, and removing it is a design change that would have to edit the GDD and CLAUDE.md too -- so it was carried back to the user, who answered "kaldir ya" -- so it IS removed: `GrantForDayCompleted`, `PowerupSettings.chargesPerDayCompleted`, the call in `OnDayCompleted`, and the two tests that covered it are gone, and the re-entrancy test that used the method as its instrument now uses `EnsureAtLeast` (the write that can genuinely land mid-effect today) while pinning the same rule. Powerups are earned two ways now: the starting stock and the Gem purchase. GDD 5.2's "earned two ways" sentence and the root `CLAUDE.md` are edited to stop promising a reward nobody ever received. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj` and on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` (with the new file temporarily added to the generated project, since Unity has not imported it yet), 0 errors; 19 new EditMode tests written, **not run** — Unity holds the project lock, so `-batchmode` was unavailable. Not exercised in the Unity Editor by me — affects: PowerupConfig.cs, PowerupConfig.asset, TutorialDirector.cs, PowerupManager.cs, GameManager.cs, PowerupBarView.cs, TutorialPowerupIntroView.cs, TutorialPowerupSpotlightView.cs, DayJson.cs, DayDefinition.cs, DayCatalogParser.cs, DayValidator.cs, TutorialDirectorTests.cs, PowerupSystemTests.cs, day_00.json, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, blueprint.md — 2026-08-27

D-116: **The powerup tutorial's two popups became authored prefabs.** The user asked to be able to edit them ("bu popupları tutorial için yaptığın prefaba çevirir misin ayrı ayrı ki ben sonra düzenleyebileyim"), and that reverses a position both view files argued for at length: *built entirely at runtime and authored nowhere -- no prefab, no scene object, no art, nothing dragged into an Inspector*. That argument was correct while nobody needed to restyle them, and worthless the moment somebody did: a panel assembled from constants in C# cannot be restyled at all, only re-programmed. The comments were rewritten rather than left contradicting the code. **The prefabs are produced by a menu step, not hand-written YAML** (`TutorialPopupSetup`, `ExpoTheExplorer > Tutorial > Build Powerup Popups`) — `procedures/scene-structure.md` names the Editor-script path the preferred one and hand-edited `.prefab` YAML the last resort, and a hierarchy of Canvas + CanvasScaler + GraphicRaycaster + Images + TMP + Button is exactly where a mistyped fileID yields a prefab that imports cleanly and is quietly missing a component. `PowerupShop` and `HUDCanvas` were born the same way. **The step REFUSES to overwrite an existing prefab**, which is the difference between a seed and an authority: from the author's first edit, a rebuild would be indistinguishable from losing an afternoon of layout. **Two prefabs, and their shapes differ for a real reason.** `TutorialPowerupIntro`'s root carries a **Screen Space - Overlay** canvas and must keep it (D-086): the day scene's InGameCanvas is Screen Space - CAMERA at sortingOrder -1, so a panel reparented under it is drawn beneath the world sprites and disappears with nothing reporting it. `TutorialPowerupSpotlight`'s root carries **no canvas at all** — its frame is reparented onto the live powerup button and inherits that button's canvas, which is what puts it *on* the button rather than over it; only its message keeps an Overlay canvas. The frame is stretched to whatever button it lands on and **keeps the offsets the prefab was authored with**, so the inset around the button is a styling decision rather than a constant in code. **Both views stopped building and started binding**, every bound field optional except the intro's dismiss button — a modal panel with no way out is a softlock behind a frozen day, so its absence is logged as an error and the step completed rather than shown. `PowerupBarView` holds the two prefab references and instantiates them; they fail differently on purpose, an unwired intro skipping its lesson (the panel was the only way to continue) while an unwired spotlight still runs it unmarked. `FindFont` was deleted with the change — it borrowed a TMP font off a count label so a code-built panel could match the game's type, and an authored prefab carries its own. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj` (with the new file temporarily added to the generated project, since Unity has not imported it) and on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj`, 0 errors; `check_blueprint.py` → `1 error(s), 6 warning(s), 4 info` — the error is the pre-existing `URP2DSceneTemplate.unity` line, and the two new INFO lines are the prefabs the blueprint now plans and the menu step has not yet created. Not exercised in the Unity Editor by me; the menu item has never been run. **Correction made in the same session:** the wiring was first left as a manual drag on the claim that a script could not write it. That was wrong — assigning a prefab ASSET to a field on a scene component is ordinary Editor work, and `HudCanvasPrefabSetup` already did exactly that; only a reference to another SCENE OBJECT would be out of reach. The menu step now finds `PowerupBarView` by TYPE (never by name) and fills both fields, but only when they are empty, so it cannot replace a variant an author deliberately pointed at. **Nothing is placed in the scene** — the popups are instantiated at runtime, and a copy in the hierarchy would be on screen from the first frame of every day; what enters the scene is two asset references. The user's remaining step is one menu click with the day scene open, plus saving the scene — affects: TutorialPopupSetup.cs, TutorialPowerupIntroView.cs, TutorialPowerupSpotlightView.cs, PowerupBarView.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-28

D-117: **A powerup is locked until the Day that introduces it.** The user's ask, 2026-08-28: "powerup tanıtılana kadar kilitli kalması gerekiyor ve bunu da üstünde belirtmemiz gerekiyor... kilit simgesi üstünde de lvl 3 yazıcak gibi." **Locked means unusable AND unbuyable.** Only dimming the HUD button would have been a half-rule: the shop sells charges for real Gems, and a charge for a powerup the player cannot press is money taken for nothing. So `PowerupShopView`'s locked row refuses the buy and hides its price — a price on something that cannot be bought reads as an invitation. **The rule is `PowerupSettings.IsUnlockedOnDay(dayIndex)`, spelled once**, and takes the day as an argument because neither side owns both halves: `PowerupConfig` has the schedule (D-115's `tutorialIntroDayIndex`) and no idea what day it is, while each screen has the day and no idea what the schedule says. **An unscheduled powerup is UNLOCKED**, not locked forever — a negative index means nobody scheduled a lesson, and reading that as "never unlocks" would let clearing a schedule silently delete a powerup from the game. Every uncertainty fails open (no config, no session, no resolved Day), the direction the key gate and the first-run Play gate already chose. **`PowerupManager` gained two pass-throughs and no logic** (`IsUnlocked`, `LockLabelFor`), because `PowerupShopView` deliberately carries no `PowerupConfig` — it reads even the PRICE through the manager, and a second scene pointer to the same asset is invisible until one of them is wrong. **Nothing touches the counts: a lock is permission, not stock.** A locked powerup keeps every charge it holds and they become usable the day it opens. **The label says "Day", not "Lvl"** — this game has no levels (root `CLAUDE.md`, Progression: the sequential-content concept is a Day), so `PowerupConfig.lockLabelFormat` ships as `"Day {0}"` with the number as `introDayIndex + 1`, the same +1 `SettingsPopupView` and `MainScreenView` apply because players count from one; the wording is a config field precisely so it can be rewritten. A format string an author breaks degrades to the bare number rather than throwing, because this runs inside a HUD render. **The lock's VISUALS are authored, not built**: an optional `lockOverlay` + `lockLabel` on each bar button and each shop row, exactly the shape the "Add" badge already has — and unwired the lock still APPLIES, it is merely unmarked, which is the safe direction. **The bar re-renders on `State.CurrentDayIndexChanged`, deliberately not on the tutorial's step event**: `ArmTutorial` fires nothing at all on a Day that authors no lesson, which is most of them, and `AdvanceToNextDay` reuses the day scene rather than reloading it, so a bar listening only to the tutorial would keep yesterday's locks for the rest of the game. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj`, 0 errors; 7 new EditMode tests covering the three-case boundary (locks before, opens ON the introduction Day, stays open after), the unscheduled fail-open, the player-facing +1, that a lock confiscates no charges, and both the broken and the rewritten label format. **The tests were not run** — Unity held the project lock for this entire session. Not exercised in the Editor by me. One manual step remains for the user: author a lock object inside each of the three powerup buttons in `SampleScene` and inside each shop row, then drag them into the new fields — affects: PowerupConfig.cs, PowerupConfig.asset, PowerupManager.cs, PowerupBarView.cs, PowerupShopView.cs, PowerupSystemTests.cs, CLAUDE.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-28

D-118: **Noise Clear equalises by COUNT, not by identity.** The user, 2026-08-28: it should not merely ask whether any ticket wants a food, it should match the NUMBER wanted and remove the surplus. **This reverses a rule the root `CLAUDE.md` stated and argued for** — *"by IDENTITY, not by count: surplus copies of a food a ticket does need are kept, because a player calls a third burger 'too many', not 'not needed'"* — and the user's answer is that "too many" is precisely what this powerup exists to clear: a board holding five burgers against two wanted IS noise, and a sweep that leaves it has not cleared the board. `TicketRequirements.ActiveTicketKeys` (a `HashSet`) gave way to a per-key BUDGET consumed as the sweep walks the board. **Counts SUM across slots** — three tickets each wanting a burger keep three; the keys collapse but the counts must not, which is the one thing a set cannot express. **The count is tray-aware** (the user's choice when asked): a ticket wanting two colas with one already in its tray still wants exactly one more, so the board keeps one. That reversed the old note's second half as well, and the old note's own argument is why it is safe — *a wrong order scatters the whole tray back onto the board*, so the scatter restores exactly what the clear removed. `OutstandingFor` was already computing this for Auto-Collect, so **the two powerups now share one definition of what is still owed** rather than each holding its own. The trays reach `PowerupEffects` as plain `BoardItem`s from `GameManager` (which holds both the board and the trays), so PowerupSystem still needs no TraySystem reference — the constraint the old note was right to protect. **Modifications need no special case**: `RequiredItemKey` carries them, so a plain hotdog is surplus against a ticket wanting mustard, the same answer identity gave. **Two existing properties are untouched and still load-bearing**: each cell is read fresh immediately before removal (because `RemoveItem` backfills from the pending-spawn queue, so collecting coordinates first would delete whatever landed in between), and a backfilled item is not re-examined — which under a budget has the sharper consequence that the board can legitimately end up holding more than the allowance, deliberately, since the queue is what the board still owes the player. Which surplus copy survives is unspecified: the first N in scan order, because a "nearest the trays" rule nobody asked for is a rule nobody can check. **The powerup is now materially stronger** — it sweeps surplus copies of wanted foods, which is most of a busy board — so `PowerupConfig`'s standing note that its price and starting stock need re-tuning is more urgent, not less. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj`, 0 errors. `ClearUnneededItems_KeepsEverySurplusCopyOfAWantedFood` was INVERTED into `..._KeepsOnlyAsManyCopiesAsTheTicketsStillNeed` (it asserted the rule that was just reversed), and `..._LeavesEveryItemAGuaranteedActiveTicketNeeds` became `..._LeavesAGuaranteedActiveTicketCompletable` because comparing the distributor's before/after counts would have pinned the old rule; what the guarantee actually requires is that one of each required key survives. Three new tests cover the cross-slot sum, the tray-aware count, and the same board with an empty tray. **The tests were not run** — Unity held the project lock for this entire session. Not exercised in the Editor by me. **GDD Section 5.2 still describes the identity rule and is a binary `.docx` I cannot edit; the user has to correct it by hand** — affects: PowerupEffects.cs, GameManager.cs, PowerupEffectsTests.cs, CLAUDE.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-28

D-119: **A locked powerup hides its background as well as its count.** The user, 2026-08-28: until it unlocks, the background and the counts should not show either. The counts already did (D-117 switches the count label off with the lock); the background did not, and the reason was mundane rather than interesting — `Background` is a child object of the button that had never been introduced to the code, and `Render` can only switch what it holds a reference to. It gets an optional serialized field beside `emptyBadge` and `lockOverlay`, dragged by the author, and unwired it simply keeps showing, which is exactly today's behaviour and therefore a safe default. **The hierarchy was READ, not guessed**: `Prefabs/UI/HUDCanvas.prefab` shows each powerup button as `[Image = the icon, Button]` with children `Background`, `Count` and `Add`. That settled two things — that "background" means the child literally named `Background`, and that the **powerup ICON is the Button's own `targetGraphic`** (`IconFor` reads `Button.image.sprite`), which is why it is deliberately left visible: the user named two things, the blocker sits on top of the icon anyway, and hiding a third would be a guess. A named field rather than a `GameObject[] hiddenWhileLocked`, for the reason the block's own comment already gives about the buttons: named fields cannot be mis-ordered, and a bag's contents no reader can predict. The shop rows are untouched — they have no background of their own and their price already hides while locked. **Also recorded because it is a live trap:** the powerup bar lives in `HUDCanvas.prefab`, not in `SampleScene`, though a comment at the top of `PowerupBarView` still says otherwise; that comment is stale and was left alone only because the file's manifest for this task did not cover rewriting it. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj`, 0 errors. Not exercised in the Editor by me, and the EditMode suite has not run at any point this session — Unity held the project lock throughout. One manual step for the user: drag each button's `Background` child into the new field — affects: PowerupBarView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-28

D-120: **Noise Clear's items fall off the board and fade instead of blinking out.** The user's ask, 2026-08-28. **It follows the split the project already made for Auto-Collect rather than inventing one**: the decision stays pure and testable (`PowerupEffects.PlanNoiseClear`), the doing lives in a MonoBehaviour that can hold a tween (`NoiseClearRunner`, `Scripts/UI`). Putting the animation inside `PowerupEffects` would have dragged DOTween and a scene into the one assembly whose value is having neither. **The visual is DETACHED, not re-created**, through two seams `BoardView` already had: `TryGetDragHandler` hands over the very object a finger would grab, and `ReleaseContainer` makes the pool stop owning that cell. A freshly spawned "ghost" sprite would have had to rebuild the item's modification layer to look right and would have drifted the first time an item's visuals changed. **Release BEFORE the model removal, and that ordering is the whole trick**: `RemoveItem` publishes `CellChanged` and backfills the cell from the pending-spawn queue, so a container still owned by the pool would be hidden mid-fall or handed straight to the item that just landed. **The plan is a closed list re-verified per cell by REFERENCE** — D-110's shape for D-110's reason: a backfill can drop a required item into a cell the plan already approved, and a coordinate alone cannot tell the two apart, so each `NoiseClearRemoval` carries the instance it saw. **THE DAY DOES NOT WAIT.** The model removal is instant and the charge is spent on the press; only the picture lingers. A powerup that froze the board to play its own animation would be spending the very time pressure it exists to relieve — and this is the powerup whose whole job is to give the player room. **This is not the old "dim the noise for a few seconds" design returning** (the 2026-08-25 correction recorded in the root `CLAUDE.md`); that bullet gained a clause saying so, with the test for a future regression stated plainly: if the board's STATE ever depends on the animation finishing, the forbidden design is back. The four numbers (fall distance, fall duration, fade duration, per-item stagger) are authored on `BoardAnimationConfig` beside every other animation number, and a stagger of 0 drops everything together. `ClearUnneededItems` survives as the data-only path: it is the fallback for a scene with no runner wired, and it is what the tests drive, because the RULE is identical either way and an animation is not something a test can see — so **a forgotten drag costs the animation, not the powerup**. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` (with the new runner temporarily added to the generated project, since Unity has not imported it) and on `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj`, 0 errors; 4 new EditMode tests pin that the planner names exactly the cells the sweep clears, that planning does not touch the board, that each entry carries its item, and that it counts trays the same way. **The tests were not run** — Unity held the project lock for this entire session — and nothing was exercised in the Editor by me; the fall has never been seen. **One manual step for the user:** add the `NoiseClearRunner` component in the day scene, wire its GameManager / BoardView / BoardAnimationConfig references, and drag it into `GameManager`'s new `noiseClearRunner` field — affects: PowerupEffects.cs, NoiseClearRunner.cs, GameManager.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.asset, PowerupEffectsTests.cs, CLAUDE.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-28

D-121: **The powerup lesson says its sentence once.** The user, 2026-08-28: the same text appeared in the intro panel and then again above the board after "Got it". It did, and it was self-inflicted. D-117 deleted the authored `tutorialUseInstruction` because it held a reworded copy of `Description` — one sentence authored twice — and that deletion was right; what followed it was not. The spotlight was pointed at `Description` instead, which **re-created the duplicate in the FLOW rather than the DATA**: the panel said it, then the frame beside the button said the very same thing seconds later. Removing a repetition from a config and restoring it in the sequence is no improvement. The `PowerupUse` step is now built with an **empty message**, so the panel explains and the pulsing frame points — they were never two explanations. **Empty rather than removed**: `TutorialStep.PowerupUse` keeps its message parameter and `TutorialDirector.CurrentMessage` keeps its reader (forced-MOVE steps still carry authored sentences), so a lesson that wants its own line is one argument away rather than a re-plumb. `TutorialPowerupSpotlightView` already switched its text object off for an empty message, so nothing was written to make this work; its codemap line now records that the dormant sentence is intended rather than broken. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj`, 0 errors. Not exercised in the Editor by me; the EditMode suite has not run at any point this session, as Unity held the project lock throughout. **Watch in the first play-test**: a forced press now relies entirely on the frame to say what to do — everything else on the bar is refused and the panel that just closed named the powerup, so it should not be ambiguous, but that is the thing to look at — affects: GameManager.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-28

D-122: **The tutorial step's message became an authored prefab.** The user, 2026-08-28: the sentence that introduces the mustard lesson on Day 0 is too small and wants a panel behind it — and then, having seen the powerup popups become prefabs, asked for this one to be one too. **A plate already existed**, which is worth saying because the ask was "add a panel": `BuildMessage` drew one at 0.55 alpha, which reads as a shadow. **The real defect was that its look lived in code** — the colour, the height, the paddings — so every tuning pass had to go through a programmer, against the root invariant that content including colours is never embedded in code. And the height was `fontSize * 3`, a guess that holds only while the message fits one line: raising the font, the entire point of the request, is exactly what pushes it onto a second and clips it. **The prefab reference lives on `BoardAnimationConfig`, not on a scene object, and that was the finding that shaped the design.** `TutorialSpotlightView` is created by `WorldTrayView`, which is a SCENE component with three instances — a prefab field there would have been three drags that must never disagree. The config asset is already handed to every one of them, so the reference costs zero drags, and an asset→asset reference is not the scene lookup this project bans. It also lands exactly where the fields it replaces lived: **`tutorialMessageFontSize` and `tutorialMessageScreenHeight` are deleted**, since the prefab carries its own font size and anchors and keeping either would leave a second authority over the same look. Both were read by `BuildMessage` and nothing else — checked by grep across the project, not assumed. The seeded prefab's plate **hugs its text** (`VerticalLayoutGroup` + `ContentSizeFitter`), so the number that decides readability can change without a second number having to change with it, and it ships opaque enough to read as a panel. `TutorialPopupSetup` grows to seed it and to write it into the config when that field is empty, so **this prefab needs no manual step at all**, unlike the two powerup ones. The ticket card's font is now only a FALLBACK for a prefab that shipped without one — the author's font wins. Its root keeps a Screen Space - Overlay canvas and must (D-086: on the ticket cards' Screen Space - Camera canvas the message was drawn and then buried, every time). **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj`, `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj` (with the setup script temporarily added to the generated project) and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj`, 0 errors; `check_blueprint.py` → `1 error(s), 6 warning(s), 3 info` — the error is the pre-existing `URP2DSceneTemplate.unity` line and the new INFO is the prefab the blueprint now plans and the menu step has not yet created. **Not exercised in the Editor by me and the menu step has not been run**, so the prefab's contents are unverified; the EditMode suite has not run at any point this session, as Unity held the project lock throughout. **One step for the user:** run `ExpoTheExplorer > Tutorial > Build Powerup Popups` — affects: TutorialSpotlightView.cs, TutorialPopupSetup.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.asset, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-28

D-123: **The main screen's two tutorial popups became authored prefabs.** The user's ask, 2026-08-28, completing the run that turned the day scene's three into prefabs (D-116, D-122). `MainScreenTutorialView` assembled a canvas, a backdrop, two panels, three labels, two buttons and a GENERATED arrow texture out of literals and multipliers; the code it replaced makes the case without help — the store hint's plate was `fontSize * 6.4f`, carrying a comment working out how the text band and the button band had been made disjoint by arithmetic, which is exactly the layout that breaks the next time somebody changes a font size. The seeded plate hugs its text instead. **Two prefabs because there are two moments**, and the store hint serves BOTH hint texts: `ShowStoreHint(message)` already drew the first-run hint and the play-blocked hint from one build, so a third asset would have existed for two strings. **The binders live in `MainScreenTutorialView.cs`, not in files of their own** — two small MonoBehaviours, the way `PowerupButton` sits in `PowerupBarView`. Each popup holds three texts, so telling them apart by TYPE inside the instance is the guess that breaks when an author adds a fourth; explicit fields are the only honest option, and no new file was needed to get them. **What is still computed, and why:** the arrow is reparented onto the store BUTTON and sized from it, because only the running layout knows where that button landed and how big it is. That is why **`storeArrowScale` survived while `canvasSortingOrder` and `storeHintFontScale` were deleted** — each prefab's own canvas and TMP carry those two, and keeping them would have left a second authority over the prefabs' look (the same cleanup D-122 made). **`CreateArrowSprite` left this file**: it generated a texture at runtime and the identical generator still exists in `TutorialSpotlightView`, so this removed one of two copies and handed the author a sprite slot for real art. Both roots keep a Screen Space - Overlay canvas and are instantiated PARENTLESS, which is load-bearing rather than tidy: a Canvas nested inside another Canvas inherits its parent's RectTransform rather than the screen's, and this view's rect is zero-sized — that once crushed the hint to one character per line down a sliver of the screen. The arrow rides the button and therefore outlives the hint, so `Cleanup` destroys it explicitly, the same trap `TutorialPowerupSpotlight`'s frame has. `MainScreenTutorialSetup` seeds both and assigns them, refusing to rebuild an existing prefab and only filling an empty field — **so there is nothing to drag**: the references sit on the one `MainScreenTutorialView` the scene has. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` and `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj`, 0 errors; `check_blueprint.py` → `1 error(s), 6 warning(s), 4 info` — the error is the pre-existing `URP2DSceneTemplate.unity` line and the INFO lines are prefabs the blueprint now plans that the menu steps have not yet created. **The menu step has not been run and nothing was exercised in the Editor by me**, so the prefabs' contents are unverified; the EditMode suite has not run at any point this session, as Unity held the project lock throughout. **One step for the user:** open MainScreen and run `ExpoTheExplorer > Tutorial > Set Up Main Screen Tutorial` — affects: MainScreenTutorialView.cs, MainScreenTutorialSetup.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-28

D-124: **The main screen tutorial stopped appearing, and the cause was an unsaved scene rather than the rewrite.** Reported straight after D-123. **Diagnosed from the files, not guessed**: `MainScreen.unity` still contained `canvasSortingOrder` and `storeHintFontScale` — two fields D-123 DELETED from the class — and contained no `welcomePrefab`/`storeHintPrefab` keys at all, while the two prefabs on disk were stamped 16 minutes LATER than the scene. So the menu step had run and built the prefabs, but the scene on disk predated the recompile: whatever the step assigned in memory was never written. With both references null the view skipped the panel, then skipped the hint, and finished — no tutorial, no complaint. **Two fixes, neither of which is the scene itself** (that is the user's save): the setup step now builds the prefabs BEFORE opening the `SerializedObject`, because creating assets and calling `AssetDatabase.SaveAssets` with unapplied edits pending on a live one risks the whole block being dropped — and that block also carries `sessionHost`, `shop` and `texts`, so losing it would leave the tutorial unwired in a way that looks identical to this. And a missing popup prefab is now an **error that names the cause and says to save the scene**, not a warning: this is the entire first-run experience failing, and it failed quietly enough that the file timestamps were the only evidence. The step's closing log already said the scene was marked dirty but not saved; that was true and insufficient, because nothing said so again at the moment it mattered. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj`, 0 errors. **The fix the user has to apply is a save**: open MainScreen, run the step, save the scene — affects: MainScreenTutorialSetup.cs, MainScreenTutorialView.cs, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-28

D-125: **The two popup binders needed files of their own, and D-123 was wrong to put them in one.** Unity refused to save both generated prefabs — *"You are trying to save a Prefab with a missing script"* — and the assets say why: each carries `m_Script: {fileID: 0}`, while `MainScreenTutorialView.cs`'s GUID appears in neither. **A MonoBehaviour must live in a file matching its class name** or Unity has no `MonoScript` to bind it to, so `AddComponent` in the setup step produced a component that resolved to nothing. **The reasoning that produced the mistake is worth recording**, because it looked sound: D-123 justified one file by pointing at `PowerupButton`, which does sit inside `PowerupBarView.cs` — but that is a plain `[Serializable]` class, and the rule it appears to demonstrate does not exist for MonoBehaviours. An analogy across a type boundary that matters. `MainScreenWelcomePopup` and `MainScreenStoreHintPopup` now have their own files; nothing about what they do changed. **The setup step now REFUSES a prefab whose binder does not resolve**, naming the file to delete, rather than silently rebuilding it. Auto-rebuilding is the tempting fix and the wrong one: during a compile error every script unloads, so the same `GetComponent<T>() == null` check returns null for perfectly good prefabs, and regenerating then would destroy real styling work — the error message says so, since a console full of compile errors looks identical to this. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj` with both new files temporarily added to the generated project (Unity has not imported them yet, so a clean build would otherwise never have compiled them — the trap in project memory), 0 errors. **Two steps for the user, neither of which can be done from here**: delete `Assets/Prefabs/UI/MainScreenTutorialWelcome.prefab` and `MainScreenTutorialStoreHint.prefab` from the Project window (so their `.meta` files go too), then run `ExpoTheExplorer > Tutorial > Set Up Main Screen Tutorial` with MainScreen open and **save the scene** — affects: MainScreenWelcomePopup.cs, MainScreenStoreHintPopup.cs, MainScreenTutorialView.cs, MainScreenTutorialSetup.cs, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-28

D-126: **The step's two modification arrows joined its prefab, which is now `TutorialStepHints`.** The user could not tune the arrows the forced-move lesson draws — the one over the board item's modification and the one beside the ticket card's row — because both were still built from literals and a generated texture. **The prefab holds all three pieces, but the STEP still decides which appear**, and that distinction is the design: `highlightModification` and a non-empty `message` are INDEPENDENT authored flags, so a step may point without speaking or speak without pointing. Burying the arrows inside a message-only prefab would have tied them together silently — instead the prefab is a palette, the view instantiates it when EITHER flag is set, and switches off what that step did not ask for. **The rename was free**: the setup step refuses to overwrite an existing prefab by design, so today's `TutorialStepMessage.prefab` could never gain arrow children and has to be deleted regardless; since a delete-and-regenerate was unavoidable, the asset stopped being called "message" once it carried arrows. The config field was renamed with it and **deliberately WITHOUT `[FormerlySerializedAs]`** — carrying the old value forward would have left the config pointing at the arrow-less prefab and the arrows would simply never have appeared, so a clean break empties the field and the view names the menu step that fills it. **The prefab's root is a plain Transform with the message's canvas as a CHILD**, because a `SpriteRenderer` — the world arrow — under a Screen Space - Overlay canvas sits at screen coordinates and looks broken in the prefab stage; the canvas keeps Overlay for the D-086 reason every tutorial canvas does. **Neither arrow stays where it is authored**: the view reparents the world one onto the board item's modification layer and the canvas one into the ticket card's row, keeping the local transform each was given, so an arrow positioned against a stand-in in the prefab lands exactly there on the real thing. Both therefore outlive the instance and are registered for teardown individually — the trap the powerup frame and the store-hint arrow already carry. **The world arrow's sorting order stays in code**: it is defined relative to the dim `TutorialSpotlightView` itself builds, so an authored number would be a second copy of a constant that must move with it, and a wrong one hides the arrow under the dim with nothing reported. **Six methods left `TutorialSpotlightView`** — `BuildModificationArrows`, `BuildWorldArrow`, `BuildCanvasArrow`, `BuildMessage`, `ApproximateWorldSize` and `CreateArrowSprite`, the **last generated-arrow texture in the project** (D-123 removed the other copy), so nothing draws an arrow in code any more and both arrows gained a sprite slot for real art. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj` (which builds `Assembly-CSharp` as a dependency) with both new files temporarily added to the generated projects, and on `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj` — 0 errors. `check_blueprint.py` → **`2 error(s), 6 warning(s), 3 info`**, and the extra error is expected and transient: *prefab on disk with no line in the blueprint prefab inventory: TutorialStepMessage.prefab*, which closes the moment the user deletes that asset. The other is the long-standing `URP2DSceneTemplate.unity` line. **Not exercised in the Editor by me and the menu step has not been run**, so the seeded prefab's contents are unverified; the EditMode suite has not run at any point this session, as Unity held the project lock throughout. **Corrected the same day, from play:** the world arrow was first PARENTED to the board item's modification layer, which kept its authored local transform exactly but made it ride the food when the player picked the item up ("ok yemekle beraber sürükleniyor istemem bunu"). It marks a CELL the player is being told to look at, so it belongs to the lesson rather than to the item: its authored local position is now read as an OFFSET from that ingredient and the arrow is parented to the spotlight view, which is at the origin with an identity transform, so the offset arithmetic is honest and the author still owns offset, rotation, scale and sprite. It also stopped dying with a piece of food, which had been throwing a DOTween destroyed-target warning on its fade. The CARD arrow keeps riding the ticket card, which is right -- that one marks a row that moves with its card. **Then a second correction, from opening the prefab: nothing was visible in it and neither arrow could be edited ("prefabta hiçbir şey görünmüyor... her iki ok da canvasta olsa ya"), and BOTH causes were mine.** The canvas sat at 0x0 in the prefab stage, so the plate and text collapsed into nothing. The first fix -- authoring a size on it -- DID NOT WORK and the asset came back 0x0, because a Canvas DRIVES its own rect and overwrites the value. The actual cause was that the canvas was NESTED: Unity gives a ROOT canvas a screen-sized rect in the prefab stage and a nested one nothing. The prefab's root is the canvas again, which it can be now that both arrows are UI Images -- the plain root existed only to host the world-space SpriteRenderer arrow, and that is gone. `messageRoot` moved from the canvas to the PLATE with it, since hiding the root would now take the arrows with it. And the card arrow was an `Image` parented OUTSIDE any Canvas, which never renders at all. Both arrows are now UI Images on the prefab's own canvas, as the user asked. That also removed the sorting-order-in-code argument this decision made two paragraphs ago: an Overlay canvas draws above every camera-rendered thing unconditionally, so the item arrow no longer needs a number set against the dim. The item arrow is bridged to the world item through SCREEN space -- the only coordinate system a world object and an Overlay canvas share -- with its authored anchoredPosition kept as the offset and scaled by the canvas, so a value tuned at the reference resolution means the same on every device. **One step for the user:** delete `Assets/Prefabs/UI/TutorialStepMessage.prefab` from the Project window, then run `ExpoTheExplorer > Tutorial > Build Powerup Popups` — the config is an asset the script saves itself, so no scene save is involved this time — affects: TutorialStepHints.cs, TutorialSpotlightView.cs, TutorialPopupSetup.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.cs, BoardAnimationConfig.asset, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-28

D-127: **The step's two arrows nudge back and forth.** The user's ask, 2026-08-28, one turn after both arrows became authored UI Images on the step-hints prefab (D-126). **The motion follows each arrow's OWN FACING, not a fixed axis**, and that is the only interesting decision here: the item arrow points down-left at an ingredient while the card arrow points right into a ticket row, so a shared axis would have read as pointing on one and drifting sideways on the other — and an author who rotates either in the prefab would have broken it. The direction is the arrow's local rotation applied to `right` (the sprite's resting direction), which lands in the parent's coordinate space, the same one `anchoredPosition` is measured in. **The two numbers live on the prefab's binder, not on `BoardAnimationConfig`**, matching where the powerup spotlight's pulse already lives: they are tuned while looking at the arrow they belong to, which is the whole reason these became prefabs. **Started after placement**, because the nudge is measured from wherever each arrow ended up — the item arrow is moved over the board item and the card arrow reparented into a ticket row, so neither resting position exists until then. **`SetLink` to the ARROW rather than to the binder**, because the card arrow is reparented into the ticket card and outlives the prefab instance; linking it to the instance would leave a tween running against a destroyed target, which is the exact DOTween warning this tutorial has already produced once. A distance or period of 0 holds the arrow still, so the animation is opt-out without a second flag. Same shape as `MainScreenTutorialView`'s store-hint arrow (`DOAnchorPos` + `Ease.InOutSine` + infinite yoyo), which is the precedent rather than a coincidence. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` with the binder temporarily added to the generated project, 0 errors. Not exercised in the Editor by me. **One step for the user:** delete `Assets/Prefabs/UI/TutorialStepHints.prefab` and run `ExpoTheExplorer > Tutorial > Build Powerup Popups`, which is already required by D-126's root-canvas fix — affects: TutorialStepHints.cs, TutorialSpotlightView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-28

D-128: **A prop that opens on its own explains itself.** The user's ask, 2026-08-28: when the drinks cabinet opens, show a picture of what it stocks (fanta, soda) and a line saying the drinks stand is open; the same for the fryer with fries; and it should be a prefab. **A celebration already existed and this joined it** rather than becoming a second system: `MetaGroundsView.CelebrateOne` already hides the real prop, stands a silhouette in its place, fills it in and focuses the camera, once per prop opened since `LastCelebratedDayIndex` — so the popup is a beat at the end of that beat, with no new trigger, no second queue and no second idea of what the player has already seen. **THE ZOOM IS HELD UNTIL "GOT IT", AND THAT COST NO CAMERA CODE** (the user's added requirement): the popup lives INSIDE `CelebrateOne`, after the silhouette is swapped back for the real prop and before the coroutine returns, so the focus that prop already has is simply never released. Dismissing hands off to whatever comes next on its own — another unlocked prop, whose own `FocusOn` travels the camera there, or the end of the queue, where `PeekAtWhatIsNext` slides to the prop now being waited for and the `finally` restores the map. **The content is authored per prop on `MetaCatalog`**: `unlockMessage` and `unlockImage`, where every other word and image in this system lives. **The message is the OPT-IN** — empty means no popup and the reveal plays exactly as before, which is what let this ship without touching a single authored catalog entry, and what keeps an unwritten prop silent instead of showing an empty box. The image falls back to the prop's own sprite, the same rule `ShopIcon` already spells in that class rather than in its callers. **The two fields are drawn only in `MetaEditorWindow`'s Day-Unlock branch**, beside `unlockAtDayIndex`: a purchased prop is one the player chose and paid for and has nothing to announce, so putting the fields there makes "purchased props never do this" structural rather than a rule someone has to remember. That window draws props field by field, so an unlisted field is invisible — it already says so about `shopIcon`, and these are the second instance. **The popup is NOT skippable by the skip catcher**: that catcher hurries an ANIMATION along, and this is the content the animation was introducing. Its dismiss button is the one part that is not optional — a popup with no way out would strand the map zoomed in behind a full-screen catcher, so a prefab missing it reports itself dismissed at once and logs an error. `IsDismissed` is polled rather than a callback because the waiter is a coroutine. **`MetaCatalog.asset` needed no hand-edit**: a missing key reads as an empty string and a null sprite, which is exactly "no popup" — the D-075 stale-asset trap is benign here rather than absent, which is worth stating so nobody "fixes" it later. The prefab's root is its Canvas (D-126) and its binder is a MonoBehaviour in its own file (D-125) — both learned the hard way earlier this session. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` and `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj`, 0 errors, with the new binder temporarily added to the generated project since Unity has not imported it. Not exercised in the Editor by me and the menu step has not been run, so the seeded prefab's contents are unverified; the EditMode suite has not run at any point this session, as Unity held the project lock throughout. **Corrected before it shipped: the seeding got its OWN menu step.** It was first folded into `Build Meta Grounds`, and the user objected — that step rebuilds the grounds container and rewires the whole view, so needing it to obtain one prefab means accepting a pile of unrelated side effects. `MetaUnlockPopupSetup` (`ExpoTheExplorer > Meta > Build Unlock Popup`) now owns both halves, the prefab and the reference, and `Build Meta Grounds` is back to not knowing the popup exists. That matches this folder, where every other step owns one concern. Removing the block also took `Set` and `FillIfEmpty` with it — shared helpers with live callers — which the build would have caught and a targeted grep did catch first; both are back. **Steps for the user:** run `ExpoTheExplorer > Meta > Build Unlock Popup` with MainScreen open and save the scene, then write an Unlock Message and drop an Unlock Image on each Day-unlocked prop in the Meta Editor — affects: MetaCatalog.cs, MetaUnlockPopup.cs, MetaGroundsView.cs, MetaUnlockPopupSetup.cs, MetaGroundsSetup.cs, MetaEditorWindow.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-28

D-129: **A tray belongs to a ticket, not to the scene.** The user, 2026-08-28: when a slot's orders finish the ticket stops showing, and the tray should go with it — after the tray's own animation has finished, not instead of it. **The fact was already in the model and only the tray never asked.** `TicketSlotManager.AssignTicket` hands a slot `null` once the Day's sequence is exhausted and leaves it that way, which `TicketCardView.RebuildContent(null)` already renders as alpha 0; `WorldTrayView.PlayDeliverySuccess`, meanwhile, ALWAYS ended by growing a new tray back in, so a full-size empty tray sat under an invisible ticket until the whole day ended. So this needed no event, no flag on the model and no new authored number: `State.TicketSlots[slotIndex] != null` is read where the tray already decides what to do next. **AFTER THE ANIMATION, NEVER INSTEAD OF IT**, on both paths and for different reasons. A delivery has already grown, lifted and faded the tray to nothing, so with no next ticket it simply does not play its re-entrance — hiding costs no tween at all and the lift the player is watching is untouched. A timeout leaves the tray in full view instead, where a hard vanish would read as a cut, so it plays `PlayTrayExit`, the exact mirror of the entrance (same duration, inverse ease), and only after `RefreshClearedTray` has finished clearing the items out of it. **That wait is why presence is POLLED rather than driven by an event**, which is the one decision here worth the words: the slot is emptied inside a synchronous cascade (`TrayManager.TryAddItem` → `deliverTicket` → `AssignTicket`) that runs at the START of a delivery, so an event-driven hide would fire while the tray was still lifting — precisely what the user asked not to happen. A poll can wait for `trayAnimating` and `deliveryInProgress` to clear; a callback cannot, and it costs one array read per tray per frame. **One entrance, two callers**: the "new tray" beat that follows a delivery is the same `PlayTrayEntrance` a slot handed a ticket again uses, so a retry or a new Day brings the tray back exactly the way a between-orders tray arrives, instead of a second animation with its own drift. **Every `SetTrayAnimating` call in the file now goes through a private method** that writes a local mirror alongside the card's copy, so "is this tray mid-animation" has a single writer and the two cannot disagree. **Dropping on a hidden tray was already impossible** — verified, not assumed: `TrayManager.TryAddItem` refuses a slot with no active ticket. Only the hover highlight needed a guard, since this view draws it and nothing else would stop it lighting up an invisible tray. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj`, 0 errors — run through a temporary copy of the generated project with `MetaUnlockPopup.cs` added to its compile list, since Unity has not imported D-128's file yet and the untouched project therefore fails on a line unrelated to this change. Not exercised in the Editor by me; the EditMode suite has not run this session (Unity holds the project lock). **No steps for the user** — nothing to wire, no prefab or scene change — affects: WorldTrayView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-28

D-130: **The HUD's day badge reads the day through the wallet source, like keys and unlike lives.** The user, 2026-08-28: they added `DayUI` (a cream 150x150 badge) and a child `Text (TMP)` to `HUDCanvas.prefab` by hand, and it should show the day they are on. The only real decision was **where the number comes from**, and it was already settled twice. The badge is **content of the shared HUD prefab**, so it appears on both the main screen and the day scene, and a prefab asset **cannot store a scene reference** — which is precisely the problem `HudWalletSource` was built for (D-013). So `DayNumberView` reads through it, exactly as `KeysView` does (D-065) and exactly as `LivesView` deliberately does **not** (D-064): the heart row is a SampleScene object and can hold a `GameManager` reference of its own, while this badge cannot. Giving the view its own `SessionHost` field would have added a **second per-scene override** to a prefab whose one existing override is already documented as the thing an "Apply All" quietly drops. `HudWalletSource` therefore gains `CurrentDayIndex` and a forwarded `CurrentDayIndexChanged`, beside the two balances and the key count. **That widens the class past its name and it is still right**: the class comment defends a narrow wallet scope, but keys were let in on the argument that they "genuinely belong here" because the readout is prefab content shown on both screens — the day badge is the same shape, and the alternative is a fourth resolver of the same two-mode question. **The subscription sits inside the live branch**, unlike the key one, because the day is the rare value with a genuine save-file mode (`PlayerProfile.CurrentDayIndex`, the same number `GameState` was loaded from): with no session there is nothing to listen to, and the fallback shows a stale day rather than Day 1 when a reference is lost. **`GameManager` remains the single writer** of `CurrentDayIndex` — nothing added here can move it. **The view renders `index + 1` and only the number**: the index stays one number everywhere it is stored or compared (the +1 is a display concern `MainScreenView` and `SettingsPopupView` already handle themselves), and the word beside it stays a label in the prefab, because authored text in code is forbidden outright by the root `CLAUDE.md` — the same split `SettingsPopupView` makes for the pause menu's readout. **Event-driven, no `Update`**: `GameState`'s setter publishes only on an actual change and the day advances once per day, so one subscription plus one render at `Start` is the whole truth; a per-frame re-stringify would be sixty reads a second for a value with a daily step. On the main screen the event never fires, which is correct rather than a gap — the day cannot advance while the player stands on the menu, and the single render already shows the day Play would open. Wiring joins `HudCanvasPrefabSetup` step 1, so `walletSource` becomes prefab data every instance inherits; the component itself is added by hand, since that tool wires references and does not create hierarchy the user authored.

D-131: **The celebration confetti runs the user's own `ParticleSystem`, filmed into a RenderTexture so it can sit over Overlay UI.** *(This decision was written twice. The first version below concluded the opposite — that the confetti had to be UI — and the reversal at the end is the one that shipped. Both halves are kept because the constraint that produced the first version is real and still governs the second.)* Original reasoning: The user asked for a celebration particle, 2026-08-29, and had already built one: `Assets/Prefabs/Confetti.prefab`, a properly tuned ParticleSystem — cone burst of 60 at t=0, 25° spread, speed 6–15, gravity 1, lifetime 5s, size 0.3→0.1, random start rotation, `Play On Awake` off. **The tuning was good and the asset was unusable, for a structural reason:** a ParticleSystem is a world renderer and a Screen Space - Overlay canvas is composited after every camera, so it cannot draw over this game's UI — and the meta map is entirely UI `Image`s, so it would have been invisible there rather than merely mis-layered. **This project has hit that wall before**: D-126's tutorial arrow was a world-space `SpriteRenderer` under an Overlay canvas that showed as an empty frame, and the fix was the same one — make it an Image. The prefab's ROOT already being a `RectTransform` is the fingerprint of the same mistake. So `ConfettiView` simulates the paper as pooled UI Images and **the numbers were ported rather than reinvented**, at 100 px per world unit; the user's asset stays on disk as the reference for that translation, referenced by nothing, with a blueprint line saying so. **The layering is per screen, and the user set both rules**: on the main screen, "the popups are already closed, it should be over everything" — `BurstOnTop`, instantiated parentless with its own Overlay canvas at 300; in the day scene, "behind the popup, in front of everything else that is behind it" — `BurstBelow`, inserted at the receipt popup's own sibling index inside that popup's canvas. **Sibling index rather than a sorting number** is the D-045 idiom, and it keeps the rule true when the hierarchy is rearranged; the walk UP to the ancestor that is a direct child of the canvas exists because the popup is usually a panel or two deep and inserting beside it there would put the confetti inside that panel, where a mask would clip it to the popup's own rectangle — the exact opposite of the ask. Nested, the instance's Canvas becomes a **nested canvas**, which is load-bearing twice: hierarchy order decides the layering, and a hundred moving Images re-mesh their own batch instead of the canvas carrying the HUD and the receipt, every frame. **There is deliberately NO config asset, and that was the user's challenge, not my design** — "e confige gerek var mı prefabdan ayarlayabilirim zaten". They were right, and D-127 had already decided it: the two arrow-nudge numbers went on the prefab's own binder rather than on `BoardAnimationConfig` because they are tuned while looking at the thing they belong to, which is the whole reason it is a prefab. A `CelebrationConfig` would have been a second copy of numbers that already live where they are edited, plus a moment enum, plus an asset to keep in step. The root `CLAUDE.md` invariant is untouched: **a prefab is data**, and nothing about the look is a literal in code. A beat that ever wants a different feel gets a prefab **variant** assigned to that call site — Unity's own mechanism, no code. **Cannon position and aim are authored as child transforms**, `up` rotated by the child's own local rotation, which is again D-127's mechanism (the arrows' motion follows their own facing, not a fixed axis) — so "bottom two corners, firing toward the centre" is where the user drags them rather than four numbers in a seed. That also deleted an origin enum and all screen-point arithmetic from the call sites. **Three beats, all on lines that already existed**: `DayCompletePopupView.ShowNow` after `popupRoot.SetActive(true)` (after, because `BurstBelow` reads the popup's place in the canvas and the parent lookup skips an inactive branch), and both of `MetaGroundsView`'s payoffs — `PropLanded` above the shake guard, so a scene with the shake turned off still celebrates, and `PropUnlocked`, so skipping the reveal still throws the paper and it lands before the hold and the unlock popup. The purchase beat was added on the user's own words ("satın alma olunca"). **Every reference is optional and silent when empty**, and `confettiPrefab` is not even reported by `DayCompletePopupView.ValidateReferences` unlike `rewardFlight`: the flight is the payout wearing a costume, this is decoration, and an error for a missing party trick trains people to ignore the console. Two locks against eating a tap — no `GraphicRaycaster` on the prefab, `raycastTarget` off on every piece. Unscaled time, because both meta animations already run on it and a day can be paused under a burst. **The burst fires ONE FRAME after `Play`**, which looks like a hedge and is not: a canvas instantiated this frame has not sized its RectTransform yet — Unity does that in the canvas update, just before rendering — and both cannons are anchored to CORNERS of that rect, so firing immediately would read every corner of a 0x0 box and stack both cannons on the same point. `MetaUnlockPopup` shares this prefab shape and never hit it because it only *shows* content and measures nothing. A frame is invisible inside a celebration, and waiting one needs no assumption about when a forced canvas update resolves a brand-new canvas. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj` (which builds `Assembly-CSharp` as a dependency), **0 errors**, with both new files temporarily added to the generated projects and removed again, since Unity has not imported them — the trap in project memory. Not exercised in the Editor by me and the menu step has not been run, so the seeded prefab's contents are unverified; the EditMode suite has not run this session. **Repaired on the way through**: `codemap-ui.md`'s `DayCompletePopupView` line was STALE and claimed `rewardFlight` is a required reference, which the code has contradicted for a while — it is outside the missing-list and only logged. **Corrected the same day, from the user opening the seeded prefab: "e bu canonlar içinde hiç bir şey yok".** They were right, and the design note this decision made two paragraphs ago was the cause. The cannons were seeded **zero-sized** on the reasoning that a cannon is only a point and a direction — true of what `ConfettiView` reads, and false of what an author needs, because a 0x0 RectTransform with no renderer is invisible AND unclickable in the prefab stage. **An authoring surface nobody can grab is worse than a slightly meaningless number**, so they now carry a 120x120 handle, which changes nothing at runtime (the burst reads the pivot and the rotation, and `sizeDelta` moves neither at pivot 0.5). **The second half of the same bug was worse because it was silent:** assigning `localRotation` from a script sets the quaternion and leaves `m_LocalEulerAnglesHint` — the value the Inspector's Rotation field actually displays — at zero. So both cannons genuinely were turned 60 degrees while their Rotation Z box read 0, and an author "correcting" that box to what it already claimed would have straightened the cannon without touching anything that looked wrong. The hint is now written through `SerializedObject` on the field the `.prefab` itself stores, rather than through an editor helper, so nothing rests on a guess about which API keeps the two in step. **And `ConfettiView` now draws the cannons** — muzzle, aim, and the two edges of the cone the spread opens — in `OnDrawGizmos`, editor-only, reading nothing but serialized fields, covering any cannon the author adds later. That is the part which makes the aim authorable at all: the rect cannot show a direction and the Inspector could not be trusted to. **Steps for the user:** open MainScreen and run `ExpoTheExplorer > Celebration > Build Confetti`, **save the scene**; do the same in SampleScene; then open `CelebrationConfetti.prefab` and tune it. Because the step refuses to rebuild an existing prefab, **the already-seeded `Assets/Prefabs/UI/CelebrationConfetti.prefab` must be deleted from the Project window first** — the same delete-and-regenerate D-125 and D-126 both needed, and for the same reason: a seeding step that overwrote an author's asset would be indistinguishable from losing their work.

**REVERSED THE SAME DAY, and the user was right both times they pushed.** "kullanmayacak mısın sana verdiğim prefabı", then "prefabın içinde yok ki particle sistem düzeltebileyim". **The Overlay constraint was not wrong** — it was confirmed from the scene files rather than assumed this time: `DayCompletePopupView` sits under `Popup Canvas` and `MetaGroundsView` under `Canvas`, both Screen Space - Overlay with no camera, so a ParticleSystem placed there genuinely never draws. **What was wrong was treating that constraint as the end of the question.** Porting the numbers into UI paper satisfied the layering and destroyed the thing the user actually wanted: an authoring surface. Fifteen serialized floats on a MonoBehaviour is not a substitute for the ParticleSystem inspector, and the cannons the first version seeded were literally empty transforms — nothing to open, nothing to tune. A design that meets every stated rule and leaves the author unable to work is a failed design, and it took two pushes to hear it.

**The bridge: the particles are staged far off-origin, an orthographic camera films only them into a transparent RenderTexture, and a RawImage draws that texture as ordinary UI.** That converts the layering into a UI question, which this project can already answer — so BOTH of the user's rules survive untouched: the RawImage's canvas is left a root Overlay canvas at 300 on the main screen (over everything, since the popups are closed at both beats) and mounted at the receipt popup's own sibling index in the day scene (under the receipt, over the rest). Neither existing canvas is touched, which matters because switching one to Screen Space - Camera — the textbook alternative — would have dropped the day scene's popup canvas below the Overlay HUD, a regression nobody asked for.

**No new Layer and no change to any scene camera**, which is the part worth stealing next time: the stage is parked at x = 10000, where neither scene has anything, so the game's own camera never frames it.
