A dedicated culling layer would have been the conventional answer and it is strictly worse here — it needs a project-settings edit, it needs every scene camera's mask kept in step, and a mask is one careless inspector click from silently showing the confetti in world space.

**`Confetti.prefab` stopped being a deleted reference and became the system's content.** It is INSTANCED into the rig twice, nested rather than copied, so tuning that one asset retunes both cannons. **Its authored aim was read rather than overridden**: the cone works out to (0.5, 0.866, 0) — up and to the right, in the screen plane, a 60-degree launch — so the LEFT cannon holder carries no rotation at all and the right one is that aim mirrored by exactly 60 degrees about Z. Neither particle instance's own transform is touched, so opening the prefab still shows the author the aim they tuned. **Exactly two properties are set on the particles at runtime**, both clock and plumbing rather than look: `loop` off, because the rig tears itself down when the burst ends, and `useUnscaledTime` on, because a day can be paused underneath a celebration. The three call sites did not change at all — `BurstOnTop` and `BurstBelow` kept their names and their contracts, which is the payoff for having put the layering behind two statics in the first place.

**`OnDestroy` is load-bearing three times over** and is where this class would most easily leak: the RawImage's canvas was DETACHED so it does not die with the rig (the reparenting trap D-126 and D-127 both already carry), the temporary texture is released, and the camera is pointed away from that texture FIRST — releasing one a live camera still targets is how a render loop ends up drawing into freed memory. Lifetime is polled off `IsAlive` rather than timed, so doubling a lifetime in the inspector needs no code change.

**The one thing that cannot be verified from outside Unity**, stated rather than buried: under URP a transparent RenderTexture needs the camera's background solid with alpha 0 and post-processing off. Both are set in code rather than left to the asset, because getting it wrong does not look like a mis-authored camera — it looks like the celebration covered the screen with a black rectangle. If that is what shows up, that is the knob. **The alpha was fine; the CLEAR was not.** First play came back as a screen full of coloured ribbons — the user read it as trails, which is the natural reading and the wrong place to look, since the particle prefab has its Trail module off and renders plain billboards. The arcs were whole trajectories: the RenderTexture was never wiped, so every frame's quads stayed and each particle painted its own path. Setting the camera's background to a transparent solid colour is supposed to clear the target and against a RenderTexture under this pipeline it does not. **The first fix for it was wrong and is worth recording so it is not tried again:** a `GL.Clear` against `RenderTexture.active` in `LateUpdate`. It stopped the ribbons and broke the frame doing it — `Renderer2D Pass: Fake or uninitialized surface is not supported for attachment 0` and `EndRenderPass: Not inside a Renderpass`, every frame — because reaching for the active render target from game code tears a hole in the pass the scriptable pipeline is mid-way through building. **That error message also named the actual cause**, which the first fix had stepped over: the target was a `RenderTexture.GetTemporary` with a depth of 0, and a pooled texture is not a real surface until something creates it while a target with no depth attachment is not one the 2D renderer can attach. So the camera's own clear had never been able to run — the ribbons were a symptom of a malformed render target, not of a missing clear. **The real fix is one allocation**: a `new RenderTexture(w, h, 24, ARGB32)` followed by `Create()`, owned and destroyed by this component, with the hand clear deleted. **The lesson is the ordering of the two attempts**: the visible symptom invited a fix at the symptom's level, and the pipeline error that followed was the one that pointed at the cause. When a render target misbehaves, suspect the target. **No prefab change and no re-seed for either round**, which is worth recording as the payoff for keeping the plumbing in code while the look stayed in the asset: two rendering bugs were each one recompile away from fixed. **Verification of the rewrite**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj`, 0 errors — and this time against Unity's own generated projects, since it had imported both files by then. Still not exercised in the Editor by me — affects: ConfettiView.cs, ConfettiSetup.cs, DayCompletePopupView.cs, MetaGroundsView.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-29

D-132: **A Day is deleted from the list it lives in, and the numbering closes behind it.** The user, 2026-08-29: the Day Editor's left-hand list should have a delete button, and deleting should renumber the Days. Both halves were partly there and neither reached the designer. **The delete button existed — at the bottom of the Day's own inspector**, a page several screens tall (food selection, ticket strip, per-ticket editor, Day Start grid, three settings blocks), which is nowhere near where someone stands when they decide a Day should go. So the row gained an X and **nothing else changed about deleting**: it calls the same `OnDeleteRequested`, because two delete paths would be two places for the unsaved-changes prompt, the file cleanup and the renumber to drift apart. `OdinMenuItem.OnDrawItem` is the right hook rather than a hand-drawn overlay, and the reason is in Odin's own doc line — it fires *after the row draws and before the row handles its own mouse input*, so the button swallows the click instead of one press both deleting and selecting. **The click is queued on `EditorApplication.delayCall`**, which is D-114's lesson applied to a new caller: this runs inside Odin's iteration over the menu items, and `OnDeleteRequested` opens a modal dialog and then calls `ForceMenuTreeRebuild` — rebuilding the list being iterated. One `AddDayRow` feeds all three places a row is created (`BuildMenuTree`, `CreateNewDay`, `OnDuplicateRequested`) so a Day added live is never the one row without a button.

**The renumber is defined as compaction, not as a shift.** `DayFileIO.Renumber` sorts the files by the `dayIndex` each one carries and rewrites them as 0..n-1 by position, so it closes *every* hole rather than only the one just made. That is deliberate and it is bigger than the ask: the Days folder had been sitting on gaps for a while (15–29 and 38–40 were missing), so the first delete after this pulls `30..42` down to `15..24`. Compaction is idempotent and needs no memory of which Day was removed; a "shift everything after N" rule needs both and still leaves the old holes. **It moves the file NAME and the `dayIndex` INSIDE it together**, because different readers use different halves and a file whose name disagrees with its content is a bug waiting for whichever reader looks second. **File-side rather than through `DayEditorModel.ToDayJson()`**, which is the load-bearing choice: routing it through the model would have made renumbering a valid-Days-only operation, and a Day with validation errors has to move with the rest or the numbering is wrong for every Day after it. **Nothing is deleted that is not immediately rewritten** — every Day is read into memory, then only the files whose path must change are removed, then all are written — which is also why no rename can clobber a Day that has not been read yet.

**A Day that never had a file renumbers nothing.** It left no hole, and moving everyone else's number because a scratch Day was thrown away would be a surprise rather than a service. **Same turn, a real bug on the line being changed:** the delete used `LastSavedDayIndex ?? DayIndex` to name the file. `LastSavedDayIndex` is set for every Day that came off disk and after every save, so a Day without one has *no file* — and the fallback meant that a never-saved Day whose Day Index had been typed over an existing Day's number deleted that other Day's file. The fallback is gone. And because the click is now queued, `OnDeleteRequested` opens by checking the Day is still in `loadedDays`: acting on a Day that already went is worse than doing nothing, since after a renumber its `LastSavedDayIndex` names a file that now belongs to somebody else.

**What is NOT adjusted, and the dialog says so before anything is written.** `MetaCatalog`'s `unlockAtDayIndex` (locations and props) and `PowerupConfig.tutorialIntroDayIndex` name a Day by number, and a compaction changes what those numbers mean. Auto-editing them would put a second writer on data the Meta editor owns, so the confirm dialog states how many Days will move and where the first one lands, and warns that anything naming a Day by number elsewhere will point at a different Day afterwards. The count is worked out the way `Renumber` works it out — the Days that have a file, in `dayIndex` order, compared against their position — so what the dialog promises is what happens.

**Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Editor.csproj` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj`, **0 errors, 0 warnings**, each confirmed to have actually invoked `csc` by touching the sources first (the stale-build trap in project memory). The Odin API was read out of the shipped assemblies rather than assumed — the `.xml` docs for `OnDrawMenuItem`/`Rect`/`IconButton`/`EditorIcons.X`, and `ikdasm` on the DLL for the one thing the docs do not carry, `OnDrawItem`'s type (`public Action<OdinMenuItem>`, a field, invoked right after `OnDrawMenuItem`). Four EditMode tests were added for `Renumber` and **have not been run — Unity held the project lock all session**, so the algorithm was instead transcribed line by line into a standalone harness and exercised over ten shapes including the real folder's: delete in the middle, delete Day 0, delete the last Day, many gaps, a lone Day at index 42, and delete-all-but-one. 10/10, with per-Day fingerprints asserting no Day is overwritten by another on the way down. That checks the logic, not the C#; the compiler checked the C#. **Not exercised in the Editor by me.** **Steps for the user:** none to wire — open `ExpoTheExplorer > Day Editor` and the X is on every row. Run the EditMode suite when Unity is free, and expect the first delete to compact the existing 15–29 / 38–40 gaps — affects: DayEditorWindow.cs, DayFileIO.cs, DayFileIOTests.cs, codemap-editor.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-29

D-133: **A Day is dragged to its place, and a reorder is the same rewrite a delete already does.** The user, 2026-08-29, immediately after D-132: they want to pick a Day up and move it. **The file half needed no new algorithm** — a reorder and the compaction after a delete are the *same operation given a different order*, so `Renumber`'s write half was lifted into a private `WriteInOrder(folder, ordered)` and `Move(folder, fromDayIndex, toPosition)` reuses it. Two implementations of "rewrite the Days as 0..n-1" would eventually disagree about one of them, and the one they would disagree about is the one that runs less often. **`WriteInOrder`'s ordering was already the thing that makes an arbitrary reorder safe, which is only visible now that there is one:** every Day is read into memory, THEN the files whose path must change are deleted, THEN all are written. A compaction never needs that (a target index is always ≤ the current one, so it could have been done in place); a reorder does — dragging a Day UP means a destination path is still occupied by a file nobody has rewritten yet. It holds because a file that keeps its path is the only file that can want that path, since a wanted path is one per position.

**`toPosition` is the caller's to compute, deliberately.** Only the window knows which gap in the drawn list the pointer was over, so `DayFileIO` takes a final position and does no interpretation; the window converts a drop slot (measured with the dragged Day still counted in the list) into that position with the one adjustment a list drag always needs. **That adjustment is the whole risk in this change** — it is off-by-one bait, and it was verified against an *independent* statement of what a drop means (the Day ends up between whatever was above the gap and whatever was below it, itself counted on neither side) across every combination of list length 2–7, contiguous and gapped starting numbers, every source row and every gap: **332 cases, 0 failures**, with no Day lost or duplicated in any of them.

**Three GUI facts are load-bearing and none of them is guessable from the API names.** `MouseDown` is NOT consumed — Odin still needs it to select the row, so the press is only remembered and a drag begins once the pointer has travelled past a threshold, which is also what keeps a plain click working. The `MouseDrag` case is deliberately NOT gated on the row's rect: by the time the pointer has moved far enough it is usually over a *different* row, and the drag belongs to the row it started on; whichever handler sees the event first starts it and `Use()` silences the rest of the pass. And the insertion line is drawn on `Repaint` from stored state rather than while handling `DragUpdated`, because `DragUpdated` is not a repaint event — drawing there draws nothing at all. The drop itself is queued on `EditorApplication.delayCall` for D-114's reason, same as D-132's delete button. Unity's own `DragAndDrop` carries the drag rather than a hand-rolled state machine, which is also where the cursor feedback comes from for free.

**A reorder is refused while any Day's number is unsettled on disk, and that is the one real constraint this feature adds.** A drop is measured against the rows *as drawn* and then the files are rewritten *in that order*, so the two orderings have to be the same one — and they are, exactly as long as every Day has a file and no Day has an edited-but-unsaved Day Index. A never-saved Day has nothing to move; a Day whose index was typed over sorts by one number in the list and another on disk. Guessing which one the user pointed at would land the Day somewhere they did not aim, so `ReorderProblem` names the Day that is in the way and stops. Days with unsaved *content* edits (index unchanged) are allowed through and get the existing Save All / Discard prompt, exactly as the delete path does.

**A completed drag confirms before it writes**, which is a real cost and was taken on purpose: a drag is a deliberate gesture, but this one rewrites every Day file, there is no undo, and D-132's warning applies again — `MetaCatalog`'s unlock days and `PowerupConfig.tutorialIntroDayIndex` name Days by number and are not adjusted. The dialog says "position", not "Day", for the destination: the Days are not necessarily numbered 0..n-1 *before* a move (a hand-deleted file leaves a hole) and always are after it, so the target is only a Day number once the rewrite has happened. **Dropping a Day back on its own place is a no-op and rewrites nothing** — including not compacting any pre-existing gaps, which the exhaustive check surfaced and which is the right behaviour: a drag that changed nothing should not rewrite twenty-five files.

**Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Editor.csproj` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.csproj`, **0 errors, 0 warnings**. Six EditMode tests added for `Move` — a Day moved down, a Day moved up (the shape a compaction can never produce), a move over a gapped folder, a same-position no-op, a clamped past-the-end position, and an unknown `dayIndex` — all using `ticketsRequiredForDay` as a per-Day fingerprint so an overwritten Day fails an assertion rather than passing as "the right count of files". **They have not been run: Unity has held the project lock all session**, which is why the algorithm and the window's position arithmetic were transcribed into a standalone harness and exercised exhaustively instead. That checks the logic; the compiler checked the C#; **the drag itself is not exercised at all outside Unity** and is the part to try first. **Steps for the user:** none to wire — drag a row in `ExpoTheExplorer > Day Editor`. Run the EditMode suite when Unity is free — affects: DayEditorWindow.cs, DayFileIO.cs, DayFileIOTests.cs, codemap-editor.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-29

D-134: **The purchase zoom holds until the confetti is done, and the confetti's own lifetime is the timing.** The user, 2026-08-29: buying a prop zooms the map in and then travels back out (D-049), and that travel was starting while the celebration was still in the air — so the payoff played out to a player already being pulled away from it. **The wait is derived, not authored**, and that was the instruction rather than a preference: "3e çektim lifetime'ı, ona bakarak yapsın zoom out'u". `ConfettiView.BurstOnTop` already returns the rig and the rig already destroys itself when its last particle dies, so `while (burst != null)` is the entire condition and the `Start Lifetime` on `Confetti.prefab` is the only number involved. An authored "hold for N seconds" beside it would have been a second copy of a duration that drifts the first time the particles are retuned — the same argument D-127 and D-131 both made for keeping numbers on the asset they belong to. **The one number added is a ceiling, and it is a const rather than a serialized field on purpose**: this coroutine holds the map zoomed in with the ScrollRect off, so an unbounded wait against a particle system somebody later gives a sixty-second lifetime is a softlock on the screen the player buys from. It is a guard, not feel, and the comment says so — the thing to tune is the lifetime. **The wait sits at the end of the `try`, not in the `finally`**, for a hard reason: `ReleaseFraming` is the last line of that finally and C# forbids `yield return` inside one. **The shake early-exit had to go**, and that is the change most likely to be mistaken for noise: `if (placementShakeSeconds <= 0f || placementShakeStrength <= 0f) yield break;` left the `try` before anything below it could run, so a scene with the shake turned off would have skipped the new wait and silently kept the old timing. It is an `if` block now, so both paths reach the wait. **A side effect nobody asked for directly, stated because it is real: the shop reopens later too.** `IsSettlingPurchase` reads the `placing` flag this coroutine owns and `MetaShopView` polls it before reopening itself after an area unlock (D-096); extending the placement extends that by the same amount. That is consistent with D-096's own words — its wait was meant to cover "the WHOLE payoff" — but it is a behaviour change that arrived as a consequence rather than a decision. **The day-unlock celebration was deliberately left alone**: `CelebrateOne` already holds the zoom on `MetaUnlockPopup.IsDismissed`, so the player is kept at the prop by the popup and a second wait would only delay the popup appearing. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj`, 0 errors. Not exercised in the Editor by me. **Settled on a cap after three passes, and the shape of the disagreement is the part worth keeping.** The full burst read as too long in play ("yok yarısı kadar beklesin çok uzun sürüyor"); a serialized cap went in; it was reverted an exchange later ("ya da dur geri al bitene kadar beklesin"); then it came back ("ya da yarısında dönsün ya yine vazgeçtim"). **The wait is now the burst ending OR `placementConfettiHoldSeconds`, whichever comes first** — default 1.5, half the current 3s lifetime, sitting with the other placement feel numbers under the Purchase placement header where every "how long does this beat last" value in this file already lives (D-127's rule). **I argued against it once, on the grounds that it puts a duration beside a duration and drifts when the particles are retuned. That objection was right about the mechanism and wrong about the situation**: the number was never going to be argued to a conclusion, it had to be felt, and a value that needs three plays to settle belongs in an inspector rather than in a code change per attempt. **Making it a CAP rather than a delay is what disarms the drift**: set it past the burst and the poll wins, so the worst a stale number can do is stop mattering — it can never stretch the hold past what is on screen. Both halves still earn their place: the poll releases a short burst immediately instead of parking the map on an empty screen, and the cap answers the one thing the emitter cannot, which is where the good part ends. It absorbed the softlock ceiling too, so there is no second bound to tell apart from it. **A new serialized field on an existing scene component needs no wiring**: absent from the scene YAML it takes its field initializer, so both `MetaGroundsView`s come up at 1.5 with nothing to drag. **No steps for the user** — nothing to wire, no prefab or scene change — affects: MetaGroundsView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-29

D-135: **Giving up on a day costs a key, and "giving up" replaced "lost" as the rule.** The user, 2026-08-29: "bu fail durumunda retry ya da main menuye basınca anahtar kaybediyoruz ya bunu settingsteki retry ve main menu için de uygula ve bu iki butona bastığımızda çıkan popupta anahtar kaybedileceğini belirt". **The hole was in the gate, not in the menu.** `SpendKeyForLostDay` read `GameState.IsAwaitingContinue`, which can only ever answer *was the day lost* — and the settings menu opens ONLY while the day is still running (`Open()` refuses on `IsAwaitingContinue` and on `dayIsOver`), so its Retry and Main Menu passed through the charge with the flag never set. A player about to lose could walk out through the pause menu for free and pay nothing for the attempt they threw away. So the gate moved **out to the callers**, each of which knows whether it is a surrender: `ReturnToMainScreenAbandoningDay` now charges unconditionally, since every caller of a method by that name is by definition one, and `RetryDay` takes a **required `givingUpOnAttempt`** — no default, because a defaulted `false` would make the next caller silently free, which is precisely the failure the flag read could hide. `SpendKeyForLostDay(bool)` became `SpendKeyForGivingUp()`.

**Deleting the flag read also deleted an ordering trap that was one line from being real.** The old comment warned callers to read `IsAwaitingContinue` BEFORE `LivesManager.RefillForNewDay` cleared it — get that backwards and the charge silently becomes free, with nothing to see in a diff. There is no flag left to read at the wrong moment.

**`RetryCompletedDay` is untouched and still costs nothing, and that is a rule rather than an omission.** The user stated it unprompted mid-task ("günü tamamlamış ama yine de retrylamak istiyorsa anahtar harcama sakın"), and it was already true by construction — the Day Complete popup's Retry is a different method that never reached the charge. It is now stated in the charge's own comment, because the *name* of the rule is what protects it: a completed day replayed for a better star score gives nothing up. The settings menu cannot become a back door to it either, since it refuses to open once the day is over.

**The confirmations name the price, and the price is scene text.** Both panels' `Question` labels end with "This will cost 1 key." — authored in `SampleScene.unity` and mirrored in `SettingsPopupSetup`'s placeholders so a rebuilt menu carries it. Nothing was added to `SettingsPopupView`: this is exactly the invariant that made D-094 build two confirmation panels instead of one shared panel whose question code would have to write. **A resource that leaves without warning reads as a bug, not as a price** — which is the whole reason the user asked for the sentence.

**The settings Retry is gated at zero keys, which is one step past the literal ask and is the same rule finishing its own sentence.** Without it, a player at zero keys restarts from the pause menu for free — the loophole D-069's step-5 gate exists to close, re-opened one menu over. It uses the scene's existing `NoKeysPopupView` through the same `HasKeyOrShow()` the Game Over Retry uses, checked on the confirmation's **Yes** rather than by disabling anything (the user's standing rule: a greyed button explains nothing, the popup explains and offers both exits). The refusing branch returns **before** `Close()`, so the pause is still held and the day stays frozen behind the explanation — the player can watch the countdown, buy a refill and press Yes again, or back out, without the attempt running on underneath. **Main Menu is deliberately NOT gated**, on either popup: blocking the only exit from a day at zero keys is a softlock, so it floors at zero (D-068).

**One scene change was structural rather than cosmetic: `Popup Canvas` 0 → 300, above `SettingsCanvas`'s 200.** `NoKeysPopupView` shares a canvas with the Game Over and Day Complete popups, and the settings menu is the first thing that OPENS it from above — at 0 the explanation would have rendered behind the panel it was explaining, which is invisible rather than wrong-looking, the worst kind. Safe because the popups sharing that canvas can never be on screen with the menu: the open button greys out and `Open()` refuses once the day is over, and the day is frozen while the menu is up, so no life can be lost and no ticket delivered behind it. `SettingsPopupSetup`'s `SortingOrder` comment (which claimed the menu sat above the two popups) was corrected rather than left to mislead the next reader.

**Side effect, stated rather than buried: the debug menu's Retry Day no longer charges a key**, where before it charged whenever it was pressed with the Game Over popup up. It passes `givingUpOnAttempt: false` — a cheat button has no business moving the economy — and this is a behaviour change nobody asked for, arrived at as a consequence of making the charge a statement instead of an inference.

**Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` and `Assembly-CSharp-Editor.csproj`, **0 errors**; the one warning is pre-existing (`ContactFilter2D.NoFilter` in `BoardItemDragHandler.cs`, untouched). Sources were `touch`ed first so `csc` actually ran, and all five changed files were confirmed present in those two projects' compile sets — both halves of the stale-build trap in project memory. **Not exercised in the Editor by me; no EditMode coverage is possible** — every file here is in the predefined `Assembly-CSharp`, which the test assembly cannot reference (D-012), so the protection this rule has is that there is exactly one copy of it to read. **Steps for the user:** none to wire — the `noKeysPopup` slot, both question texts and the canvas order were written into `SampleScene.unity` directly. Restyle the two added sentences if the wording or language should differ; they are scene text, so nothing in code cares — affects: GameManager.cs, SettingsPopupView.cs, GameOverPopupView.cs, SROptions.Expo.cs, SettingsPopupSetup.cs, SampleScene.unity, CLAUDE.md, key-plan.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-29

D-136: **An unfinished location's shop says COMING SOON, and the catalog validator's own warning is the trigger.** The user, 2026-08-29: "ya bu 2. metanın dükkanına coming soon yazar mısın orayı daha ekleyemedim", then immediately: "bunu dinamik yap tabii zaten meta editörde validation warning olarak var eğer o warning varsa herhangi bi metada coming soon yamalı". The second message is the whole decision — the first would have been satisfied by an `if (location.Id == "meta2")`, which is exactly what D-015 forbids, and the user reached for the condition that already existed instead. `MetaCatalogValidator` has warned since it was written that a location with an empty `items` list "would render as a bare background"; that warning is now `MetaLocation.HasNoItems`, **one predicate with two readers** — the Meta window's warning and `MetaShopView`'s label — so what the author is warned about while authoring and what the player is told while playing cannot drift apart. Neither side counts the list for itself.

**The question is asked of the CATALOG, and it is asked BEFORE `MetaPurchase`.** The cheap version of this feature is "the offer list came back empty, show the label", and it is wrong in a way that only appears late: a location where the player has bought every prop also lists nothing, and that location is **finished**, not unstarted — promising them more is a promise made by an empty list rather than by anyone who authored content. `HasNoItems` asks about the catalog; the offer list asks about the save file. They are different questions that happen to share an answer today.

**No id and no location count appears in code.** Whichever location is empty says so — meta2 today, the fourth one later — which is the same property that made a second restaurant "an asset to fill in, not code to write" (D-015). The words themselves are a const in `MetaShopSetup` beside `MARKET`, `NAME` and `PRICE`, not a catalog field: **which** locations show the label is data, but the sentence is identical on every one of them, so it is furniture rather than content. A per-location message would be a field authored to the same value every time.

**The label is OPTIONAL and deliberately out of `RequiredFields`.** A shop built before this exists comes up with a null reference and behaves exactly as it does today — an empty list — where making it required would have every already-built shop log an error at `Start` over a label, which is a worse failure than the one it reports. It goes up in `RebuildRows` and comes down in `ClearRows`, one owner each way: that is the same discipline `ClosePreview` enforces for the purchase ghost, and for the same reason D-024 recorded — a second place to hide something is how the first shop's ghost learned to survive the panel that summoned it. Walking from an unfinished location to a finished one therefore cannot leave the label standing over rows.

**Wiring rides the D-108 upgrade path rather than a rebuild.** `UpgradeExisting` gained one guarded per-field branch, the same shape that added the X to an already-styled shop, so the author's layout survives and one Ctrl+Z takes the addition back out. The label copies the **viewport's** rect instead of carrying hand-written numbers — the list's own area is where a "there is nothing here" message belongs, and it stays centred through a restyle — but it is copied, not parented: a child of the viewport would scroll out of the sheet. It is built inactive and placed last, after the wrap branch that moves the viewport down past the title and the heading row, because reading that rect earlier would put the label over both.

**Steps for the user:** open `MainScreen` and run `ExpoTheExplorer > Meta > Build Meta Shop` once, then save the scene. Nothing to drag. The heading row stays visible behind the label; hiding it would need a second serialized reference for a strip nobody is reading.

**Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Data`, `Assembly-CSharp`, `Assembly-CSharp-Editor` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode`, **0 errors** (sources `touch`ed first so `csc` actually ran; every touched file was already in its compile list, so the D-115-era "clean build that lies" trap does not apply). The EditMode suite was not run — no Unity in this session — but a test was added that asserts the predicate and the warning fire on the same locations, which is the only place their drift could be caught. Not exercised in the Editor by me: the menu step has not been run and the label has never been seen on screen. affects: MetaCatalog.cs, MetaCatalogValidator.cs, MetaShopView.cs, MetaShopSetup.cs, MetaCatalogValidatorTests.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-29

D-137: **A Day that draws its own opening board opens with exactly that board, and may not be saved unless one of the tickets on screen can be served from it.** The user, 2026-08-29: "ya editörde start board var ya eğer onun üstünde item varsa o bölüm özelinde oyun ilk başladığında hiç bir item spawn olmamalı ve validatör de eğer boarda bir şey konmuşsa ilk 3 biletten en az 1inin tamamlanabilir olduğunu kontrol etmeli eğer 1 bilet tamamlanabilir değilse kaydetmeye izin vermemeli ve uyarmalı". Two halves of one decision, and the second is what makes the first safe: **taking the spawner out of the opening takes a guarantee with it, so the guarantee moves to the author and the save gate collects it.**

**The suppression is the CALLER's decision, not the distributor's.** `GameManager.ApplyDayStartBoardPreSeed` — the one method all four day-start paths run through — sets `openingAssignmentsWithoutDistribution` to `TicketSlotCount` when the Day authored a Day Start entry, and `OnTicketAssigned` spends that counter instead of calling `BoardDistributor.OnOrderPlaced`. `BoardDistributor` itself learns nothing about day start: it stays the plain, unit-testable module CLAUDE.md Section 5 asks for, and **no timer or per-frame poll appears anywhere** — D-044's rule (spawning is order-triggered, full stop, reaffirmed by the user after a time trigger was built and reverted) is untouched, because nothing new is *triggered* here. One existing trigger is *withheld*, for the opening alone.

**A counter, not a flag, and it is set unconditionally.** "The opening" is not one event: filling the three slots publishes `TicketAssigned` three times, and each one would otherwise spawn required items on top of the arrangement the designer drew. Counting is exact rather than approximate precisely because the pre-seed runs immediately before `FillEmptySlots`/`ResetSlotsForNewDay` with nothing able to publish in between; it counts assignments rather than tickets, so a Day whose sequence is shorter than three still lands on zero (an exhausted sequence still publishes). Setting it **to 0** for a Day with no authored board is the half that is easy to omit and would be invisible: advancing from an authored Day to a plain one would otherwise silence the plain Day's opening too.

**The round is skipped, not run-and-discarded.** `OnOrderPlaced` is what makes a ticket *guaranteed* for the rest of the day — that selection is sticky by design — so letting it run and throwing the spawns away would burn the opening tickets' turn at the lottery. Skipped, they enter it at the first real round, which is what lets the Day recover normally the moment a slot resolves.

**"At least ONE of the tickets on screen" is BoardDistributor's own bar, not a weaker version of it.** GDD Section 4 requires that the player always have the ingredients for at least one ticket, never for all three; one completable ticket is a move, a move frees a slot, a freed slot brings the next ticket and the distribution the opening skipped resumes from there. Zero completable tickets is a player staring at a board with nothing to do until something times out. The count is `GameState.TicketSlotCount`, never a literal 3, and a Day shorter than the slot row is judged on the tickets it has.

**The rule could not have existed before this change, which is why D-001 Phase 4 deleted its ancestor.** While the distributor still ran at open, "is this Day completable" was true *by construction* on every `OnOrderPlaced` call, and the `DaySolvabilityChecker` that pre-validated it was checking something the runtime guaranteed anyway. Suppressing the opening is exactly what makes an authoring-time check meaningful again — and `DayValidator.ValidateDayStartBoard` is deliberately narrow: it fires only when a Day Start entry exists, so every Day without one behaves as it always has and this gate never touches it.

**Completability is judged by the delivery's own rule, through `TicketRequirements`.** The board is counted as a multiset of `RequiredItemKey` — food plus modification combo — exactly as `TraySlot.Matches` counts a delivered tray, so an item carrying a modification nobody ordered is a *different item* rather than a loose match, and a ticket wanting two colas is not served by one. That required a **Ticket-free `RequiredCounts` overload**: the validator holds a `ResolvedTicketEntry` and no `Ticket`, and building one there would drag `TicketFactory`, a name roll and a patience clock into a validation pass. Splitting the rule out rather than restating it also **closes a gap that file recorded about itself** — its tutorial rule had declined to check whether a forced move's item satisfies its tray, on the grounds that doing so would be the fourth copy of a consolidated rule. There is now a way to ask without copying anything. (That particular tutorial check is still not made; it is a different pairing and remains open.)

**An error, not a warning.** Most of this file's advisory rules are warnings on the stated principle that overruling a designer on a design decision is not the gate's job. This is not a design decision: a board that serves nothing on screen is a Day that cannot be opened, and refusing to save one is the same call `ValidateTutorial` already makes about a tutorial pointing at an empty cell. The Day Editor needed no change at all — `DayEditorModel.Validate` already runs `DayValidator` over `ToDayDefinition()`, board timeline included, and `Save` is gated on `IsValid`.

**Seven existing Days fail the new rule and are NOT fixed here** — days 06, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, 19, checked offline against their JSON. 06 and 14 place a single item; 15–19 place three drinks against tickets that want a main dish. They will refuse to save until re-authored, and at runtime they now open on that thin board instead of a distributed one. That is the rule working, and it is content work of its own; the user was told before the change was approved.

**Verification**: `dotnet build` on `Assembly-CSharp` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode` (which project-references Core and DaySystem, so both genuinely recompiled), **0 errors**. No new files, so the D-115-era "clean build that lies" trap does not apply. **The EditMode suite was NOT run** — the Unity Editor holds the project lock in this session — so the 16 new tests are written and compiling but unexecuted; run them in the Test Runner. Nothing was exercised in the Editor by me: the suppression has not been seen on screen, and the new Save refusal has not been seen in the Day Editor. affects: GameManager.cs, DayValidator.cs, DayBoardTimelinePlayer.cs, TicketRequirements.cs, DayValidatorTests.cs, DayBoardTimelinePlayerTests.cs, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-29

D-138: **The market button says when there is something the player can actually afford, and hops until they look.** The user, 2026-08-29: "ana menüde metashop market butonunun altına canbuy diye bi şey ekledim kodun ana menüye dönüldüğünde ya da yeni bi satın alım yapıldığında kontrol edip eğer satın alınabilir bi şey varsa o canbuyın aktifleşip 1-2 saniyede bir zıplama animasyonu yapmasını istiyorum". An authored object under the market button, switched on by a rule and animated while it is on.

**AFFORDABLE, not merely offered — and that is a different question from the one the shop list answers.** `MetaPurchase.ShopItems` deliberately KEEPS rows the player cannot pay for (D-019: seeing the price is what makes a prop worth saving for), so the cheap version of this badge — "the offer list is non-empty" — would be lit for every player who has not bought the location out, which is a nag that never stops and therefore says nothing. The new `MetaPurchase.HasAffordableOffer` runs `Evaluate` and short-circuits on the first `Ok`, so the badge and the BUY it is pointing at cannot reach different conclusions. This is the same shape D-136 gave `HasNoItems`: one predicate in the rules layer, asked by the screen, rather than a screen deriving a rule from a list's length.

**The user asked for two triggers and got them for free, because the shop already had one door.** Every transition of this state machine ends in `ApplyVisibility` (D-024's single-owner rule), so hanging the badge's recompute there covers *returning to the main screen* (`Start` closes the shop) and *a purchase* (`OnConfirmBuyClicked` ends on `SetOpen(false)`) with no new call sites at all. The purchase's close runs AFTER the ownership key is written and the wallet has spent, so the prop just bought is already out of the count — the ordering that makes this work was not designed for it, it was already load-bearing for D-049.

**Two events cover what the state machine cannot see move**, and they are the whole reason this is not just three lines in `ApplyVisibility`. `GameState.SoftMoneyChanged`: the powerup shop sells on this same screen (D-105) and the debug menu grants here, so money changes while the meta shop sits closed — subscribed to the STATE rather than to either of those views, because `Wallet` is the single writer every spender already goes through, which makes one subscription cover spenders that do not exist yet. And `MetaGroundsView.ViewedLocationChanged`, which is new.

**That event is the one this codebase spent three steps explaining the absence of, and the argument that kept it away is exactly what expired.** D-027 deleted it, and `ViewedLocation`'s comment justified the omission: the shop rebuilds its list on every open, and the only moment the player can walk to another location is while the panel is closed — so a publisher would have been written before its subscriber. The badge is ALIVE in precisely that closed window. It is published from the **end of `Refresh`** and NOT from `Step`, on an actual change of the resolved location: `Step`'s clamp can land back where it started, and `Refresh` is the only place that knows what was really drawn, so a redraw (a purchase, a re-open) publishes nothing and "the location changed" cannot decay into "something happened".

**The FIRST resolve publishes too, and that is load-bearing rather than tidy.** Unity does not order `Start()` across GameObjects, so `MetaShopView` cannot know whether it woke before or after the grounds resolved their opening location — and an unresolved `ViewedLocation` reads as "nothing to buy". Publishing the null → first-location change makes both orders end correct: either the shop is already listening and is told, or it wakes later and reads `ViewedLocation` for itself. Without it the badge would be silently wrong on roughly half of scene loads, with nothing on screen to say which half.

**Hidden while the shop or a preview is up.** The market button is drawn ON TOP of the panel by design, so a badge that stayed would hop over the very list it is pointing at — telling the player to go somewhere they already are.

**OPTIONAL, and out of `ValidateReferences`** — the same call D-136 made for `comingSoonLabel`, for the same reason: a shop built before this exists has a null here and behaves exactly as it did, where making it required would have every already-built shop log an error over an ornament. The hop is one DOTween sequence (hop, land, rest, loop) rather than a yoyo, because the REST is the point: a badge that never stands still stops reading as "look here" and becomes decoration. Height, hop duration and rest are serialized knobs (16 / 0.34 / 1.15 → a ~1.5s period, inside the user's "1-2 saniyede bir"), the badge's authored Y is captured once at `Start` and restored whenever the hop is killed, and nothing is cached: the answer depends on the balance AND the location AND the owned set, and a cached bool would be a fourth place those three facts are known.

**Steps for the user:** select the "canbuy" object in `MainScreen` and drag it into `MetaShopView`'s new **Can Buy Badge** field. Nothing else; the shop builder was not touched.

**Verification**: `dotnet build` on `ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.MetaSystem`, `Assembly-CSharp` and `ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode`, **0 errors** (the test project resolved `HasAffordableOffer` from the freshly built MetaSystem assembly, so the D-115-era stale-DLL trap did not apply; no new files, so the missing-from-compile-list trap does not either). **The EditMode suite was NOT run** — no Unity in this session — so the 8 new tests compile but are unexecuted. Nothing was exercised in the Editor by me: the badge has never been dragged into the field and the hop has never been seen on screen. affects: MetaPurchase.cs, MetaShopView.cs, MetaGroundsView.cs, MetaSystemTests.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-29

D-139: **Every ticket carries a customer's face, drawn beside the name and owned by the ticket.** The user, 2026-08-30: "art dosyasının altına characters diye bir dosya ekledim içinde karakter fotoları var ve istiyorum ki bu karakter fotoları ticket card header altındaki customer photo phramede çıksın", then twice pinning the shape down themselves — "framei silmeden üstüne koyacaksın değil mi" and "yine rastgele foto atacaksın değil mi her bilete isimlerde yaptığımız gibi". Both answers are yes, and both are load-bearing: the frame survives, and the draw is the name's draw.

**The attachment point is `CustomerName`, and there is no other.** A portrait is the same *kind* of fact — per-ticket flavour, decided once, rendered by the card — so it was added at every point the name already exists (`TicketGenerationConfig`, `TicketFactory`, `Ticket`, `TicketEntryFactory`, `TicketCardView`) and at no new one. No new file, no new asset, no new type.

**Domain data, not a view concern, for a mechanical reason rather than a tidiness one.** `TicketCardView.RebuildContent` runs every time the slot's ticket reference changes, so a face picked inside the view would be a second writer of this customer's identity and would be free to hand the *same* ticket a different face on a rebuild. `Ticket.CustomerPortrait` is written once in the constructor and never again. It is the class's first UnityEngine type by name (`Sprite`), though not in substance — `RequiredItems` has always held `FoodItemConfig` ScriptableObjects.

**It is the LAST constructor parameter and optional, which is the whole reason this change touched no tests.** Seven `new Ticket(...)` call sites in the EditMode suite and `TicketFactory.Create` pass five or six positional arguments; a seventh optional one leaves every one of them compiling untouched. Null is a legal value meaning "no portraits authored yet", not an error.

**Names and faces are drawn INDEPENDENTLY, and cannot be paired.** `names.json` holds 313 `{id, name, gender}` entries; `Assets/Art/Characters` holds 28 files named `1..30.png` (3 and 24 absent). The ids do not line up and there are eleven times more names than faces, so a portrait is its own uniform draw. Two cards can wear the same face exactly as they can already wear the same name. Gender is **not** matched: the names carry a `gender` field and the portraits carry no metadata at all, so matching would need per-photo authoring that does not exist — a follow-up, not a variation of this.

**The list lives on `TicketGenerationConfig`, not `TicketCardVisualsConfig`.** That asset is the authority on *who the customer is* — it is the runtime's only reader of this file, precisely because it owns `namesDatabase` (D-004…D-007) — while the visuals config answers what a *card* looks like and is keyed on `PatienceType`. An empty list is a legal authored state: `PickRandomCustomerPortrait` answers null and the frame stays exactly as the prefab author left it.

**The draw is on the RUNTIME path only, and that is not an implementation detail.** `PickRandomCustomerPortrait` is a separate method rather than a line inside `TicketFactory.Create` because `Create` is the AUTHORING path and `DayContentGenerator` hands that factory a **seeded** `System.Random` — one extra draw inside it would shift the stream and re-roll every already-generated Day for the same seed. `TicketEntryFactory` builds the played ticket off `GameManager`'s unseeded factory, and that is where both the name and the face are picked. There is deliberately **no** `CustomerPortraitOverride` to match `CustomerNameOverride`: a Day authors names, nothing asked it to author faces, and an override nobody sets is a second authority waiting to drift from the draw.

**The card draws the face and owns none of it — the frame is untouched.** `SetCustomerPhoto` writes `.sprite` and `.enabled` and nothing else: no colour, no alpha, no `SetActive`. That is the whole contract that answers the user's first question. `CustomerPhotoFrame` keeps its authored beige and its 50x50 footprint; the portrait goes into a **white child Image inside it**, and since the art is transparent-background, the beige reads as the frame it is. Wiring the field to the frame's own Image is legal and makes the photo *replace* the frame instead — the tooltip says so, and says why the child is the right answer.

**OPTIONAL, and out of `ValidateReferences`** — the same call D-138 and D-136 made, for the same reason: failing validation sets `isValid` false and takes the *whole card* down (an invisible ticket) over a decoration whose degraded mode is the card exactly as it looked yesterday. It warns once at `Initialize` instead, naming the prefab, and it also has to survive the `TicketCard UpDown` variant until both are wired.

**Verification**: `dotnet build` is **not trustworthy in this checkout** and this is worth recording — only 6 of the 20+ asmdefs have generated `.csproj` files, `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` project-references a dozen that do not exist on disk, and **none of the six compiles `Ticket.cs`, `TicketFactory.cs`, `TicketEntryFactory.cs` or `TicketCardView.cs`**. A green build on the existing projects would have said nothing about four of the five files changed. Verified instead by generating three throwaway csprojs (deleted afterwards) that borrowed the real Unity/package reference sets and compiled the actual source folders: (1) Core + TicketSystem + DaySystem + BoardDistribution — **0 errors**; (2) all 89 `Assets/Scripts` files as one assembly — the only files that errored were `HapticsBinder.cs` and `DebugMenuBinder.cs`, on the third-party assemblies (InputSystem, NiceVibrations, SRDebugger) deliberately pruned from the graph, and **none of the five changed files produced an error**; (3) the same plus all 35 EditMode test files — every `new Ticket(...)` call site compiled unchanged, confirming the trailing-optional parameter. **The EditMode suite was NOT run and nothing was seen in the Editor**: no sprite has been dragged into the config, no child Image exists in the prefab yet, and no portrait has appeared on a card. affects: Ticket.cs, TicketGenerationConfig.cs, TicketFactory.cs, TicketEntryFactory.cs, TicketCardView.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-30

D-140: **The upcoming prop's percentage sits ON the prop, not above its head.** The user, 2026-08-31: "bu metada unlock at day itemlerinin üstündeki % kısmını itemin üstüne alabilir miyiz". The caption belongs to the art that is filling up, so it is drawn centred on it: in `MetaGroundsView.CreateUpcomingLabel` the label's anchor and pivot are both (0.5, 0.5) instead of a top anchor with a bottom pivot. A top-anchored caption is free to float into a taller neighbour's airspace, which is the same reasoning D-045 applied to the ghost's depth, one axis over. `upcomingLabelOffset` survives as a nudge from that centre rather than a gap above the head, and the value authored in `MainScreen.unity` (16, meaning "16 above the prop") was rewritten to 0 in the same task — a serialized number whose meaning changes silently is the trap D-075 and D-007 were both about. Nothing about WHICH prop is previewed or HOW FAR along it is moved: `MetaResolver.NextDayUnlock` still decides, the silhouette + fill layers are untouched, and the Meta Editor's canvas draws no percentage at all, so the two views cannot disagree here. **Verification**: no Unity run — the change is two rect fields on a code-built label plus one scene scalar; there is nothing for a compile or the EditMode suite to catch, and the thing to look at is the label's position on the real props. affects: MetaGroundsView.cs, MainScreen.unity, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-31

D-141: **The star that flies to the star counter gets its own size, 2.5x the coin's.** The user, 2026-08-31: "bu day complete popupta yıldızlar küçük başlıyolar ... yıldız aldıkça çıkıp yıldız UIya uçan yıldızlar 2.5 kat daha büyük olsun". **The finding that made this a two-minute change instead of a hunt: the code's "gem" IS the star on screen.** `DayRewardFlightView` calls it `gemSprite`/`gemFlightTarget` because the ledger pays gems per star, but the scene wires that sprite to `Art/UI/Popup/DayCompleteUI/Star.png` and that target to the HUD's star counter — the name is the ledger's word and the sprite is its costume. Nothing in the file said so, which is why the note now does: the two names have already been read as two different things. That confusion had a cost. The star and the coin shared one `flightIconSize` at 48, so the star the player had just earned arrived at the counter drawn the size of one tenth of a total. `Launch` and `SpawnIcon` now take the size as an **argument** and the star has its own serialized `starIconSize`, landed at **180**; the coins keep 48. The number arrived in two passes — 2.5x asked for, then "ya 1.5 kat daha buyutur musun" on sight — which is itself the argument for the field: an icon size is a LOOK, it was always going to be tuned by eye, and the shared field could not have been moved a second time without dragging ten coins along. 180 is 3.75x the coin and nearly twice the 100px star it launches from, so the icon deliberately reads as bigger than its own origin. The asymmetry is the design, not a tuning accident: there are at most three stars and each is a whole star, against up to `coinCount` coins that have to read as a spray of change rather than ten separate prizes — one shared field could only ever be wrong for one of the two. **This does not touch a star the scene owns** (D-062: nothing in the game moves or sizes those); the three 100px objects in `StarRow` still seat at scale 1 exactly where the scene put them, and what grew is the icon the code builds. Nothing about the payout moved — same amounts, same `DayRewardPurse` shares, same commit-on-exit guarantee, same haptics, same ordering rules. **`starIconSize: 180` is also written into `SampleScene.unity` rather than left to the C# initializer**, the same call D-140 made for `upcomingLabelOffset` and for the reason D-075 exists: a serialized MonoBehaviour that does not name a newly added field is this project's known stale-serialization trap. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on the shipped csprojs is untrustworthy here (D-139's finding — `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` is skipped when `ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.LivesSystem.csproj` fails first on an unrelated missing Core reference, so its clean result says nothing). Verified instead with a throwaway csproj (deleted afterwards) compiling all of `Assets/Scripts` plus `Assets/Data` against the real reference set: the only files that errored were `SROptions.Expo.cs`, `DebugMenuBinder.cs` and `HapticsBinder.cs`, all on the third-party assemblies (SRDebugger, NiceVibrations) pruned from the graph, and **`DayRewardFlightView.cs` produced none**. **Not seen in Unity**: nobody has watched a star fly at 180px, and whether it reads as a hand-off or as a pop is exactly the thing only the real popup can answer — both sizes are serialized and tunable without code. affects: DayRewardFlightView.cs, SampleScene.unity, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-31

D-142: **A Day introduces what is new — a food, or a modification with its +/- sign — and the level stops while it says so.** The user, 2026-08-31: "şimdi yeni bi item geldiğinde oyuna bunu tanıtan bölüm başı çıkacak popup istiyorum bunu bi liste içinde tutup işte new item diyip üstünde item resmi olacak şeilde popup ve kaçıncı bölümde gösterileceği falan olmalı hatta bunu dayeditörde yapalım en altta bi seçenek olsun introduce new item gibi işaretleyince bi sekme çıksın oraya ben sprite ve itemin ismini yazayım", then twice widening it on sight — "ya bu new item muhabbetine modificasyonları da eklesek ya addition remove işaretiyle beraber" and "ama tabii itemsa new item modificationsa new modification yazmalı başlıkta".

**THE ONE THING THE USER ASKED FOR THAT THE PROJECT CANNOT GIVE IS A DRAGGED SPRITE, and the reason is worth stating because it decided the whole schema.** A Day is a JSON file under `Resources/Days`; sprites do not live under `Resources`, so a Day file has nothing a path could point at and can only ever name art by **id**. That is not a limitation this feature worked around — it is the better answer, and the user chose it when asked: an id resolves to a `FoodItemConfig` or a `ModificationConfig` that already owns **both** the picture and the display name, so the popup shows the very asset the player picks up off the board a minute later, by construction rather than by an author keeping two copies in step. The Day Editor pays that back as a **picture picker**: the same tile grid the Start Board cell editor uses, fed by this Day's own food selection. What the user writes is the optional NAME OVERRIDE and the line under it — marketing copy, which the catalog's content key should not have to be.

**The block is `runtime.itemIntro { enabled, items[] }`**, beside `tutorial` and shaped like it. `enabled` is not a designer convenience: `JsonUtility` hands back a zeroed instance for a missing object rather than null, so absence has to be carried by a field that must be typed on purpose — and it doubles as the "Introduce New Item" checkbox, which is why **unticking keeps the authored rows** instead of deleting them. It is a LIST from the first commit, unlike the tutorial which earned its list on the second authored step (D-083): the user asked for one, and a Day that brings a burger and its drink in the same morning is the ordinary case, not a generalisation waiting for an example.

**An entry is EITHER a food OR a modification, and the two ids ARE the discriminator.** There is no `kind` string — the shape `TutorialStepJson` uses — and the difference is the point: there, the kind selects between step SHAPES that share no fields and a typo had to fail loudly; here the ids resolve against different catalogs and an empty one is simply not that kind, so a third field naming which of two obvious things this is would be a **second authority free to disagree with the ids beside it**. `isAddition` rides along because `false` is a real value for a modification (a removal), which is exactly why absence could not be carried by the bool. The parser reads `modificationId` FIRST, so a hand-edited file naming both resolves to the more specific thing rather than being dropped; `DayValidator` says so out loud. **Whether a direction is LEGAL has one authority, `ModificationConfig.AllowedDirection`** — the editor's right-click flip seeds and constrains from it, the validator warns from it, and neither owns a copy.

**The popup carries no art decisions and no strings.** It is a COPY of `MetaUnlockPopup.prefab` (the user's own reference: "tasarım olarak mainscreendeki yeni bi şey açılmayı kullanabilirz") rather than a reuse, so the meta grounds' popup and the day's can be restyled apart. Both headers — "NEW ITEM!" and "NEW MODIFICATION!" — are **authored objects in the prefab** and `Bind` switches one on; two objects rather than one header plus two strings in code, because the words belong where every other player-facing string in this project lives and the longer of the two needs its own font size. The +/- sprite comes from **`TicketCardVisualsConfig` held on the prefab** — an asset reference, the one reference shape a prefab can carry, exactly as `TicketCardsView.DirectionSpriteFor` reads it — so the badge on this popup and the badge on a ticket card cannot disagree, and **no new sprite field was added anywhere**. `Bind`'s direction argument is a **nullable bool**: "not a modification" is a real third state, and a plain `false` would be indistinguishable from a removal, which is the mistake that puts a "-" badge on a burger.

**The day stops while it reads.** `GameManager.ShowItemIntrosForCurrentDay` hangs off `ApplyDayStartBoardPreSeed` — the ONE call site all four day-start paths already share, immediately after `ArmTutorial`, for the reason D-082 put the tutorial there: one call site instead of four, and it cannot drift out of step with the board it opens on. The coroutine takes its hold on its **first line**, which runs synchronously inside `StartCoroutine`, so the day cannot tick a frame behind a popup that is about to appear; the release is in a `finally`, which also covers the routine being stopped mid-popup. `StopItemIntros` is the braces to that belt — it kills the routine, **destroys the live popup** (a parentless instance would otherwise sit on screen with nothing left to dismiss it) and releases again, safely, because `ReleasePause` is a `HashSet` remove. It runs at the top of every day start and from `OnDestroy`, the rule **D-105** set for every pause holder, and its token is a dedicated object rather than `this`, since this class is the WRITER of that set. **Retries re-introduce, deliberately**: a retried Day is the Day played from the top, and an introduction that appeared only on the first attempt would be missing for precisely the player who is struggling with it.

**Everything degrades to the day exactly as it played before.** The prefab field is optional (unwired: no introductions, and a warning naming the field only when a Day actually authored some), an unresolvable id costs its own popup and not the Day — unlike a bad ticket or board id, because an introduction is a greeting and dropping the day would punish the content for the decoration — and every `DayValidator` rule here is a **warning, never an error**: no way of getting an introduction wrong makes a Day unplayable, so refusing the Save would be the gate making a design call it is not entitled to (compare `ValidateTutorial`, which errors, because that describes moves the player is locked into).

**Verification**: `dotnet build` on the shipped csprojs is untrustworthy here (D-139/D-141's finding). Verified instead with a throwaway csproj (kept out of the repo) compiling **all 162 files** under `Assets/Scripts`, `Assets/Data`, `Assets/Editor` and `Assets/Tests` against the real reference set, with the project's own prebuilt `Library/ScriptAssemblies` DLLs **excluded** so a stale assembly could not answer for a source file. The only errors were the two in `HapticsBinder.cs`, on the NiceVibrations (Lofelt) assembly pruned from that graph; **none of the nine files this decision touched produced one.** The prefab was written by transforming `MetaUnlockPopup.prefab` and then checked structurally — 47 objects, no dangling internal fileID, no duplicate anchor, no unparented object, all eight serialized fields bound. **Not seen in Unity**: nobody has opened the prefab or played a Day that introduces anything, and the two things only the editor can answer are whether the hand-written YAML imports clean and whether the reflowed layout reads well. `check_blueprint.py` reports 0 errors, 7 warnings — identical to the count at session start. affects: DayJson.cs, DayDefinition.cs, DayCatalogParser.cs, DayValidator.cs, NewItemIntroPopup.cs, GameManager.cs, DayEditorModel.cs, NewItemIntroPopup.prefab, SampleScene.unity, DayCatalogParserTests.cs, DayEditorModelConverterTests.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md, codemap-editor.md — 2026-08-31

D-143: **Every popup in the game fades in, through one helper, at one authored speed — and nothing fades out.** The user, 2026-08-31: "ya projedeki bütün popupların fade in ile gelmesini istiyorum bütün projedeki popupları incele", then, asked where the number should live and whether closing should match: "config'e ekle, kapanışta fade out istemiyorum". **The inventory came first and it is the reason this is one decision rather than nine.** Fourteen popups exist across three shapes that share nothing but "become visible": nine scene objects toggled with `SetActive` (day complete, game over, no keys, the settings menu, its retry and main-menu confirmations, the powerup shop, the meta shop sheet, the meta buy confirmation), two prefabs instantiated per use (`MetaUnlockPopup`, `NewItemIntroPopup`), and three that already faded (`TutorialPowerupIntroView` and the two first-run tutorial plates). **`PopupFade.In(GameObject, float)` is a static helper and that is a decision, not a default.** A base class cannot reach a popup that is instantiated, and a MonoBehaviour would have had to be added to eleven objects across two scenes and four prefabs — one of which gets forgotten eventually, and a forgotten fade fails silently. A call on the line after the `SetActive` cannot be forgotten: it is in the line of sight of whoever is reading the show path. It takes a **duration rather than the config**, so the helper depends on nothing in this project and stays a leaf. It `AddComponent<CanvasGroup>` when one is absent, which is always — `unitymap.md` shows not one popup root in either scene carrying a `CanvasGroup`, so requiring eleven manual component additions would have made a fade into a wiring chore. It calls `DOKill()` on the group **before** writing alpha, which is the load-bearing line: a popup closed and reopened faster than it fades would otherwise have two tweens writing one alpha. It deliberately does **not** `SetUpdate(true)` — this project has never touched `Time.timeScale` (`SettingsPopupView` says so at the top of its file), and an unscaled tween would assert a pause mechanism that does not exist here. **The speed is `BoardAnimationConfig.popupFadeInDuration` (0.2s), one field for all fourteen**, because popups arriving at different speeds is the kind of inconsistency nobody names but everybody feels — and the drift had already started: `MainScreenTutorialView.FadeIn` carried a hardcoded `0.25f`, a tuning number in code that the root invariant forbids. Both halves moved out, the mechanism to `PopupFade` and the number to the config. `TutorialPowerupIntroView` was deliberately **left alone**: it already fades correctly from a duration authored on its prefab, and folding it in would have silently retuned a tuned panel. **The two instantiated popups hold the config themselves** rather than being handed it by their spawner. The fade is the popup's own presentation, and this way `GameManager`'s day-start sequence and the 90KB `MetaGroundsView` did not have to be touched at all — the fade fires on the **last** line of `Bind`, because instantiate and bind share a frame and fading earlier would fade up a panel still carrying the prefab's authored placeholder. **`MetaShopView` needed a transition check** the others did not: `ApplyVisibility` runs from three sites and re-runs while the shop is open, so it reads `activeSelf` **before** its two `SetActive` calls — without that the sheet would restart its fade from zero on every row change and read as a flicker, and fading both sides of the sheet/confirm swap would cross-dissolve them and let the map show through the gap. **An unwired config or a 0 duration degrades to today's instant appearance, never to an invisible popup**, and every `animConfig` here is optional and outside `ValidateReferences` for the reason every optional animation reference in this project is: each of these popups is modal or blocking, so an empty slot must never be able to suppress the only way out of one. **No fade out anywhere, by the user's explicit decision** — a popup goes away in the frame it is dismissed, so the tap that closed it feels answered rather than acknowledged and then processed. The asymmetry is intentional and is not an oversight for a later task to "complete". **Verification**: Unity's own compile is not evidence here — the Editor is open and `Assembly-CSharp.dll` was written at 16:10:20, before seven of the eleven edits. `dotnet build` on the shipped csprojs is untrustworthy for the reason D-139/D-141 record. Verified instead with a throwaway csproj (deleted afterwards) compiling all 112 files under `Assets/Scripts` and `Assets/Data/DataScripts` against the real reference set, with the prebuilt `Library/ScriptAssemblies` Expo DLLs **excluded** so a stale assembly could not answer for a source file: the only errors were in `SROptions.Expo.cs` and, once it was excluded, the two in `DebugMenuBinder.cs` that look for the other half of its partial — both on SRDebugger, pruned from that graph. **None of the eleven files this decision touched produced an error.** `check_blueprint.py` reports 0 errors, 7 warnings — identical to the count at session start. **Not seen in Unity**: nobody has watched a popup fade. 0.2s is a first guess at a number that was always going to be judged by eye, and it is one field away from being retuned. **The editor side is outstanding and is the user's**: run `ExpoTheExplorer > Re-serialize Data Configs` (the `.asset` predates the field, so an Inspector edit will not stick until the key is written — D-075), then drag `BoardAnimationConfig.asset` into seven slots and save both scenes. affects: BoardAnimationConfig.cs, PopupFade.cs, DayCompletePopupView.cs, GameOverPopupView.cs, NoKeysPopupView.cs, SettingsPopupView.cs, PowerupShopView.cs, MetaShopView.cs, MetaUnlockPopup.cs, NewItemIntroPopup.cs, MainScreenTutorialView.cs, codemap-ui.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-31

D-144: **Telemetry identity lives in its own file, and that file is what a save wipe must not touch.** The user is building a playtest telemetry system (plan: `.claude/telemetry-plan.md`, rev 2, approved) whose first shippable slice is identity: `installationId` (this install/device), `playerId` (the logical playtester), and — from Step 3 — `runId` (one Day attempt, RAM only). This step builds the first two and nothing else: no Firebase, no run, no gameplay code touched. **The decision worth recording is not the code, it is the second file.** `telemetry_identity.json` sits beside `player_profile.json` and deliberately outside it. The cheaper move was two new `PlayerProfile` fields, and it was rejected because `PlayerProfileStore.Delete()` is built to know NOTHING about its payload — which is exactly what makes "reset the player" exact rather than a list of fields somebody can forget to clear. Exempting two fields from that delete would end the property for the sake of saving a file. It is also what keeps `Delete Save File` and the new `Reset Game + New Test Player` two genuinely different debug operations instead of one with a flag: the first is a development reset and KEEPS the player, the second hands the device to the next human. The plan's §4 requirement — "resetting local save must never delete the old player's Firebase data" — is therefore structural here rather than a rule someone has to remember, and it is pinned by `DeletingThePlayerProfile_LeavesTheTelemetryIdentityUntouched`, a test that exists specifically to fail the day someone folds the fields back in, because nothing else in the project would notice. **`TelemetryIdentityStore` is shaped after `PlayerProfileStore` with one deliberate divergence: `Load` returns NULL where that class returns defaults.** "No profile" has a correct answer — a new player with authored starting money — so that store can hand one back. "No identity" has none: an installation id cannot be defaulted, only MINTED, and minting is a write. A `Load` that quietly returned a fresh identity would let any caller that forgot to save re-roll the installation on the next launch, and the analytics side would see a hundred single-run devices instead of one device with a hundred runs — with nothing looking broken while it happened. So absence is reported honestly and `LoadOrCreate` is the only path that mints, writing before it returns. **There is no `Delete()` on it at all**, and the absence is load-bearing: erasing an identity orphans every run this device already sent, which is the outcome the whole design exists to prevent. **A playerId is never renamed, only minted** — all three debug commands START a new player. A rename looks tidier and is silently wrong: runs already written keep the id they were written with, so renaming splits one tester across two ids in the report while showing a single name in the panel. Minting splits them too, but says so — the ordinal increments and the log names both ids. **The panel refuses all three commands inside a day**, which is stricter than `Delete Save File` beside it (that one deletes mid-day and merely declines to reload) and is the user's call: changing WHO IS PLAYING underneath a live `GameSession`, a running attempt and — from Step 3 — an open run would attribute half an attempt to each of two testers. It commands owners rather than assigning, the rule D-092 set for this panel: the save goes through `PlayerProfileStore.Delete`, the identity through `PlaytestTelemetry`, so each file keeps exactly one writer and this method knows the ORDER, not the contents. **`PlaytestTelemetry` is static in a project with no singletons**, because its consumer cannot be handed a reference: SRDebugger constructs `SROptions` from a `[RuntimeInitializeOnLoadMethod]` with no scene presence, so D-013's "serialize it, never search the scene" has nothing to serialize onto — and an identity outlives every scene while D-012 leaves this project no persistent one. It caches because SRDebugger POLLS its readouts, so a store built per read would put a `File.ReadAllText` on that path several times a second. It is deliberately dumb and deliberately untested: every rule worth pinning lives in the store, which takes an explicit path and an injected `Func<DateTime>` — the second user of the wall-clock seam `KeyManager` established. The new assembly's `references` array is EMPTY and pre-declares nothing against Step 3's needs, since an unused reference is a dependency arrow that is not real. Tester ids typed by hand are validated and REFUSED rather than repaired: a tester who typed "T 001" and silently got "T001" would believe they entered the first, and the note beside the phone would disagree with the report from then on. affects: ExpoTheExplorer.Systems.TelemetrySystem.asmdef, TelemetryIdentity.cs, TelemetryIdentityStore.cs, TelemetryIds.cs, PlaytestTelemetry.cs, TelemetryIdentityTests.cs, ExpoTheExplorer.Tests.EditMode.asmdef, SROptions.Expo.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, fingerprint.md — 2026-08-31

D-145: **The powerup lesson points with the same arrow as every other tutorial moment, and the frame is gone.** The user, 2026-08-31: "ya bu powerup kullandırtmak için kullandığımız tutorialpowerupspotlightı diğer tutoriallarda kullandığımız okla değiştirmek istiyorum". **The inventory settled it before any code was written**: the project owns exactly ONE arrow, `Art/UI/Arrow.png`, and three of the four tutorial moments already pointed with it -- both `TutorialStepHints` arrows (board item, ticket row) and `MainScreenStoreHintPopup`'s. The powerup press was the only one drawing something else: four coloured bars around the button, breathing between full and 0.35 alpha. So this is not a restyle of one prefab, it is the last holdout joining a gesture the game already had. **The frame did not survive in reduced form** -- the user asked for a replacement, not a second marker, so the `Frame` object, its four bars, its `CanvasGroup`, `pulsePeriodSeconds` and `pulseMinimumAlpha` all left the prefab and the file together, and `frameGroup` left with them. **The new block is `MainScreenTutorialView.AttachArrowTo` line for line, and copying it was the decision**: the two moments are the same problem (an arrow on a button whose size only the running layout knows), and the store-hint version had already paid for two non-obvious lines -- the **pivot forced to centre**, without which the authored rotation swings the arrow about its BASE and moves the whole body an arrow-length sideways, and the **size taken from the button's height** rather than authored, which is the one thing a prefab genuinely cannot know. **No shared helper was extracted**, deliberately: three arrows now exist and all three move differently -- one is reparented into a ticket row and rides that card, one stays on its own canvas and merely marks a board CELL, and this one rides a HUD button -- so a common attacher would have had one caller per branch and hidden the differences that matter. `abstraction-level.md` refuses that trade. **`localRotation` is deliberately not reset**, which is the one line that is NOT a copy of the frame's: the old code flattened the frame to identity because a rectangle has no facing, while an arrow's rotation is the author's only expression of WHERE IT POINTS, and code that normalised it would silently un-author the change this decision exists to enable. **The bounce keeps `SetUpdate(true)` from the pulse it replaces.** That is not an inherited habit: the forced-press step holds the day still, and a tween on scaled time would freeze mid-bounce with it -- the same reasoning the intro panel's fade records, and the opposite of `PopupFade`'s deliberate refusal (D-143), because that helper serves popups in a game with no pause. **Nothing else moved.** `Create(instance, targetButton, message)` keeps its signature, so `PowerupBarView` is untouched; the message half and its own Overlay canvas are untouched (the press step still authors an empty sentence -- D-121 -- so that half stays dormant); `SampleScene`'s prefab slot is a reference to the ASSET and stays valid; and the view still dims nothing, for the reason D-115 gives. **The numbers stay beside the arrow they belong to** -- `arrowScale` and `bouncePeriodSeconds` are serialized on the view, not on `BoardAnimationConfig` -- which is the precedent both neighbours set: `TutorialStepHints` keeps its nudge numbers on its own prefab and `MainScreenTutorialView` keeps `storeArrowScale` on its own view. These are tuned while looking at the arrow, so a shared asset would put the dial in another room. **THE ARROW DOES NOT RIDE THE BUTTON, AND THAT WAS LEARNED THE HARD WAY.** The first version reparented it onto the live button exactly as the frame had been, and in play it was invisible with nothing reporting a thing -- no error, no warning, a wired reference and a clone in the hierarchy. `HUDCanvas` is **Screen Space - Camera at sortingOrder -1**, so everything on it draws UNDER the world sprites; the frame had escaped that only by hugging the button, down in the strip of screen the board never reaches, while an arrow points UPWARD and the first thing it reached was the board's bottom row. This is **D-086 collecting its second victim inside one prefab** -- the same rule that already forced the sentence onto its own Overlay canvas -- and it is why the rule is now worth stating as a CANVAS rule rather than a panel rule: on this scene, anything that must be SEEN above the world belongs on an Overlay canvas, whatever its size. So both halves now sit on that one Overlay canvas and are **positioned over the button through SCREEN space** rather than parented to it, which is the mechanism the sentence had used all along; `TryMeasureButton` serves both, handing back the button's top edge in the canvas's local space and its height in the canvas's units.
