The height is taken in **pixels and divided by the overlay canvas's `scaleFactor`** rather than read off the button's rect, so the two canvases' scalers are free to diverge without silently resizing the arrow. `Bind` opens with a single `Canvas.ForceUpdateCanvases()`, because instantiate and bind share a frame and without it both rects are still the zeroes they were serialized with. **What the fix cost is the one property the reparenting bought**: the arrow no longer rides the button, so a bar that moved mid-step would leave it behind. Nothing moves the bar while a step is armed, and the arrangement that had that property was one nobody could see. **What it removed is a whole failure mode**: nothing is lifted onto another object any more, so the reparent-outlives-its-host trap is gone from this file and `Dismiss` is one `Destroy` plus a tween kill. The store hint keeps its reparented arrow and is now the only one that has to -- the main screen has no world sprites to hide behind. **Verification**: the prefab YAML was hand-written with Unity open, reusing the retired `Top` bar's fileIDs for the new `Arrow` object so no id was invented, and the view's `arrow` slot is wired in the file rather than left for a drag. `dotnet build` on the shipped `Assembly-CSharp.csproj` is worthless here for the reason D-139/D-141 record -- all 61 of its errors are in the `Editor` and `LivesSystem` csprojs it references and it never reaches this file. Verified instead the way D-143 was: a throwaway csproj (deleted afterwards) compiling all 116 files under `Assets/Scripts` and `Assets/Data/DataScripts` against the real reference set with the prebuilt Expo DLLs excluded, where the ONLY errors were the same three plugin-dependent files that graph always reports -- `SROptions.Expo.cs`, `DebugMenuBinder.cs`, `HapticsBinder.cs`, on SRDebugger, InputSystem and NiceVibrations. **Not seen in Unity**: nobody has watched the arrow bounce over a powerup button. 1.35x the button height and a 0.7s bounce are the store arrow's numbers borrowed wholesale, and the arrow's rotation, offset and size are now the author's to judge in the prefab stage -- which is the point of the change. The first version WAS seen in Unity, which is the only reason the canvas trap above is in this entry rather than waiting to be rediscovered. **THE SENTENCE HALF WAS RETIRED IN THE SAME TURN, by the user deleting the `Message` object and saying so** -- "message sildim messagela ilgili şeyleri de kaldır". This view had carried a sentence since it was written and **no step ever authored a line for it**; D-121 kept the plumbing because a step wanting its own words was "one argument away". That is a fair reason to keep something for one day and a bad one to keep it for three, and it is the same argument the frame's own removal settled: dead plumbing waiting for a caller. So `messageText`, `messageGap` and `ShowMessage` are gone, `Create` lost its third parameter, and its single caller lost the `tutorial.CurrentMessage` argument. **`TutorialStep.Message` and `TutorialDirector.CurrentMessage` stay** -- the MOVE steps do author sentences and read them through `TutorialStepHints`; what left is one READER, not the data. The parameter was **deleted rather than defaulted**, because an argument nobody passes is the same dead plumbing under a shorter name. **The canvas outlived the sentence it was built for**, so `messageCanvasRect` became `overlayCanvasRect` and the prefab object `MessageCanvas` became `OverlayCanvas`: it is now the only thing in this prefab that draws above the world, and a field named for the thing it no longer holds is how the next reader gets misled. **Where the arrow rests is authored, and that was a root-invariant fix rather than a convenience.** The first version put it at `0.75x` the arrow's size above the button and bounced it `0.3x` -- two tuning numbers in code, which CLAUDE.md forbids, and the practical cost was exact: the user asked how to move the arrow down and the answer was "you cannot, it is in a `.cs` file". `arrowOffset` (a Vector2 from the middle of the button's top edge, negatives allowed, measuring the arrow's CENTRE) and `bounceDistance` replaced them, in canvas units like `TutorialStepHints`' nudge rather than as multiples, because an author tuning by eye thinks in distance. The user set it to 25 within a minute of it existing, which is the whole argument. affects: TutorialPowerupSpotlightView.cs, TutorialPowerupSpotlight.prefab, PowerupBarView.cs, codemap-ui.md, blueprint.md — 2026-08-31

D-146: **The deferred powerup lesson dims the screen down to the arrow, the powerup and the three timers, and says why.** The user, 2026-08-31: "time resetin dinamik ksımında sadece ok powerupın kendisi ve 3 biletin de süre kısımları dışındaki yerlerin karartılmasını istiyorum ve powerup descriptiondaki gibi bak biletin süresi azaldı kullan bunu gibi bi şey yeazmasını istiyorum". **THE RULE IS THE AUTHORED TRIGGER, NOT THE POWERUP'S NAME.** The dim appears on a press step whose `tutorialUseTrigger` is `TicketPatienceBelow`, which today is exactly Time Reset and tomorrow is whatever is scheduled that way. Writing `if (type == PowerupType.TimeReset)` in a UI file would have put a content decision in the wrong place and silently left the next scheduled powerup out; the trigger already encodes the only thing that matters -- this lesson fires MINUTES after its panel was read, against a board the player is busy with, so it has to say what changed. An AtDayStart press is asked for while the day is still frozen and nothing has moved, and those two lessons are byte-for-byte unchanged. **The mechanism was already in the project, and it was MOVED rather than copied.** `TutorialSpotlightView` had built a camera-sized black sheet at sorting order 500 and lifted the lit pieces over it since the move-step tutorial shipped. Copying that would have meant two copies of three constants whose correctness is that they agree -- the exact duplicate this project deleted twice (D-123 and D-126 each removed a second arrow-texture generator). So `TutorialDim` is a new plain class holding the sheet, `LiftSprites`, `LiftElement`, `Curtain` and the restore, and the old view lost 134 lines with its public API untouched. It is **a class rather than a component** because the sheet is parented to whichever view runs the step -- a view that dies without restoring still takes its own sheet down; what it cannot take down are the sorting orders it borrowed from objects that outlive every step, which is the whole reason the bookkeeping exists. `Build` **returns false** when there is no camera, so a caller can refuse to half-apply a dim: lifts over a sheet that does not exist read as a rendering bug, not a lesson. **What stays lit, and why each one needs a different trick.** The powerup button and the three ticket TIMER TRACKS each get their own sorting scope above the sheet (a `Canvas` with `overrideSorting`), because both live on Screen Space - CAMERA canvases at -1 where the sheet covers them. The **arrow needs nothing at all**: it sits on the spotlight prefab's Overlay canvas, which composites above everything the camera renders -- the property D-145 moved it there for, paying for itself the same day. The timer track is `TicketCardView.TimerTrack`, a new read-only property derived from the divider template's PARENT rather than a fourth serialized field, because that is the expression `RebuildTimerDividers` already treats as the track: two names for one object can disagree, and this way re-parenting the bar moves both. It is the second property on that card that exists purely for the tutorial to point at. **D-115's "IT DELIBERATELY DIMS NOTHING" is sharpened, not reversed.** That rule was never about dimming being wrong here -- it was about not hiding the timer bars, since they are the reason the powerup is being pressed. Dimming everything EXCEPT them says the same thing louder, and the rule's own words are what picked the lit set. **EVERY slot's timer is lit, not just the ticket that tripped the trigger**: the player has to learn to read the bar, three clocks with one of them red teaches that better than one clock, and it means the lesson does not have to know which ticket tripped it -- which is `TutorialDirector`'s business. **The sentence is a READER, not a second plate**, and that came directly from the user asking "bu mesaj işi bölüm başında çıkanlar gibi olacak değil mi". It borrows `TutorialStepHints` off `BoardAnimationConfig` -- the same prefab the day's other tutorial lines use -- shown message-only with `HideArrows()`, which is precisely the palette-not-a-layout property D-126 built it for and its first use of "speak without pointing". So there is one authored look and one authored spot on screen for every in-day sentence, and this view still owns no text object of its own: the `messageText`/`messageGap`/`Message` child deleted hours earlier (D-145) stayed deleted. **What is shared is the plate; the words are not** -- `PowerupSettings.tutorialUseMessage` is per powerup, and the day-start lines live in the Day JSON, so retyping one touches nothing else. **`tutorialUseMessage` is not D-117's deleted field returning.** That one held "Try it now - it drops what the orders still need into the trays" beside a Description saying the same, and deleting it was right. This one describes the MOMENT: "Time is running out on this order. Use Time Reset." is only true while a ticket is red, which Description can never say. The test for drift back into a duplicate is exactly that -- a line that would still read correctly at any other moment of the day belongs in Description or nowhere. It is written straight into `PowerupConfig.asset` for all three powerups (empty for the two AtDayStart ones) so the D-075 stale-asset trap cannot bite. **Every new reference is optional and every failure costs the decoration, never the lesson**: no `BoardAnimationConfig` on the prefab means no dim and no line; no camera means no dim; an unwired `TicketCardsView` means **no dim at all** rather than a dim over dark timers, because hiding the one thing the lesson points at is worse than not dimming. That last reference is a SCENE reference on a component living in the HUDCanvas prefab, so it can only ever be a per-scene override -- the same constraint `gameManager` records there, and the same standing warning about Apply All. **THE TICKET THAT TRIPPED THE STEP IS LIT WHOLE, on the user's follow-up** -- "aslında tetikleyen biletin hem kendisi hem de uyarı sistemi var ya ünlemli falan o da karartılmasın". Lighting the CARD rather than a list of its parts is what made that one line instead of three: `DangerImage`, `Clock` and `TicketTimerBar` are all DIRECT CHILDREN of `TicketCard`, so the exclamation and the clock ride along on the one lift and neither needed a reference of its own. The lit card is deliberately not also handed its bar -- a lift inside a lift is a nested sorting scope that changes nothing and would need explaining forever. The other two slots keep bars only, so the screen reads as "this order is in trouble, and here is how the other two compare" rather than as three lit cards with nothing to choose between. **Nobody knew which ticket it was, and that gap was the interesting part.** `TutorialDirector.NotifyTicketPatienceRatio` is fed a bare fraction on purpose -- its comment says the class "still knows nothing about tickets" -- so the arming moment threw the answer away. It now takes the slot index as well and STORES IT WITHOUT EVER CONSULTING IT, which keeps that property intact: the caller still decides what "closest to running out" means, and an int slot is no more a ticket than `TargetTraySlotIndex` is a tray. `TriggeringTicketSlotIndex` answers -1 outside a deferred step, the same sentinel and the same answered-here-not-at-the-call-site reasoning `SpotlightTraySlotIndex` documents, and it is cleared in `Advance` beside `armed` because two fields describing one step must move together or a second deferred step inherits the first one's ticket for its whole wait. **It is recorded AT THE ARMING, not per tick**, and that is a real behavioural choice rather than an implementation detail: by the time the player reaches for the powerup a different ticket may be the most urgent, and moving the spotlight then would relight the screen under someone who is already looking at the card it lit. `ADeferredUseStep_RemembersTheSlotThatArmedIt` pins both halves. **The new parameter is REQUIRED, not defaulted**, which cost ten edits in `TutorialDirectorTests` and is the same call D-135 made for `givingUpOnAttempt`: a defaulted -1 compiles at every call site and costs the lesson its highlight the first time somebody adds a caller and forgets. `GameManager`'s loop became indexed to carry the slot, since a `Ticket` carries no slot number, and the comment claiming it "needs no memory of which ticket it was" was corrected rather than left to rot. **A NESTED SORTING CANVAS TOOK THE BUTTON'S CLICKS AWAY, and it is worth recording because nothing reported it.** The user, minutes after the dim first ran: "ya tıklayamıyorum ben butona bozuldu". Unity registers every `Graphic` against its NEAREST enabled `Canvas` ancestor, and a `GraphicRaycaster` only ever tests the graphics registered to its OWN canvas -- so the `Canvas` added to the powerup button for sorting moved it out of `HUDCanvas`'s raycaster's sight and the one thing the player was allowed to press went dead. The button still drew and still sat above the dim, so the step became impossible in exactly the way a broken game looks, with no log anywhere. **The lift is now two methods**, `LiftElement` for display-only things and `LiftInteractiveElement`, which adds a raycaster beside the canvas. The split is the honest shape: whether a lifted thing is pressable is a property of the thing, not a detail of one call site. The comment the fix replaced had claimed sorting order "does not touch" clicks -- written when the method had only ever lifted display-only ticket cards, so the claim had never once been exercised. **That is the lesson worth keeping from this entry: a mechanism moved to a second caller has its unstated assumptions tested for the first time**, and this one carried them in a comment instead of a guard. **Teardown order is load-bearing**: raycasters are destroyed before canvases, since `GraphicRaycaster` carries `[RequireComponent(typeof(Canvas))]`, and each canvas's `overrideSorting` is cleared before its own destroy, so a refused removal leaves an inert leftover rather than an element parked above a dim that no longer exists. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on the shipped csprojs is untrustworthy here for the reason D-139/D-141 record. Verified as D-143 and D-145 were: a throwaway csproj (deleted afterwards) compiling **151 files -- every gameplay and data source plus every EditMode test** except the three that need the Editor assembly this graph excludes on purpose, and earlier the same run over all 117 non-test files under `Assets/Scripts` and `Assets/Data/DataScripts` against the real reference set with the prebuilt Expo DLLs excluded -- the only errors were the same three plugin-dependent files that graph always reports (`SROptions.Expo.cs`, `DebugMenuBinder.cs`, `HapticsBinder.cs`, on SRDebugger, InputSystem and NiceVibrations). `check_blueprint.py`: 0 errors, 7 warnings, identical to session start. The codemap's stale count went from 36 to 29, because the seven lines this touched were repaired. **Not seen in Unity**: nobody has watched the dim. The two things to check in play are the move-step tutorial, which the extraction touched, and whether 0.72 opacity leaves the three timer bars reading as the brightest thing on screen. affects: TutorialDim.cs, TutorialSpotlightView.cs, TutorialPowerupSpotlightView.cs, PowerupBarView.cs, TicketCardView.cs, TutorialDirector.cs, TutorialDirectorTests.cs, GameManager.cs, PowerupConfig.cs, PowerupConfig.asset, TutorialPowerupSpotlight.prefab, codemap-ui.md, codemap-core.md, blueprint.md — 2026-08-31

D-147: **Time Reset rescues ONE order — the one closest to running out — and the lesson dims down to that same card.** The user, 2026-08-31: "ya bi değişiklik yapıcaz time reset powerupı artık sadece süresi en az olan biletin süresini sıfırlayacak buna göre hem açıklamasını değiştirt hem de bu diğer biletlerin sürelerini de karart artık". **Refilling all three had made it the powerup with no wrong moment to press**: three clocks for one charge, best value whenever the board happened to be busy, and never a decision. One clock makes it a rescue -- you spend it on the order you are about to lose -- and that is a real change to what the player is choosing between, not a nerf dressed as a tweak. **"CLOSEST TO RUNNING OUT" IS THE EMPTIEST BAR, NOT THE FEWEST SECONDS**, and this was put to the user as a question because the two genuinely disagree: an Impatient ticket holding 20 of its 45 seconds (0.44) is in less trouble than a Patient one holding 40 of its 150 (0.27), even though the Patient one has twice the seconds. The user chose the fraction, which is the definition this project ALREADY means by urgency everywhere the player can see it -- it turns the timer bar orange and red, it sets the tip tier (CLAUDE.md, Tip Tiers), and it fires the tutorial's deferred trigger. A seconds rule would have made Time Reset save a ticket other than the red one the player is watching, which is the exact failure the whole change exists to avoid. **The rule is spelled once.** `PowerupEffects.MostUrgentActiveTicketSlot` is public, and its three readers are the powerup (what to refill), `GameManager`'s patience feed (when the lesson arms) and the lesson's lit set (which card stays bright). That sharing is the point rather than a tidy-up: a hand-rolled loop in `GameManager` had computed the same thing, and two loops agreeing by coincidence would let one edit make the lesson dim the screen down to a ticket the press does not save. Consolidating also fixed a quiet difference -- the old loop skipped resolved tickets only by way of a null check, while the shared helper skips DELIVERED and CANCELLED ones, which a mid-cascade array can still be holding. **The name changed with the behaviour**: `ResetActiveTicketTimers` became `ResetMostUrgentTicketTimer`, because a method that still said "Timers" while touching one is the kind of stale label this project has had to correct twice in the same week. **What deliberately did NOT change**: the refill is still to that ticket's OWN authored `TimeLimitSeconds` (a flat "+30s" would rewrite per-Day patience balancing from a powerup); this is still the only place `Ticket.RemainingSeconds` moves BACKWARD, the third-writer boundary the file documents; and no-work-costs-no-charge still holds, now reached by asking about one ticket instead of three -- with three full tickets the most urgent one is simply already full. **The lesson's lit set shrank to one card** for the same reason the description changed. The other two slots' timer bars had been kept bright on the argument that a red bar needs something to be compared against -- true, but that argument belonged to a powerup that refilled all three. Lighting a bar this press will not touch promises something the charge does not deliver, and the staging has to say what the powerup does. **The description is authored, and one edit covered both places it appears**: `PowerupConfig.asset`'s `timeReset.description` went from "Every waiting order gets its full time back." to "Refills the clock on the order closest to running out.", read by the shop panel and by the tutorial's intro panel alike. `CLAUDE.md`'s locked-rules line was rewritten too, since "refreshes active ticket timers" is now false. **Balance is flagged, not silently retuned**: this makes Time Reset strictly weaker while its authored `gemCost` (10) and `startingCharges` (1) stand where they were. Pricing is the user's call and was reported rather than adjusted. **Verification**: a throwaway csproj (deleted afterwards) compiling 151 files -- every gameplay and data source plus every EditMode test except the three needing the Editor assembly that graph excludes on purpose -- against the real reference set with the prebuilt Expo DLLs excluded. The only errors were the three plugin-dependent files that graph always reports. The nine Time Reset tests were rewritten rather than patched, and the two that carry the change are `RefillsOnlyTheEmptiestBar` and `MeasuresUrgencyAsAFractionOfEachTicketsOwnLimit`, the second of which fails the moment anybody "simplifies" the rule to fewest-seconds. `check_blueprint.py`: 0 errors, 7 warnings, unchanged from session start. **Not seen in Unity**: nobody has pressed the new Time Reset, and the tutorial that teaches it is still unable to dim, because `PowerupBarView.ticketCardsView` is an unwired scene reference (D-146's outstanding drag). affects: PowerupEffects.cs, GameManager.cs, PowerupBarView.cs, PowerupEffectsTests.cs, PowerupConfig.asset, CLAUDE.md, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-31

D-148: **Firebase is in, it reaches Firestore from the Editor, and the two risks the telemetry plan was most worried about are closed by measurement rather than argument.** Step 1 of `.claude/telemetry-plan.md`: Firebase project `expo-the-explorer` (Spark/free tier, `eur3`), Anonymous Auth enabled, Firestore in **production mode** with the plan's §M.1 rules published — `runs/{runId}` accepts a create/update whose `authUid` matches the caller and whose `environment` is a string, `read` and `delete` are `false` for every client, and everything outside `runs` is denied. **Google Analytics was declined at project creation and `FirebaseAnalytics.unitypackage` was not imported**, deliberately: it auto-collects device model, OS, language, IP-derived country, screen size and — on Android — the Advertising ID, and sends `first_open`/`session_start` on its own, all of which contradicts the plan's §27 privacy stance. Imported: **Auth and Firestore only** (SDK 13.16.0). There is no longer a `FirebaseApp.unitypackage` to import first — the core ships inside every product package, and `FirebaseAppCheck` is a different product (abuse attestation), not the core. **The smoke test passed all three steps on the real machine**: dependencies available, anonymous sign-in returned a UID, and a rules-compliant document reached `runs/`. That closes **O.1** (Unity 6.3 against an SDK tested on older editors) and **O.2** (.NET Standard 2.1 with Firestore, historically the profile that breaks) — O.2 mattered most because the fix would have been an api-compatibility change affecting the whole project, and it is now off the table. It also proves the RULES, since the write had to satisfy them to land. **The Android app was registered in the console even though this machine has no Android Build Support**, and that turned out to be load-bearing rather than a hedge: Firebase generates `StreamingAssets/google-services-desktop.json` — the file that lets it initialise in the Editor — from `google-services.json`, so an iOS-only registration would very likely have failed the smoke test at step 1. That generated file is the project's **first and only** `StreamingAssets/` entry, which the blueprint's own note says must be a recorded decision rather than a quiet addition; it is accepted on two conditions, that nothing hand-written joins it and that it stays one generated file. **Two macOS obstacles were hit and are worth recording because anyone repeating this setup will hit both.** First, EDM4U tried to install CocoaPods through the system Ruby 2.6 and failed building a native gem extension (`ruby/config.h` missing from the current Xcode SDK) — a macOS breakage, not a Firebase one; the fix is `brew install cocoapods` (1.17.0 installed), and it matters only for real iOS device builds, not for the Editor. Note Unity launched from the Dock may not see `/opt/homebrew/bin`, so it wants a full restart before the first device build. Second, **Gatekeeper quarantined all three native `.bundle` files** (`FirebaseCppApp-13_16_0`, `FirebaseCppAuth`, `FirebaseCppFirestore`) because Google does not notarize them, and Unity's dialog offers "Move to Trash" — which would delete the SDK's natives. The fix is `xattr -r -d com.apple.quarantine` scoped to `Assets/Firebase/Plugins/x86_64`, then a full Unity restart, because Gatekeeper caches its refusal per process. The bundles are universal (`x86_64 arm64`) despite the folder name, so Apple Silicon needs nothing further. **The smoke test itself writes into the real `runs` collection, not a scratch one** — a correction to the plan, which predated the rules: a write to `smoke_test/` would have been refused by the rules and would have proven nothing about connectivity, whereas a rules-compliant write tests SDK, auth and rules together. Its row is tagged `environment: "smoketest"` so it can be filtered or deleted, and `FirebaseSmokeTest.cs` is deleted in Step 4. **THE SDK IS NOT COMMITTED, and that was forced rather than preferred**: it is 250 MB over 148 files and `FirebaseCppApp-13_16_0.bundle` alone is 106 MB — past GitHub's 100 MB hard limit, so committing it would make the repo unpushable the day a remote is added (there is none today) and unpicking it from history afterwards means a rewrite. So the SDK is ignored and re-imported on a fresh clone, while the three CONFIG files ARE committed: they are ~4 KB, they are this project's Firebase identity, and they are client config rather than secrets. Ignoring it needed edits in BOTH .gitignore files, which is worth stating because the second one is easy to miss: the Unity-level `ExpoTheExplorer/.gitignore` carries `!/[Aa]ssets/**/*.meta`, a negation that force-keeps every meta under Assets and therefore beat the repo-root rules for the SDK's FOLDER metas — leaving a fresh clone holding metas for folders that do not exist, which Unity deletes on import and dirties the tree before anyone has done anything. The SDK also scatters files outside `Assets/Firebase/` (`Plugins/iOS`, `Plugins/tvOS`, `Editor Default Resources/Firebase`), and those are ignored too on the principle that half a committed SDK is worse than none. The re-import recipe — including "do not import FirebaseAnalytics" and the `xattr` quarantine command — lives in the .gitignore comment itself, where someone hitting a broken fresh clone will actually look. affects: FirebaseSmokeTest.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md, Assets/Firebase/ (vendor), Assets/ExternalDependencyManager/ (vendor), Assets/StreamingAssets/google-services-desktop.json (generated), ProjectSettings.asset (iOS bundle identifier com.karanaka.expotheexplorer, companyName Karanaka) — 2026-08-31

D-149: **A Day attempt becomes a RUN, and `failed` stops being a guess.** Step 3 of `.claude/telemetry-plan.md`: a run opens when a Day attempt starts, accumulates in RAM, snapshots every 15 seconds, and finalizes as `completed`/`failed`/`quit` — all of it through `ITelemetrySink`, which in this step is `LogTelemetrySink` and prints to the Console. No Firebase; Step 4 swaps the sink and changes nothing else. **The whole decision is where `failed` comes from**, because the plan's first revision got it wrong and the user caught it. Three candidate sources were tried and all three are wrong, which is why the reasoning is written into `DayAttemptEnd` itself rather than left here: (1) *"LivesDepleted means failed"* — it does not, because a paid Continue refills lives and the SAME attempt carries on, so losing every life is an event INSIDE a run that can happen more than once and can happen in a run that ends `completed`; (2) *"read `IsAwaitingContinue` when the run is finalized"* — by then it is **always false**, because `LivesManager.RefillLivesAndResume` clears the flag BEFORE refilling (D-103) and every terminal path calls it, so this was not occasionally wrong but universally wrong, and would have stamped `quit` on every genuinely lost day; (3) *"use `RetryDay`'s `givingUpOnAttempt`"* — it answers a different question, "does this cost a key", and both the Game Over popup and the settings menu pass `true` while only one of them is a loss. So the fact is captured **where it is still true**: `GameState.DayAttemptEnded` is published as the FIRST statement of `GameManager.RetryDay` and `ReturnToMainScreenAbandoningDay`, carrying `DayAttemptEnd.Lost` or `.GivenUp` read from `IsAwaitingContinue` at that instant. **That position is the contract**, and the codemap, the blueprint and the two call sites all say so, because moving either publish below `RefillForNewDay` breaks it silently. `GameState.DaySessionStarted` is the other half, published from the END of `DayLifecycleManager.ResetForNewDay` — the one place all four day-start paths already pass through, which matters because they agree on nothing else (`RetryDay` publishes `DayRetried`, `AdvanceToNextDay` publishes only `CurrentDayIndexChanged`, and `RetryCompletedDay` publishes **nothing**). Last statement, so the counters above are already zeroed when it goes out. **Total gameplay surface: two event properties, one enum, three publish lines, and no subscriber outside telemetry.** **A deviation from the plan's own §J table, taken deliberately and recorded here:** that table had the binder subscribe to `TicketDelivered`, `TicketCancelled` and `TraySlotScatterBegin` and keep its own tallies. It does not. `DayLifecycleManager` already counts deliveries, timeouts and wrong deliveries and is their single authority, so `TelemetryBinder` READS them at snapshot time — three fewer subscriptions, no second tally free to drift, and one unchecked assumption dropped (that `TraySlotScatterBegin` fires only on wrong deliveries). The ordering this depends on was verified: `DayAttemptEnded` fires before `ResetForNewDay` zeroes anything, so a finalize always reads the attempt's real numbers. `livesDepletedCount` is the one figure telemetry counts itself, because `DayLifecycleManager` does not keep it and should not — running out of lives is not a day failure. **`OnDestroy` and `OnApplicationPause` flush but never assign a status**, on purpose: if a run is still `in_progress` there, nothing in the game ever said how it ended, and "in_progress with a stale timestamp" is the honest answer analytics turns into an abandonment. Writing `quit` there would put a confident word on a guess and make a real quit indistinguishable from a crash. The first run is opened in `TelemetryBinder.Start` rather than by the event, because `DaySessionStarted` for that Day already fired inside `GameManager.Awake` — waiting for it would miss every scene's opening run and record only its retries. `ITelemetrySink` was added HERE and not in Step 2, which is exactly the condition Step 2's preflight set for it: it now has two implementations, a test fake, and a job as the Firebase boundary. The `TelemetrySystem` assembly's `references` array is **still empty** — nothing in it knows what a `GameState` is, which is what keeps it testable. affects: DayAttemptEnd.cs, GameState.cs, DayLifecycleManager.cs, GameManager.cs, RunStatus.cs, RunTelemetryState.cs, ITelemetrySink.cs, LogTelemetrySink.cs, PlaytestTelemetry.cs, TelemetryIds.cs, TelemetryBinder.cs, RunTelemetryStateTests.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-31

D-150: **The runs reach Firestore, and the swap cost exactly what the seam promised: two new files and one deletion.** Step 4 of `.claude/telemetry-plan.md`. `FirebaseBootstrap` brings Firebase up once per process and installs `FirestoreTelemetrySink`; `PlaytestTelemetry.Sink` gains the single writer its own Step 3 note had already named. **Nothing else moved** — not `TelemetryBinder`, not `RunTelemetryState`, not the three gameplay publish lines from D-149 — which is the result `ITelemetrySink` was created for and the evidence that Step 3's seam was drawn in the right place. If this step had needed the run state or the interface to change, that would have been proof the abstraction was wrong. **`FirestoreTelemetrySink` is the only file in the project that names `FirebaseFirestore`**, and it sits in `Assembly-CSharp` because the SDK's DLLs are auto-referenced by the predefined assembly and by no asmdef; referencing them from one would mean `overrideReferences: true` plus naming every Firebase DLL in `precompiledReferences`, redone on every SDK update. So after four steps the `TelemetrySystem` assembly's `references` array is **still empty**. **Two write-shaping decisions carry the whole schema's honesty.** `startedAt` is written only on a run's FIRST write, tracked in a `HashSet<string>` of run ids: every write is `SetAsync(MergeAll)`, so re-sending it each heartbeat would push the run's start time forward every 15 seconds and make every run in the report look instantaneous — a bug that would have produced plausible, wrong numbers rather than an error. `completedAt` is written only when the status is `completed`; a `failed` or `quit` run leaves it null and is read through `lastUpdatedAt`, which its terminal write has just set — so "when did this end" is answerable for every run while "when was it finished" stays true of only the ones that were. `authUid` and all three timestamps are added by the SINK, never by `RunTelemetryState`, which has no Firebase reference and must not gain one; two Step 3 tests (`DoesNotCarryAuthUid`, `DoesNotCarryTimestamps`) exist purely to keep that boundary. **Failure is a supported outcome rather than an error path.** Dependency failure, auth failure and write failure each degrade to the Console sink, `WriteRun` cannot throw, and there is no retry — the next heartbeat resends the whole document, which makes a lost write self-healing without a queue, a backoff or a background job (plan §14). `FirebaseBootstrap` reuses an existing anonymous session when the SDK restored one, so the usual launch costs no round trip, and it deliberately never re-signs-in on "New Test Player": that would strand an unusable account per reset and put an async wait in the middle of handing a phone to the next tester. **The debug panel gained four readouts and a Force Snapshot button, and they earn their place on a specific argument**: a telemetry failure is silent by design — the game keeps playing and the Console sink keeps working — and on a DEVICE there is no Console at all, so without them the honest answer to "is this recording?" is "find out tomorrow". `Writes OK / Failed` is the diagnostic pair: a climbing failure count while `Backend` still reads `Ready` means the rules are rejecting the document, not that the connection is down, and the two need opposite fixes. Force Snapshot ships the run as the last heartbeat left it, and that limitation is stated in the code rather than hidden: refreshing the counters first is `TelemetryBinder`'s job and the panel has no reference to it, because giving it one would mean the scene lookup D-013 rules out. **`FirebaseSmokeTest.cs` was deleted in this turn**, along with its codemap line, exactly as its own note had scheduled in D-148 — the line's survival past this step was designed to be the tripwire for forgetting the file, so leaving it would have been the failure it was written to catch. affects: FirebaseBootstrap.cs, FirestoreTelemetrySink.cs, FirebaseSmokeTest.cs (DELETED), SROptions.Expo.cs, blueprint.md, codemap-core.md — 2026-08-31

D-151: **While a bought prop is landing, the whole screen is deaf.** The user, 2026-08-31: "bu satın alma sırasında animasyon bitene kadar input almasın, oyun hiç bi şeyte dokunulmaz olsun taa ki animasyon bitsin". `MetaGroundsView.RefreshAfterPurchase` now raises a transparent, full-screen, tap-eating `Image` the moment it claims the framing, and drops it the frame `IsSettlingPurchase` goes false — so the block covers the entire payoff (the drop, the shake, the confetti hold and the map's journey back out) and not one beat of it more. **It asks the question the shop already asks.** `MetaShopView.ReopenWhenTheGroundsSettle` has polled that same property since D-096; reusing it means the block lifts and the bottom sheet returns in the same frame by construction, where a duration of its own would have been a fifth number to keep in step with the four this animation is already made of — and D-134 had just moved one of them. **The blocker gets its OWN overlay Canvas at `short.MaxValue`, and that is the entire decision.** The obvious implementation was `CreateSkipCatcher`, which has sat in this file since D-041 — but it is parented to the grounds' Canvas, which is correct for its own job and wrong for this one: `MainScreen.unity` also holds `HUDCanvas`, `PowerupShop` and `NoKeysPopup` as separate Canvases, so a catcher inside the grounds' hierarchy would have left the HUD's powerup buttons, and the shop behind them, live while the prop was in the air. "The meta screen is untouchable" and "the game is untouchable" are different promises, and the user asked for the second. It is a ROOT `GameObject` for the same reason — parented under this screen it would be back inside the one canvas it exists to outrank. **Deliberately NOT skippable**, which is the other difference from the catcher next to it: that one lets a player hurry three unlock animations along, this one exists so a payoff cannot be cut short by a stray tap — including the tap the player was already making when they pressed BUY. A blocker that outlives its animation is the one failure here the player could not get out of, so three things end it: the poll's `finally`, `OnDisable` (the block is a root object, so nothing about this component being torn down would take it with it), and a second purchase stopping the first routine before raising a fresh one. Neither half leans on Unity disposing a stopped iterator — `RaiseInputBlock` drops whatever is standing first — and nothing is disabled and no mode flag is set, so an object that somehow died early would leave the game simply playable again rather than in a state someone has to clear. Ruled out on the way: the powerup shop, whose purchase has no coroutine and no tween and therefore no animation to wait out; and a shared "input gate" component, which would abstract a rule with one caller. **Verification**: `dotnet build` on the Unity csproj (compile only). The thing to look at is a playtest — buy a prop and hammer Play, the market button and the HUD's powerup buttons while it lands. affects: MetaGroundsView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-08-31

D-152: **The guaranteed-ticket required pool counts a ticket's tray as well as the board — but only its OWN tray.** The user, 2026-09-01: "garanti bilet sisteminde sadece boarda bakmasın traydeki malzemeleri de hesaba dahil etsin". BoardDistributor.SpawnMissingRequiredItems compared its needed multiset against CountItemsOnBoard() alone, and an item the player has dragged into a tray has LEFT the board — so every later round in that ticket's life re-read the requirement as unmet and spawned a duplicate on top of what the player had already banked, steadily clogging the board with food nobody needed. Each guaranteed ticket's needs are now reduced by what its own tray slot holds, consumed per requirement (two of a combo with one banked still needs the second), keyed through the same RequiredItemKey the board is counted through so a plain burger never satisfies a modified one. Deliberately NOT a single "board + all trays" pool: an item in ticket A's tray is committed to A and unavailable to B, so a global pool would under-spawn and leave B permanently short — the same failure TrayManager.TryAddItem's onAccepted ordering exists to prevent, and the way back into D-044's unrecoverable position. The board therefore stays the only shared pool. Safe against the guarantee itself, since TryAddItem runs the batch check the instant a tray fills, so a tray can never hold a complete correct set and the credit can never zero an unfinished ticket's need — "at least one active ticket is completable" still holds by construction, now with the tray counted as part of completable. Wired as an optional trayContentsForSlot ctor delegate (GameManager passes `slot => TrayManager?.GetContents(slot)`, read live rather than snapshotted, same argument as TrayContentsSnapshot's comment); omitting it means every tray reads empty, which is the pre-D-152 behavior and the honest default for the EditMode tests and the tray-less main screen. Systems.BoardDistribution keeps referencing only Core and Data — no asmdef change, no new dependency arrow, no new balancing field, no Day JSON or .asset change — rationale: the requirement was always "the minimum set needed to make ACTIVE tickets completable" (GDD Section 4), and a ticket holding two of its three items in the tray needs one item, not three; counting only the board answered a question the player had already partly solved — affects: BoardDistributor.cs, GameManager.cs, BoardDistributionTests.cs — 2026-09-01

D-153: **A LOCATION opening is celebrated the way a PROP opening is.** The user, 2026-09-01: "bu yeni prop açılınca fryer ve drink fridgeteki gibi yeni metaya girince de kutlama ve popup yapabilir miyiz". The prop half of that ask needed no code at all — D-128 already made the celebration data-driven, so a new Day-unlocked prop earns the fill-reveal, the Success buzz, the confetti and the popup the moment someone authors its `unlockMessage` (`drinkFridge` and `fryer` have one; `saucesStand` does not, and opens silently, which is the opt-in working). The LOCATION half was missing entirely, and missing in the way that is hardest to notice: `TryStartCelebration` asked only `DayUnlocksBetween`, which is a query about a location's ITEMS, and `Meta2` opens on day 15 with zero items authored — so an empty list returned, the early return fired, and the player was set down on brand-new grounds in total silence. `MetaResolver.OpenedBetween` is now asked alongside it: the location-sized version of the same question, on the same half-open `(since, current]` window and the same `GameSession.LastCelebratedDayIndex` marker, so the two cannot come to disagree about which day boundary has been paid out — and the day-0 starting location, whose window never opens, is silent by construction rather than by a special case. `MetaLocation` gained the same `unlockMessage`/`unlockImage` pair `MetaItemDefinition` carries, spelling `HasUnlockPopup` the same way, which is what lets the whole thing reuse `MetaUnlockPopup` — no second prefab, no second opt-in rule; `UnlockImage` falls back to `backgroundSprite`, the one image every location is guaranteed to have. `CelebrateLocation` runs FIRST, before any prop, because "you have arrived somewhere new" is the frame the props that follow stand inside; announcing a fryer and only then the whole location tells the story backwards. It is deliberately NOT a second reveal: there is no silhouette to fill and nothing to zoom in on, since the subject is the entire grounds at the default framing `Refresh` has already drawn — so the beat is precisely the two things a prop's reveal ENDS with (buzz, confetti), then the popup. It reuses `HapticMoment.PropUnlocked` rather than authoring a moment, because the haptic vocabulary answers "what did this feel like" and not "what was it": a location opening feels like a prop opening (gradual, unlike `PropLanded`'s impact), and a second enum entry mapped to the same Success preset would be a distinction no thumb can feel. The opt-in is checked in `TryStartCelebration`, not inside the coroutine, because unlike a prop a message-less location has nothing to fall back on — starting a celebration for it would raise the input block and hold the screen to show nothing. Nothing new is persisted: the marker that already gated the prop queue gates this too, and it is still written once, after the whole queue finishes, so an interrupted arrival replays rather than being swallowed.

D-154: **The meta grounds say how far through buying a location the player is, and how many days the next one is away.** The user's ask, 2026-09-02: "metaya progress bar eklemek istiyorum satın alımlarda ne kadar ilerlendiğine dair. satın alınan ürün / bütün satın alınabilir ürünler", then "üstünde de sonraki mekana şu kadar gün şeklinde bi yazı olsa". Scope was confirmed before any code: PER-LOCATION rather than expo-wide, and in the grounds location bar rather than the shop sheet — both chosen by the user against the alternatives, and both follow the screen, since the shop, the grounds and the prev/next walk are all already per-location. Mechanically: `MetaPurchase` gains a `MetaPurchaseProgress` readonly struct (`Owned`, `Total`, `Fraction`, `HasOffers`, `IsComplete`) and a `Progress(location, ownedKeys)` that fills it; `MetaGroundsView.DrawLocationBar` gains the owned-key set as a parameter and a `DrawPurchaseProgress` half, plus three optional references (root, fill Image, TMP label) and four tuning fields. Four decisions worth keeping visible. (1) **Area-locked props are IN the denominator, and this is the whole shape of the measure.** `ShopItems` deliberately hides a prop whose area is not owned (D-019, the user's 2026-08-21 instruction), so a denominator taken from that list would SHRINK by one the moment the Square went through — the bar would travel backwards on the frame the player did the most expensive right thing available to them. A completion bar that can fall reads as a bug and no correctness in the numbers repairs how it looks; the cost, named rather than hidden, is that a fresh location shows a low fraction against props it cannot offer yet. It is pinned by `Progress_AreaLockedProp_IsCountedSoTheBarCannotGoBackwards`, which asserts the empty shop and the unchanged total in the same test so the two can never quietly converge. (2) **Day-unlocked props are in NEITHER half.** Their keys never enter the save file, so one could never be counted as owned — in the denominator alone it would put 100% permanently out of reach, and a bar that cannot fill is worse than no bar. (3) **Only a purchase animates the fill; opening the screen and walking between locations SNAP.** The flag is the `placing` that `RefreshAfterPurchase` already claims before it redraws (D-049), so there is no second piece of state to keep in step. Walking must not animate for a reason that is design rather than performance: travelling from Meta1's 8/12 down to Meta2's 0/3 would show the player their progress draining away over half a second. And a bar that fills itself on every screen open turns the payoff of an actual purchase into background noise. (4) **The locked hint became a COUNTDOWN, reusing the label that was already there.** "Unlocks on Day 12" became "Next expo in 3 days" — the same moment described, but the player knows what it costs them without first recalling which day they are on, and the number now falls on its own as they play. No `+1` survives: it is a difference between two day indices, so the catalog-position-to-player-facing-day conversion cancels. The two sentences are serialized fields rather than literals, against the rest of Scripts/UI's habit of inlining them, on the ground that a caption the user has ALREADY reworded once is exactly the one that belongs in the Inspector — retyping it in Turkish is now an Inspector edit. NOTE **no editor builder was added**, deliberately: fe48bff deleted `MetaGroundsSetup` and `MainScreenSceneBuilder` as one-shot builders, so the three objects are authored by hand and dragged in, and the new header says so rather than naming a menu step that no longer exists — which is the trap the class's own "Bound by ExpoTheExplorer > Meta > Build Meta Grounds" header still sets for the next reader. NOTE `StarScoreBarView` was considered for reuse and rejected: its `Prepare`/`BeginFill`/`PositionOf` vocabulary is the day-complete star payout and it travels from EMPTY, where this must travel from the value already on screen; its DOTween/Ease/`KillFill` shape is copied instead of its class. NOTE verification is compilation plus seven new EditMode tests — the tests were not RUN, because the Unity Editor held the project lock and a batch-mode run would have risked the Library — affects: MetaPurchase.cs, MetaGroundsView.cs, MetaSystemTests.cs, codemap-core.md, codemap-ui.md — 2026-09-02

D-155: **An area gate asks whether the area is STANDING THERE, not whether it was bought.** The user, 2026-09-02, on the bar D-154 had just shipped: "ya bitirdim satın alımları her şeyi aldım ama 14/15 kaldı böyle bir bug var". Root cause, found by parsing MetaCatalog.asset rather than by reading code: `meta1` authors 15 purchasable props, and one of them — `tent`, 100 coins — sits behind `requiresAreaId: fryer`, while `fryer` is a **DayUnlock** prop (opens day index 5) with `unlocksArea: 1` ticked. `MetaResolver.IsAreaSatisfied` asked `IsOwned` of the area, and a Day-unlocked prop's key NEVER enters the save file (D-019's own rule), so the gate could never be satisfied: the tent never reached the shop and never could. The player had bought all 14 reachable props and was stuck one short. **The bar did not cause this; it made it visible** — before D-154 an unreachable prop was simply a prop nobody ever saw, and nothing on screen counted it. Fix: `IsAreaSatisfied` gains `currentDayIndex` and asks whether the area prop is PRESENT — owned for a Purchase area, day-reached for a DayUnlock one — which is the predicate the authoring already assumed. Four decisions worth keeping visible. (1) **The rules changed, not the catalog.** `fryer.unlocksArea: 1` beside `tent.requiresAreaId: fryer` is coherent intent — "the fryer opening on Day 6 puts the tent up for sale" — and rewiring the tent to `building` (the cheap one-line asset fix, offered and declined) would have contradicted the authoring and left `unlocksArea` a dead flag on every Day-unlocked prop, ready to lay the same trap again. (2) **The gate now walks the WHOLE chain, not one link**, which is stricter than what it replaced: tent → fryer → building, so an unbought building keeps the tent locked rather than selling the player something to stand beside a fryer that is not drawn. (3) **The walk is ITERATIVE with a step cap, and that is a correctness requirement rather than a style choice.** The natural spelling — ask `IsActive` of the area, which asks this of its area — recurses, and a catalog authoring a CYCLE (A behind B's area, B behind A's) would overflow the stack and take the game down. `MetaCatalogValidator` rejects a prop requiring its OWN area and nothing longer, and the user chose not to add a validator rule for this class today, so the guard has to live in the predicate: the cap is `location.Items.Count`, since a chain that repeats no prop cannot be longer, and it costs a counter rather than the HashSet a visited-set would allocate on every prop of every redraw. (4) **`MetaPurchase.Progress` was NOT given a day parameter.** It counts every purchasable prop and never asks about areas, so D-154's "only a purchase moves this measure" survives intact — and the denominator stays 15 before and after the fryer opens, which is exactly why the bar does not jump. NOTE the five call sites (IsActive, NextDayUnlock, DayUnlocksBetween, Evaluate, ShopItems) all already had `currentDayIndex` in hand, so nothing new is plumbed. NOTE `IsActive` was split: the "has it arrived by its own rule" half became `StandsOnItsOwnTerms`, which is what the area walk calls so it never re-enters `IsActive`. NOTE verification: all three assemblies compile, five new EditMode tests were added (the fryer case, the bought-area no-change case, the chain, the cycle, plus two signature updates), and the new rule was SIMULATED against the real MetaCatalog.asset — day 0-4 leaves the tent blocked at 14/15, day 5 onward reaches 15/15. The tests were not RUN: the Unity Editor held the project lock — affects: MetaResolver.cs, MetaPurchase.cs, MetaSystemTests.cs, codemap-core.md — 2026-09-02

D-156: **A tray only accepts an item some gesture actually picked up.** The user, 2026-09-02, on Day 0's forced-move tutorial: "hardallı olan hotdogu ... alıp sürüklersem traye yanlış verdin diyip o hotdogu başka bi yere atıyor ... sonra hata veriyor 2,2de hotdog yok bu tutorialı çalıştırmıycam ... sadece highlightlı sürüklenebilmeli". Three reported symptoms, ONE cause. `BoardItemDragHandler.OnPointerDown` refuses a press on any cell the armed step does not name and sets `pickupRefused`, which correctly stops `OnBeginDrag`/`OnDrag`/`OnEndDrag`/`OnPointerUp` — the item never lifts and never follows the finger. **It cannot stop the tray, and the reason is an ordering fact about UGUI: `eventData.pointerDrag` is assigned AFTER `pointerDown` has been dispatched**, so nothing this class does inside its own refusal can un-nominate it as the gesture's drag object. Release over a tray and `WorldTrayView.OnDrop` reads that same `pointerDrag`, finds a valid handler holding a valid item, and `TryAcceptDrop` — which asked about the item, the delivery lock and `IsTrayDropAllowed(slot)` — never asked the one question that mattered. So on Day 0: pressing the mustard hotdog at (2,0) and releasing over tray 0 (**the step's own target tray, so the tutorial's tray gate passed**) delivered it; ticket 1 wants a plain hotdog, so the batch check failed, and the wrong-order scatter emptied (2,0) — which is step 2's source cell, so `EnsureCurrentTutorialStepIsPossible` aborted the tutorial on the next step change ("expects an item at cell (2,0)"). With the tutorial gone every gate opens, which is the third symptom: the OTHER plain hotdog at (3,2) then delivered as correct. Fix: `BoardItemDragHandler` exposes `WasPickupRefused`, and `TryAcceptDrop` refuses on it **above** the tutorial gate, since "was this picked up" is the more basic question. **The flag is per-GESTURE state that outlives its gesture, so only the finger may read it** — `respectTutorialGate` is renamed `fromPlayerDrag` and now guards both checks, because Auto-Collect makes no gesture and a stale `true` (left by a press refused under a popup) would silently make that item un-collectable forever. **`TutorialDirector` was not touched**: its rules were correct throughout, which is why reading them proved nothing — the hole was in who was allowed to ignore them. **Deliberately NOT fixed by disabling the non-source items' colliders**, the other way to make "only the highlighted item is draggable" literally true: `BoardItemDragHandler` is the single writer of its own collider (the wrong-order shake already documents that rule), and a tutorial reaching in would be the second. `EnsureCurrentTutorialStepIsPossible`'s abort stays as the safety net it was written to be — with this hole closed, board-to-board relocation already refused while armed, and powerups already refused during a forced move, no reachable path removes a step's source item any more. NOTE verification: `dotnet build Assembly-CSharp.csproj` → 0 errors (the one warning, `ContactFilter2D.NoFilter` at BoardItemDragHandler.cs:347, is pre-existing and untouched). Not exercised in the Unity Editor by me — affects: BoardItemDragHandler.cs, WorldTrayView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-09-02

D-157: **The tutorial ghost re-reads both of its endpoints on every lap, because a DOMove's start could not be re-read at all.** The user, 2026-09-02: "ilk tutorialdaki hayalet bilgisayarda doğru yerden başlıyor ama telefonda daha geriden başlıyor". `BuildGhost` built the loop as an `AppendCallback` that put the ghost back on the source item, followed by `ghostObject.transform.DOMove(end, ...)`, and its own comment claimed the callback made the origin self-correcting. **It did not: DOTween evaluates a Tweener's START value exactly once, at its first update (`Startup`), and a `Sequence` loop rewinds a nested tween without re-running that** — so the reposition was overwritten by the cached start on the very next update, and every lap set out from that one point. **The cached point was a spot the item was passing through.** `WorldTrayView` defers the whole spotlight one tween tick on purpose (BoardView creates item containers in its own `Start`, and Unity does not order `Start` across root objects), and at Day Start the item is still arcing in from BoardView's `startingPoint` on a `DOJump` over `PopInDuration` — so the ghost's origin was a point on that arc. **It presented as a phone-only bug because it genuinely is aspect-dependent**, which is the part worth keeping: `startingPoint` is a fixed scene transform, while a cell's world position is camera-derived (`BoardView` computes `cellSize` and `boardOrigin` from `cam.aspect`; cellSize is 1.09 at 9:16 against 1.46 at 3:4), so the arc's length — and the absolute distance any given fraction of it represents — changes with the screen's shape. Small enough to pass unnoticed in a wide editor window, plainly "further back" at 9:16. Fix: the travel is a `DOVirtual.Float(0,1)` driving an explicit `Vector3.Lerp(travelStart, trayTarget.position, progress)`, with `travelStart` written by the lap callback — so both ends are live values read on every update and nothing can be captured mid-animation again. **Duration and `Ease.InOutSine` are unchanged, so the motion itself is identical**; only the origin moved. The DESTINATION was not stale today (`PlayTrayEntrance` only scales and fades a tray, "right at rest position (no movement)") and is read live anyway, because it costs nothing inside the same expression and it stops a tray that ever learns to move from re-opening this. A lap that begins after the item is destroyed leaves `travelStart` at its previous value deliberately — the ghost finishes the arc it was drawing rather than snapping to the origin. NOTE the general rule this leaves behind: **inside a looping DOTween Sequence, only the END of a shortcut tween is a value you may choose; the START is whatever the transform held at the first update.** Anything that must vary per lap has to drive the property itself. NOTE verification: `dotnet build Assembly-CSharp.csproj` → 0 errors (the one warning, `ContactFilter2D.NoFilter` at BoardItemDragHandler.cs:347, is pre-existing). DOTween ships here as a DLL, so the Startup/loop semantics above are its documented behaviour rather than something read out of source; the replacement is correct either way. Not exercised on a device by me — affects: TutorialSpotlightView.cs, codemap-ui.md — 2026-09-02

D-158: **Every canvas in the game is 1080 reference units wide, on every device — the height is what varies.** The user, 2026-09-02: "ya ben prefabları düzenledim ama boyutları benim prefabda gördüğümden farklı görünüyor oyunda". Two independent causes behind one symptom, both in `CanvasScaler` and neither in code — verified first by ruling code out: no popup script writes `sizeDelta` or `localScale` anywhere, and every `SetParent` in the project already passes `worldPositionStays: false`, so the classic reparent-rescale trap was not it. **(1) Nine prefab canvases, `MainScreen`'s `Canvas` and `InGameCanvas` used `MatchWidthOrHeight: 0.5`**, which blends the width and height ratios (`scaleFactor = (w/1080)^(1-m) * (h/1920)^m`) and therefore makes the canvas's runtime rectangle depend on the device's ASPECT: 978x2120 reference units on a 1179x2556 phone, 966x2147 on a 1080x2400 one, and exactly 1080x1920 only at 9:16. So a panel authored at 885.6 wide — 82% of the reference width in the prefab window — renders at 91% on a tall phone, and a 1250-tall one drops from 65% to 59%. Nothing is "resized"; the rectangle it is measured against moved. Match 0 pins the canvas to exactly 1080 units wide everywhere and lets the height grow on tall screens, which is the right trade for a PORTRAIT game with centred, fixed-width panels: the constrained dimension is width, and extra height reads as slack rather than as distortion. **(2) SampleScene's `Popup Canvas` was `ConstantPixelSize`**, i.e. one UI unit = one device pixel, no scaling at any resolution — and `NoKeysPopup`, `GameOverPopup` and `DayCompletePopup` all sit on it. That is a strictly worse version of the same bug (a 885.6-wide panel would be 118% of an iPhone SE's width and 75% of an iPhone 15's), and it is why the fix could not stop at the match value. It is now `ScaleWithScreenSize` 1080x1920 match 0 like everything else. **`Backgroundcanvas` and `ShopCanvas` are misconfigured the same way and were deliberately LEFT** — `Background` is inset by `-191, -413` in what are currently raw pixels, and the shop is a whole screen; both need a look with the user's eyes rather than a batch edit, and neither was in the report. NOTE the numbers were computed against four real device resolutions rather than reasoned about, because the whole failure mode here is that the aspect-blend's effect is invisible until you put a number on it. NOTE this is a VISIBLE change by construction — anything that had quietly settled at the 0.5 blend moves, and the three popups on `Popup Canvas` had never been scaled at all. NOTE the editor-side half that no file can carry: the Game view must be set to a **1080x1920 portrait** resolution, since that is the rectangle every prefab is authored against and a Game view at any other aspect will still disagree with the prefab window. NOTE two adjacent findings left open, both reported and neither touched: ProjectSettings permits autorotation to BOTH landscape orientations for what is an entirely portrait UI, and nothing in the project reads `Screen.safeArea` — affects: 9 UI prefabs, MainScreen.unity, SampleScene.unity — 2026-09-02

D-159: **A modal popup is responsible for the taps that land beside it.** The user, 2026-09-02: "ya bu metada buy dedikten sonra onay ekranı sırasında continue day butonuna basılabiliyor bu olmasın basılamasın popup dışı basıldıysa sadece popupdan çıksın". The meta shop's confirm popup could be tapped *through*: `ConfirmPopup` is a full-screen stretch RectTransform carrying **no Graphic at all**, so a tap beside its Box hit nothing, fell through the canvas, and reached `PlayButton` — a sibling of the whole shop under the same Canvas — which starts the day. Worse, `ApplyVisibility` deliberately switches the shop's own full-screen backdrop (`panel`) OFF while a preview is up, so for the whole life of the popup nothing on this screen was holding the raycast. `MetaShopView` now builds a transparent full-screen `Button` as `ConfirmPopup`'s **first child** in `Start` (`CreateConfirmCatcher`) and points its click at the existing `ClosePreview`. **This reverses D-038**, which deleted the authored backdrop on the reasoning that CANCEL should be the only way out and that swallowing taps "costs nothing, because the ScrollRect is already disabled" — the second half was the error, since the ScrollRect was never the only thing underneath. Three properties of the placement carry the fix and are written into the method: *inside* `confirmPopup`, so its visibility is the popup's own `SetActive` and not a second state to keep in step (D-024's leak, in a new costume); *first sibling*, because UGUI hit-tests later siblings over earlier ones, so Box's BUY and CANCEL keep winning their own taps; *stretched 0..1*, because `ConfirmPopup` is itself anchored 0..1 with a zero sizeDelta. **Built in code rather than authored**, which is the one thing here worth arguing with later: a serialized field would be a slot the scene does not fill, and D-038 is the proof — the last backdrop on this popup was a scene object and a scene object is exactly what got deleted. `MetaGroundsView.CreateSkipCatcher` made the same call for the same reason. Fully transparent rather than a dim, because the box is placed over the ghost so the player can see where the prop would stand, and dimming the map would hide the subject of the preview. CANCEL stays a REQUIRED reference: the catcher is invisible, and "tap somewhere" is not discoverable.

D-160: **A shop lists what is for sale, so a powerup that has not been introduced is not a row at all.** The user, 2026-09-02: "ya bu powerup shopta eğer powerup tanıtılmadıysa listelenmese ya". **This does not change the rule, only what it looks like** — the lock D-117 built is intact and still spelled once in `PowerupConfig.IsUnlockedOnDay`, still read through `PowerupManager.IsUnlocked`, still guarded in `Buy` as well as in `RefreshAll`. What changed is the appearance of a locked row: D-117's half-measure showed the powerup's name, an owned count of `0`, no price and a dead BUY, which the player reads as a broken row rather than as a locked one. **The prefabs proved it was never finished**: `lockOverlay` and `lockLabel` were `{fileID: 0}` in both `PowerupShop.prefab` and `HUDCanvas.prefab`, so the lock badge D-117 designed had never actually been authored and the row had nothing on it saying why it refused. **The row hides at its ROOT, not by blanking its contents**: the three rows sit under a layout group, so a hidden row closes its gap where three emptied labels would leave a band. That needed a new serialized `GameObject` per row rather than `buyButton.transform.parent` — the code would otherwise be asserting a hierarchy it cannot see, and one reparented button would hide the wrong object or the whole sheet. All six slots were wired directly in the two prefabs (each row root resolved as the parent of that row's already-wired Buy button), so nothing was left for the author to drag. **THE OPEN BUTTON GOES WITH THE LAST ROW, and that is the half worth arguing with later**: the three powerups are introduced on Days 7, 8 and 10, so for the first six Days this ask leaves the sheet with a title and nothing under it — a store button that opens emptiness is a worse thing to have built than the rows it replaced. `PowerupShopView` therefore hides `openButton.gameObject` when it lists nothing, which is why it now also subscribes to `CurrentDayIndexChanged` (the event `PowerupBarView` D-117 and `DayNumberView` already use): the rows never needed it, since `Open` refreshes before the panel appears, but the button is on screen continuously and `AdvanceToNextDay` moves the index without reloading the scene. **Only the main screen is affected by that** — `SampleScene` removes `PowerupShopButton` from its instance outright, and the day scene's own path cannot reach an empty shop, since a locked powerup in the bar is not pressable, so whatever opened the shop is itself a listed row. **`lockOverlay`/`lockLabel` were kept, unreached**, as the degradation for a forgotten `root`: a row that cannot hide is still visible and still refuses to sell, and that row should say why. `PowerupBarView`'s lock is untouched and is the one that still draws in the normal case — the bar teaches a powerup, the shop sells it. affects: `PowerupShopView.cs`, `PowerupShop.prefab`, `HUDCanvas.prefab`.

D-161: **The powerup shop's layout is authored in its prefab, and neither scene keeps a copy of it.** The user, 2026-09-03: "ya ana menüde bu shop farklı gözüküyor oyun içinde farklı ama prefab aynı değil mi". The prefab WAS the same -- `PowerupShop.prefab`, instanced in both scenes -- and the difference was entirely in the instances' `m_Modifications`. **The measurement is the decision**: `MainScreen` carried 152 property overrides and every single one was byte-identical to the prefab, while `SampleScene` carried 174 identical ones plus exactly three that differed -- `openButton: none` (deliberate, D-105) and the shop root's `m_SizeDelta` at `1250 x 2500` against the prefab's `978 x 2118`. That one pair was the whole visible difference: every object in the sheet is anchored to that root, so the day scene's shop was ~28% wider and ~18% taller than the menu's. **The identical overrides were the more expensive half**, and they are why this was reported as intermittent rather than as a layout bug: a recorded override wins over the prefab even when its value matches today, so a prefab edit to any of those properties silently does not reach that scene. The locked set covered the three row roots, `Header`, and every row child (`Icon`, `Name`, `Owned`, `Price`, `Star`, `Buy`) -- so the shop looked edit-proof from the inside out, while `PowerupShopButton`, `Image`, `Panel`, `Title`, `CloseButton`, `Rows` and `Label` were free and did propagate. This is the same mechanism as D-158's "prefabları düzenledim ama oyunda farklı görünüyor", one layer down. **176 and 152 entries were removed, and exactly four references kept**: `sessionHost` in both (a scene object can only be a per-scene override), plus `openButton: none` and the redundant-but-harmless `panel` in `SampleScene`, and `m_Name`. `m_RemovedGameObjects` was left alone, so the day scene still has no `PowerupShopButton` at all. The edit is deletions only -- 608 and 704 lines, zero added -- and `MainScreen` therefore changed by nothing at all, since all 152 of its overrides were no-ops. **STILL OPEN, deliberately not touched: there is a SECOND shop.** `HUDCanvas.prefab` authors a whole `PowerupShop` hierarchy of its own, with its own `PowerupShopView` (`m_PrefabInstance: 0`, and the prefab's guid appears nowhere in that file). HUDCanvas is instanced in both scenes, so each screen runs two `PowerupShopView`s; nothing opens the HUDCanvas one, because `SampleScene` overrides `PowerupBarView.shop` to point at the `PowerupShop.prefab` instance instead. Deleting a duplicate hierarchy is a structural decision, not a layout fix, so it was reported rather than done -- and it is why D-160 wired `root` into both files. affects: `MainScreen.unity`, `SampleScene.unity`.

D-162: **A day is dealt, not started: every ticket clock is held until the player's first accepted input.** The user, 2026-09-03: "ya bu oyuna geçtiğimizde süreler ilk inputa kadar başlamasa ya". **The whole change is one bool and one early return, because this game has exactly one clock**: `GameManager.Update` → `TicketSlotManager.Tick(Time.deltaTime)` is the only `Time.deltaTime` in the runtime outside animation code — nothing else in the game moves on its own (the same fact D-094's comment rests on), so one gate freezes every timer there is. **It is its own flag, `GameState.IsAwaitingFirstInput`, and NOT a fourth entry in `GameManager.pauseHolders`** — the holder set (D-105) would have done it in two lines, and the argument against it is already written on that Update line: all four gates mean "hold the ticket clock", they are held by different things for different reasons, and a combined condition makes it impossible to tell from a stuck clock which one forgot to let go. The second reason is semantic and reaches another file: `IsPaused` means "a panel is up over a running day", `SettingsPopupView` reads it that way, and borrowing it would have made a freshly opened day indistinguishable from an open menu. **"First input" means the first press the game ACCEPTS as play, and both callers report it after their own existing refusal gate.** `BoardItemDragHandler.OnPointerDown` reports right after `pickupRefused` — a press the Continue popup or the tutorial swallowed produced no move, so it must not start the clock either, while a press that got through has started the day whatever becomes of the gesture (a pickup snapped straight back still counts; the board has been touched, which is what was being waited for). `PowerupBarView.OnPressed` is the second caller and it exists for a concrete hole rather than for symmetry: **Auto-Collect fills a tray**, so without it a player could deliver whole orders with every clock frozen. That one now READS `TryUse`'s return value, which restates the "a press with nothing to collect costs no charge" rule (GDD 5.2) one more time — an effect that did not run changed nothing and starts nothing. Its empty-charge branch, which opens the shop, deliberately reports nothing: a player buying a powerup before their first move has not made one, and the shop holds the clock itself while it is open (D-105), so closing it leaves the day still waiting. **Armed at all four day starts, not just at scene load** — `Awake`, `RetryDay`, `RetryCompletedDay`, `AdvanceToNextDay`, each beside the `DayLifecycleManager.ResetForNewDay` line they already share. Four call sites rather than one shared helper because there is no shared day-start method in this class: the four paths disagree about what they pay out, revert and persist, and the reset block is the only thing they agree on. The retry paths are where it matters most, since all three are reached from a popup the player is mid-tap on — without the hold, the new day's clock starts under a finger that has not seen the new board. In `Awake` it sits BEFORE `FillEmptySlots`, so the very first frame of a day is a still one. **What was deliberately left running: a Continue resuming MID-day.** D-099 already decided what happens at that moment (the deferred timeouts resolve before the clock restarts), and re-arming the hold there is a different question about a day that is already underway — raised with the user and left for them to ask for. Nothing is shown on screen: no "tap to start" prompt, because a board of full timer bars that visibly begin to drain on the first touch is the same information without a label.

D-163: **In the meta shop, the fade moved off the ROW and onto the BUY.** The user, 2026-09-03: "metashopta eger item alinamiyorsa tamami fade olmak yerine sadece buy butonu kirmizi olup fade olsun". Ş5 (D-035) dimmed the whole row through a `CanvasGroup` on its root, on the reasoning that alpha over the panel's dark background reads as darker — which it does, but it dims the wrong things: the icon, the name and the price are exactly what makes a prop worth saving for, and a row that goes pale as a whole reads as the prop being withdrawn from sale rather than as one the player cannot afford YET. The fade now sits on the BUY button alone, on top of the red D-037 already paints it, so the two signals say the same thing about the same control instead of the fade talking about the row while the colour talks about the button. **Nothing else about D-035 changed:** still dimmed and never disabled, because that BUY has to keep opening the preview — seeing where a prop would stand and what it costs is the whole reason to save for it, and a disabled row is dead furniture. The popup's own BUY is still what refuses, and visibly. The load-bearing choice is that the `CanvasGroup` carrying the fade is **BUILT on the buy button at runtime, not authored** (`EnsureBuyGroup`: `GetComponent` then `AddComponent`, idempotent, called from `Awake` and again from `Bind` so a caller that ever binds before activating cannot silently lose the fade). This screen has now made that same call three times — `MetaShopView.CreateConfirmCatcher` (D-159) and `MetaGroundsView.CreateSkipCatcher` are the others — for one reason: the row template lives in the SCENE, so a serialized slot is a slot the next re-author can forget to fill, and the bug comes back with nothing reporting it. A `CanvasGroup`'s defaults are what make it safe to add blind — `interactable` and `blocksRaycasts` both start true, so the unaffordable BUY keeps its tap and only `alpha` is ever written. The `group` field is gone from `MetaShopRowView`; the row-root `CanvasGroup` still sitting in `MainScreen.unity` (authored alpha 1) is simply never written to again, which is what let this ship with **no scene edit at all**. `unaffordableAlpha` (0.4) survives as the single knob and now means the BUY's alpha.

D-164: **An unaffordable BUY in the confirm popup starts the day.** The user, 2026-09-03, straight after D-163: "eger confirm popupta yine buy tiklarsa direkt oyuna yonlendirsin". Ş5 had **closed** that button — `confirmBuyButton.interactable = affordable` — and closed it for a good reason: it was dead, calling `Evaluate`, getting `NotEnoughMoney`, logging, and leaving the screen exactly as it was, which is a button that asks a question whose answer is already on screen. Making it live again is a **reversal, not a regression**, because the press now does the one thing that answers the refusal: it takes the player to a day, to go and earn the price. A disabled `Button` raises no `onClick` at all, so being pressable is the **mechanism** here rather than a look — which is also why the fade matters: with the disabled tint gone, an unaffordable BUY would come out BRIGHTER than an affordable one, so the red is now joined by an explicit alpha. That alpha is read off `MetaShopRowView.UnaffordableAlpha` rather than copied onto `MetaShopView`, so the row and the popup cannot come to disagree about a single refusal (D-037's argument about the two colours, applied to the third signal), and its `CanvasGroup` is **built** by `EnsureConfirmBuyGroup` for `CreateConfirmCatcher`'s reason — plus one more that only applies to a config-shaped value: a new serialized field can arrive from an already-authored scene as **0**, and a 0 here is an invisible button.

The redirect branches on the **verdict**, not on a second price-vs-balance comparison, because `MetaPurchase` is the single authority on why a buy is refused and a screen holding its own opinion is how the two drift apart. Only `NotEnoughMoney` redirects; `AlreadyOwned`, `AreaLocked` and `NotForSale` keep the buzz, since a trip to the kitchen does not fix any of them.

**The dependency arrow is inverted on purpose, and that is the load-bearing part.** "Go to the game" is not `SceneFlow.LoadDay()` — it is Play's two gates and then the load: the key gate (D-069) and the first-building gate (D-090), the latter sitting on the only path into the game. Calling the scene loader from the shop would have made this button a **bypass for D-090**. So the day has to be started through `MainScreenView.OnPlayClicked`, and the shop cannot hold a reference to `MainScreenView`: `MainScreen -> Tutorial -> MetaSystem` is an existing chain, so that field would close a cycle and break the one-directional invariant. `MetaShopView` therefore publishes `PlayRequested` (an `EventBus<MetaItemDefinition>`, payload = the prop they were looking at) and `MainScreenView` subscribes — every arrow still pointing `MainScreen -> MetaSystem`, the gates un-duplicated, and the shop never learning what starting a day involves. The subscriber field is **optional and fails to the OLD behaviour**: unwired, the request reaches nobody and the button logs its refusal as before. That is the correct direction to fail here, because the other one is a hole in the gate.

**Known corner, deliberately left alone:** a player who owns nothing and can afford nothing is redirected into D-090's gate and pointed back at the store — a loop. It is only reachable if the starting balance is below the cheapest prop, which is already a softlock without this change (they can neither buy nor play), and it is `MetaEconomyGUI`'s job to catch. The gate stays.

D-165: **A tray is a place you can take an item FROM, and Day 3 is where the game says so.** The user, 2026-09-03, after three passes at the shape: "kola 2. tepside başlasın hardallı sosisli boardda ve beklenen hardallıyı alıp ilk tepsiye koyacak sonra da tutorial triggerlanacak ve diyecek ki içeceği de buradan al". Day 3 is the Day that introduces the drink, so the lesson and the new item are the same object. **The step list gained its SECOND authored shape**, which is exactly the condition D-082 named as the thing that would justify one — it wrote "a second tutorial moment is the thing that would justify those, and it does not exist yet", and this is that moment.
